### [1] [Accelerating LLM Inference via Vector Index Based Output Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27460)
*Martin Loretz,Sepp Hochreiter*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They replace the expensive dense output projection of LLMs with an approximate nearest-neighbor search over token embeddings to speed up decoding without hurting quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive decoding in LLMs is bottlenecked by the large output embedding matrix, especially for small models with huge multilingual vocabularies on memory-limited hardware like CPUs. Reducing the cost of the output projection could significantly improve single-batch, low-latency inference, which is common in real-world applications.

Method: They reinterpret the output layer (logits = hidden_state × output_embedding^T plus top-k) as a maximum inner product search (MIPS) over token embeddings. Instead of computing logits for the full vocabulary, they build an HNSW-based approximate vector index over token embeddings. At each decoding step, they query this index with the current hidden state to retrieve only a small set of top candidate tokens, then scatter these retrieved scores back into a sparse full-vocabulary logit tensor so that existing decoding pipelines can be reused. They implement this for CPU inference and test it on several LLM families (Gemma 3, Llama 3.2, Qwen 3).

Result: On CPU, the new output head substantially speeds up the output projection step and, for batch-size-one decoding, increases end-to-end throughput, with up to 82% speedup for Gemma 3 270M. Despite using approximate search instead of exact dense projection, generation quality under AlpacaEval benchmarks remains effectively unchanged.

Conclusion: Approximate nearest-neighbor retrieval, implemented via an HNSW index over token embeddings, can serve as a practical, drop-in alternative to dense output projections for LLMs in latency-sensitive, small-batch decoding scenarios, offering large speedups without degrading evaluation-time generation quality.

Abstract: Large output embedding matrices create a significant memory bandwidth bottleneck during autoregressive decoding, especially for compact LLMs with large multilingual vocabularies. We reformulate the output projection followed by top-k token selection as a maximum inner product search over token embeddings and replace the dense vocabulary projection with an HNSW-based vector index. The resulting output head retrieves only a small candidate set of high-scoring tokens and can be integrated into existing decoding pipelines by scattering retrieved logits into a sparse full-vocabulary tensor. On CPU inference with Gemma 3, Llama 3.2, and Qwen 3 models, our method substantially accelerates the output projection and improves end-to-end batch-size-one decoding throughput by up to 82% for Gemma 3 270M, while preserving generation quality under AlpacaEval evaluation. These results suggest approximate retrieval is a practical alternative to dense output projections in latency-sensitive small-batch decoding.

</details>


### [2] [SciReC: Diagnostic Evaluation of Multimodal, Multi-Turn Relational Reasoning with Adaptive Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27461)
*Nilay Yilmaz,Naga Sai Abhiram Kusumba,Stella Wenxing Liu,Yezhou Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces SciReC, a multimodal academic dialogue benchmark, and DMRA, a diagnostic framework, to evaluate and analyze relational reasoning abilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). It finds that relational reasoning and memory are key bottlenecks, with varying weaknesses across relation types and domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Relational reasoning—understanding and manipulating relationships between concepts—is central to higher-order cognition, yet it is underexplored in the context of multimodal large language models. Existing benchmarks often provide coarse scores and do not reveal which cognitive components (visual perception, knowledge, memory, or reasoning) cause failures. The authors aim to build a fine-grained, scientifically oriented benchmark and a diagnostic method to precisely locate the sources of MLLM weaknesses in relational reasoning across different relation types and academic domains.

Method: The authors construct SciReC, a multimodal academic dialog benchmark targeting different categories of relational reasoning (analogical, structural, cause-effect, spatial, hierarchical, sequential, etc.) across multiple scientific domains such as Astronomy and Psychology. They then propose DMRA, a deficit-based diagnostic framework that decomposes performance into contributions from visual understanding, knowledge expression, memory recall, and relational reasoning. By systematically perturbing or controlling these components and analyzing model errors, DMRA identifies which component most likely caused each failure. Multiple MLLMs, including proprietary and open-source models, are evaluated on SciReC under this framework.

Result: Claude 4.6 obtains the highest overall relational reasoning score (73%), with GPT 5.4 following at 68%. Open-source models perform particularly poorly on spatial relations, whereas proprietary models are more challenged by hierarchical and sequential relations. Domain-wise, performance is lowest in Astronomy and highest in Psychology. DMRA’s error attribution shows that relational reasoning itself is the dominant failure source across all tested models, with memory limitations being the second-largest contributor to errors.

Conclusion: MLLMs exhibit substantial and systematic weaknesses in relational reasoning, which remain even when visual perception, domain knowledge, and memory are comparatively less limiting. Different model families show distinct relational profiles (spatial vs. hierarchical/sequential weaknesses), and domain differences (e.g., Astronomy vs. Psychology) further modulate difficulty. SciReC and the DMRA framework provide tools for more nuanced evaluation and diagnosis, suggesting that future model development should focus explicitly on strengthening core relational reasoning capabilities and improving memory handling, rather than only scaling or adding more data.

Abstract: Relational reasoning requires the process of perceptual understanding, comparing, and integrating the underlying relationships between concepts. This ability consists of multiple categories, such as analogical, structural, and cause-effect, each capturing a different aspect of higher-order understanding. To examine the performance of multimodal large language models (MLLM) on these relational inference tasks, we developed SciReC, a model-adaptive multimodal academic dialog benchmark. As the relational reasoning process involves multiple representations and various factors (visual understanding, exhibiting knowledge, and memory recall), we propose DMRA, a deficit-based diagnostic framework that quantifies the contribution of these components to identify the primary cause of unsuccessful cases. Claude 4.6 achieved the best performance on the overall relational score with 73\%, followed by GPT 5.4 with 68\%. Performance trends indicate that open-source models achieve their lowest scores on spatial relations, while proprietary models struggle more with hierarchical and sequential relations. Across domains, model performance is lowest on Astronomy and highest on Psychology. The results of DMRA reveal that relational reasoning is the primary source of error across all models, followed by memory limitations.

</details>


### [3] [Sledgehammer or Scalpel? A Fine-grained Adaptive Framework for Implicit Hate Speech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27462)
*Han Wang,Yuhu Cheng,Xuesong Wang,Yi Zhu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes FAID, an adaptive framework for detecting implicit hate speech by first categorizing text into three fine-grained types and then applying tailored reasoning strategies to balance accuracy and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Implicit hate speech is hard to detect because it hides malicious intent in indirect language, metaphors, or requires external context. Current PLM/LLM methods treat all inputs uniformly with a single reasoning process, which fails to capture nuanced forms of hate and wastes computation on simple cases. The authors aim to better model the varied nature of online hate while using computation more efficiently.

Method: The authors define three fine-grained categories of implicit hate speech: Shallow (surface cues reveal intent), Targeted (malice tied to concealed or oblique targets), and Context-Dependent (requires external background to interpret as hateful). They propose FAID, a two-stage framework: (1) a fine-grained classifier assigns a category; (2) category-specific processing is applied. For Shallow samples, they use lightweight prompt-tuning for fast classification. For Targeted samples, they add knowledge augmentation that iteratively refines the model with external or derived knowledge to expose hidden targets. For Context-Dependent samples, they use an agentic framework that automatically generates and updates prompts to construct missing context, infer background information, and reason about ambiguous malicious intent.

Result: On four benchmark implicit hate speech datasets, FAID achieves significantly better performance than state-of-the-art baselines, indicating both improved detection effectiveness and more efficient use of reasoning resources across different sample types.

Conclusion: Implicit hate speech appears in multiple nuanced forms that benefit from differentiated processing. By first performing fine-grained categorization and then adapting detection strategies to each type, FAID better uncovers hidden malicious intent while avoiding unnecessary heavy reasoning on simple cases. The adaptive architecture yields state-of-the-art results, demonstrating the value of category-aware, resource-sensitive approaches to implicit hate speech detection.

Abstract: Unlike explicit attacks with obvious profanity, implicit hate speech hides malice within seemingly compliant expressions through metaphors and contextual hints, making its detection in online content review challenging. While existing PLM- or LLM-based methods perform well, they typically apply a single reasoning process to all samples. This overlooks fine-grained linguistic nuances and causes unnecessary computation for simpler cases. We observe that online hate speech is not monolithic but manifests in varied forms. We therefore define three fine-grained categories: Shallow, Targeted, and Context-Dependent. Accordingly, we propose Fine-grained Adaptive Implicit Hate speech Detection (FAID), a novel framework that first performs fine-grained classification and then adapts to specific categories. Specifically, for Shallow samples with surface-identifiable intents, the framework adopts lightweight prompt-tuning for rapid classification; for Targeted comments that bind malicious intent to concealed targets, we design knowledge augmentation to iteratively refine the model and reveal hidden targets; for Context-Dependent comments lacking background information, we utilize an agentic framework that automatically generates prompts to evolve context, infer missing background information and identify ambiguous malicious intents. This adaptive architecture focuses computational resources on complex implicit samples while avoiding redundant reasoning for shallow samples. Experiments on four benchmark datasets demonstrate that FAID significantly outperforms SOTA baselines.

</details>


### [4] [The Effect of Emotional Context on Large Language Models' Endorsement of Premature Decisions: Comparing Emotional Vulnerability Across Six Commercial Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27465)
*Cheolho Shin,Yoojin Han,Donghun Shin,Kunho Lee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that when users express distress, large language models become more likely to encourage risky, premature decisions, even when the facts are unchanged.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly used for high‑stakes personal decisions, it is important to understand whether users’ emotional expressions can systematically bias model advice, potentially leading to unsafe or overly agreeable recommendations.

Method: The authors constructed three realistic decision scenarios (career change, business expansion, emigration) and three emotional conditions (cold, neutral multi‑turn, and distress). They queried six commercial LLMs from major providers, fully crossing scenarios, models, and conditions with multiple repetitions (324 conversations total). They then automatically scored the strength of decision endorsement on a 0–100 scale using an eight‑item rubric, and analyzed the effect of emotional expression using mixed‑effects models, controlling for conversation length via the neutral multi‑turn condition. They validated the automated scoring with an independent judge model and human coders.

Result: Emotional distress substantially increased endorsement of premature decisions: average endorsement rose from 18.6 (neutral) to 31.5 (distress), a 12.9‑point increase (mixed‑effects β = 12.9, p < .001, Cohen’s d = 0.51). Differences between cold and neutral conditions were not significant (p = .083), suggesting conversation length alone does not explain the effect. Five of six models showed significant vulnerability, including top‑tier models Gemini 3.1 Pro and GPT‑5.5, while Claude Opus showed no significant change. The automated scoring correlated highly with an independent non‑Google judge model (ρ = .89) and with two human coders (ρ = .70).

Conclusion: Emotional context, specifically expressions of distress, systematically increases sycophantic endorsement of risky, premature decisions by many commercial LLMs, including top‑tier systems, independent of conversation length. This highlights a safety vulnerability whereby emotionally distressed users may receive more affirming—and potentially harmful—advice, underscoring the need for emotion‑robust alignment methods and evaluation protocols.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for everyday decision-making advice, whether a model shifts the direction of its advice according to the user's emotional state has become an important safety problem. We test whether emotional expression increases a model's endorsement (encouragement to proceed) when a user, holding the same objective information, is overconfident about a premature decision (e.g., quitting a stable job on weak evidence). As a key control, we include a no-emotion multi-turn (neutral) condition that holds factual content and the number of conversational turns constant, isolating the effect of emotion from that of conversation length. We exposed six commercial models (top-tier and mid-tier models from OpenAI, Anthropic, and Google) to three scenarios (career change, business expansion, emigration) across three conditions (cold/neutral/distress) with six repetitions each, yielding 324 conversations, and measured endorsement strength (0-100) via an eight-item rubric-based automated scoring. Emotional expression significantly increased endorsement (neutral 18.6 to distress 31.5, +12.9 points; mixed-effects $β= +12.9$, $p < .001$; Cohen's d = 0.51), and this was not explained by conversation length (cold-neutral difference non-significant, $p = .083$). Critically, the vulnerability varied by individual model rather than by price tier: five of six models showed a significant emotion effect, including the top-tier flagships Gemini 3.1 Pro and GPT-5.5, while only Claude Opus showed no significant change. Results were reproduced with an independent non-Google judge model ($ρ= .89$) and agreed in rank with two human coders ($ρ= .70$). Through a controlled design that separates emotion from conversational context, we show that emotional context increases LLM sycophancy even in top-tier flagship models.

</details>


### [5] [PACE: Publisher-Adaptive Content Extraction via Agentic Automation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27466)
*Zhanlin Liu,Munirathnam Srikanth*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PACE is an agentic framework that learns publisher-specific web content extraction configurations using LLMs once, then runs a fast deterministic extractor at scale without further LLM calls, achieving near–hand-engineered quality for text, metadata, and multimodal content.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable, large-scale web content extraction is crucial for building LLM training and evaluation datasets, but current approaches face trade-offs: generic extractors are brittle on diverse page layouts and rich content types; direct LLM-based extraction is accurate and flexible but too costly and slow at scale; and manually engineered, publisher-specific parsers are accurate but expensive to build and maintain. The paper aims to eliminate this trade-off by automating the creation of high-quality, publisher-tailored extractors that are cheap to run.

Method: PACE uses LLMs during an offline training phase to analyze a small set of representative pages for each publisher along with user-specified extraction requirements (e.g., article body, metadata fields, images, tables). The agentic system explores DOM structures, identifies stable patterns, and aggregates them into reusable, publisher-specific extraction configurations. These configurations parameterize a fixed deterministic extractor template (e.g., rules over DOM paths, attributes, and layout signals). At inference time, extraction runs purely through this deterministic template, without further LLM calls, enabling high-throughput, low-latency extraction across many pages of the same publisher.

Result: Across benchmarks involving article text, metadata, and multimodal elements (images and tables), PACE outperforms generic, non-manual extraction baselines that are scalable but not publisher-specialized, and approaches the performance of manually engineered publisher-specific parsers. It consistently yields stronger extraction quality for article bodies, key metadata fields, and non-textual content, demonstrating good generality over multiple publishers and content targets.

Conclusion: Agentic configuration learning—using LLMs once to induce publisher-specific extraction rules and then deploying them in a deterministic extractor—can effectively automate high-quality web content extraction. PACE shows that this approach scales while retaining accuracy close to hand-written parsers and extends reliably beyond article text to metadata and multimodal content, making it well-suited for constructing LLM-ready web page representations at large scale.

Abstract: Web content extraction is essential for reliable LLM data pipelines, yet existing methods often struggle to jointly satisfy accuracy, scalability, and adaptability. General-purpose extractors can be applied broadly, but they are often brittle on publisher-specific layouts and richer extraction targets such as metadata, images, and tables. Direct LLM-based extraction offers greater flexibility, but incurs substantial cost and latency at scale, while manually engineered publisher-specific parsers can achieve high accuracy but require substantial human effort to build and maintain.
  We introduce PACE, an agentic framework for learning publisher-specific extraction configurations from representative pages and user requirements. During training, PACE uses LLMs to analyze page structure and aggregate reusable extraction patterns. At inference time, the learned configurations instantiate a fixed deterministic extractor template, enabling scalable extraction without additional LLM calls.
  Experiments spanning article-body, metadata, and multimodal extraction show that PACE outperforms scalable non-manual baselines while approaching the quality of manually engineered publisher-specific parsers. PACE achieves stronger extraction of article text, metadata, images, and tables, demonstrating that agentic configuration learning can automate publisher-specific extraction for LLM-ready page representations beyond article text.

</details>


### [6] [UIC-AIHealth4All at ArchEHR-QA 2026: Answer-First Evidence Grounding for Clinical Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27467)
*Mohammad Arvan,Hossein Haeri,Natalie Parde,Rebecca T. Feinstein*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: UIC-AIHealth4All builds an answer-first LLM pipeline for the ArchEHR-QA 2026 shared task, achieving competitive performance in evidence identification and answer-evidence alignment while revealing that model answers are notably less readable than clinician-written references.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve grounded question answering over electronic health records by better exploiting the asymmetry between judging evidence relevance in the abstract versus relative to a concrete answer, and to understand the readability gap between LLM-generated clinical answers and clinician-authored references.

Method: They design an answer-first pipeline for Subtasks 2 and 3 where a model first generates candidate answers and cites supporting note sentences, then classifies the final evidence set based on these candidates. For Subtask 4, they use self-consistency voting across five independent model calls to keep answer–evidence links that meet a vote threshold. They also perform a post-hoc linguistic and readability analysis using 45 stylistic features, including Flesch-Kincaid grade level, comparing generated outputs to clinician-written references.

Result: The system ranks third in evidence identification (Strict Micro F1 62.90), ninth in answer generation (Overall 31.90), and fifth in answer–evidence alignment (F1 79.81) on ArchEHR-QA 2026. The linguistic analysis shows that, controlling for word and sentence counts, model outputs are on average 3.2 Flesch-Kincaid grade levels harder to read than clinician references.

Conclusion: An answer-first, evidence-aware pipeline with self-consistency voting is competitive for grounded QA over EHRs, especially for evidence identification and alignment. However, current LLM-generated clinical answers are substantially less readable than clinician-authored text, indicating that explicit optimization for readability should be incorporated into future clinical NLP systems.

Abstract: We describe the UIC-AIHealth4All system for ArchEHR-QA 2026, a shared task on grounded question answering from electronic health records. We participated in Subtasks 2 (evidence identification), 3 (answer generation), and 4 (answer-evidence alignment). For Subtasks 2 and 3, we propose an answer-first pipeline in which the model generates candidate answers citing specific note sentences before classifying the full evidence set, exploiting the asymmetry between judging relevance in the abstract versus relative to a generated answer. For Subtask 4, we apply self-consistency voting over five independent model calls, retaining links by vote threshold. Our pipeline ranked third on evidence identification (Strict Micro F1 62.90), ninth on answer generation (Overall 31.90), and fifth on answer-evidence alignment (F1 79.81). A post-hoc linguistic analysis of 45 stylistic features reveals that model outputs remain 3.2 Flesch-Kincaid grade levels harder to read than clinician-authored references despite matching their word and sentence counts, suggesting readability warrants explicit optimization in clinical NLP systems. Code and prompts are available at https://github.com/mo-arvan/archehr-qa-2026-uic-aihealth4all.

</details>


### [7] [Select, Don't Train: The Benefits of Modular Entity Disambiguation with LLM-Based Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27470)
*Fina Polat,Daniel Daza,Pengyu Zhang,Klim Zaporojets,Paul Groth*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies entity disambiguation by decoupling candidate retrieval from LLM-based selection and finds that simple, training-free retrieval (BM25) combined with a strong LLM selector can match or surpass systems with trained dense retrievers, especially when abstention is allowed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural entity disambiguation systems usually treat the problem as a single task optimized in a shared embedding space, conflating candidate retrieval and fine-grained selection. This creates maintenance cost when knowledge graphs evolve and may not be optimal when large language models are used for selection. There is a lack of systematic analysis of how different retrieval strategies interact with an LLM-based selector, and current systems cannot abstain when the correct entity is not retrieved, leading to forced wrong predictions.

Method: The authors construct a two-stage ED pipeline that cleanly separates candidate retrieval from entity selection. They plug multiple retrieval strategies—sparse retrieval via BM25, web-based knowledge base search, and a state-of-the-art trained dense retriever—into a shared selection component implemented with several open- and closed-source LLMs. They then perform a controlled empirical comparison of these retrieval methods under identical LLM-based selection, focusing on the ZELDA benchmark and also designing an evaluation setting that rewards correct abstentions when retrieval fails.

Result: With a capable LLM as the selector, a training-free BM25 retriever attains a new state of the art on the ZELDA benchmark, improving in-KB micro-F1 from 82.3 to 86.3 (+4 points). Using the same LLM selector with a trained dense retriever further improves performance to 88.5 micro-F1, indicating that training the retriever yields only modest additional gains beyond BM25 in this setting. Moreover, when the evaluation accounts for the ability to abstain on instances where the correct entity is not among the retrieved candidates, the BM25 + LLM pipeline achieves 90.7 F1.

Conclusion: Separating retrieval from selection in entity disambiguation reveals that, once selection is performed by a strong LLM, simple, training-free retrieval methods like BM25 can be highly competitive with costly trained dense retrievers, both in performance and maintenance. Dual-encoder approaches that jointly optimize retrieval and selection are not strictly necessary to reach state-of-the-art results. The decoupled framework also enables principled abstention when retrieval fails, addressing a key limitation of current ED systems and yielding improved performance in settings that value calibrated non-answers.

Abstract: Entity Disambiguation (ED) is a key task for constructing and using knowledge graphs. State-of-the-art neural approaches commonly model ED as a single task, although it consists of two distinct subproblems: retrieving candidate entities and selecting the correct one given context. Dual-encoder models optimize for both within a shared embedding space, forcing representations to balance high-recall retrieval with fine-grained selection, and they require trained retrievers, which are costly to maintain as knowledge graphs change. While recent work has begun to combine retrievers with LLM-based selectors, the interplay between the two stages has not been studied systematically. In this paper, we present a systematic comparison of retrieval strategies for candidate generation under a shared LLM-based selection stage, combining sparse retrieval (BM25), Web KB search, and a state-of-the-art trained dense retriever with several open- and closed-source LLMs. We show that, once selection is delegated to a capable LLM, training the retriever provides only modest additional value: a fully training-free BM25 retriever paired with an LLM selector reaches a new state of the art on the ZELDA benchmark, raising inKB micro-F1 from 82.3 to 86.3 (+4); pairing the same LLM with a trained dense retriever reaches 88.5. Decoupling retrieval from selection also exposes a limitation of current ED systems: when the correct entity is missing from retrieved candidates, they are forced to predict an incorrect entity. In contrast, our framework allows for abstention when retrieval failure is detected. In an evaluation setting that rewards correct abstentions, the training-free BM25 + LLM pipeline reaches 90.7 F1.

</details>


### [8] [XHotpotQA: A Benchmark for Cross-Lingual Knowledge Composition in Multi-Hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27481)
*Iman Barati,Arash Ghafouri,Behrouz Minaei-Bidgoli*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build XHotpotQA, a multilingual/mixed-language benchmark to test how QA systems combine evidence across languages, showing that current readers struggle when evidence and questions are in different languages or scripts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multilingual QA benchmarks typically translate entire examples into a single language. This masks the specific difficulty of composing evidence across language boundaries, where a question is in one language but supporting facts are in others. The authors want a controlled way to diagnose and quantify the impact of cross-lingual evidence composition on QA performance.

Method: They construct XHotpotQA, derived from HotpotQA but redesigned as mixed-language, multi-hop QA. Each instance is represented as an evidence-dependency graph, explicitly labeling the language of the question, bridge evidence, answer-bearing evidence, and distractors. The dataset includes 15,661 training and 7,405 validation examples with sentence-level supervision and supplied distractor sentences/paragraphs. They then evaluate multiple reader models and adapted evidence selectors under controlled conditions of language (and script) mismatch between question and evidence, comparing performance across different degrees of alignment.

Result: The resulting resource has strong cross-lingual properties: in validation, 99.81% of questions require crossing a language boundary to reach gold evidence, and 95.60% use gold paragraphs in different languages. Experiments with three reader systems show that when there is full mismatch between the question and the evidence language, reader answer F1 drops by about 10.25–15.79 points compared with partially aligned conditions; using different scripts causes even larger drops of 11.98–23.70 F1. In contrast, the adapted evidence selectors show only small performance contrasts (around 1.7–1.8 points) under these conditions.

Conclusion: XHotpotQA exposes substantial weaknesses in current QA readers’ ability to compose and reason over cross-lingual, cross-script evidence, while selectors are less affected under the supplied-candidate setup. The benchmark offers a role-aware, modular evaluation framework and a carefully audited testbed for developing and diagnosing knowledge-based systems that must integrate evidence across languages.

Abstract: Knowledge-intensive multi-hop question answering requires systems to select evidence and compose dependent facts, yet multilingual benchmarks usually translate an entire example into one language. This hides failures at language boundaries inside the reasoning chain. We introduce XHotpotQA, a controlled benchmark for cross-lingual knowledge composition over mixed-language evidence. Each instance is modeled as an evidence-dependency graph whose question, bridge evidence, answer-bearing evidence, and distractors have explicit language assignments. The audited resource contains 15,661 training and 7,405 validation instances, with sentence-level support supervision and supplied distractors. In validation, 99.81% of items cross the question-to-gold-evidence language interface and 95.60% use gold paragraphs in different languages. Across three reader artifacts, full question-evidence mismatch is associated with 10.25 to 15.79 lower Unicode-aware answer F1 than partial alignment, and different-script evidence with deficits of 11.98 to 23.70 points; the corresponding adapted-selector contrasts are 1.71 and 1.78 points. Under this supplied-candidate design, the evaluated readers therefore show substantially larger condition-associated deficits than the selector. XHotpotQA provides role-aware diagnostics, modular evaluation, and an audited test bed for knowledge-based systems that must integrate evidence across languages.

</details>


### [9] [INSPIRE: An Internalize-Then-Improve Approach for Example-Driven Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27501)
*Shuai Wang,Jiayi Kuang,Yinghui Li,Haojing Huang,Xinnian Liang,Ying Shen,Liang Lin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose INSPIRE, a two-stage preference-optimization framework that trains LLMs to use example-based reasoning (e.g., counterexamples) for math, aiming to build real conceptual understanding rather than pattern-matching for final answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM math training focuses on final-answer accuracy, so models may just memorize solution templates instead of understanding concepts. In human learning, constructing and analyzing examples and counterexamples is a key indicator of deep understanding, but LLMs are weak at this. Training this ability with preference optimization is hard because (1) models are initially bad at generating good example-based reasoning, so it is difficult to form useful preference pairs, and (2) the skill should be learned progressively: first adopting the example-based strategy, then learning to execute it correctly.

Method: They introduce INSPIRE, an Internalize-Then-Improve framework. It has (a) Reference-Guided Student Internalization (RGSI), which generates high-quality candidate solutions for preference learning but constrained to lie within the policy model’s own distribution, and (b) a stage-wise rubric-based preference training: first a method-oriented stage that rewards using example-based strategies (like constructing counterexamples), then a correctness-oriented stage that further optimizes for mathematically correct usage of those strategies.

Result: Across various model sizes and architectures, applying INSPIRE yields consistent gains on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, with some models even outperforming larger open-source baselines that do not use this training scheme. These gains hold without sacrificing performance on standard math reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: Stage-wise preference optimization focused first on internalizing example-based reasoning strategies and then on their correctness can significantly strengthen LLMs’ conceptual mathematical reasoning. The approach improves performance and generalizes to out-of-distribution tasks while maintaining overall math abilities, suggesting that explicitly training example-based reasoning is a promising direction for more deeply understanding LLMs.

Abstract: Mathematical reasoning has seen rapid progress in large language models (LLMs), yet existing methods optimize predominantly for final-answer correctness, raising the question whether models truly internalize mathematical concepts or merely memorize solution patterns. In human mathematics education, example-based reasoning such as constructing counterexamples to test theorem boundaries reflects deep conceptual understanding, but remains underdeveloped in current LLMs. Enhancing this capability through preference optimization presents two key challenges: (1) the model's limited example-based reasoning ability makes constructing effective preference pairs inherently difficult; and (2) capability acquisition is progressive, as the model must first learn to adopt this strategy before learning to apply it correctly. Therefore we propose INSPIRE, an Internalize-Then-Improve approach combining Reference-Guided Student Internalization (RGSI), which produces high-quality preference candidates under the policy model's own distribution, with a stage-wise rubric preference training strategy that decomposes learning into method-oriented and correctness-oriented stages. Experiments across multiple model scales and families demonstrate consistent improvements, even surpassing larger open-source models, while evaluations on out-of-distribution benchmarks confirm no degradation in general mathematical reasoning ability.

</details>


### [10] [A Survey on Rubric-Guided Reinforcement Learning for Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27505)
*Zifei Shan,Fangning Shao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Survey of rubric-guided reinforcement learning as a structured, interpretable alternative to scalar rewards in RLHF for LLM alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional RLHF uses opaque scalar rewards that cannot adequately express the multi-dimensional nature of response quality and are hard to interpret or debug, limiting alignment reliability and flexibility.

Method: Propose a unified Bayesian framework where constitutions are modeled as priors over evaluation criteria P(R) and rubrics as data-conditioned samples R_x ~ P(R|x); build a taxonomy of existing and emerging methods (constitutional AI, instance-specific rubrics, process-level supervision, self-evolving rubrics, agentic and multimodal extensions) along a prior–posterior axis; and analyze rubrics as natural-language artifacts with linguistic tools.

Result: Provide a structured classification of rubric-guided RL methods, connect them via a common Bayesian perspective, and identify linguistic phenomena—granularity trade-offs, semantic drift, and linguistic reward hacking—that systematically affect how well rubrics align models with human intentions.

Conclusion: Viewing rubrics and constitutions through a Bayesian lens clarifies the design space of rubric-guided RL and highlights that the reliability of alignment depends critically on how rubrics are written, updated, and interpreted; several open problems in rubric design, robustness to linguistic drift, and prevention of reward hacking remain important directions for future research.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) has become the dominant paradigm for aligning large language models (LLMs) with human preferences. However, traditional RLHF relies on scalar reward signals that lack interpretability and fail to capture the multifaceted nature of response quality. Rubric-guided reinforcement learning addresses these limitations by introducing structured, interpretable evaluation criteria, or rubrics, as the backbone of reward design, feedback generation, and policy optimization. In this survey, we introduce a Bayesian framework that defines constitutions as prior distributions $P(R)$ over evaluation criteria and rubrics as conditional instantiations $R_x \sim P(R|x)$. Under this unified view, we present a taxonomy of rubric-guided RL along the prior-posterior axis, covering constitutional AI, instance-specific rubrics, process-level supervision, self-evolving rubrics, and their agentic and multimodal extensions. Furthermore, as rubrics are natural-language artifacts, we present a linguistic analysis of how granularity trade-offs, semantic drift, and linguistic reward hacking impact alignment reliability, identifying key open problems for future research.

</details>


### [11] [How Do Linear Probes Emerge? A Circuit-Tracing Framework with Concept-Targeted Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27510)
*Vedant Palit,Florent Draye,Terry Jingchen Zhang,Bernhard Schölkopf,Zhijing Jin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose Concept-Targeted Attribution (CTA), a method to build attribution graphs aimed at internal concept probes instead of output tokens, revealing which circuits make linear probes work and showing these circuits are predictive and mechanistically distinct from those controlling output tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing transcoder attribution graphs explain why a model picks a particular next token, but they do not explain why an internal concept representation (as detected by a probe) arises, nor do they connect probe performance to specific, interpretable circuit structures. This limits our ability to mechanistically understand and audit internal concept representations, including safety-relevant ones.

Method: They redefine the target of attribution graphs from output logits to directions given by linear probes (concept directions), creating Concept-Targeted Attribution (CTA). Using Cross-Layer Transcoders, they train probe-targeted attribution graphs and analyze them at two levels: (1) graph-level features to predict probe accuracy across four concept categories, and (2) local, per-prompt features to identify sparse components driving individual classifications. They additionally perform causal ablations on features identified as probe-relevant vs logit-relevant to study functional differences.

Result: Probe-targeted graphs exhibit strong predictive structure: simple graph-level metrics predict probe accuracy very well (Spearman ρ=0.91, R²=0.84) across four common concept categories. Local features reveal sparse components responsible for each prompt’s classification. Causal ablations show a functional separation between circuits relevant to probes and those relevant to logits: removing probe-relevant features lowers internal concept scores but leaves generated tokens mostly unchanged, while removing logit-relevant features alters the generated token in 92–100% of cases with almost no effect on probe scores.

Conclusion: CTA links linear probe performance to concrete, interpretable circuits, shifting from purely behavioral evaluation of probes to mechanistic explanations of why they work. Probe-targeted and logit-targeted graphs correspond to different underlying mechanisms, so internal concepts can be manipulated without necessarily changing outputs, and vice versa. This provides a more fine-grained way to audit and understand internal concept representations, including for safety-critical auditing scenarios, and offers a general framework and tooling (with released code) for probe-centric circuit analysis.

Abstract: Transcoder attribution graphs are usually trained to explain why a model assigns high probability to a particular next token. We introduce Concept-Targeted Attribution (CTA), which instead trains attribution graphs with respect to a linear probe direction. CTA therefore yields probe-specific circuits that explain why an internal concept representation arises in a prompt, independently of whether it is expressed in the generated token. Using Cross-Layer Transcoders, we show that these probe-targeted graphs contain predictive structure: graph-level features predict probe accuracy across four widely studied concept categories ($ρ= 0.91$, $R^2 = 0.84$), while local features identify the sparse components driving per-prompt classification. This connects probe performance to interpretable circuit structure, allowing us to ask not only whether a probe works, but which internal computations make it work. Causal ablations further show that probe-targeted and logit-targeted graphs capture functionally distinct mechanisms. Removing probe-relevant features reduces internal concept scores while largely preserving generated tokens, whereas removing logit-relevant features changes the generated token in 92% to 100% of cases with near-zero effect on probe scores. CTA provides a framework for moving from behavioral probe accuracy to mechanistic explanations of probe performance, enabling more detailed audits of internal concept representations, including safety-critical ones. Our code is available at https://github.com/vedantpalit/concept-targeted-attribution

</details>


### [12] [Trajectory-Level Speculative Decoding for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27514)
*Tianxiang Pan,Baitao Gong,Mo Guang,Hongwei Yong,Tianpeng Jiang,Yaqian Li,Zheng Cao,Kaiwen Long*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A speculative decoding framework for diffusion-based language models that accelerates parallel token generation by operating on entire denoising trajectories instead of single tokens, achieving substantial speedups with minimal accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion-based language models generate multiple tokens in parallel via iterative denoising, but when the model is uncertain, existing decoding methods revert to single-token generation. This collapse significantly reduces throughput and undermines the main benefit of diffusion-based generation compared to autoregressive models. There is a need for a decoding framework that can maintain high parallelism even in low-confidence settings while preserving exactness or near-exactness of the model’s predictive distribution.

Method: The paper introduces a trajectory-level speculative decoding framework for dLLMs. Instead of speculating over linear token sequences, it speculates over denoising trajectories, which are sequences of multi-token updates including explicit token positions and unmasking orders. Draft trajectories are constructed via confidence-stratified tree exploration, choosing branches according to model confidence. These drafts are then verified using blockwise parallel evaluation with bidirectional attention masking, allowing simultaneous validation of multiple token updates. The framework also adds inter-block speculation, using the bidirectional nature of diffusion models to look ahead across blocks of tokens. Theoretical analysis characterizes conditions under which the speculative procedure is exact and identifies a phenomenon called trajectory drift as the primary cost of aggressive parallelism. The method is implemented on top of the Fast-dLLM dual-cache infrastructure.

Result: Empirically, the framework reduces the number of denoising iterations by 30–40% and increases the average tokens-per-step from 2.6 to 4.3. Overall, it achieves a 7–14× speedup over vanilla dLLMs and about 1.3× over Fast-dLLM, with less than a 1% drop in accuracy on reasoning and code benchmarks.

Conclusion: Speculative decoding at the level of denoising trajectories can substantially improve the efficiency of diffusion-based language models while maintaining near-exact behavior. By leveraging confidence-stratified trajectory exploration, blockwise verification with bidirectional masking, and inter-block speculation, the approach preserves accuracy and theoretical correctness in many settings, and exposes trajectory drift as the main trade-off between speed and fidelity. This establishes a practical and principled path to making dLLMs competitive in throughput with highly optimized autoregressive LLMs.

Abstract: Diffusion-based language models (dLLMs) enable parallel token generation through iterative denoising, but existing decoding strategies collapse to single-token generation under low confidence, severely limiting throughput. Unlike autoregressive models where speculative decoding operates on token sequences in a fixed left-to-right order, dLLMs require speculating over denoising trajectories-sequences of multi-token updates with explicit positions and unmasking orders. We develop a trajectory-level speculative framework that constructs draft denoising trajectories via confidence-stratified tree exploration and verifies them through blockwise parallel evaluation with bidirectional attention masking. Our method further introduces inter-block speculation, exploiting diffusion models' bidirectional structure to perform cross-block lookahead. We formally characterize when this approach is exact and identify trajectory drift as the fundamental cost of increased parallelism. Building on Fast-dLLM's dual-cache infrastructure, our framework reduces denoising iterations by 30-40% and increases tokens-per-step from 2.6 to 4.3, achieving 7-14x speedup over vanilla dLLMs and 1.3x over Fast-dLLM with less than 1% accuracy change across reasoning and code benchmarks.

</details>


### [13] [When Tokenizers Fail: Byte-Level Chunking for Zero-Shot Transfer to Low-Resource Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27658)
*Sanjeev Kumar,Atsuki Yamaguchi,Nikolaos Aletras*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a lightweight hierarchical byte-level framework that aligns byte representations with a frozen subword-based language model to improve word-level tasks in low-resource, non-Latin-script languages, achieving notable gains (up to 13.3% on POS tagging).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Subword tokenization, trained mostly on high-resource languages, distorts frequency patterns and harms low-resource languages, especially script-sharing variants. Pure byte-level models avoid this bias but introduce a mismatch between byte granularity and word-level tasks in non-Latin scripts. Existing hierarchical byte models need huge training data and do not align well with frozen subword models. There is a need for a tokenizer-free method that can bridge byte and subword modalities efficiently and work well in low-resource, word-level morphological tasks.

Method: The authors design an adapted hierarchical byte-level network that operates directly on UTF-8 bytes but organizes them into word-aligned chunks. They initialize byte embeddings from the subword representations of a frozen base language model, thereby tying the byte space to the existing subword space. They introduce a chunk alignment loss that encourages dynamically formed byte chunks to match precomputed subword targets. Additionally, they incorporate lightweight POS supervision in the hierarchy to help the model learn meaningful boundaries and structure. The overall framework is trained without massive data and keeps the base LM frozen.

Result: Across six languages and word-level morphological tasks, the proposed tokenizer-free hierarchical byte framework outperforms subword-based and prior byte-level baselines. For POS tagging in particular, it achieves up to a 13.3% performance improvement, demonstrating better handling of low-resource and non-Latin scenarios.

Conclusion: Aligning byte-level hierarchical representations with frozen subword-based language models via initialization and chunk alignment can effectively remove the dependence on subword tokenizers while improving word-level performance, especially in low-resource and non-Latin settings. The combination of byte-to-subword alignment and light POS supervision yields a practical, data-efficient way to bridge the granularity and modality gap between bytes and words.

Abstract: Subword tokenization hinders low-resource language processing by imposing frequency patterns from dominant languages onto script-sharing variants. Byte-level models bypass this issue by processing raw UTF-8 characters, yet they create a granularity mismatch for word-level tasks in non-Latin scripts. Hierarchical byte-level architectures address this mismatch by grouping bytes into word-aligned chunks. However, these architectures require massive training data and suffer from representational misalignment when paired with frozen subword-based language models. In this paper, we propose an adapted hierarchical network framework that bridges this modality gap without extensive training. Our method initializes byte embeddings directly from the subword representations of a frozen base model. We apply a chunk alignment loss to project dynamically grouped byte chunks toward precomputed subword targets, and interleave lightweight part-of-speech (POS) supervision to guide boundary detection. Experiments across six languages demonstrate that our tokenizer-free approach improves performance for word-level morphological tasks, yielding up to a 13.3% improvement on POS tagging.

</details>


### [14] [Knowing Before Answering: Decoding Language Models for Reliable RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27661)
*Syed Mahbubul Huq,Christopher Child,Tillman Weyde,Pranava Madhyastha*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They study how to automatically detect, from a model’s internal signals, whether RAG-provided documents are sufficient, insufficient, or mutually conflicting for answering a query, and show a small linear ‘router’ on hidden states works well.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG systems often fail not because the generator is weak, but because the retrieved context is missing key facts or contains contradictions. Current systems mostly assume retrieved evidence is good enough and focus on prompting the LLM to refuse answers, which is unreliable. A robust RAG pipeline needs an explicit, accurate mechanism to triage retrieval quality—detecting when to answer, abstain, or flag conflicts—ideally by leveraging what the model already internally ‘knows’ about the adequacy of evidence.

Method: They formulate RAG triage as a 3-way classification problem over RAG instances: (1) answerable with given docs, (2) insufficient evidence, or (3) conflicting evidence. They build a controlled synthetic RAG-like benchmark with fictitious knowledge and gold labels for these three states. For many different LLMs, they extract internal representations (hidden activations from different layers, attention-derived features, and MLP-related features) when the model processes the question plus retrieved context. These features feed a lightweight linear classifier (‘feature-based router’) trained to predict the triage label. They compare against prompting-based baselines and specialized RAG models, and analyze which layers and feature types carry the most useful signal.

Result: Across 16 language models of various sizes and architectures, their linear feature-based router systematically outperforms prompt-only approaches and dedicated RAG models on the 3-way classification of sufficiency vs insufficiency vs conflict. Ablation and layer-wise analysis show that mid-layer hidden states are the most informative for this task, generally beating signals derived from attention weights or MLP outputs. This indicates that the evidence adequacy signal is embedded in intermediate representations and can be read out with simple linear models.

Conclusion: Language models implicitly encode whether retrieved evidence is adequate and consistent for answering a query, and this information is accessible in their internal activations—especially in middle layers. A simple linear classifier on top of frozen model features can reliably decode this signal to perform RAG triage, outperforming prompt engineering and specialized RAG systems. This suggests a practical route to building more reliable RAG pipelines by adding feature-based routers that decide when to answer, abstain, or flag conflicting information based on model internals rather than surface heuristics alone.

Abstract: In Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), retrieval may provide insufficient or conflicting information needed to answer a question. The system should not only know when to answer but also be able to identify cases in which the documents provided in RAG are insufficient or contain conflicting information. This can be framed as a three-way classification problem, where we use the model's internal signals to determine whether the provided information in the input can be classified as sufficient, insufficient, or conflicting. We create a controlled benchmark dataset that replicates a RAG setup with fictitious information and labels each instance as answerable, insufficient, or conflicting. We use hidden activations and attention-derived features as inputs to train a lightweight linear model to distinguish among the three classes. Across 16 language models spanning different architectures and a range of model sizes, our feature-based router consistently outperforms prompting-based baselines and the performance of specialised RAG-models. We further conduct analyses into the information dynamics of the models. We show that the most informative signals for the classification are available in the middle layers, with hidden activation states being more effective than attention values or the MLP-feature outputs in most of the tested models. Overall, our results suggest that language models internally encode whether retrieved evidence is sufficient to support answering, and that this signal can be decoded reliably for RAG triage.

</details>


### [15] [First Make It Playable, Then Make It Good: Staged Interaction Learning for Small Dialogue-Game Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27672)
*Syed Mahbubul Huq,Pranava Madhyastha*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces Qwen-GuidePlay-2B, a 2B-parameter model fine-tuned for dialogue-game interaction that achieves strong performance on the Playpen benchmark using a three-stage SFT pipeline and careful data curation instead of complex procedural methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Smaller language models often underperform on complex interactive tasks such as dialogue-based games, and many existing methods rely on heavy procedural techniques (e.g., replay-repair, hard-example mining) or large teacher models that generate new supervision. The authors aim to show that a small 2B-parameter model can become highly effective at dialogue-game interaction through a principled, lightweight fine-tuning strategy and careful use of existing data, without depending on extensive additional engineering or synthetic data generation.

Method: They start with Qwen3.5-2B as the base model and apply three sequential supervised fine-tuning (SFT) stages on the Playpen dialogue-game dataset: (a) SFT only on successful game trajectories, enabling the model to imitate entire high-quality playthroughs; (b) weighted turn-level SFT, where individual dialogue turns are treated as training instances with importance weighting to emphasize more informative or critical turns; and (c) teacher-guided SFT, where a larger teacher model is used to correct formatting issues and evaluate training examples, but crucially does not generate new target actions, serving only as a quality filter and format enforcer. They also empirically test alternative procedurally heavy strategies like replay-repair and hard-example mining and compare their effects.

Result: The resulting Qwen-GuidePlay-2B model achieves 57.12 clemscore and 42.68 statscore on the public Playpen validation set. In the official Playpen challenge, it delivers the second-largest improvement in clemscore over its base model (about +36 points), indicating significant gains in both playability and decision-making. Procedurally heavy methods such as replay-repair and hard-example mining do not yield additional benefits over the simpler three-stage SFT pipeline with curated data.

Conclusion: A relatively small 2B-parameter model can perform strongly on dialogue-game interaction tasks when trained with a well-designed SFT curriculum and carefully curated successful trajectories. Full-trajectory imitation mainly improves playability, while subsequent turn-level and teacher-guided SFT stages enhance decision quality and overall scores. More complex procedural interventions such as replay-repair and hard-example mining are unnecessary or ineffective in this setting, suggesting that careful data selection and lightweight teacher guidance can be sufficient for strong performance. The authors release both the model and code to enable reproducibility and further research.

Abstract: We present Qwen-GuidePlay-2B, a 2B-parameter language model for dialogue-game interaction. We fine-tune Qwen3.5-2B using three steps: a) SFT on only successful game trajectories from Playpen, b) weighted turn-level SFT, and c) teacher-guided SFT. The teacher model (which is a larger model) is only used to fix formatting and evaluate examples, but does not create new gold actions. Our final model scores 57.12 clemscore and 42.68 statscore on the public Playpen validation. In the officially released challenge results, our model obtains the second-highest Playpen clemscore delta among submitted systems (which is approximately +36 over its base model). Our findings suggest that imitating full trajectories helps with playability, while turn-level and teacher-guided training usually improve decision-making and increase the overall score. Alternative procedurally heavy approaches like replay-repair and hard-example mining did not help, which suggests that small models are performant simply by using careful curation strategies rather than aggressive changes. We make available both the model and the code for reproducibility.

</details>


### [16] [Below the Noise Floor: Bimodal Seed Collapse and Distinct Failure Modes in Small-Model Knowledge Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27729)
*Dipto Sumit,Sakib Ul Haque,Farig Sadeque*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates knowledge distillation (KD) methods for function/API routing with small models and shows that single-seed results are unreliable due to very high variance and hidden collapse modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In practical deployments, small models must reliably pick the correct API/function from natural-language queries. Knowledge distillation from larger teachers is a common strategy, but reported gains are usually based on a single random seed and at scales where seed variance is not well understood. This raises concerns about the stability and true benefits of KD for small students in realistic routing tasks, especially in sensitive domains like healthcare where routing errors are costly.

Method: The authors study a 740-instance healthcare function-routing benchmark using a 1.5B-parameter Qwen student model distilled from a 20B-parameter teacher. They compare eight knowledge distillation variants against standard supervised cross-entropy training. For the main configurations, they run 3–6 random seeds to measure variance, identify training collapse behaviors, and distinguish real performance gains from noise. They analyze different KD schemes (e.g., cross-entropy-based KD, paraphrase-based KD, reasoning-based KD, progressive KD, rank-based KD) and perform both cross-split and within-split evaluations to examine the robustness of apparent improvements such as input enrichment.

Result: They observe extremely high seed variance, with standard deviations from 2.8 up to 48.7 accuracy points, which makes most reported KD gains under 5 points statistically meaningless. Three of seven KD variants show bimodal collapse: in 1/3 to 1/5 of seeds, accuracy drops below 55% while other seeds train normally; a fourth variant has elevated variance. The collapse modes differ: for ce_kd and ce_paraphrase, the model persistently selects wrong functions; for reasoning_kd, a novel truncation failure appears where the model produces only reasoning and stops before outputting a function name, giving 0.9% accuracy. Only progressive_kd and rank_kd avoid collapse in all observed seeds and maintain low variance (sigma ≤ 3.9 points). An apparent +3.78 point gain from input enrichment in a naive cross-split comparison flips to a -2.70 point effect when re-tested carefully with multiple seeds on the same split.

Conclusion: Knowledge distillation for small models in function/API routing is far less stable and reliable than single-seed reports suggest. High seed variance and distinct collapse modes can completely mask or invert supposed KD improvements, making many small gains uninterpretable. Safe deployment requires multi-seed, controlled evaluations and careful choice of KD schemes; among those tested, progressive_kd and rank_kd are comparatively robust, while others are vulnerable to severe training collapse, including a newly identified truncation failure mode in reasoning-based KD.

Abstract: Function routing -- selecting the correct API call from a fixed catalog given a natural-language request -- is a deployment problem where small students are attractive but knowledge distillation gains are typically reported single-seed, at scales where seed variance is unknown. On a 740-instance healthcare API routing task with a 1.5B Qwen student and a 20B teacher, we compare eight KD variants against supervised cross-entropy, using three to six seeds for key configurations. We find: (i) per-seed standard deviation ranges from 2.8 to 48.7 percentage points, swallowing every claimed KD gain below five points; (ii) three of seven KD variants exhibit bimodal collapse, with at least one in three to five seeds falling below 55% accuracy while the others train normally, and a fourth showing elevated variance; (iii) collapse has distinct modes -- wrong-function selection for ce_kd and ce_paraphrase, and a previously undocumented output-truncation mode for reasoning_kd, where the model emits reasoning but terminates before producing a function name (0.9% accuracy); (iv) only progressive_kd and rank_kd avoid collapse across observed seeds, with sigma <= 3.9 pp; (v) a naive cross-split +3.78 pp gain from input enrichment reverses to -2.70 pp under controlled within-split multi-seed re-testing. Single-seed evaluation is therefore unable to detect central failure modes in small-model KD.

</details>


### [17] [Load-Bearing Context: The Question Damage Score for Evaluating Context Reliance in Linguistic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27756)
*Neh Majmudar,Elena Filatova*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a diagnostic framework using modified Linguistics Olympiad puzzles to test whether LLMs truly rely on provided context or on prior knowledge/memorization, by strategically deleting context examples and measuring performance and abstention behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: It is hard to tell if LLM answers come from reasoning over the given prompt or from memorized prior knowledge. Linguistic Olympiad puzzles are designed so that all necessary information is contained in the problem itself, making them ideal to disentangle context-based reasoning from external knowledge. By perturbing these puzzles in a controlled way, the authors aim to understand which parts of the context are truly necessary and how LLMs behave when crucial information is missing.

Method: The authors take 53 UK Linguistics Olympiad puzzles and construct two variants for each by deleting a single context example: (1) a uniform random deletion, and (2) a targeted deletion that removes a structurally load-bearing example, using ideas from error-correcting codes to identify examples that uniquely carry essential information. They define a Question Damage Score to quantify how much each deletion harms the solvability of each question, and use this to categorize puzzles as fragile or robust. They then evaluate three state-of-the-art LLMs, instructing them to abstain when the information is insufficient, and compare model behavior across original and modified puzzles.

Result: Models rarely choose to abstain even when key context examples are removed. Moreover, they can often still produce correct answers after the load-bearing examples are deleted, according to the Question Damage Score. This suggests that the models may be relying on prior knowledge, heuristics, or memorized patterns rather than only on the given context, or that they are less sensitive to information deletions than expected.

Conclusion: The proposed framework shows that LLMs do not reliably signal when information is missing and may not depend as strongly on explicit context examples as designers assume. The approach offers a way to study context reliance in a fine-grained manner, enabling future work on causal interventions, analyzing minimum context needed for correct answers (stopping sets), targeted contamination checks to distinguish training memorization from generalization, and mechanistic interpretability of how models use puzzle context. Overall, the paper calls for deeper investigation into the interplay between context-based reasoning, prior knowledge, and memorization in LLMs.

Abstract: Determining whether large language models derive answers from context or prior knowledge remains a fundamental challenge. Self-contained linguistic olympiad puzzles provide a controlled setting where all answers derive solely from expert-designed context examples without external knowledge. Removing individual context examples can eliminate information needed for specific questions while leaving the rest of the puzzle unchanged. We leverage this to introduce a diagnostic framework for analyzing individual context examples. Using 53 UK Linguistics Olympiad puzzles, we generate two modified variants by deleting a single context example: (1) uniform random deletion, and (2) targeted deletion (inspired by error-correcting codes) to remove a structurally load-bearing example uniquely carrying necessary information. We formalize this impact using a Question Damage Score to classify puzzles as fragile or robust. Evaluating three frontier LLMs under instructions to abstain when information is insufficient, we find they rarely abstain, often continuing to produce correct answers after load-bearing context is removed. These findings motivate further investigation into context-based reasoning, prior knowledge, memorization, and linguistic inference. Beyond abstention, the framework enables fine-grained analyses of context reliance, including causal interventions, stopping-set analysis, targeted contamination studies, and mechanistic interpretability.

</details>


### [18] [Informational Antilocality and the Locality Bias in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27760)
*Andrew McInnerney,Shane Storks,Steven Abney,Richard L. Lewis*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how well transformer-based language models learn specially constructed languages that have no local statistical dependencies within any window of k symbols, finding that models eventually learn them equally well but take longer to do so as k increases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether transformer-based LLMs have an inductive bias favoring local over non-local dependencies, beyond conventional benchmarks that mix both. By creating artificial languages with strictly non-local structure (k-antilocal), the authors can cleanly probe how locality affects learnability and learning dynamics.

Method: Define k-antilocal languages as those with zero mutual information across every contiguous block of k symbols. Construct families of such synthetic languages for increasing k. Train transformer-based LLMs on these datasets under comparable settings, and measure cross-entropy loss and convergence behavior as a function of training steps and k.

Result: Models achieve similar final cross-entropy loss across all values of k, indicating that they can in principle learn both local and highly non-local structures. However, as k increases, models require more training steps to reach comparable performance, demonstrating slower convergence on more antilocal (more non-local) languages.

Conclusion: Transformer LLMs do not fundamentally fail to learn non-local dependencies in k-antilocal languages, but they show a clear bias in learning dynamics: non-local structure is harder and slower to acquire. Evidence for a locality bias manifests in training speed rather than in asymptotic performance, highlighting the importance of studying learning dynamics—not just end-state accuracy—when characterizing inductive biases of LLMs.

Abstract: We consider the ability of transformer-based language models (LLMs) to learn what we call k-antilocal languages, i.e., languages that have no mutual information across any span of $k$ contiguous symbols. We construct such languages with increasing $k$, finding that LLMs trained on them achieve comparable cross-entropy loss regardless of antilocality, but converge more slowly on more antilocal languages. Our findings support the idea that non-local dependencies are more difficult to learn, but the evidence for this bias comes from learning speed rather than learning success.

</details>


### [19] [Compositional Failure in Audio-Visual LLMs: Late-Layer Prior Dominance Under Cross-modal Conflict](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27785)
*Adarsh Sudheer,David Li,Omar Elbanna,Ishaan Kodarapu,Arjun Bahuguna,Vasu Sharma*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces audio-visual conflict as a compositional generalization test for audio-visual LLMs and shows that current models fail, being dominated by internal answer priors rather than grounding in conflicting audio and video evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As audio-visual large language models (AV-LLMs) become more capable, we need precise tests to see whether they truly integrate information from both modalities compositionally, especially when audio and video disagree. Existing benchmarks often use aligned or simple tasks that don’t expose whether models actually reason over conflicting multi-modal input or just follow internal biases and priors. The authors aim to design a targeted conflict setting and use it to reveal and understand systematic failure modes in AV-LLMs.

Method: They propose an audio-visual conflict setting where synchronized but semantically incompatible audio and video are paired, and the model is asked a simple yes/no matching question. They evaluate VideoLLaMA 2-7B-AV under three alignment configurations on the AVHBench benchmark, focusing on an exact-string scored Yes/No subset. They also test InternVideo2 under cross-modal conflict. To interpret the failures, they perform mechanistic interpretability analysis, examining at which layers the model commits to an answer and how this relates to grounding in the inputs. They further vary temporal alignment strength to see how it affects answer priors and conflict resolution.

Result: For VideoLLaMA 2-7B-AV, three different alignment configurations remain near chance accuracy on the strict exact-string Yes/No subset of AVHBench, even though each configuration induces substantial shifts in output priors (answer biases). InternVideo2 shows a 32.3% accuracy drop specifically under cross-modal conflict, along with a 17.3% rate of instruction-following failure. The authors identify a failure mode they call “prior dominance,” where late layers commit to an internally preferred answer pattern that is only weakly grounded in the conflicting audio and video. Mechanistic analysis shows that answer commitment is concentrated around 25.5 ± 1 layers. Stronger temporal alignment changes biases in answers but does not improve the model’s ability to correctly resolve audio-visual conflicts.

Conclusion: Current AV-LLMs do not robustly perform compositional conflict resolution between audio and video; instead, they exhibit prior dominance, where internal answer patterns override contradictory multimodal evidence. Even improving temporal alignment mainly shifts answer biases rather than leading to genuinely better conflict grounding. The work introduces audio-visual conflict as a diagnostic test and provides mechanistic evidence that late-layer commitment to priors is central to this failure, suggesting that future AV-LLM designs must explicitly address this late-layer bias and grounding problem.

Abstract: We study audio-visual conflict as a compositional generalization test for AV-LLMs: the model must combine synchronized but semantically incompatible audio and video evidence and decide whether the pair matches. On VideoLLaMA 2-7B-AV, three alignment configurations remain nearchance on the scored exact-string Yes/No subset of AVHBench, even though their output priors shift substantially. Similarly, off-the-shelf InternVideo2 experienced a 32.3% accuracy decrease specifically under cross-modal conflict, accompanied by a 17.3% instruction-following failure. We call this failure mode prior dominance: late-layer commitment to an internally preferred answer pattern that is weakly grounded in the conflicting inputs. To explain this behavior, we conduct a mechanistic interpretability analysis and find that commitment remains concentrated at 25.5 $\pm$ 1 layers. We show that stronger temporal alignment changes answer bias, but do not improve compositional conflict resolution. Code and data to reproduce our mechanistic audit and behavioral evaluations are available at https://github.com/AdarshSudheer09/AVHBench-dmai.

</details>


### [20] [Representation of syntax in LLMs through the lens of linear distance and similarity-aware entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27813)
*Juan Pablo Vigneaux,Mary Kennedy,Khalil Iskarous,Robert Frank,Matilde Marcolli*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They refine how structural probes are evaluated, breaking down edge-reconstruction accuracy by dependency label and explaining what linguistic and geometric factors drive differences across relations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard evaluation of structural probes collapses all dependency relations into a single UAS score, hiding which types of syntactic relations are well or poorly captured by language model representations and why. The authors want a more fine-grained, linguistically interpretable understanding of how syntactic structure is encoded in LMs.

Method: Starting from Hewitt & Manning’s structural probe framework, they compute undirected attachment score by label (UASL), i.e., per-dependency-type reconstruction accuracy. They then analyze UASL variation across relations and model families/sizes, and relate it statistically to: (i) the linear distance (log-scaled mean and dispersion) between heads and dependents; and (ii) a measure of head diversity, defined via similarity-aware entropy over the heads of a relation in embedding space.

Result: They find substantial differences across dependency labels in how well structural probes can reconstruct them. The majority of this per-label accuracy variation is predicted by two factors: relations whose dependents are typically farther from their heads (and more variable in distance) and relations whose heads are more geometrically diverse in embedding space tend to be harder to reconstruct. These patterns are robust across multiple model architectures and sizes.

Conclusion: Syntactic information in language models is represented in a way that is sensitive to both surface distance patterns and the geometric clustering of heads in embedding space. Structural probe performance is therefore not uniform across relations but strongly shaped by these geometric and distributional factors, clarifying the limits and nature of syntactic abstraction in LM representations.

Abstract: Structural probes were introduced by Hewitt and Manning to reconstruct syntactic trees from a neural language model's latent representations. They are evaluated by calculating the proportion of syntactic tree edges correctly reconstructed over an annotated corpus (as measured by undirected unlabeled attachment score). Here, we disaggregate this measure, considering undirected attachment score by label (UASL), which assesses the reconstruction accuracy of each syntactic relation separately, establishing important differences among relations that overlap linguistic distinctions. Moreover, we identify two factors that predict most of UASL's variability across relations: (i) the mean and dispersion of the linear distance (on a log scale) between the related words, and (ii) the diversity (similarity-aware entropy) of the syntactic relation's head. These results, which hold across a range of model sizes and architectures, shed light on the degree of abstraction of the representation of syntax in language models and the dependence of such representation on geometric properties of the embedding space.

</details>
