### [21] [PersonaEdit: Representative Sample Selection for Personalized Model Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27816)
*You-Mei Huang,Chung-Chi Chen,An-Zi Yen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PersonaEdit proposes a clustering-based sample selection strategy to make model editing practical and efficient for personalizing LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing personalization methods for LLMs often rely on retrieval-based systems, which can fail with poor retrieval quality and long-term interactions. Model editing offers a way to directly inject user-specific knowledge into a model, but scaling it to large amounts of personalized data is challenging due to computational cost and interference between edits. This motivates a method that can select a small yet representative subset of user data for editing while maintaining personalization performance.

Method: The paper introduces PersonaEdit, a strategy that clusters hidden representations of user data and uses proportional stratified sampling to select representative editing samples. The model is then edited using only these selected samples to incorporate personalized knowledge. The approach is evaluated both as a standalone editing method and in combination with retrieval-based prompt augmentation to test whether edited internal knowledge and retrieved external context complement each other.

Result: Experiments show that model editing can effectively personalize LLMs and that PersonaEdit’s clustering-based sample selection retains most of the personalization performance while significantly reducing the number of samples needed for editing. Additionally, combining model editing with retrieval-based prompt augmentation leads to further improvements, indicating that the two approaches provide complementary benefits.

Conclusion: PersonaEdit demonstrates that careful, representation-based sample selection enables efficient and scalable model editing for LLM personalization. By reducing editing samples without sacrificing much performance and working well in conjunction with retrieval-based methods, model editing emerges as a practical component of future personalized LLM systems.

Abstract: Personalization has attracted growing interest in LLM applications, yet existing retrieval-based approaches depend heavily on retrieval quality and degrade in long-term interactions. Model editing, which directly modifies internal model parameters to incorporate new knowledge, has demonstrated effective knowledge modification capabilities in factual knowledge editing tasks and may provide a potential solution for personalization. However, scaling model editing to personalization is non-trivial. Editing large amounts of user data increases computational cost and causes interference among edits, motivating the need for effective sample selection. To address this issue, we propose, PersonaEdit, a hidden representation clustering strategy that selects representative editing samples through proportional stratified sampling. Experiments show that model editing is effective for personalization, and that our selection strategy preserves most of the performance while substantially reducing the number of required editing samples. Beyond standalone editing, we find that combining model editing with retrieval-based prompt augmentation further improves personalization, as edited knowledge and retrieved context provide complementary information. These results demonstrate the potential of model editing as an efficient and scalable approach for LLM personalization.

</details>


### [22] [Synthetic Linguistic Agency: How an Embodied Mortal Agent Learns Linguistic Affordances through Consequential Social Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27843)
*Sixin Chen,Taizhou Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper defines and demonstrates “synthetic linguistic agency” in AI systems by giving an artificial agent a vulnerable, persistent body whose survival depends on its linguistic interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current language models can talk persuasively but lack a real, continuing life that can be helped or harmed by what they say. Without such vulnerability and persistence, their language use is missing a key dimension of agency and social meaning. The authors want to formalize this missing piece—linguistic agency—and explore how it might arise in artificial systems so we can better understand synthetic empathy, responsibility, and strategic human–AI interaction.

Method: 1) The authors propose a theory of linguistic agency based on three components: embodiment (a body that can act and suffer consequences), linguistic participation (interaction that changes both speaker and partner), and precariousness (a future that can be sustained or lost). They operationalize these as criteria for “Synthetic Linguistic Agency” (SLA) and use them to identify existing systems that already partially satisfy SLA. 2) They then design a new model using Homeostatically Regulated Reinforcement Learning to build an Embodied Mortal Agent (EMA) whose survival is explicitly at stake. The EMA’s internal “life” depends on maintaining homeostatic variables; it learns, via reinforcement learning, how different utterances influence a human (or simulated) partner’s willingness to protect or endanger it, and chooses language by forecasting how partner responses affect its remaining life. Controlled experiments vary whether the EMA’s body persists or is reset between interactions and track how its linguistic choices and the partner’s behaviors co-adapt over time.

Result: The experiments show that: (a) The EMA’s linguistic behavior depends systematically on its embodied state and lived social history; (b) Its utterances measurably change partner behavior, especially their inclination to protect or jeopardize the agent; (c) Its speech policy adapts over experience with particular partners, reflecting learned expectations about each partner’s responses; (d) When the EMA’s bodily state persists over time, its linguistic choices alter the trajectory of the same continuing life; when its body is reset, interaction histories still shape social behavior, but no longer affect the agent’s ongoing viability. Together, these findings satisfy the proposed operational criteria for SLA in this setup.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that it is possible to construct artificial systems that exhibit synthetic linguistic agency, in the sense that their language use is grounded in a persistent, vulnerable body and shaped by social histories that affect their continued existence. This has important implications for the design and governance of future AI: agents with enduring bodies, memories, and futures may develop forms of synthetic empathy and strategic interaction that could influence how humans care for, negotiate with, and regulate them. The work calls for systematic research into how such agents should be built, understood, and governed.

Abstract: Contemporary language models can converse fluently and influence human decisions, yet their exchanges do not enter a continuing, vulnerable life of their own. Linguistic-agency theory identifies this missing connection as linguistic agency and characterizes it through embodiment, linguistic participation, and precariousness: a body that acts and bears consequences, interaction that changes both agent and partner, and a future that can be sustained or lost. Two coordinated studies examine how this organization can appear in artificial systems. First, we translate these relations into inspectable criteria for Synthetic Linguistic Agency (SLA) and identify several existing SLA systems. Second, building on Homeostatically Regulated Reinforcement Learning, we develop a mortality-grounded linguistic-reinforcement-learning model and instantiate it in an Embodied Mortal Agent (EMA). The EMA learns how ways of speaking change a partner's willingness to protect it and chooses expressions by considering what those responses mean for its remaining life. Controlled experiments show that linguistic choices depend on the EMA's body and social history, change partner behavior, and adapt through experience with particular partners. When bodily consequences persist, linguistic choices alter the future of the same life; when the body is reset, their social effects remain but no longer shape continued viability. The resulting EMA exhibits SLA under our operational definition. This work motivates further research on synthetic empathy and strategic human-AI interaction: how artificial agents with persistent bodies, histories, and futures might develop and express empathy, and how people might care for, negotiate with, or govern them.

</details>


### [23] [EvoHarmBench: Breaking Content Moderation with Iterative Human-Like Evasion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27844)
*Ruijie Jian,Benlei Cui,Ting Ma,Haidong Ding,Kangwei Liu,Ziwen Xu,Longtao Huang,Hui Xue,Ziqiang Zhu,Junjie Li,Haiwen Hong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces EvoHarmBench, a dynamic adversarial benchmark that iteratively evolves attacks to test LLM-based content moderation systems, revealing high evasion rates even in top commercial models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static, one-shot benchmarks for harmful content detection do not capture the interactive, adversarial nature of real-world platforms where users adapt their content in response to moderation feedback. This leads to a large gap between offline benchmark performance and real deployment effectiveness. The authors aim to close this gap by proposing an evaluation method that better reflects real adversarial dynamics.

Method: They design EvoHarmBench, a dynamic adversarial evaluation framework that operates at the level of semantic clusters of harmful content. The framework runs an iterative optimization loop that automatically evolves evasion strategies, jointly optimizing for two objectives: (1) successfully bypassing content moderation (evasion success) and (2) maintaining human readability. They build the benchmark from 5,002 real-world adversarial samples collected from content platforms, group these into 229 semantic sub-clusters spanning five violation categories, and then systematically attack widely used LLM-based defense (moderation) models using the framework.

Result: Across 229 semantic sub-clusters and five violation categories, the framework exposes substantial weaknesses in leading commercial LLM moderation systems. After twelve optimization iterations under a readability constraint, adversarially evolved content can evade state-of-the-art LLM moderators with an attack success rate of 80.3%, indicating a severe robustness gap relative to what static benchmarks suggest.

Conclusion: Dynamic, iterative adversarial evaluation is crucial for accurately assessing the robustness of content moderation systems. Static benchmarks significantly overestimate real-world safety performance. EvoHarmBench demonstrates that even top commercial LLM moderators are highly vulnerable to evolved adversarial strategies, motivating the community to adopt dynamic adversarial benchmarks. The authors commit to releasing data, framework, and code to foster progress in content safety research.

Abstract: Existing evaluations of harmful content detection rely predominantly on static benchmarks, which struggle to reflect the interactive adversarial ecosystem of real-world content platforms where users continuously revise their expressions in response to moderation feedback. This mismatch creates a significant performance gap between offline benchmark scores and online deployment effectiveness. To the best of our knowledge, we present EvoHarmBench, the first dynamic adversarial evaluation framework for content moderation systems. The framework employs an iterative optimization loop that evolves evasion strategies at the semantic-cluster level, while simultaneously optimizing for evasion success and human readability. We systematically evaluate LLM-based defense models which are widely used in real world moderation systems. The evaluation covers 229 semantic sub-clusters across five violation categories, derived from 5,002 real-world adversarial samples collected from content platforms. Our experiments reveal substantial vulnerabilities even in leading commercial systems: after twelve optimization iterations, the attack success rate under readability constraints reaches 80.3% within SOTA LLM moderators. We will release the full benchmark data, evaluation framework, and code to encourage a shift from static benchmarking toward dynamic adversarial evaluation in content safety research.

</details>


### [24] [AI Writers Have a Consistent Stylometric Footprint, but AI Editors Do Not](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27855)
*Zhengyang Shan,Yukyung Lee,Sophie Hao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that AI-generated and AI-edited texts have different stylometric signatures, so they should be treated as distinct phenomena in detection and analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most prior work treats all AI-produced text as a single category and focuses on fully generated text. However, in real-world use, large language models are widely used to edit or refine human-written text. It is unclear whether stylometric methods that distinguish AI-generated text from human text also work for AI-edited text, and whether generation and editing leave similar or different stylometric traces.

Method: The authors analyze stylometric features of texts produced by 8 different LLMs across 5 domains. They compare three types of text: purely human-written, fully AI-generated, and human-written text that has been edited by an LLM. They compute a wide range of stylometric features, emphasizing entropy, lexical diversity, and lexical density, and test which features robustly separate AI-generated from human text, and which features characterize AI-edited text relative to its human-written source.

Result: They find that AI-generated text exhibits a consistent stylometric footprint across models and domains, driven mainly by increased entropy and lexical diversity, while other features vary by domain and generator. In contrast, AI-edited text does not share this footprint: compared to its human sources, it only slightly increases lexical diversity and actually decreases entropy. Instead, lexical density becomes the strongest signal associated with AI editing. Stylometric features can therefore distinguish AI-edited from AI-generated text, but perform much worse at distinguishing AI-edited from purely human-written text.

Conclusion: AI generation and AI editing are stylometrically distinct processes. "AI text" is not a single homogeneous category: generated and edited texts leave qualitatively different traces and should be modeled, detected, and evaluated separately. Detection tools optimized for AI-generated text may not reliably identify AI-edited text, which has important implications for authorship attribution, academic integrity, and AI governance.

Abstract: Text generated by large language models (LLMs) has been shown to be stylometrically distinct from human-written text \citep{andreDetectingAIAuthorship2023, shahDetectingUnmaskingAIGenerated2023, oparaStyloAIDistinguishingAIGenerated2024, soto2024fewshot, liLinguisticDifferencesAI2025, selviogluFeatureExtractionAnalysis2025}. But LLMs are increasingly used not only to generate text but also to edit human writing, and it is unclear whether the two leave the same trace. We show that AI generation leaves a consistent ``stylometric footprint'': a small subset of features, primarily entropy and lexical diversity, consistently separates AI-generated text from human writing across 8 LLMs and 5 domains, while the remaining features depend heavily on the domain and generator. AI editing, however, does not reproduce the same footprint. Relative to their human-written sources, AI-edited texts show only a small increase in lexical diversity and a decrease in entropy, rather than the joint increase that characterizes AI generation. Lexical density, which contributes little to generation, instead becomes the dominant editing-associated signal. Stylometric features therefore separate AI-edited text from AI-generated text but are substantially less effective at separating it from human-written text. Our results suggest that ``AI text'' is not a single phenomenon: generation and editing leave qualitatively different stylometric traces and should be studied separately.

</details>


### [25] [OpenStamp: A Watermark for Open-Source Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27899)
*Miroojin Bakshi,Saksham Rastogi,Danish Pruthi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose OpenStamp, a watermarking method for open‑source LLMs that hard‑codes watermark logic into the final projection layer, improving detectability and robustness with little performance loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing probabilistic watermarking methods adjust token sampling during generation, which works for closed models but fails for open-source LLMs because users can see and disable the watermarking logic. There is a need for a watermark that is usable in open-weight settings, remains detectable, is difficult to remove or fine-tune away, and remains robust under paraphrasing and other attacks.

Method: They modify only the final projection (unembedding) layer of an open-source LLM so that the watermarking pattern is encoded directly into the model weights rather than in external sampling code. The method subtly reshapes the logits so generated text carries a statistical watermark that can be detected later. They empirically evaluate on two base models, compare with prior watermarking schemes, and test robustness against paraphrasing attacks and attempts to remove the watermark via post-hoc fine-tuning.

Result: Across two evaluated models, OpenStamp yields higher watermark detection performance than prior methods while maintaining similar or only minimally degraded task capabilities. Experiments show the watermark persists under paraphrasing and is more resistant to removal or weakening via additional fine-tuning than earlier open-source watermark baselines.

Conclusion: Encoding the watermark directly into the unembedding layer provides an effective way to watermark open-source LLMs: it cannot be trivially disabled at inference time, remains robust under common attacks like paraphrasing and fine-tuning, and preserves model utility. OpenStamp is offered along with code and pre-watermarked versions of several popular open-source models to facilitate adoption.

Abstract: With the growing prevalence of large language model (LLM) generated content, watermarking is considered a promising approach for attributing text to LLMs and distinguishing it from human-written content. A prominent class of techniques embeds subtle but detectable signals in generated text by modifying token sampling probabilities. However, such methods are unsuitable for open-source models, where users have white-box access and can easily disable watermarking during inference. In this work, we introduce OpenStamp, a watermarking technique that encodes the watermarking logic directly into the model weights by modifying only the final projection, or unembedding, layer. Through experiments across two models, we show that OpenStamp achieves superior detection performance, with minimal degradation in model capabilities compared to prior methods. The implanted watermark is explicitly designed, and empirically confirmed, to be more robust to paraphrasing attacks and harder to scrub off through post-hoc fine-tuning than prior open-source watermarks. To enable developers to watermark their models, we release our code alongside watermarked versions of 4 popular open-source models.

</details>


### [26] [LandingAgent: A Reference-Annotated Dataset and Agentic Generation Framework for Landing Pages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27902)
*Injun Baek,HyeongSeok Lee,Yearim Kim,Junhoo Lee,Nojun Kwak*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces LandingBench and LandingAgent, a dataset and agentic framework for generating target-grounded, reference-guided landing pages that improve faithfulness, presentation quality, and layout diversity over direct LLM prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based webpage generators can produce code from natural-language prompts but typically result in generic-looking landing pages and may introduce unsupported persuasive claims. There is a need for systems that can create persuasive, high-quality landing pages for specific targets by reusing successful structural patterns from real sites while avoiding direct copying and hallucination.

Method: The authors construct LandingBench, a dataset that abstracts real landing pages into structured reference profiles capturing section sequences, layout patterns, tone, visual emphasis, and CTA structures. On top of this, they design LandingAgent, a three-phase agentic pipeline: (1) profile the target to understand its value proposition and requirements, (2) build a reference-guided wireframe by adapting patterns from LandingBench, and (3) iteratively refine the generated page via critique-guided polishing. They then compare LandingAgent with direct prompting baselines using multiple qualitative criteria.

Result: LandingAgent outperforms direct prompting approaches on metrics including target grounding (faithfulness to the described offering), conciseness, readability, aesthetics, and structural diversity. The experiments indicate that using abstracted references and an agentic multi-phase process leads to better-structured, more persuasive landing pages than single-shot LLM generation.

Conclusion: Reference-guided, agentic generation grounded in structured abstractions of real landing pages offers clear benefits over direct prompting for landing-page creation. LandingBench provides reusable patterns without copying, and LandingAgent demonstrates how a multi-stage pipeline can translate these patterns into higher-quality, more diverse, and better-grounded landing pages. This framework and dataset can serve as a foundation for future work on goal-oriented web interface generation.

Abstract: Landing pages are goal-oriented web interfaces that must communicate a target-specific value proposition while organizing information flow, visual hierarchy, and calls to action (CTA). Although large language models can generate plausible webpage code from natural-language prompts, direct generation often yields generic templates and unsupported persuasive claims. We study target-grounded, reference-guided landing-page generation, where a system must create an executable page for a new target by adapting reusable patterns from real pages without copying them. We introduce LandingBench, a reference-profile dataset that abstracts real landing pages into section sequences, layout patterns, tone descriptors, visual emphasis, and CTA structure. Building on LandingBench, we propose LandingAgent, a three-phase agentic framework that profiles the target, constructs a reference-guided wireframe, and refines the page through critique-guided polishing. We evaluate LandingAgent against direct prompting on faithfulness, conciseness, readability, aesthetics, and structural diversity. Experiments show improved target grounding, presentation quality, and layout diversity. Code is available at https://github.com/IAURAI/LandingAgent.

</details>


### [27] [What Makes Agent Memory Useful for Reliable Unanswerable Question Handling?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27924)
*Chuanyuan Tan,Junjie Yu,Yuxin Wang,Yining Zheng,Xipeng Qiu,Wenliang Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper systematically studies how different types of memory in agentic RAG systems affect large language models’ ability to correctly handle unanswerable questions, finding that some memory forms help but are fragile and highly dependent on representation and transferability of behavioral guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM-based agents are increasingly deployed in real applications, it is crucial that they recognize and correctly handle questions that cannot be answered, instead of hallucinating. Many agent systems use memory (past trajectories, rules, experiences), yet it is unclear when and how these memories actually improve performance on unanswerable questions, and whether such improvements transfer across models and datasets. The paper aims to clarify the role, robustness, and transferability of memory in this safety-critical setting.

Method: The authors adopt a unified agentic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) framework and instantiate four representative memory methods that differ in how they store and use past experience (e.g., procedural, rule-based, behavioral/trajectory-focused). They evaluate these memories across three datasets centered on unanswerable questions and with two different base LLMs. They also study cross-model and cross-dataset transfer of stored memories, compare decision-guidance vs. trajectory-shaping uses of memory, and analyze how different memory representations and compositions affect robustness under dataset shift.

Result: Experiments show that memory can improve handling of unanswerable questions, but benefits are selective and fragile when the pattern of answerability changes across datasets. Memories often transfer better across different base models than across different datasets, implying that shifts in answerability patterns are more problematic than architectural/model changes. UAQ improvements are more stably preserved when memory guides high-level decisions (e.g., whether to abstain) rather than reshaping full action trajectories. Procedural and rule-based memory representations yield the most reliable gains, and combinations where procedural guidance is augmented with behavioral signals work best.

Conclusion: Reliable use of memory for unanswerable-question handling is not mainly about storing more experience; it hinges on encoding and preserving behavioral guidance that generalizes across contexts. Procedural and rule-based memories, especially when combined with complementary behavioral signals and deployed as decision guidance, offer more robust gains than trajectory-focused memory. Designing agent memories for safety should prioritize transferable decision rules over sheer volume of past trajectories.

Abstract: Reliable handling of unanswerable questions (UAQs) is critical for trustworthy LLM-based agents. Although memory is widely used in agent systems, its role in reliable UAQ handling remains unclear. We present a systematic study of agent memory for UAQ handling under a unified agentic RAG framework, evaluating four representative memory methods across three UAQ-related datasets and two base models.
  We find that memory can improve UAQ performance in some settings, but such gains are selective rather than universal and remain fragile under dataset shift. Interestingly, cross-model memory reuse is often more feasible than cross-dataset transfer, suggesting that shifts in answerability patterns pose a greater challenge to memory reuse than changes in the base model itself. We further find that UAQ gains are more strongly preserved through decision guidance than through trajectory shaping, and that memory effectiveness depends strongly on representation. In particular, procedural and rule-based memories often provide the most reliable support for UAQ handling, while memory composition is most effective when procedural guidance is combined with complementary behavioral signals. Overall, our findings suggest that reliable UAQ memory depends less on storing larger amounts of experience and more on preserving transferable behavioral guidance.

</details>


### [28] [Entity-Memory Graph Retrieval Improves Evidence Coverage in Long-Conversation Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27925)
*Shumao Sun*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces an entity-memory graph retrieval method for dialogue QA and shows it improves evidence recall over dense retrieval, though not final-answer F1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense retrievers often miss relevant dialogue turns in long multi-turn conversations because they rank each turn independently by cosine similarity, ignoring discourse structure and entity continuity. The authors want to test whether adding lightweight graph structure over dialogue memories (turns) can increase retrieval coverage of gold evidence without changing the downstream reader model, and to quantify this effect cleanly and robustly.

Method: They represent each dialogue turn as a Memory node in a graph, connect repeated entities across turns via Entity nodes, and add directed chronological edges between adjacent Memories. At query time, retrieval proceeds in stages: (1) entity gating selects relevant entities, (2) semantic fusion combines signals, (3) one-hop chronological recovery expands to neighboring memories, and (4) dense backfill completes the context to the budget. They construct a matched dense retrieval baseline that shares embeddings, query vectors, context length, answer instructions, and evaluation pipeline, so the only systematic difference is the graph structure and traversal logic. Experiments are run on 1,986 questions from ten LoCoMo conversations using GPT-3.5 and DeepSeek as extractive readers under four paper-eligible configurations. They measure evidence recall at various top-k values and final-answer F1, and also swap embeddings to test robustness.

Result: On LoCoMo, graph retrieval improves official evidence recall at top-25 from 79.75% to 84.48%, and this recall advantage holds across top-k values from 5 to 50. However, the improved retrieval coverage does not translate into a statistically supported improvement in final-answer F1 under any matched context cutoff. Results are consistent across four configurations using GPT-3.5 and DeepSeek as extractors. Embedding changes do not yield a reliable F1 difference, but recall remains sensitive to the specific embedding model used, indicating mixed embedding robustness.

Conclusion: Adding an entity-memory graph over dialogue turns improves retrieval coverage of gold evidence compared with a strong dense baseline while carefully controlling for reader and protocol, isolating the benefit to graph structure. Nonetheless, the experiments do not demonstrate a corresponding gain in final-answer F1, nor do they claim model or embedding equivalence or generalization beyond the LoCoMo dataset. The main contribution is showing that lightweight discourse-aware graph retrieval can measurably increase evidence recall in long-conversation QA, even if downstream answer quality remains unchanged in this setting.

Abstract: Entity-Memory graph retrieval keeps dialogue turns as verbatim Memory nodes, links repeated mentions through shared Entities, and connects adjacent Memories with directed chronological edges. At query time the retriever moves from Entity gating through semantic fusion and one-hop chronological recovery to dense backfill. The path can keep a neighboring Memory that dense cosine ranking would otherwise omit. A matched dense control shares the Memory and query vectors, context budget, requested answer protocol, and evaluator, isolating graph structure from changes to the reader.
  On 1,986 questions from ten LoCoMo conversations, graph retrieval raises official evidence recall at top-k 25 from 79.7468% to 84.4842%. The recall advantage is supported from top-k 5 to 50, while no matched cutoff supports an overall final-answer F1 difference. Four paper-eligible requested configurations support empirical robustness across the tested GPT-3.5 and DeepSeek extractors on both outcomes. Embedding robustness is mixed: F1 has no supported contrast, but recall is sensitive to the embedding artifact. The comparison isolates a retrieval-coverage gain from graph structure. It does not establish a final-answer F1 gain, model or embedding equivalence, or cross-dataset generalization.

</details>


### [29] [Lexically conditioned realization ambiguity in Korean predicate morphology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27966)
*Wonjun Oh,KyungTae Lim,Jungyeul Park*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper argues that in Korean, you cannot always predict the surface form of a predicate just from its morphemes and grammatical labels; lexical and semantic factors and realization classes are needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional morphological analysis often assumes that once you know the canonical morphemes and their grammatical categories, you can mechanically derive the surface word forms. The paper challenges this assumption for Korean, where certain predicates with similar stem-ending configurations behave differently on the surface. The authors want to clarify where purely morphological representation breaks down and what additional information is required.

Method: The authors examine a theoretically revealing subset of Korean predicates, focusing on homonymous or near-homonymous verb/adjective stems that participate in different inflectional realization patterns. They compare regular versus digeut irregular pairs, regular versus bieup irregular pairs, and reu irregular versus reo irregular pairs. By contrasting their surface realizations under similar stem-ending configurations, they show systematic divergences tied to lexical identity and realization class membership, not to morphology alone.

Result: They find that sequences of canonical morphemes and grammatical category labels do not uniquely determine surface forms for these classes of Korean predicates. Formally similar stem-ending combinations can yield different surface realizations depending on which lexical item and which realization class is involved. The mapping from abstract morphology to phonological surface form is therefore underdetermined without additional lexical-semantic information.

Conclusion: Korean surface realization cannot be fully captured by bare morphological representation; instead, lexical meaning, subcategorization, and semantic role structure help identify the intended predicate, which in turn fixes its realization class, which then determines the surface form. This shows that morphological analysis and surface realization must be distinguished, and that the lexicon must encode realization-class information beyond canonical morpheme sequences.

Abstract: This paper examines Korean surface realization as distinct from morphological analysis. It asks whether a sequence of canonical morphemes and grammatical category labels uniquely determines the corresponding surface form. The answer is negative for a restricted but theoretically revealing class of Korean predicates. In these cases, formally identical or near-identical stem-ending configurations yield different outputs depending on lexical identity and realization class membership. We analyze this phenomenon as homonymy with inflectional divergence, focusing on regular versus digeut irregular pairs, regular versus bieup irregular pairs, and reu irregular versus reo irregular pairs. These cases show that stem shape and ending alone do not always determine surface realization. Instead, lexical meaning, subcategorization, and semantic role structure help identify the intended predicate; the predicate determines the realization class; and the realization class determines the surface form. Korean realization thus reveals a limit of bare morphological representation.

</details>


### [30] [QUORUM: QUality-Optimized Routing Using Multiple annotators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27974)
*Antonio Purificato,Maria Sofia Bucarelli,Andrea Bacciu,Amin Mantrach,Fabrizio Silvestri*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes QUORUM, a budget-aware routing framework that decides when to use humans vs LLMs for data annotation to maximize quality under a fixed budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data labeling is expensive and slow when done by humans, yet fully automating annotation with LLMs is unreliable, especially for difficult instances. Existing routing methods that rely on model confidence or uncertainty are not sufficiently robust. There is a need for a principled way to combine human and LLM annotators under budget constraints while maintaining or improving label quality.

Method: Introduce QUORUM, a framework that (1) uses feature-based signals to estimate the difficulty of each instance; (2) dynamically routes instances either to human annotators or LLMs depending on estimated difficulty and remaining budget; and (3) allows multiple annotations per instance and aggregates them using agreement-based rewards to enhance reliability. The method is evaluated on varied annotation tasks (closed- and open-ended, English and multilingual).

Result: Across a variety of annotation tasks and languages, QUORUM achieves up to 34.4% improvement in annotation quality while simultaneously reducing annotation cost by 8.8% relative to strong competing methods.

Conclusion: Intelligent, difficulty-aware routing between human and LLM annotators under a fixed budget can substantially improve the cost–quality trade-off in data annotation. QUORUM’s feature-based difficulty estimation and agreement-based multi-annotation scheme make it more effective than confidence- or uncertainty-based routing baselines, providing a practical path to scalable, reliable annotation pipelines.

Abstract: Data annotation remains a central bottleneck in natural language processing, requiring human effort to obtain high-quality labels at scale. While Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a fast and cost-effective alternative, their reliability is highly instance-dependent: they perform well on simple inputs but often fail on examples requiring nuanced reasoning or contextual understanding. In this work, we address this challenge with QUORUM (QUality-Optimized Routing Using Multiple annotators), a budget-aware routing framework that dynamically assigns each instance to human or LLM annotators under a fixed annotation budget. Unlike prior approaches relying on model confidence or uncertainty estimates, QUORUM leverages feature-based signals to estimate instance difficulty and supports multiple annotations per instance, combining them through agreement-based rewards to improve reliability. We evaluate QUORUM across diverse closed- and open-ended annotation tasks in English and multilingual settings, and QUORUM improves annotation quality by up to 34.4% while reducing costs by 8.8% over competing methods. Code can be found at https://github.com/amazon-science/QUORUM.

</details>


### [31] [Predicting Turn-Taking Outcomes in Multi-Party Conversation: Interpretable Modelling of Speech and Gaze Dynamics with Interpersonal Closeness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27988)
*Mark Dourado,Karim Haddad,Henrik G. Hassager,Stefania Serafin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how gaze, speech loudness, and interpersonal closeness predict whether conversational turn transitions are smooth gaps or overlapping speech in noisy, four-person dialogues, using interpretable logistic regression models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Smooth turn-taking is crucial for natural conversation, especially in complex, noisy, multi-party settings. Existing work often focuses on dyadic, cleaner settings or treats signals in isolation. There is a need to understand how multimodal cues—particularly gaze—and social factors like interpersonal closeness jointly predict when speakers take or relinquish the conversational floor, and whether this leads to gaps or overlaps.

Method: Using the GaMMA corpus of free four-person conversations, the authors focus on moments just before turn-taking events. They extract a set of interpretable, behaviorally inspired features: gaze transition motifs, gaze-based behavioural contrasts, gaze entropy, mutual gaze measures, and gaze-based addressee identity, plus speech features derived from loudness and self-reported interpersonal closeness (IOS). They then train logistic regression classifiers to predict whether a floor-transfer event will result in a gap or an overlap, evaluating performance via ROC AUC and testing robustness across different noise conditions.

Result: Gaze-related features alone contain substantial predictive information about upcoming floor transitions. When combined with speech loudness, classification performance improves to an ROC AUC of about 0.76 ± 0.04. Loudness tends to reflect who currently controls the floor, while specific gaze patterns—such as dispersion and who is being gazed at—capture listener readiness to speak and attempts at competitive entry. These patterns remain informative even in noisy audio conditions.

Conclusion: Gaze is a strong and relatively noise-robust cue for predicting turn-taking outcomes in multi-party conversation, and it complements acoustic cues like loudness. By using interpretable features and simple logistic regression models, the study shows that both floor control (via loudness) and listener readiness/competition (via gaze dispersion and addressing) are jointly encoded in multimodal behaviour. This supports the idea that effective models of turn-taking in natural, noisy settings should integrate gaze dynamics with speech cues and account for interpersonal relationships among participants.

Abstract: Smooth speaker transitions are fundamental to effective conversation and rely on an interlocutor's ability to predict when to enter the conversation. This ability depends on accurately interpreting and expressing the verbal and non-verbal cues that signal when a speaker wishes to take or relinquish the floor. The process becomes even more complex in noisy, natural, multi-party settings, with multiple interlocutors available. This study models how gaze and speech, together with perceived interpersonal closeness, signal conversational floor changes in free four-person dialogue. Using the GaMMA corpus, we trained logistic regression models using interpretable, behaviourally motivated features extracted before each turn-taking event to classify floor-transfer outcomes as gaps or overlaps. Predictors included gaze features such as transition motifs and behavioural contrasts, entropy, gaze-based addressee identity, and mutual gaze, alongside speech features derived from speaker loudness, as well as perceived interpersonal closeness (IOS) between speakers. Results show that gaze features capture predictive structure, and that combining them with loudness improves performance (ROC AUC = 0.76 +- 0.04). Loudness reflected speaker control, while gaze dispersion and addressing indexed listener readiness and competitive entry. Performance remained robust across noise conditions, indicating that gaze provides a complementary, noise-resilient cue to turn-taking dynamics.

</details>


### [32] [Beyond Global Scalars: Synergizing Token-Level Statistics and Deep Semantics for Adversarial AIGC Text Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28009)
*Peiming Li,Yifan Wang,Zhiyuan Hu,Shiyu Li,Zheng Wei,Yang Tang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces NeuroStat, a robust detector for machine-generated text, and MOSAIC, an adversarial benchmark, showing NeuroStat outperforms existing methods under attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rapidly improving large language models can generate text that is hard to distinguish from human writing, while current detectors are fragile, especially under adversarially manipulated or mixed human-AI text. There is a need for more robust detection methods and a benchmark that systematically evaluates adversarial robustness.

Method: The authors first construct MOSAIC, a 16,000-sample benchmark covering a wide range of adversarial attack types and granularities on machine-generated text. Then they propose NeuroStat, an end-to-end detector built on a single causal language model backbone that jointly uses token-level probabilistic logits (statistics) and deep semantic hidden states. They fuse these via Macro-State Residual Modulation, which adjusts local convolutional features using global uncertainty signals, and they apply orthogonal and contrastive losses to enforce complementary representations between statistical and semantic branches.

Result: On the MOSAIC benchmark, existing detection methods experience large performance drops under adversarial attacks, while NeuroStat retains strong performance, showing much higher robustness and setting new state-of-the-art results for adversarial machine-generated text detection.

Conclusion: Combining fine-grained statistical information with semantic representations within one model, and carefully fusing them with modulation and specialized losses, yields a much more robust detector of machine-generated text. The MOSAIC benchmark and NeuroStat framework together provide a new standard and evaluation protocol for adversarial text detection.

Abstract: The rapid evolution of large language models necessitates robust machine-generated text detection. Existing paradigms typically follow two isolated tracks. Training-free methods rely on global statistical scalars such as perplexity, while training-based methods utilize semantic hidden states. Both approaches exhibit fundamental vulnerabilities in adversarial scenarios. Global scalars act as lossy compressions that obscure local probabilistic burstiness in interleaved texts, whereas pure semantic models overfit to specific fingerprints and remain susceptible to spoofing. To expose these flaws, we introduce MOSAIC, a comprehensive adversarial benchmark comprising 16000 samples across a full-granularity attack spectrum. To address these challenges, we propose NeuroStat, an end-to-end framework bridging the statistical and semantic gap. NeuroStat captures uncompressed token-level probabilistic logits alongside deep semantic hidden states from a single causal language model backbone. We fuse these heterogeneous signals through Macro-State Residual Modulation, which adaptively calibrates local convolutional features using global uncertainty indicators. Orthogonal and contrastive losses further ensure the learning of complementary representations. Extensive experiments demonstrate that NeuroStat maintains exceptional robustness on MOSAIC compared to the severe degradation of state-of-the-art methods, establishing a new standard for adversarial text detection. Code and the MOSAIC benchmark are available at https://github.com/TencentBAC/NeuroStat.

</details>


### [33] [Twin Worlds: Equivariance-Based Abstention for Evidence-Grounded Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28018)
*Vy Nguyen,Ziqi Xu,Jeffrey Chan,Estrid He,Feng Xia,Renqiang Luo,Erik Cambria,Xiuzhen Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Twin Worlds, a framework that detects when LLM answers are not truly grounded in given evidence by checking how answers change under structured substitutions of entities, and uses these failures as a signal to abstain instead of hallucinating.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often hallucinate in knowledge-intensive tasks, especially when the context partially matches their memorized knowledge. Existing abstention methods focus on predicting uncertainty or checking if evidence seems sufficient, but they do not directly verify whether the model’s reasoning is actually grounded in the given evidence versus its internal parametric memory. The authors aim to design a more faithful abstention mechanism that reacts to the interaction between context entities and the model’s memorized associations, and that can better catch confident but ungrounded answers.

Method: They propose Twin Worlds (TW), an equivariance-based abstention framework. The key idea: if a model is truly grounded in evidence, then when you substitute entities in the input with type-preserving alternatives that keep the relational structure intact, the model’s answer should change in a correspondingly consistent way (equivariance). TW builds multiple “worlds” by performing typed substitutions of entities in the original input to reduce the influence of model priors while preserving relations among entities. The model is queried on these transformed worlds; violations of equivariance (i.e., inconsistent or inappropriate answer transformations across worlds) are detected and used as a signal that the answer is not reliably grounded, prompting abstention.

Result: Across four knowledge-intensive reasoning benchmarks and three different LLM backbones, TW more accurately identifies ungrounded answers than methods based solely on uncertainty estimation or evidence sufficiency checks. Empirically, TW improves reliability by better detecting when answers are not supported by the provided evidence, leading to superior abstention performance compared to the baselines.

Conclusion: Equivariance under structured entity substitutions is an effective criterion for assessing whether LLM reasoning is grounded in evidence. By constructing multiple relationally consistent but entity-substituted worlds and using violations of equivariance as an abstention trigger, Twin Worlds improves the reliability of knowledge-intensive reasoning. This framework outperforms existing uncertainty- and sufficiency-based approaches and suggests that explicitly probing how model outputs transform under context perturbations is a powerful way to detect hallucinations and encourage abstention when evidence is insufficient.

Abstract: Knowledge-intensive reasoning requires Large Language Models (LLMs) to ground answers in provided evidence. When evidence is insufficient, it is desirable that models abstain rather than confidently generating unsupported answers. Existing abstention methods rely on uncertainty estimation or evidence sufficiency checks, but neither tests whether the reasoning process for generation, driven by the interaction of provided evidence and the model's internal memory parameters, is actually grounded in the evidence. A key contributing factor is that entity mentions in context activate memorised associations, causing models to generate plausible responses ungrounded in evidence. We propose Twin Worlds (TW), a framework for improving reliability in knowledge-intensive reasoning through equivariance-based abstention: unlike invariance, which requires outputs to remain unchanged, equivariance requires outputs to transform correspondingly under entity substitutions. A model grounded in the evidence should produce answers that shift consistently when entities are substituted while their relations are preserved. TW constructs multiple worlds via typed substitutions of the original input that preserve relational structure while reducing parametric priors, and uses equivariance violations as an abstention signal. Across four benchmarks and three model backbones, TW identifies when answers are not reliably grounded in the provided evidence and outperforms uncertainty- and sufficiency-based baselines.

</details>


### [34] [A Shaky Voice Is Not Always a Dodge: Benchmarking Textual and Vocal Evasion Detection in Earnings Calls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28040)
*Mirae Kim,Seonghun Jeong,Youngjun Kwak*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces DualEvasion, a benchmark that jointly analyzes textual and vocal dimensions of evasion in earnings call Q&A, showing that current multimodal models poorly detect vocal confidence and lag behind humans, especially for unconfident answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work treats evasion in earnings calls as a one-dimensional, text-only phenomenon, ignoring how vocal delivery contributes independent information about whether executives are being evasive or confident. There is a need to systematically study and benchmark evasion as a multidimensional construct that spans both what is said and how it is said, especially in high-stakes financial communication.

Method: The authors construct DualEvasion, a benchmark of 505 annotated question-answer pairs from 60 earnings calls. Each answer receives two independent labels: textual evasion (direct vs. evasive) and vocal cues operationalized as speaker confidence (confident vs. unconfident). They then evaluate state-of-the-art multimodal models on this dataset, analyzing their performance on detecting both textual evasion and vocal confidence, and test whether giving models speaker-level reference segments improves detection of vocal confidence.

Result: Multimodal models can handle some aspects of the task but particularly underperform in detecting vocal confidence, with their largest weaknesses on unconfident responses. Error analysis shows that these models tend to interpret acoustic cues in an absolute sense instead of normalizing them relative to each speaker’s baseline speaking style. Introducing speaker-level reference audio segments slightly improves performance but still leaves a pronounced gap compared with human annotators.

Conclusion: Evasion in spoken earnings calls is fundamentally multidimensional, requiring joint modeling of textual content and vocal delivery. Current multimodal models are not yet capable of robustly capturing vocal confidence, especially for unconfident responses, partly because they lack speaker-relative calibration. The DualEvasion benchmark exposes these limitations and provides a foundation for developing better models that integrate textual and speaker-normalized acoustic cues for evasion detection.

Abstract: Existing approaches to evasion detection in earnings calls focus on textual transcripts, treating evasion as a single-dimensional phenomenon. We argue that evasion in spoken communication is inherently multidimensional: beyond what executives say, how they say it carries independent and complementary information. To study these dimensions jointly, we introduce DualEvasion, a benchmark for evasion detection across text and audio in earnings call Q&A. The benchmark contains 505 annotated question-answer pairs from 60 earnings calls, each with two independent labels: textual evasion (direct vs. evasive) and vocal cues operationalized as speaker confidence (confident vs. unconfident). Our experiments show that state-of-the-art multimodal models struggle to detect vocal confidence, particularly on unconfident responses. Our analysis suggests these models interpret acoustic cues in isolation rather than relative to each speaker's baseline. Providing speaker-level references yields modest improvements, but a substantial gap with human performance remains.

</details>


### [35] [SimpCue: Cue-Based Prompting for Multilingual Text Simplification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28042)
*Mehrzad Tareh,Horacio Saggion,Stefan Bott*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies whether adding explicit linguistic complexity cues to prompts improves multilingual sentence-level text simplification with an LLM, finding only modest, metric- and language-dependent gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While large language models can simplify text via prompting, it is unclear if explicitly providing linguistic information about sentence complexity in the prompt helps them produce better simplifications, especially for Easy-to-Read guidelines and in multiple Romance languages.

Method: The authors perform multilingual sentence-level Easy-to-Read simplification experiments in Catalan, Spanish, and Italian using the Qwen3-8B model. They design three prompting conditions: a baseline prompt without extra information, a gold-cue prompt including gold-standard linguistic complexity cues, and a predicted-cue prompt with automatically predicted cues. They generate simplifications under each condition and evaluate the outputs using automatic metrics (SARI, BLEU, chrF, BERTScore) and a complementary manual qualitative analysis.

Result: The predicted-cue prompting condition achieves the best overall performance across SARI, BLEU, chrF, and BERTScore, but the improvements over the baseline are small. Gold-cue prompting does not consistently outperform the baseline, and its effectiveness differs by language. Overall, cue-based prompting has only limited and variable impact on simplification quality.

Conclusion: Adding linguistic complexity cues to prompts can affect multilingual Easy-to-Read simplification with LLMs, but the benefits are modest and depend on the evaluation metric and language. Automatically predicted cues slightly help more than gold cues, suggesting that cue-based prompting is not a straightforward or universally beneficial strategy for LLM-based text simplification.

Abstract: Text simplification aims to make complex texts easier to understand while preserving their original meaning. Recent large language models can perform simplification through prompting, but it remains unclear whether adding explicit linguistic information about sentence complexity to the prompt improves their outputs. We investigate this question for multilingual sentence-level Easy-to-Read simplification in Catalan, Spanish, and Italian. Using Qwen3-8B, we compare a baseline prompt, a gold-cue prompt enriched with gold linguistic cues, and a predicted-cue prompt enriched with automatically predicted cues. We evaluate the outputs using SARI, BLEU, chrF, and BERTScore, and complement this evaluation with a manual qualitative analysis. Predicted-cue prompting obtains the best overall scores across all four metrics, although the gains over the baseline are small. Gold-cue prompting does not consistently improve over the baseline, and results vary across languages. These findings indicate that cue-based prompting can influence multilingual Easy-to-Read simplification, but its benefits are modest, metric-dependent, and language-dependent.

</details>


### [36] [CNeo-Bench: Diagnosing Large Language Models on Chinese Neologisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28053)
*Kaiyan Zhao,Zhongtao Miao,Zheyong Xie,Shaosheng Cao,Yoshimasa Tsuruoka*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build a benchmark to test how well LLMs understand and manipulate Chinese internet neologisms, and show current models largely struggle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chinese neologisms often rely on creative phonetic or visual mechanisms that differ from typical word formation, and existing benchmarks do not specifically target this kind of linguistic creativity. The authors want to systematically measure how well LLMs can recognize and actively use these mechanisms rather than only approximate meanings.

Method: They construct CNeo-Bench, a dataset of 4,759 Chinese neologisms with gold definitions, categorized into 5 top-level and 9 finer-grained mechanism-based classes. They design a two-tier evaluation: (1) definition generation to see if models can explain the neologism, and (2) mechanism operation tasks such as restoring the original source form. They then evaluate 18 different LLMs on these tasks, and also run a few-shot in-context learning analysis on 1,058 particularly difficult items.

Result: Most LLMs perform poorly, typically below 40% accuracy on definition generation. In several subcategories, models can describe neologisms but fail to correctly manipulate their underlying mechanism: instead of recovering the exact source form, they output paraphrases or semantic equivalents. Few-shot prompting considerably improves performance on many hard cases but still leaves a significant number of errors.

Conclusion: Understanding and operating on Chinese neologisms is still a substantial open challenge for current LLMs. Benchmarks like CNeo-Bench reveal a gap between semantic recognition and precise form-based manipulation, and show that better modeling—beyond simple prompting—is needed to handle the creative, mechanism-rich nature of Chinese neologisms.

Abstract: Chinese neologisms exploit diverse and unique linguistic mechanisms, such as phonetic substitution (e.g., 886 for ``bye-bye'') and visual character decomposition that are rare in other languages. We introduce CNeo-Bench, a benchmark of 4,759 such neologisms with reference definitions, organized into five top-level categories and nine subcategories by the linguistic mechanism behind each expression. CNeo-Bench is paired with a two-tier evaluation framework that separates whether a model can describe a neologism from whether it can operate on its underlying mechanism. Evaluating 18 LLMs, we find that Chinese neologisms remain an open challenge; most models fall below 40\% on definition generation, and on several subcategories a systematic recognition-manipulation gap emerges: models describe neologisms correctly but, in source-form restoration tasks, substitute a semantic equivalent (paraphrase) for the source form rather than producing the source form itself. A few-shot analysis on 1,058 hard items shows that in-context examples can solve many difficult cases, but leave a noticeable portion of errors remaining, indicating challenges beyond prompting alone can address.

</details>


### [37] [H-Scale: Hessian-Guided Scale Refinement for NVFP4 Sub-Byte LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28113)
*Hao Yu,Zheng Li,Dayiheng Liu,Jianwei Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes H-Scale, a lightweight post-processing method to refine per-group scales for NVFP4 quantization on NVIDIA Blackwell, improving LLM inference accuracy with no runtime cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NVFP4’s ultra-fine-grained micro-block format (e.g., group size 16) enables flexible representation and good handling of local weight distributions and outliers, but creates a large, sensitive space of per-group scaling factors. Existing post-training quantization techniques mostly tune quantized weights while largely ignoring optimal scale selection, which can heavily affect model accuracy, especially for LLM inference on new hardware like Blackwell. A method is needed to better choose these scales while remaining hardware-aware and efficient.

Method: Introduce H-Scale, a post-processing algorithm that refines per-group NVFP4 scales after standard PTQ. Rather than minimizing raw weight reconstruction error, it uses a diagonal second-order proxy, computed from calibration activations, to approximate the effect of weight perturbations on layer outputs. It then selects hardware-valid group scales that directly minimize this proxy. H-Scale is implemented as a drop-in replacement for simple RTN-style (round-to-nearest) scale selection within various NVFP4 quantization pipelines, requiring only modest offline calibration and adding no overhead during inference.

Result: Using a fixed evaluation protocol on mainstream LLMs, integrating H-Scale into multiple NVFP4 quantization baselines consistently improves their performance. Many of these H-Scale–enhanced NVFP4 variants achieve accuracy closer to the BF16 full-precision reference than their original counterparts, demonstrating the effectiveness and generality of the method across architectures and quantization setups.

Conclusion: Per-group scale selection is a crucial but underexplored component of ultra-fine-grained NVFP4 quantization. By leveraging a diagonal second-order proxy from calibration activations, H-Scale provides an effective, hardware-compatible way to refine these scales, improving LLM inference quality without incurring runtime overhead. This positions H-Scale as a practical, plug-and-play enhancement for NVFP4-based PTQ pipelines on NVIDIA Blackwell GPUs.

Abstract: The NVIDIA Blackwell architecture, with native support for the ultra-fine-grained NVFP4 format, opens new opportunities for accelerating large language model (LLM) inference. NVFP4's micro-block design, such as a group size of 16, offers strong representational flexibility for capturing local weight distributions and isolating outliers, but it also introduces a large and highly sensitive space of per-group scaling factors. Existing post-training quantization (PTQ) methods primarily focus on refining quantized weight values, leaving this scale-selection step underexplored. To address this gap, we propose \textbf{H-Scale}, a lightweight post-processing method for NVFP4 per-group scale refinement. Instead of minimizing plain weight reconstruction error, H-Scale selects hardware-valid group scales using a diagonal second-order proxy derived from calibration activations, thereby targeting layer output perturbation more directly. It is designed as a drop-in replacement for RTN-style scale selection in diverse NVFP4 pipelines, requires only modest offline calibration, and introduces strictly zero overhead at inference time. Under a fixed evaluation protocol, experiments on mainstream LLMs show that H-Scale generally improves a broad range of NVFP4 baselines and brings several variants closer to the BF16 reference.

</details>


### [38] [Nested Byte-Level Vocabularies Are Cheap to Deploy and Expensive to Share: A Pre-Registered Negative Result](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28151)
*Christos Koutsiaris*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how a single byte-level BPE language model can support multiple vocabulary sizes (“caps”) by slicing embeddings/heads based on prefix-nested merge rules, and evaluates accuracy, efficiency, and robustness versus fixed-vocabulary specialists.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tokenizers are usually trained for a single vocabulary size, forcing practitioners to pick one trade-off between sequence length, accuracy, and deployment cost. The authors want to know whether a single model can flexibly operate at multiple vocabulary sizes without retraining, how close it can get to the performance of specialized models at each size, and what the accuracy/robustness trade-offs are.

Method: They exploit the fact that byte-level BPE vocabularies are ordered by merge rules, so truncating the merge list gives a smaller, nested vocabulary. They train “multi-cap” language models that share a body but can run at different vocabulary sizes by: (1) using a control token indicating the active cap, and (2) slicing the embedding and output layers so only the relevant prefix of tokens is active. They preregister five main claims, including quantitative margins, seeds, comparisons, and a stopping rule. They train 30 models with 3.1M and 10.6M parameters on 200M tokens each, run a battery of checks on logit equivalence, compare against fixed-cap specialist models at each vocabulary size, and conduct a 2x2 ablation separating the presence of the control token from the output restriction. They also test robustness under typographical noise and analyze how performance penalties relate to the fraction of training devoted to each cap.

Result: Slicing is numerically exact: when comparing sliced models to their corresponding restricted full models, logits match bit-for-bit across 76 checks, and deployment can remove about 66% of weights without affecting latency. However, the shared multi-cap model underperforms fixed-cap specialists: at 32k vocabulary it is worse by 3.64% bits per byte (missing a 1% non-inferiority margin), and at 8k it is worse by 2.96% (missing a 2% margin. The ablation shows the control token has negligible, statistically uncertain effect (+0.07% to +0.13%), while output restriction hurts more (+0.47% to +1.19%), and the two factors behave as substitutes. Multi-cap training improves robustness: under typographical noise, the same multi-cap checkpoint loses 12.5–15.4 fewer points in its fine (larger-vocab) mode and even beats each fixed-cap specialist at that specialist’s vocab size. A control model without cap token or output restriction is equally robust, indicating robustness gains stem from multi-granularity training, not explicit conditioning. The per-cap performance penalty closely tracks each cap’s share of training examples.

Conclusion: A single byte-level BPE language model can be cleanly sliced to operate at multiple vocabulary sizes using tokenizer prefix nesting, with exact numerical equivalence and substantial parameter savings at deployment. However, there is a measurable accuracy cost relative to fixed-cap specialists, largely attributable to output space restriction rather than the cap-indicating control token. Multi-cap training notably improves robustness to typographical noise, and this benefit appears to come from exposure to multiple granularities in training rather than from conditioning on cap tokens. The relationship between per-cap penalties and training data allocation provides a concrete, testable prediction for scaling this approach in future work.

Abstract: A byte-level BPE tokenizer is an ordered list of merge rules, so applying only a prefix yields a vocabulary whose token identifiers are the first rows of the full vocabulary. This prefix nesting allows one language model to operate at several vocabulary sizes, use a control token to indicate the active size, and be deployed at any trained size by slicing its embedding and output head. We pre-registered five claims, including margins, seeds, contrasts, and a stop rule, and trained 30 models with 3.1M- and 10.6M-parameter bodies on 200M tokens each. Slicing is numerically exact: across 76 checks, a sliced model reproduces the restricted full model's logits bit for bit and removes 66% of deployed weights without changing latency. However, the shared model trails a fixed-cap specialist by 3.64% bits per byte at 32k against a 1% margin, and by 2.96% at 8k against a 2% margin. A 2x2 ablation separating the control token from output restriction finds that the token changes performance by +0.07% to +0.13%, with all intervals crossing zero, while output restriction costs +0.47% to +1.19%; the factors are substitutes rather than complements. Multi-cap training nevertheless improves robustness: under typographical noise, the same checkpoint degrades 12.5--15.4 points less in its fine mode and outperforms each fixed-cap specialist at that specialist's vocabulary size. A control with neither cap token nor output restriction is equally robust, attributing this benefit to multi-granularity training rather than conditioning. The per-cap penalty tracks each cap's share of training rows, yielding a falsifiable prediction for future work.

</details>


### [39] [FinExam-10K: When Retrieval Helps Financial Reasoning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28155)
*Yan Lin,Jingyu Sun,Zhongliang Guo,Qing Li,Zhuohan Xie,Yuxia Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduction of FinExam-10K, a large-scale benchmark for testing financial exam reasoning of AI models on CFA and FRM questions, along with evaluations and a gated FunctionGraph-RAG system that yields modest but statistically significant gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of AI on finance exams are fragmented and do not jointly reflect the full structure and difficulty distribution of professional certifications like CFA Levels I–III and FRM Parts I–II. There is also a need to distinguish between simple coverage/knowledge lookup and genuine reasoning over supplied context, and to understand whether tool-augmented methods such as RAG and function-calling actually improve reliability on hard, expert-level financial problems.

Method: The authors construct FinExam-10K, a curated and expert-reannotated dataset of 10,198 English multiple-choice questions covering CFA and FRM syllabi. They split it into: (1) a public subset and a sequestered subset used for a continuously maintained leaderboard; (2) a Full-Coverage Track with all questions; and (3) a Context-Complete Reasoning Track, where each item has all needed information in the prompt. They benchmark 17 models on these tracks, including a difficulty-stratified “Hard” band. They further test two tool-augmented methods—Function-RAG and FunctionGraph-RAG—that use retrieval and function-calling to support reasoning, then train a gating model (using only public data) that decides per question/initial answer whether to trigger FunctionGraph-RAG. They evaluate accuracy gains and statistical significance, particularly on the 5,088 held-out questions.

Result: The best-performing model reaches 85.29% accuracy overall, but performance drops sharply on the Hard band: 34.68% on the Full-Coverage Track and 54.57% on the 372 hard, context-complete questions. All 17 models consistently fail on the same 47 context-complete items, indicating persistent blind spots. Naively applying Function-RAG or FunctionGraph-RAG fixes hundreds of wrong answers but also flips many previously correct ones, resulting in negligible or negative net improvement. A learned gate that selectively invokes FunctionGraph-RAG on 7.9% of held-out questions increases accuracy from 70.83% to 71.23%, a small yet statistically significant gain (p = .0446).

Conclusion: FinExam-10K provides a comprehensive, high-difficulty benchmark for assessing AI capabilities on professional financial exams, with separate tracks to isolate reasoning over given context from general domain knowledge. Current models, even strong ones, still struggle with the hardest CFA/FRM-style items and share systematic failures. Tool-augmented approaches like FunctionGraph-RAG are not uniformly beneficial and must be applied selectively. A simple gating mechanism, trained on public data, can yield modest but statistically significant improvements, highlighting both the potential and the limitations of tool-augmented reasoning for expert financial question answering.

Abstract: Professional financial examinations require models to combine domain knowledge, calculation, and judgment, yet no benchmark covers the full CFA and FRM structure under one protocol. We introduce FinExam-10K, to our knowledge the largest reported English benchmark for this setting, with 10,198 expert-reannotated questions spanning CFA Levels I-III and FRM Parts I-II. We release 5,110 questions and sequester 5,088 for a quarterly maintained leaderboard. To separate coverage from local answerability, we report a 10,198-item Full-Coverage Track and a 7,625-item Context-Complete Reasoning Track, which is the primary basis for claims about reasoning from the supplied record. Across 17 models, the best accuracy is 85.29% overall. On the frozen Hard band, the best score is 34.68% on the Full-Coverage Track and 54.57% on the 372 context-complete items. All 17 models share 47 context-complete failures. Function-RAG and FunctionGraph-RAG rescue hundreds of errors but also overturn many correct answers, producing little or negative net gain. A gate trained only on public data decides from the question and initial response when FunctionGraph-RAG should run. On the 5,088 held-out items, the gate invokes FunctionGraph-RAG for 7.9% of questions and improves accuracy from 70.83% to 71.23% (p = .0446).

</details>


### [40] [Text Restoration of Ancient Documents with Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28170)
*Shibingfeng Zhang,Edoardo Caraffa,Annafelicia Zuffrano,Maddalena Modesti,Giovanni Colavizza*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how well language models can help restore missing text in damaged ancient manuscripts and under what conditions they are most useful.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Paleographers restoring ancient manuscripts often face physical gaps (lacunae) where the text is missing. Manual restoration is slow, uncertain, and affected by recurring issues such as formulaic vs. non-formulaic passages and unknown lengths of missing text. With recent advances in language models, there is an opportunity to systematically test whether these models can assist, rather than replace, experts in reconstructing lost text under realistic constraints.

Method: The authors design several experimental scenarios that mimic real manuscript damage, differing in where in the document the gap appears and whether the length of the missing text is known. They select and apply language models with different architectures according to which models are best suited to each scenario. In addition, they develop decoding strategies tailored to manuscript restoration, including methods to better align model output with real lacuna boundaries despite mismatches between physical character spans and the models’ tokenization into subword units. They then evaluate model outputs both quantitatively and qualitatively.

Result: The study finds that current language models cannot fully automate manuscript restoration: expert oversight remains essential. However, models can substantially assist paleographers, especially in certain document sections and when the length of the missing text is known. Performance differs sharply between formulaic and non-formulaic content and between scenarios with or without lacuna length information. The proposed decoding strategies improve alignment between generated text and the physical gaps, enhancing practical usefulness.

Conclusion: Language models are promising assistive tools for restoring ancient manuscripts but are not standalone solutions. Their effectiveness depends strongly on document structure and prior knowledge of lacuna length. By systematically analyzing performance across formulaic vs. non-formulaic content and lacuna-length-aware vs. unaware settings, the paper provides concrete guidance for designing restoration workflows and tools that integrate language models into paleographic practice.

Abstract: Purpose - This study investigates the feasibility of restoring missing text caused by physical lacunae in damaged ancient manuscripts using language models.
  Methodology - The study proposes different scenarios to replicate real-world conditions. Language models of different architectures are applied according to their suitability to each scenario. We also propose several decoding strategies that further enhance performance and address the discrepancy between lacuna boundaries and the models' tokenization schemes.
  Findings - The results reveal that text restoration of these documents cannot be fully automated, but it can serve as a useful tool to assist paleographers in their work. Model performance varies greatly depending on which structural part of the document needs to be restored and whether the character length of missing text is available.
  Originality - This is the first study and to analyze model performance on formulaic and non-formulaic content and the impact of lacuna length awareness in manuscript restoration. Both are recurring challenges in paleographers' manual restoration work. Through systematic comparison and both qualitative and quantitative analysis of different models' performance under varying settings, this study offers a guideline for developing assistive tools to support paleographers.

</details>


### [41] [Embedding Models for Stance-Aware Argument Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28283)
*Angelo Sparacino,Francesca Toni,Adam Dejl*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Paper studies how dense embedding models can simultaneously capture topic relevance and stance (support/attack) in argument retrieval, finds two opposite failure modes, and proposes a data-centric training curriculum with stance-inverted arguments to fix them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In argument mining and computational argumentation, we need systems that can retrieve not just text about the same topic as a claim, but arguments that correctly support or attack that claim.
