Current semantic search with dense embeddings tends to capture topical similarity but often ignores or mishandles the direction of the stance (support vs. attack). Attempts to fix this by training on stance signals can cause over-reliance on explicit polarity cues (like the words 'supports' or 'refutes'), hurting generalization. The paper is motivated by the lack of models that robustly handle both topic relevance and directional stance reasoning in a unified retrieval pipeline.

Method: 1) Empirically probe existing dense embedding models on stance-aware argument retrieval tasks to analyze how they treat topic relevance vs. stance. 2) Apply contrastive training to encourage models to distinguish supporting from attacking arguments for a claim, revealing an overcorrection failure mode focused on polarity keywords. 3) Introduce diagnostic word-ablation metrics: systematically remove or mask certain words (like explicit stance markers) from inputs and measure performance drops to quantify reliance on superficial lexical cues vs. deeper semantic reasoning. 4) Propose a data-centric training strategy combining a balanced argument curriculum (carefully controlling distributions of stance/topic combinations) with LLM-generated, stance-inverted argument variants to break spurious lexical correlations and force models to encode deeper directional logic in their embeddings.

Result: Experiments show that baseline dense embedding models are biased toward topical similarity and largely ignore instructional stance, leading to poor stance-aware retrieval. Contrastive fine-tuning improves stance sensitivity but induces a new failure mode: models over-focus on explicit stance or polarity words and sacrifice topic understanding. The proposed diagnostic ablations confirm this over-reliance on polarity keywords. With the balanced curriculum and stance-inverted training data, sufficiently strong embedding models learn more robust directional semantics: they become less sensitive to the removal of polarity keywords while improving overall performance on stance-aware argument retrieval benchmarks, indicating better integration of topic relevance and stance in the learned embeddings.

Conclusion: Dense embedding models used in semantic search pipelines are not naturally equipped to perform reliable stance-aware argument retrieval: they overemphasize topical similarity and, when naively corrected, can swing to an overdependence on polarity cues. The paper concludes that targeted data-centric interventions are effective: a balanced argument curriculum combined with LLM-augmented stance-inverted examples encourages models to learn genuine directional logic about support vs. attack. Diagnostic word-ablation metrics provide a useful tool to reveal and track these failure modes. Overall, the work shows that with appropriately designed training data and evaluation metrics, modern embedding models can achieve more nuanced, stance-aware argument retrieval, improving the foundations for downstream reasoning tasks in computational argumentation.

Abstract: In computational argumentation, obtaining arguments that explicitly support or attack given claims is a critical precursor to downstream reasoning tasks. When these supporting and attacking arguments are to be retrieved using semantic search methods, they need to be assessed for topic-relevance to the claims of interest as well as for correctness of their (positive or negative) stance towards the claims. In this paper we explore how dense embedding models (hereafter, models), powering modern retrieval pipelines, can serve as the basis of semantic search incorporating this dual assessment. We show experimentally that existing models struggle with asymmetric reasoning, exhibiting a strong bias toward topical overlap while ignoring instructional stance. We also show that correcting this bias via contrastive training triggers a new failure mode where models over-correct, over-fixating on polarity keywords (e.g., "supports" or "refutes") at the expense of the semantic topic. We thus introduce diagnostic word-ablation metrics to quantify this phenomenon and propose a data-centric solution. By implementing a balanced argument curriculum alongside LLM-augmented, stance-inverted arguments, we force the (embedding) models to learn deeper directional logic rather than exploiting superficial lexical shortcuts. Our evaluation demonstrates that, for sufficiently powerful models, this approach can alleviate the observed overcorrection, achieving further improvements in stance-aware argument retrieval.

</details>


### [42] [A Probabilistic Interpretation of KV Cache Eviction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28293)
*Renato Geh,Alex Chen,Daniel Israel,Aditya Grover,Guy Van den Broeck*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper formalizes KV-cache eviction as a probabilistic expectation estimation problem, proves it is computationally hard in general, and proposes sampling-based and corrected decoding strategies that make eviction more principled and robust while maintaining competitive performance under the same compression budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KV-cache eviction in large language models is widely used to reduce memory and improve throughput, usually via heuristic rules for which key-value entries to drop. While effective empirically, these methods lack a formal foundation, making it unclear how to optimally choose eviction strategies, quantify their impact, or correct for the information lost when entries are removed. The authors are motivated to put KV eviction on firm theoretical ground, understand its computational complexity, and connect it to a probabilistic framework that can guide better algorithm design and decoding-time correction.

Method: The authors: (1) formally define the KV eviction problem and analyze its computational complexity, proving that finding optimal eviction strategies is hard; (2) recast KV eviction as a probabilistic inference problem, where evicting entries corresponds to estimating an expectation under a distribution over cache states; (3) propose to approximate this expectation via sampling-based methods, enabling stochastic estimators of the effect of eviction; (4) leverage this probabilistic view to develop a decoding-time correction mechanism that accounts for the contribution of evicted entries, which prior work ignored; and (5) reinterpret existing heuristic eviction methods as zero-variance biased estimators and show how to adapt them to support decode-time correction within this probabilistic framework.

Result: The study demonstrates that KV eviction, when treated as probabilistic expectation estimation and coupled with decode-time correction, yields eviction strategies that are more robust across different tasks compared to prior heuristic methods. Empirical evaluations show that the proposed probabilistic-and-corrected approach maintains competitive, and in some settings improved, performance while operating under the same KV compression budget as existing techniques.

Conclusion: KV-cache eviction is inherently a computationally hard optimization problem, but viewing it through a probabilistic lens turns it into an expectation estimation task that can be practically approximated via sampling. This view not only explains prior heuristic methods as biased estimators but also enables a new capability: decode-time correction for evicted entries. The resulting probabilistic eviction plus correction framework offers a more principled and robust way to compress KV caches without sacrificing model quality, matching or outperforming existing approaches under equivalent compression constraints.

Abstract: The premise and promise of KV (cache) eviction is simple: higher throughput can be achieved by evicting some entries from the KV cache, at a negligible cost to quality. This holds empirically for many existing methods, though most rely on creative heuristics for selecting which entries to drop. Despite recent advances, the problem of KV eviction has remained informal in the literature. This paper aims to properly formalize this problem through the lens of probabilistic reasoning and reveal what can be learned from this perspective. Concretely, we (1) formalize the problem of KV eviction and, unfortunately, prove that it is computationally hard, (2) show that by framing it probabilistically, KV eviction reduces to the problem of expectation estimation, which can be approximated through sampling, (3) show that through this probabilistic interpretation, correcting for evicted entries during decoding---a previously ignored problem---becomes feasible, and (4) reveal that existing methods in the literature are zero-variance biased estimators that can be easily adapted in order to enable decode time correction. In practice, we show that this probabilistic version of KV eviction coupled with decode time correction is more robust to different tasks compared to existing eviction methods and achieves competitive performance at the same compression budget.

</details>


### [43] [BanglaMed-QA: A Question Answering System for Healthcare Support in Bangla](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28329)
*Rowzatul Zannat,Abdullah Al Shafi,K. M. Azharul Hasan,Atia Shahnaz Ipa*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents BanglaMed-QA, a Bangla medical question answering system built on a curated QA knowledge base, specialized linguistic resources, supervised question classification, and similarity-based answer retrieval, achieving strong automatic and human-evaluated performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical QA systems are important for delivering trustworthy health information, but low-resource languages like Bangla lack datasets and systems tailored to their linguistic characteristics. This creates an information access gap for Bangla speakers, particularly in the medical domain where accuracy and clarity are critical. The paper aims to reduce this gap by creating a dedicated Bangla medical QA resource and system.

Method: The authors first construct a structured Bangla medical knowledge base containing 4,493 QA pairs across 9 categories and 506 diseases. They design domain-specific root word dictionaries and synonym sets to improve semantic understanding, and use POS tagging for anaphora resolution. For question processing, they train supervised machine learning models for question categorization and identify SVM as the best-performing model. For answer retrieval, they compute multiple similarity measures (cosine, Jaccard, BM25, Levenshtein) between the input query and stored questions and combine them using soft and hard voting schemes to select the most relevant QA pair.

Result: The system is evaluated automatically and via human judgments. Automatically, it reaches a 95% F1 score on its QA task, indicating high accuracy in retrieving correct answers. Human evaluators give an average satisfaction score of 0.9 out of 1.0, suggesting that users find the answers highly satisfactory in practice.

Conclusion: BanglaMed-QA demonstrates that a carefully constructed knowledge base, combined with linguistic resources and supervised learning plus similarity-based retrieval, can yield an effective medical QA system for a low-resource language. The strong automatic metrics and high human satisfaction indicate that the system is suitable for real-world deployment and can help alleviate the medical information gap for Bangla-speaking populations.

Abstract: Medical question answering (QA) systems have become crucial tools for providing reliable health information. But they remain very unexplored for low-resource languages like Bangla due to limited datasets and systems tailored to these languages. To address this, we introduce BanglaMed-QA, a robust QA system specifically designed for the Bangla medical domain. The process begins with building a structured medical knowledge base that includes 4,493 QA pairs in 9 categories under 506 diseases. To improve semantic comprehension, domain-specific root word dictionaries and synonym sets are proposed, in addition to part-of-speech tagging for anaphora resolution. We adopt supervised machine learning models in which SVM is found to be the best model to categorize questions. Multiple similarity metrics, including cosine, Jaccard, BM25, and Levenshtein, are applied with soft and hard voting methods for query matching. The performance of the QA system has been evaluated in two aspects, with a 95% F1 score in an automated evaluation and an average human satisfaction rating of 0.9 out of 1.0. This validates the real-world application of BanglaMed-QA in closing the healthcare information gap for Bangla speakers.

</details>


### [44] [PersonaForge: Realistic Multi-Turn User Simulation for Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28378)
*Hanglong Lv,Dawei Zhu,Lei Li,Bowen Ye,Huaqiu Liu,Yifan Song,Bofei Gao,Weimin Xiong,Jinhao Dong,Chenhong He,Lingpeng Kong,Qi Liu,Tong Yang,Fuli Luo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PersonaForge is a framework to simulate realistic multi-turn user–agent interactions and a benchmark to train and evaluate LLM agents in settings that match real-world usage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most current LLM training data and benchmarks focus on single-turn, fully specified queries, but real-world interactions with agents are mostly multi-turn and under-specified. This mismatch creates a gap between how agents are used and how they are optimized and evaluated. The paper aims to close this gap by providing realistic, controllable simulations of user behavior and an evaluation suite tailored to multi-turn, persona-driven interactions.

Method: The authors first analyze 16K real-world user–agent sessions to characterize interaction patterns, finding that around 76% are multi-turn. They then design PersonaForge, a user simulation framework with: (1) a four-dimensional persona space to define different user types, (2) SOUL-driven behavioral control (a structured set of behavior components calibrated to real-user statistics) to govern how simulated users behave across turns, and (3) Reverse Deep Construction, which builds multi-turn dialogues starting from authentic seed queries and expanding them into realistic conversations. Using this pipeline, they generate a 6.3K-record training dataset and a 138-task benchmark (PersonaForge-Bench) spanning 20+ professional domains with four-dimensional scoring criteria. They fine-tune Qwen3.5-27B with this data and run ablation studies to isolate the contribution of different components (e.g., SOUL, adaptive simulation).

Result: Training Qwen3.5-27B on PersonaForge-generated data improves overall composite performance by 4.1% on PersonaForge-Bench, with particularly strong gains in Task Completion (+6.0%) and Response Quality (+6.8%). The trained agents require fewer dialogue turns and tool calls to solve tasks, indicating more efficient interactions. Ablation experiments show that removing SOUL components or adaptive simulation reduces these gains, highlighting their importance.

Conclusion: Model training and evaluation that assume single-turn, fully specified queries miss critical aspects of real-world agent usage, which is predominantly multi-turn and persona-dependent. PersonaForge provides a systematic way to simulate realistic user behaviors and create multi-turn datasets and benchmarks, leading to measurable improvements in agent effectiveness and efficiency. The framework and benchmark form a foundation for future work on multi-turn, agentic LLMs aligned with real user interaction patterns.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used as agentic workflow executors, yet existing training data and benchmarks largely assume informationally complete, single-turn queries. Our analysis of 16K real-world sessions shows that 75.9% of interactions are multi-turn, revealing a substantial gap between how users interact with agents and how such systems are trained and evaluated. We introduce \textbf{PersonaForge}, a user simulation framework for synthesizing realistic multi-turn user--agent interactions. PersonaForge combines a four-dimensional persona space, SOUL-driven behavioral control calibrated to real-user statistics, and Reverse Deep Construction grounded in authentic seed queries. Using PersonaForge, we construct a 6.3K-record training dataset and \textbf{PersonaForge-Bench}, a manually annotated 138-task benchmark spanning over 20 professional domains with four-dimensional scoring. Experiments on Qwen3.5-27B show that PersonaForge training improves the composite score by +4.1%, with gains across all four dimensions and the largest improvements in Task Completion (+6.0%) and Response Quality (+6.8%). Further analyses show that PersonaForge-trained agents use fewer turns and tool calls, suggesting improved interaction efficiency, while ablations confirm the contribution of SOUL components and adaptive simulation. Together, PersonaForge and PersonaForge-Bench establish a foundation for training and evaluating agents under realistic multi-turn user interaction.

</details>


### [45] [When Linguistic and Internal Confidence Diverge in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28382)
*Hefan Zhang,Bingquan Zhang,Ming Cheng,Saeed Hassanpour,Weicheng Ma,Soroush Vosoughi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper examines whether large language models’ verbal self-reported confidence aligns with their internal uncertainty measures across many tasks and models, and finds that alignment is partial, task- and model-dependent, and often poorly calibrated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Users and applications increasingly rely on LLMs’ verbal confidence statements (e.g., “I’m 90% sure”), assuming these reflect the model’s true uncertainty. However, it is unknown how well this linguistic confidence corresponds to quantitative internal measures such as logits or entropy. Understanding this alignment is crucial for building reliable systems that use LLM self-assessment for safety, calibration, or routing decisions.

Method: The authors evaluate 30 models from three families on 8 classification and 2 generation tasks. For classification, they compare verbal confidence ratings to logits-based confidence along three axes: (1) association (instance-level correlation between verbal and logit confidence), (2) magnitude agreement (how closely numerical levels match), and (3) calibration (whether reported confidence matches empirical accuracy). For generation, they compare linguistic confidence to semantic-entropy-based uncertainty. They explore the impact of instruction tuning, prompt design (including attitude cues and score exemplars), and perform regression analyses to see how distributional properties of reported confidence and model metadata explain alignment patterns.

Result: They find that linguistic and internal confidence often diverge. Instance-level association is generally weak but improves on easier items and for stronger base models. Instruction-tuned models tend to report higher confidence and sometimes higher association, yet their confidence gaps are larger and calibration is worse. Prompt modifications mostly shift the distribution of reported confidence: attitude cues systematically inflate confidence without improving alignment, whereas carefully designed score exemplars can preserve rank-order information if they avoid collapsed (e.g., always-high) confidence values. Regression results indicate that the shape and dispersion of confidence score distributions largely account for observed alignment patterns, while model metadata (e.g., size, family) matters less once these are controlled for.

Conclusion: Verbal confidence from LLMs is best viewed as a lossy channel that can retain some rank-order information about underlying uncertainty but does not reliably preserve calibrated probabilities. Greater dispersion in verbal confidence can make it more informative for ranking but does not guarantee calibration. Therefore, linguistic confidence should not be assumed trustworthy by default; it needs multi-axis evaluation (association, magnitude, calibration, etc.) before being integrated into downstream reliability or safety mechanisms.

Abstract: Users often ask large language models (LLMs) to report how confident they are, but it is unclear whether such linguistic confidence tracks the model's internal confidence. We study this question across 8 classification tasks, 2 generation tasks and 30 models from three families. For classification, we compare linguistic confidence with logits-based confidence along three axes: association, magnitude agreement and calibration. For generation, we test whether linguistic confidence tracks semantic-entropy-based uncertainty. The axes frequently diverge. Instance-level association is weak on average, although it improves on easier items and for stronger base models. Instruction-tuned models often report higher confidence and sometimes show higher association, but they also have larger confidence gaps and worse calibration. Prompt design mostly changes the distribution of reported confidence. Attitude cues inflate confidence without improving alignment, while score exemplars can preserve rank-order signal when they avoid collapsed confidence values. Regression analyses show that distributional properties of confidence scores explain much of the observed alignment pattern, with model metadata playing a smaller role after controls. These results support a lossy-channel view of linguistic confidence. A more dispersed verbal confidence distribution can carry useful rank information, but it does not make the scores calibrated. Linguistic confidence should therefore be evaluated with multi-axis diagnostics before being used in downstream reliability pipelines.

</details>


### [46] [CultureConverse: A Multilingual Multi-turn Simulation Harness for Culturally Grounded Assistance in East and Southeast Asia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28405)
*Bryan Chen Zhengyu Tan,Weihua Zheng,Thong T. Doan,Bich Ngoc Doan,Jia Wang Peh,Xiaoyuan Yi,Jing Yao,Xing Xie,Nancy F. Chen,Zhengyuan Liu,JinYeong Bak,Wafi Shamdi,Soo Kai Chie,Liew Yu Siong,Aina Azyyati Binti Mohamad Rezal,Lew Yan Yan Vanessa,Huadan Wu,Dylan Raharja,Nadya Yuki Wangsajaya,Akane Fukushige,Kazushi Kato,Koji Inoue,Tatsuya Kawahara,Jaehyung Seo,Dongjun Kim,Seungyoon Lee,Zi Haur Pang,Rui Yang Tan,Charibeth Ko Cheng,Maria Regina Justina Estuar,Jann Railey Montalan,Pham Minh Duc,Roy Ka-Wei Lee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They present CultureConverse, a multilingual simulation and evaluation framework for multi-turn, culturally grounded assistant dialogues, plus a large dataset and benchmarks showing model performance and fine-tuning gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cultural benchmarks for LLMs mostly use single-turn, factual MCQs that oversimplify culture and miss realistic, multi-turn, practical help scenarios. There is a need for scalable, multilingual evaluation that reflects how users actually ask for culturally sensitive assistance, especially in diverse East and Southeast Asian contexts.

Method: They build CultureConverse, a simulation and evaluation harness that generates multi-turn, culturally grounded dialogues where the assistant must infer cultural constraints from partial information while helping the user. The framework covers 10 East and Southeast Asian regions, 58 subgroup identities, and 7 domains. It yields a dataset (CultureConverse-DS) with two splits: benchmark episodes for evaluation and oracle-guided dialogues for gold references. They evaluate 18 models on the benchmark, compare automatic scores with human annotation, and fine-tune models on a subset of high-quality samples to measure in-domain and out-of-domain gains on other cultural and safety benchmarks.

Result: CultureConverse-DS comprises 14,610 benchmark episodes and 274,295 oracle-guided dialogues. Among 18 evaluated models, GPT-5 mini scores highest on assistance quality. Human annotation suggests the automatic evaluation is a good proxy for human judgments. Fine-tuning on 27,860 selected samples improves cultural assistance performance within the benchmark and transfers to improved performance on separate cultural MCQ and safety classification benchmarks.

Conclusion: The work shows that multi-turn, simulation-based cultural evaluation is feasible, scalable, and better aligned with real-world assistant use than traditional MCQ-style cultural tests. CultureConverse and CultureConverse-DS provide an effective benchmark and resource for measuring and improving LLM cultural competency, and the released harness and prompts enable broader, interactive evaluation and future research in this area.

Abstract: Current cultural evaluations for large language models (LLMs) often reduce culture to single-turn factual recall via MCQs, failing to capture a common use case: users seeking practical help over multiple turns in culturally grounded scenarios. We introduce CultureConverse, a scalable, multilingual simulation and evaluation harness for culturally grounded assistant dialogue that covers 10 East and Southeast Asian regions, 58 subgroup identities, and 7 domains. Each simulated and evaluated episode produces a scored interaction where the assistant assists the user and infers cultural constraints from partial information. The resulting CultureConverse-DS dataset contains 14,610 benchmark (evaluation) episodes and 274,295 oracle-guided (gold-mode) dialogues. In our benchmark evaluation of 18 models, GPT-5 mini achieves the highest assistance quality. Human annotation experiments suggest that our evaluation framework is a sufficient proxy for human judgment. Performance gains from fine-tuning on 27,860 high-quality CultureConverse-DS samples improve in-domain assistance and transfer out-of-domain to cultural MCQ and safety classification benchmarks. We release the harness, both splits, and judge prompts to support interactive evaluation of cultural competency.

</details>


### [47] [A Unified Framework to Elicit Structured Feedback for Interpretable Multi-Trait Essay Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28407)
*Shihang Yang,Sanwoo Lee,Ningning Zhao,Yunfang Wu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes HiFTS, a unified autoregressive framework for multi-trait Automated Essay Scoring that jointly generates hierarchical rubric-grounded feedback and trait/holistic scores, achieving strong performance and coherent feedback on Chinese CFMS-34 and English ASAP++ datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-trait AES methods tend to either separate feedback generation from scoring or treat each trait independently. This leads to inconsistencies between scores and feedback and weakens alignment with complex, rubric-based grading where traits are interdependent. There is a need for an approach that tightly integrates rubric-grounded reasoning, feedback, and scoring within a single framework.

Method: The authors introduce HiFTS, an autoregressive model that first generates hierarchical chain-of-thought (CoT) feedback and then predicts trait-level and holistic scores. They distill rubric-grounded hierarchical CoT feedback from a teacher LLM into a student model, so the student jointly learns feedback and scoring. Training is further optimized via Group Relative Policy Optimization with a composite reward that balances score agreement with references, score calibration, feedback quality, and structural validity of the feedback. During inference, a lightweight global prior is used to provide holistic guidance and prevent reasoning drift for long-form outputs. They also construct CFMS-34, a Chinese multi-trait AES dataset with holistic scores and 34 rubric-based traits for 951 essays.

Result: On the CFMS-34 and ASAP++ datasets, HiFTS performs well on both holistic and trait-level scoring metrics while also producing coherent feedback that aligns with the scoring rubrics. The method shows that jointly modeling hierarchical feedback and scores improves both scoring accuracy and rubric-consistent feedback quality compared to baselines.

Conclusion: Jointly generating hierarchical rubric-based feedback and scores within an autoregressive framework, enhanced by teacher-student CoT distillation and reinforcement optimization, is effective for multi-trait AES. HiFTS improves score–feedback consistency, rubric alignment, and scoring accuracy across languages and datasets, and the newly introduced CFMS-34 dataset provides a benchmark for Chinese multi-trait essay evaluation.

Abstract: Multi-trait Automated Essay Scoring (AES) requires rubric-grounded reasoning across interdependent traits, rather than isolated score prediction. Existing feedback-enhanced methods often decouple feedback from scoring or assess traits independently, weakening score--feedback consistency and rubric alignment. We propose HiFTS, a unified autoregressive framework that generates hierarchical CoT feedback before predicting trait-level and holistic scores. HiFTS distills rubric-grounded hierarchical CoT feedback from a teacher LLM and trains student models to jointly generate feedback and scores. HiFTS further applies Group Relative Policy Optimization with a composite reward balancing score agreement, calibration, feedback quality, and structural validity. At inference, a lightweight global prior provides holistic guidance to reduce drift during long-form reasoning. We also introduce CFMS-34, a Chinese multi-trait AES dataset with 951 essays annotated with holistic scores and 34 rubric-based traits. Experiments on CFMS-34 and ASAP++ show that HiFTS achieves strong holistic and trait-level scoring while producing coherent, rubric-aligned feedback.

</details>


### [48] [Are These Modules Worth Their Cost? A Paradigm-Level Accuracy-Cost Analysis of In-context Learning Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28432)
*Jiayan Lin,Yujia Liu,Zijin Hong,Zheng Yuan,Yilin Xiao,Hao Chen,Qinggang Zhang,Xiao Huang,Feiran Huang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Paper systematically analyzes cost-accuracy tradeoffs of in-context-learning (ICL) text-to-SQL pipelines across many design paradigms and model backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ICL text-to-SQL systems have become increasingly complex, using many pipeline components around a base LLM. Benchmarks usually report only overall accuracy, so we lack clear understanding of how each module (e.g., examples, schema handling, refinement) individually contributes to accuracy versus its cost. This makes it hard to design cost-effective pipelines and to compare paradigms fairly across models.

Method: Implement a unified ICL text-to-SQL framework and factor it into five common pipeline modules (e.g., how contexts/examples are chosen, schema presentation, refinement mechanisms). Instantiate 17 distinct “paradigm-level” configurations by turning these modules on/off or varying their design. Evaluate each paradigm across four LLM backbones that differ in capability and reasoning behavior. Measure execution accuracy and detailed token-level cost (input/output tokens). Attribute marginal contribution and cost of each paradigm and analyze interactions among modules (stacking effects). Test whether the derived configuration guidelines transfer to five additional backbones without re-tuning per paradigm.

Result: 1) Execution-feedback refinement is the only module that consistently improves accuracy across all tested models while remaining cheap in token usage. 2) Most other modules yield benefits only under certain backbone-dependent conditions. 3) Input token cost is driven mainly by pipeline structure (e.g., how much context/schemas/examples are stuffed in), while output token cost depends more on the specific backbone’s generation behavior. 4) Combining multiple modules usually improves accuracy (stacking), but the way improvements compose depends on backbone capability. 5) Under a fixed budget, investing in a more sophisticated pipeline around a mid-tier model often yields higher accuracy than using a frontier model with a minimal pipeline. 6) The authors derive a tiered, cost-aware set of design recommendations that generalize to new backbones without needing to search over all paradigms again.

Conclusion: Careful design of the ICL text-to-SQL pipeline can matter more for cost-effective accuracy than simply using the strongest available backbone. Execution-feedback refinement stands out as a universally beneficial, low-cost component, while other modules should be chosen with awareness of backbone-specific interactions and token-cost profiles. The proposed cost-aware, tiered configuration guidelines help practitioners systematically choose pipeline complexity and model strength under budget constraints and transfer well to additional models without per-paradigm optimization.

Abstract: Recent advances in in-context learning (ICL) text-to-SQL have substantially improved execution accuracy on public benchmarks by assembling increasingly elaborate pipelines around the base generator, yet existing studies typically report aggregate end-to-end accuracy, without quantifying the marginal accuracy-cost contribution of individual design choices. Consequently, providing a unified, paradigm-level cost-accuracy quantification remains a critical challenge for understanding and configuring modern text-to-SQL. To address this, we instantiate 17 paradigm-level configurations across five recurring modules of the ICL text-to-SQL pipeline under a single controlled implementation, and attribute each paradigm's marginal contribution and incurred cost across all four backbones spanning diverse capability levels and reasoning styles. Our analysis reveals that execution-feedback refinement is the only paradigm whose benefit holds universally at consistently low cost, while most other modules help only under backbone-dependent conditions. Token accounting shows that input demand is more closely tied to pipeline structure, whereas output demand is more sensitive to backbone generation behavior. Cross-module analysis further shows that stacking improves accuracy on most backbones, although how the gains compose varies with backbone capability. We also find that a fixed budget is often better spent engineering a more elaborate pipeline over a mid-tier backbone than upgrading to a frontier model with a lean pipeline. These findings distill into an actionable, cost-aware tiered guideline that transfers to five additional backbones without per-paradigm search.

</details>


### [49] [Fidelity Is Not Enough: Dispatch-Level Instrumentation for Agentic Datasheet Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28439)
*Qing Ye,Meng-Hsuan Lin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper examines how to detect silent failures in agentic document-extraction systems by logging and analyzing tool calls, showing that standard fidelity metrics can miss fabricated answers and that tool instrumentation improves observability but not intrinsic accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional fidelity metrics for document-extraction agents only compare extracted values to source documents. They can mistakenly mark runs as successful even when the model never consulted the source and fabricated answers. The authors were motivated by a real incident where a structured-output constraint disabled tool use, yet the model produced seemingly correct answers with invented source text. This highlighted the need to understand and detect such silent failures in multi-tool, agentic pipelines, and to clarify what benefits the tool layer actually provides in deployed systems.

Method: The authors construct an "agentic benchmark" of 37 hand-curated claims over multiple document components. They fully log the dispatch record of tool calls during extraction runs across several model families. From these logs, they build (1) a rule-based classifier that attributes different kinds of failures based on which tools were or were not called, and (2) a very simple silent-failure detector that looks only at the pattern of tool usage, not the extracted values. Separately, they build a "causal chamber"—a physical measurement setup that can test whether certain claims in datasheets hold in the real world, thereby serving as an independent oracle for a subset of claims. They also conduct controlled perturbations and evaluate three deployed model stacks to see how toolization affects portability, observability, and accuracy.

Result: The silent-failure detector, which uses only two rules on tool-call patterns and never inspects the answer content, raises no false alarms on 207 fidelity-passing clean runs across three model families. It also reliably flags all 50 deliberately planted failures that withheld exactly the tools targeted by its rules, ensuring perfect recall for that fault class. However, its power against runs that do call tools but still answer incorrectly remains unmeasured. The causal chamber can directly test only 2 of the 37 claims, due to physical and practical constraints, and the authors categorize the reasons why the other claims cannot be graded in this way. Experiments across three deployed model stacks show that one stack’s problems arise from the serving infrastructure rather than model capability. Overall, they find that adding a tool layer improves portability and observability of extraction systems but does not inherently improve accuracy, and that its extra cost is justified mostly when documents are too large for the model’s context window.

Conclusion: Standard fidelity metrics for agentic document extraction can be dangerously misleading because they ignore whether the source was actually consulted. Logging tool calls and analyzing tool-usage patterns enables simple, rule-based detectors that can capture a meaningful class of silent failures with low false-positive rates, though they do not address all failure modes. A physically grounded oracle (the causal chamber) is feasible but only for a narrow subset of claims, and many real-world assertions resist direct physical verification. In practice, the main value of tools in deployed LLM stacks lies in making systems more portable across models and more observable for debugging, rather than in boosting raw extraction accuracy; their premium is most worthwhile when tasks exceed the model’s context window. This suggests that reliability work on agentic systems must go beyond fidelity metrics and incorporate richer instrumentation and, where possible, independent oracles.

Abstract: One model passed our fidelity check without ever opening the datasheet. We found it while qualifying models for an internal extraction service: a structured-output constraint had silently disabled tool use, and the model answered anyway, with fabricated source text. Only the per-tool trace exposed it. Fidelity -- whether an extracted value matches the source -- is the standard measure for agentic document extraction, and it scores that run a success. We therefore log every tool call in an agentic benchmark of 25 hand-curated claims over three components, with 12 more on a fourth, 37 in all. From that dispatch record we build two instruments: a rule-based failure-attribution classifier, and a silent-failure detector whose two rules check only which tools were called, never the extracted value. The detector raises no flag on 207 clean fidelity-passing extractions across three model families, and recovers all 50 planted faults that withhold exactly the tools its rules check. The two results are not symmetric: the first bounds the false-positive rate, the second is recall by construction, and detection power against runs that call their tools and still answer wrongly is unmeasured. A second, independent oracle, a causal chamber that tests whether the datasheet's claims hold under physical measurement, is intentionally partial: it confirms only what the apparatus can exercise, a verifiable envelope of 2 of those 37 claims, and we give a taxonomy of why the rest are not physically gradable. Under a controlled perturbation, fidelity passes throughout while the chamber verdict flips exactly at the measurement uncertainty. Across three deployed model stacks (one destabilised by its serving stack, not by any capability gap) the tool layer buys portability and observability rather than accuracy, and earns its premium only once a document outgrows the context window.

</details>


### [50] [Sliding-window beats linear attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28444)
*Alexia Jolicoeur-Martineau,Rhea Sanjay Sukthanker,Pashmina Cameron,Emy Gervais*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Sliding Window Attention (SWA) with sink tokens matches or outperforms retrofitted linear attention for long-context LLM inference, while being cheaper, faster, and requiring no post-training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quadratic-time self-attention in LLMs leads to rapidly growing memory and energy costs because keys and values for all previous tokens must be stored, making long-context inference inefficient and unsustainable. Although linear attention retrofits have been proposed as a fix, they have not been rigorously compared against simpler, stronger baselines.

Method: The authors retrofit existing LLMs with Sliding Window Attention augmented with sink tokens (special positions with global attention) and compare them against LLMs post-trained with various linear attention mechanisms. They evaluate across multiple models and a range of downstream tasks, with emphasis on long-context reasoning benchmarks such as Needle-in-a-Haystack and BABILong, measuring accuracy, speed, and memory usage.

Result: Across multiple LLMs and tasks, SWA with sinks performs on par with or better than post-trained linear attention variants. On long-context reasoning tasks like Needle-in-a-Haystack and BABILong, SWA delivers 2–10× higher performance than linear attention while also exhibiting lower memory use and very high inference speed. These gains are achieved without any additional post-training cost for SWA.

Conclusion: A simple Sliding Window Attention with sinks is a strong, low-cost baseline that typically outperforms retrofitted linear attention in long-context reasoning while being faster and more memory-efficient. For reducing inference memory cost in practice, the authors recommend adopting SWA rather than investing in post-training linear attention retrofits, suggesting that linear attention may need training from scratch or heavy post-training just to match SWA.

Abstract: Due to the nature of quadratic attention, Large Language Models (LLMs) consume a lot of memory and energy. Every new token costs more than the previous one. For each additional token, the keys and values must be stored in memory indefinitely, which is unsustainable.
  Several alternatives have been proposed to fix the quadratic scaling problem, one of which is retrofitting LLMs to use Linear Attention. This idea has attracted a lot of attention, given its promise to solve the quadratic scaling problem with state-of-the-art performance at low cost. However, this line of research has not been properly compared to simpler baselines.
  In this work, we show that Sliding Window Attention (SWA) with sinks performs as well or better than post-trained Linear Attention models. We observe this across multiple LLMs on various downstream tasks. For long-context reasoning tasks (Needle-in-a-Haystack and BABILong), SWA achieves massively higher performance (2 to 10 times higher than linear attention). SWA requires no post-training, is extremely fast, and requires low memory; therefore, making it an extremely cheap and reliable solution.
  To reduce inference memory cost, we strongly recommend switching to SWA instead of post-training linear models. Linear attention models may have shown some promise, but they likely require to be trained from scratch or extensive post-training in order to even match SWA.

</details>


### [51] [Acquire, Repair, Preserve: A Diagnosis-Guided Post-Training Recipe for Small-Model Dialogue Game Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28458)
*Nan Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes why small open-weight language models fail in interactive dialogue games and proposes a three-step training recipe that significantly improves in-domain interactive performance while keeping static benchmark scores roughly unchanged.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static NLP benchmarks don’t adequately test whether models can maintain dialogue state, follow feedback, and choose valid actions in multi-turn interactive settings. In the LM Playschool Challenge, a 2B parameter model shows many local decision errors (repeated guesses, malformed actions, ignoring fresh feedback), not just broad knowledge gaps. The authors are motivated to design training methods that specifically fix these local decision and mechanics-related failures while preserving general capabilities.

Method: They work with a 2B open-weight model on the LM Playschool dialogue-game benchmark. Their training recipe has three stages: (1) supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on broad game participation data to improve general ability to engage in these games; (2) targeted repair of mechanically checkable failures in a specific dialogue-game family, using turn-local preference pairs that encourage correct over incorrect actions conditioned on the immediate context and feedback; (3) regularization or other strategies to preserve general capabilities, checking that static benchmark performance remains similar to the baseline model. They then evaluate on public and closed in-domain clemscore metrics as well as out-of-domain variants and standard static benchmarks.

Result: On the official Playschool evaluation, their trained model boosts public clemscore from 10.67 to 38.92 and closed in-domain score from 13.41 to 41.17 relative to the baseline 2B model, indicating much better interactive performance in-domain. Static benchmark performance is almost unchanged (44.14 vs. 44.24), so gains did not come at the cost of general capability collapse. However, out-of-domain clemscore is still low at 7.88, and improvements are mostly in unseen variants within the specifically targeted dialogue-game family, not across very different games.

Conclusion: Supervised fine-tuning on broad game data accounts for most of the capability gain in interactive dialogue games, while additional turn-local supervision is effective for repairing specific mechanically-detectable decision failures when the error signals are precise. The benefits of this targeted supervision mainly transfer within the same dialogue-game family and do not automatically generalize out-of-domain. Overall, the work highlights that interactive competence requires addressing both broad knowledge and fine-grained local decision-making, and that it is possible to significantly boost such competence without sacrificing static benchmark performance.

Abstract: Interactive dialogue games test a capability that static benchmarks largely leave implicit: a model must carry state across turns, interpret feedback, and choose valid actions under changing constraints. We study this setting in the LM Playschool Challenge with a 2B open-weight model, and find that many failures are not only broad knowledge failures but also local decision failures: repeated guesses, malformed actions, and violations of feedback that the model has just seen. These diagnostics motivate a training recipe organized around three steps: acquire broad game participation through supervised fine-tuning, repair mechanically verifiable failures within one targeted dialogue-game family using turn-local preference pairs, and preserve general capabilities beyond these dialogue games. In the official final evaluation, our submission improves public clemscore from 10.67 to 38.92 and closed in-domain score from 13.41 to 41.17, while approximately preserving aggregate static performance (44.14 vs. 44.24 for the baseline). Out-of-domain clemscore remains low at 7.88, with the largest gains concentrated in unseen variants of the targeted family. Our results suggest that broad SFT brings most of the model's capability improvement; turn-local supervision can be effective when failure detection is precise, with observed transfer concentrated primarily within-family.

</details>


### [52] [Stranger, Fan, or Peer? A Systematic Study on the Role of Interlocutor in Persona-Based Dialogue Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28467)
*Daniela Occhipinti,Malvina Nissim,Marco Guerini*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper examines how visibility of speaker biographies at different stages (training, inference, evaluation) affects persona-based dialogue generation and biography leakage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persona-based dialogue models often copy biography text instead of naturally expressing personas, and prior work conflates who sees which biography across training, inference, and evaluation. The authors want to disentangle these stages to understand how biography visibility drives persona expression vs. leakage.

Method: They use a dialogue dataset paired with speaker biographies and systematically vary visibility of target and interlocutor biographies during training and inference. An LLM judge performs author identification to detect biography leakage. They analyze how different visibility configurations affect copying of biographical text and leakage patterns between target and interlocutor turns.

Result: (i) Training-time biography visibility has a stronger effect than inference-time visibility on whether models express personas naturally or copy biography text. (ii) Models trained with access to interlocutor biographies copy less target-biographical text than those trained without that access; changing visibility only at inference time yields weaker and inconsistent effects. (iii) Under asymmetric disclosure, where only the interlocutor sees the target's biography, target information tends to leak into interlocutor turns, making dialogues with such leakage easier for the LLM judge to attribute, especially when it can see interlocutor turns.

Conclusion: Biography leakage and copying behaviors in persona-based dialogue systems largely arise from how interlocutor biography visibility is configured across training and inference. Properly separating and controlling biography visibility at training, inference, and evaluation is necessary to build more robust persona-based dialogue models and to interpret their behavior correctly.

Abstract: Persona-based dialogue systems are usually conditioned on speaker biography, but dialogues involve at least two participants, and who has access to whose biography can vary across training, inference, and evaluation. Prior work often neglected these aspects, obscuring mechanisms that only appear when biography visibility is toggled separately across training, inference, and evaluation, a three-stage factorisation that prior work has largely treated as a single factor. We study this factorisation on a dataset of dialogues paired with speaker's biographies, varying whether the target and interlocutor speakers see each other's biographies during training and inference, and using an LLM as a judge to perform author identification. We find that (i) training-time visibility, more than inference-time visibility, determines whether models express persona traits through dialogue or fall back on copying biographical text (a known problem/phenomenon in persona-based generation); (ii) models trained with interlocutor-biography visibility copy less target-biographical text than models trained without it, while changing visibility only at inference time has a less consistent effect; and (iii) under asymmetric disclosure, where only the interlocutor sees the target biography, target content leaks into interlocutor turns more often, and dialogues containing such traces are easier for the judge to identify, especially when interlocutor turns are visible. These results suggest that biography leakage into generated turns is an artefact of how interlocutor visibility is configured across training and inference, and separating the three stages is necessary.

</details>


### [53] [ContextPilot: Teaching Agents for Proactive Context Management via Fine-grained RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28476)
*Zhuoshi Pan,Qizhi Pei,Junru Lu,Honglin Lin,H. Vicky Zhao,Di Yin,Xing Sun*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ContextPilot is a proactive context management framework that equips LLM agents with richer context-editing tools and a tailored RL algorithm to handle long-horizon tasks more effectively and compactly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon, multi-turn tasks require LLM agents to repeatedly retrieve and integrate dispersed information over extended interactions. Naively keeping the entire history causes an ever-growing context, which is inefficient and may harm reasoning. Existing proactive context management approaches let the model edit its working context, but they are limited by a narrow toolset (only search, delete, summarize), inefficient exploration that ignores the differing impact of context actions, and coarse-grained RL credit assignment that attributes final rewards equally to all context edits. There is a need for a framework that offers richer context tools and more precise learning signals to optimize context management for long-horizon reasoning.

Method: The paper introduces ContextPilot, a proactive context management framework for LLM agents. First, it extends the set of available tools beyond search, deletion, and summarization to include global planning, long-term memory management, and soft context offloading (adaptive compression) operations. Second, it proposes a reinforcement learning algorithm tailored to context management: it leverages changes in context and entropy to identify which context-editing decisions are critical and should be branched for further exploration; then, for each context-editing action, it estimates action-level advantages by aggregating outcomes from all branched trajectories that include that action. This yields finer-grained credit assignment and more efficient policy learning for when and how to edit context.

Result: On long-context QA and deep search benchmarks, ContextPilot consistently outperforms prior proactive context management baselines across multiple underlying LLMs. It achieves better task performance while maintaining a more compact working context, demonstrating both improved effectiveness and efficiency in long-horizon agentic reasoning settings.

Conclusion: Richer context tools, combined with a context-aware RL procedure that focuses exploration on impactful edits and assigns credit at the action level, enable LLM agents to manage their working context more intelligently. ContextPilot shows that proactive, learned context management can substantially improve long-horizon performance while reducing context size, suggesting a promising direction for scalable agentic reasoning in LLMs.

Abstract: Long-horizon agentic tasks require large language models (LLMs) to iteratively retrieve, integrate, and maintain dispersed information across multi-turn interactions, but preserving all interaction histories leads to a continuously growing working context. Recent proactive context management methods allow models to edit their own working context with specialized tools, yet they still face three key limitations: (1) a limited toolset restricted to search, deletion, and summarization, with no support for global planning, long-term memory, and adaptive compression; (2) inefficient exploration that treats context management actions uniformly despite their heterogeneous impacts on final outcomes; and (3) coarse-grained credit assignment that assigns the final trajectory-level reward to all intermediate context editing actions during RL. To bridge these gaps, we introduce ContextPilot, a proactive context management framework for long-horizon agentic reasoning. Our approach systematically augments the toolset with planning, long-term memory, and soft context offloading tools. We further propose an RL method tailored for context management, which uses context and entropy variation to identify critical editing decisions for branch sampling and estimates action-level advantages from all branched trajectories that pass through the corresponding context editing action. Experiments on long-context QA and deep search tasks show that ContextPilot achieves stronger performance with a more compact working context, consistently outperforming existing baselines across various base models and benchmarks. Code is available at https://github.com/Tencent/ContextPilot.

</details>


### [54] [Blind Men and the Elephant: Probing the Epistemic Myopia of LLMs under Long-Tail Divergent Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28478)
*Zhuoshi Pan,Junru Lu,Yan Qian,H. Vicky Zhao,Di Yin,Xing Sun*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ElephantBench introduces a benchmark to test whether LLMs can recall multiple, conflicting factual accounts instead of just one canonical answer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard factual QA assumes a single correct answer, which hides whether LLMs actually store and can retrieve multiple, conflicting accounts of obscure (long-tail) facts. The authors want to measure and diagnose this "epistemic myopia"—the tendency of models to remember or output only the dominant account—using a reproducible and auditable setup.

Method: They build ElephantBench, a closed-book knowledge probe of 1,094 questions created via a graph-based pipeline over a low-exposure web corpus. The pipeline: (1) retrieves related documents around candidate facts, (2) detects naturally occurring disagreements to form multi-account QA items, (3) verifies each distinct answer against its source documents and other authoritative web sources, and (4) has human annotators review the records. They then evaluate 32 LLMs on this benchmark, and conduct corpus-analysis to relate web exposure patterns to model recall behavior.

Result: Even the best model recalls both divergent accounts for only 52.4% of questions; on almost all remaining items it retrieves just one account and misses the other. Larger model sizes and stronger inference-time reasoning methods increase recall but leave significant gaps. Corpus analysis reveals exposure imbalance: models tend to reproduce the majority/dominant account, and higher exposure for the minority account correlates with more complete multi-account recall.

Conclusion: ElephantBench functions as a robust, source-traceable benchmark for probing epistemic myopia in LLMs’ parametric memory, showing that current models often fail to retain or surface multiple legitimate accounts of long-tail facts. The graph-based pipeline offers a scalable way to convert long-tail corpora into auditable knowledge probes, supporting future work on improving the epistemic rigor and completeness of LLM knowledge representations.

Abstract: Factual question answering (QA) typically assumes a single canonical answer, obscuring whether large language models (LLMs) retain divergent accounts of long-tail facts. To address this gap, we introduce ElephantBench, a closed-book knowledge probe comprising 1,094 questions generated through an auditable graph-based pipeline. The pipeline retrieves related documents from a low-exposure web corpus, identifies naturally occurring disagreements, and converts them into multi-account QA records. Each answer is verified against the originating documents and authoritative public web sources and is then reviewed by human annotators. Across 32 models, even the strongest model recovers both accounts on only 52.4% of questions, while on nearly all remaining questions it recalls one account but omits the other. Scaling model size and inference-time reasoning improve recall but do not eliminate this incompleteness. Corpus analysis further shows that exposure imbalance favors the dominant account, whereas greater minority-side exposure is associated with more complete recall. These findings establish ElephantBench as a reproducible knowledge probe for diagnosing epistemic myopia in parametric memory. More broadly, our graph-based benchmark construction pipeline provides an efficient and scalable way to turn long-tail corpora into source-traceable knowledge probes, supporting efforts to evaluate and advance the epistemic rigour of next-generation LLMs. Code is available at https://github.com/Tencent/ElephantBench.

</details>


### [55] [NL2AGBench: Benchmarking LLM Auto-Formalization for AlphaGeometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28481)
*Samuel Xiao,Judy Song,Rory Hu,Ziliang Zong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build a benchmark to test how well LLMs translate English geometry problems into AlphaGeometry’s formal language, evaluate 10 models with execution-based checks, find big gaps between closed- and open-source models, and analyze error types and mitigation strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are good at language and math reasoning but it’s unclear how well they can convert informal math problems into the precise formal syntax required by neuro-symbolic systems like AlphaGeometry. AlphaGeometry is highly capable but hard to use because humans must manually rewrite problems in its DSL, creating a bottleneck that limits practical deployment.

Method: They construct NL2AGBench, a benchmark of English geometry problems paired with formal AlphaGeometry DSL representations. They verify translations via execution in AlphaGeometry instead of text similarity. They run 10 open- and closed-source LLMs of various sizes on this benchmark and measure executable translation rate, syntactic validity, and logical/geometric faithfulness. They build an error taxonomy (syntax vs logic) and test mitigation techniques such as few-shot prompting, fine-tuning, and human-provided hints.

Result: Closed-source frontier models achieve over 80% executable translation accuracy, whereas even the largest open-source models often fail to maintain geometric constraints and generate formally valid representations. The error taxonomy reveals distinct patterns in syntax vs logic mistakes, and interventions like few-shot examples, task-specific fine-tuning, and human hinting produce consistent, though not complete, improvements across different model families.

Conclusion: LLMs can substantially automate translation of English geometry problems into AlphaGeometry’s DSL, especially in closed-source frontier models, but open-source models lag behind and struggle with both syntax and geometric logic. Execution-based evaluation is a more reliable metric than textual similarity. Error categorization and targeted mitigation strategies improve performance, but fully robust automatic formalization remains an open challenge, motivating further research on model training and better interfaces between natural language and formal theorem provers.

Abstract: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in natural language understanding and mathematical reasoning. However, their ability to translate informal mathematical problems into formal representations remains underexplored. This limitation is particularly important for neuro-symbolic geometry systems such as AlphaGeometry, whose theorem-proving engine requires inputs in a specialized domain-specific language (DSL). Although AlphaGeometry achieves near-IMO gold-medalist performance, manually converting natural-language problems into its formal syntax remains a significant usability bottleneck. To address this challenge, we introduce the Natural Language to AlphaGeometry Benchmark (NL2AGBench), which evaluates LLMs in translating English geometry problems into AlphaGeometry-compatible formal representations. NL2AGBench uses execution-based verification within AlphaGeometry to assess translation quality rather than relying solely on textual similarity. We evaluate ten state-of-the-art open- and closed-source LLMs across multiple parameter scales and analyze executable translation accuracy, syntactic correctness, and error characteristics. Our experiments reveal a substantial performance gap between closed- and open-source models: leading closed-source models achieve executable translation rates above 80%, while even the largest open-source models struggle to consistently preserve geometric constraints and produce valid formalizations. We introduce an error taxonomy distinguishing syntax and logic errors and investigate mitigation strategies, including few-shot prompting, fine-tuning, and human-guided hinting, which yield measurable improvements across multiple model families.

</details>


### [56] [Ladders in Chaos: When, How, (and Perhaps Why) Does Test-Time Scaling Improve LLM Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28496)
*Di Wu,Sergey Troshin,Christof Monz,Antske Fokkens,Vlad Niculae*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper compares sequential vs. parallel (i.i.d. + reranking) test-time scaling methods for LLM-based machine translation, finding that sequential sampling has a higher performance ceiling, improves fluency and naturalness, but can harm accuracy at large budgets; its benefits are partly due to larger target-side context and are robust to temperature but sensitive to how context is constructed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs can be improved at test time by generating multiple candidate outputs, there are two main paradigms—sequential and parallel sampling—whose behavior and trade-offs in machine translation are not well understood. The authors aim to clarify which paradigm works better, under what conditions, and why, especially when inference budgets (number of samples) vary.

Method: The authors empirically compare sequential sampling (where each new translation attempt conditions on previous ones) with parallel, independent sampling plus reranking, under varying sampling budgets. They perform multidimensional manual evaluations of Best-of-N translations focusing on fluency, naturalness, and accuracy. They also run controlled experiments to isolate mechanisms, especially the role of expanded target-side context, and conduct ablations on sampling temperature and different ways of constructing sequential context.

Result: Sequential sampling achieves a higher performance ceiling than parallel sampling, particularly with small sampling budgets, by producing a more diverse and useful set of candidate translations. Human evaluations show that sequential sampling significantly improves fluency and naturalness but may reduce accuracy when many samples (large budgets) are generated. Controlled studies indicate that access to larger target-side context is a key factor in its success. Ablation studies show that sequential sampling is robust across temperatures but its performance depends notably on how the sequential context is built.

Conclusion: Sequential test-time scaling can be a more powerful strategy than parallel i.i.d. sampling for LLM-based machine translation, especially when inference budgets are limited, primarily due to its ability to leverage previous outputs as additional target-side context. However, at high budgets it can hurt accuracy, and its effectiveness depends on careful design of the sequential context. These findings suggest that future work should focus on better context construction mechanisms and balancing fluency gains with accuracy preservation in sequential self-improvement setups.

Abstract: Two forms of test-time scaling for Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as effective and widely adopted paradigms: sequential, in which later answer attempts depend on earlier ones, and parallel, such as i.i.d. sampling with reranking. In this study, we investigate their properties in translation. First, our study shows that sequential sampling has a higher performance ceiling, providing a more diverse and effective pool of samples, particularly under smaller sampling budgets. Second, we interrogate the nature of test-time scaling through a multidimensional manual analysis. Human analysis of the Best-of-$N$ translations demonstrates that sequential sampling substantially improves translation fluency and naturalness, but can degrade accuracy when inference budgets are large. Finally, we suggest an explanation of the mechanism through which sequential scaling improves machine translation. Our controlled analysis partially attributes the success of sequential self-improvement to the model's access to a larger target-side context. Ablation experiments on sequential sampling demonstrate its robustness across different sampling temperatures, while also revealing sensitivity to context construction, suggesting directions for future improvement.

</details>


### [57] [Phoneme- and Word-Level Metrics Using Self-Supervised Speech Representations for Forced Alignment Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28508)
*V. S. D. S. Mahesh Akavarapu,Michael Daniel,Gerhard Jäger*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose two automatic, reference-free metrics (PCMI and WACS) using self-supervised speech representations to evaluate forced aligners across many languages without manual timestamps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating forced alignment quality usually needs human-annotated timestamps, which is expensive and limits large-scale, especially multilingual, analysis. The authors want scalable, language-agnostic, and reference-free evaluation methods that can still discriminate good from bad alignments and work in low-resource, complex languages.

Method: They derive two corpus-level metrics from self-supervised speech representations. PCMI clusters SSL-based frame representations and measures mutual information between these clusters and the phoneme labels produced by the aligner, capturing label–acoustics agreement. WACS compares repeated word tokens: it encodes word segments using SSL representations and uses dynamic time warping to compute similarity between realizations of the same word, aggregating this into a consistency score. They test robustness by applying random and systematic perturbations to alignments, then compute these metrics for multiple alignment systems on 85 FLEURS languages, compare them with timestamp-based quality measures, and validate them against manually annotated alignments in 45 DoReCo languages plus two additional low-resource languages.

Result: Both PCMI and WACS consistently degrade as synthetic perturbations are applied, indicating sensitivity to alignment errors. Across 85 languages and various aligners, the metrics clearly distinguish high- from low-quality alignments and show strong correlation with standard, timestamp-based alignment quality metrics. They also perform well in phonologically complex, low-resource languages. An open-source Python implementation is released.

Conclusion: Self-supervised speech representations enable effective, scalable, and reference-free evaluation of forced aligners. The proposed PCMI and WACS metrics are robust to perturbations, correlate well with traditional gold-aligned evaluations, generalize across 80+ languages (including low-resource and complex phonologies), and are packaged as a reusable open-source toolkit for the community.

Abstract: Forced alignment evaluation typically requires manually annotated timestamps, limiting large-scale and multilingual analysis. We introduce two corpus-level metrics based on self-supervised (SSL) speech representations for reference-free forced alignment evaluation: Phoneme-Cluster Mutual Information (PCMI) and Word Acoustic Consistency Score (WACS). PCMI measures agreement between aligned phoneme labels and clusters induced from SSL-speech representations, while WACS measures consistency of repeated word realizations using dynamic time warping similarity between word representation sequences. Using both random and systematic perturbations, we show that PCMI and WACS degrade consistently under alignment perturbations. We further analyze the metrics across multiple alignment systems on 85 languages from FLEURS, validate them against manually annotated alignments from 45 languages in DoReCo, and evaluate them on two phonologically complex low-resource languages. The metrics effectively separate high- and low-quality alignments and correlate strongly with timestamp-based alignment quality measures. Our results demonstrate that SSL-speech representations enable scalable, reference-free forced alignment evaluation. The metrics are available as an open-source Python package at https://github.com/mahesh-ak/forced-aligner-metrics.

</details>


### [58] [A Formal Limitation on Learning Human Language From Textual Corpora](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28560)
*Emily Cheng,Ryan Cotterell*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper uses information theory to derive fundamental limits on how well any text-based representation (including LLM hidden states) can recover a speaker’s intended meaning from the utterance alone, and validates these limits empirically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand, in a precise and model-agnostic way, how much of a speaker’s intended meaning is in principle recoverable from linguistic form alone, versus what is irreducibly dependent on context. This addresses debates about how far purely text-trained systems can go in understanding meaning, and provides theoretical limits that apply to any representation, including those learned by large language models.

Method: The authors model language use as a joint probability distribution over meanings, contexts, and utterances. Using information-theoretic tools, they derive upper bounds on the probability that any decoder, fed only a representation of the utterance (from arbitrary featurizers like LLM hidden states), can correctly infer the intended meaning. They decompose the remaining uncertainty about meaning into an irreducible component and a component that can only be resolved with extralinguistic context. They then run experiments on controlled artificial languages, Mandarin zero-pronoun resolution, and color reference tasks to empirically check how tight and predictive these bounds are.

Result: They obtain general-purpose upper bounds—independent of the specific model or featurizer—on decoding accuracy from text-only representations. The theory shows that certain uncertainty about meaning cannot be eliminated by better representations of form alone. Empirical studies across artificial languages, Mandarin zero pronoun resolution, and color reference tasks confirm that actual model performance respects these bounds and that context-sensitive phenomena exhibit substantial irreducible uncertainty from form alone.

Conclusion: There are intrinsic, information-theoretic limits on how accurately a listener (or model) can recover intended meaning from linguistic form alone, no matter how powerful the representation or how much text supervision it is trained on. Part of the uncertainty in interpreting meaning is irreducible without extralinguistic context. This constrains the capabilities of text-only language models and clarifies which aspects of understanding inherently require access to context beyond the utterance.

Abstract: Can a listener recover what a speaker means from the form of an utterance alone? We answer this question information-theoretically, and for a listener given by any featurizer of text, including the hidden states of contemporary large language models. Modeling language use as a joint distribution over meanings, contexts, and utterances, we derive upper bounds on the probability that a decoder recovers a speaker's intended meaning from a representation of the utterance. The bounds are governed by the uncertainty that form leaves about meaning, which splits into an irreducible part and a part that only (extralinguistic) context, but never the utterance alone, can resolve. Because these quantities are intrinsic to language, no representation, however much text or supervision produced it, can surpass them; the bounds hold whether the space of meanings is discrete or continuous. Experiments on artificial languages, Mandarin zero-pronoun resolution, and color reference provide empirical evidence in support of the theory.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [59] [Time Capsule of Testable Human Knowledge: 41 Years of Jeopardy! in a Single Free Local Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27459)
*David Noever,Forrest McKee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper compares IBM Watson’s Jeopardy!-playing DeepQA system with modern, small, open-weight language models by running a 9 GB Qwen2.5-14B (4-bit) model on the complete Jeopardy! clue corpus (1984–2025) and shows that today’s portable models capture a similarly rich, culturally significant body of knowledge—while generalizing beyond their training time—at a fraction of the cost and footprint.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the knowledge-capturing role of AI systems has evolved since IBM Watson, and to quantify how much general cultural knowledge a modern, compact, open-weight language model can demonstrate on a standardized, large-scale benchmark (Jeopardy! clues), including clues that postdate its training data.

Method: The authors take an open-weight 14B-parameter language model (Qwen2.5-14B quantized to 4-bit, ~9 GB) and run it against the entire open Jeopardy! clue dataset of 529,939 clues from 41 seasons (1984–2025). They enforce a strict forced-response setting and evaluate answers using exact and fuzzy matching. They additionally separate clues into those that existed before versus after the model’s training cutoff, and compare performance to IBM Watson (whose curated corpus and tuning directly targeted Jeopardy!) and to Claude Opus 4.8 on post-cutoff clues.

Result: The 9 GB Qwen2.5-14B model correctly answers 67.0% of all Jeopardy! clues under the strict evaluation protocol and exceeds 85% accuracy on factoid-style categories. On clues aired after its training cutoff, the local model maintains 65% accuracy, while Claude Opus 4.8 achieves 95%, whereas Watson—whose knowledge base was frozen and Jeopardy-specific—scores effectively 0% on these unseen clues by construction.

Conclusion: Modern, compact, open-weight language models can act as portable “snapshots” of a culture’s accumulated general knowledge, similar in spirit to IBM Watson’s curated corpus but vastly cheaper, more flexible, and not tied to a single historical moment. Unlike Watson’s frozen, Jeopardy-specific dataset, these models generalize to clues that did not exist at training time, showing that the capability to answer broad cultural questions has moved from specialized server-room systems to small, distributable artifacts that can be stored, moved, or even placed in a time capsule.

Abstract: In 2011, IBM's Watson was something like a sealed capsule of its era's queryable knowledge. Its DeepQA system defeated the strongest human Jeopardy! champions, but the knowledge that let it do so lived in a curated billion-document corpus running on a cluster of POWER7 servers, frozen at build time and impossible to move or copy. We show that the same kind of artifact, a snapshot of what a culture can answer, is now portable and essentially free.
