We evaluate a single 9 GB open-weight model (Qwen2.5-14B, 4-bit) against the complete open Jeopardy! clue dataset, 529,939 clues across all 41 broadcast seasons from 1984 to 2025. To our knowledge this is the first time a model has been run over the full corpus. The 41 years mark only how long the questions were collected. What they test is far older and broader: the accumulated body of human general knowledge a culture considers worth knowing, from ancient history and dead languages to science, literature, and geography, with a verified answer for every item. The model answers 67.0% of all clues under a strict forced-response protocol with exact and fuzzy matching, and exceeds 85% on factoid categories. We treat training-data exposure as something both systems share rather than a flaw unique to language models. Watson's case is in fact the more extreme one. Its corpus was assembled to contain Jeopardy answers and it was tuned on past clues, and it could not answer anything outside that curated distribution. The decisive test is whether a model can answer clues that did not exist when it was built. On clues aired after its training cutoff, the local model holds 65% and Claude Opus 4.8 holds 95%, while Watson by construction scores zero. The capability survives the move from a server room to a file you could seal in a time capsule, and unlike Watson it is not frozen to its own moment.

</details>


### [60] [Rating the Raters: Rasch Measurement Theory for LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27463)
*Pratik S. Sachdeva,Nathan Boudol*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Applies Rasch measurement theory to analyze how LLMs behave as raters compared with humans, revealing systematic differences and arguing RMT should be standard in LLM evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluation treats models as examinees, judges, or raters but usually collapses model, items, and raters into a single score, obscuring what is actually being measured and hiding biases or miscalibration. The authors want a principled measurement framework that decomposes these factors and offers diagnostics for reliability and bias, especially for sensitive constructs like hate speech.

Method: Use Rasch measurement theory, specifically many-facet Rasch models, to decompose ordinal ratings into latent scales for items, raters, and other facets. As a case study, they take the Measuring Hate Speech corpus (already designed with RMT) and collect annotations from nine LLMs of different families and capabilities. They then fit a series of many-facet Rasch models to compare LLM and human raters across multiple dimensions such as severity, item calibration, robustness to question order, sensitivity to target identity, and rating scale usage.

Result: The Rasch analyses reveal that LLMs differ systematically from human annotators: they have different levels of rating severity/leniency, show different calibration at the level of individual items, react differently to changes in question order, exhibit distinct sensitivities to the identity of the target in hate-speech examples, and use the rating scale in patterns unlike humans. These structured differences would not be visible under standard evaluation methods that aggregate scores without modeling facets.

Conclusion: Rasch measurement theory provides a powerful, diagnostic framework for decomposing and understanding LLM evaluations across paradigms (as examinees, judges, and raters). The case study on hate-speech annotation shows that RMT surfaces important biases and miscalibration in LLM raters that standard practices miss. Therefore, RMT should be incorporated into the standard toolkit for evaluating LLMs and for designing and auditing LLM-involved measurement instruments.

Abstract: LLMs now sit on every side of evaluation: as examinees scored on benchmarks, judges of other models' outputs, and raters of human-generated content. Each paradigm can be viewed as a measurement problem, where a latent property of an object is probed with items from an instrument (e.g., benchmark) by raters. Standard evaluation practices often neglect the contributions of each core component to the end result, limiting our understanding of what is being measured. Rasch measurement theory (RMT) is well-suited to this kind of problem. RMT decomposes ordinal ratings into separable facets on a common scale. It further provides a battery of diagnostics that can identify miscalibrated measurements and rater biases. We present a case study of RMT applied to the LLM-as-rater paradigm using the Measuring Hate Speech corpus, whose construct was itself built under RMT. We fit a series of many-facet Rasch models to annotations from nine LLMs spanning families and capability levels. Our analyses show that LLMs systematically differ from human raters in severity, item-level calibration, question-order robustness, target-identity sensitivity, and rating scale use, which all would be obscured by standard evaluation practice. Overall, we argue that RMT belongs in the toolkit for evaluating LLM-as-examinee, -judge, and -rater paradigms.

</details>


### [61] [Not All Explanations Are Sought: Information-Seeking Psychology for Human-Centered XAI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27464)
*Andrea Beretta,Salvatore Rinzivillo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that explainable AI should be designed based on how and why people actually seek information, not just on making explanations available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current human-centered XAI often assumes that if good explanations are available, people will see and use them appropriately. But psychology shows that people’s information-seeking is strongly shaped by motives and biases, which can lead them to want too much of the wrong information or too little of the right information. The paper aims to bridge this gap.

Method: Conceptual/position paper. The authors import Sharot and Sunstein’s framework of information-seeking motives (instrumental, hedonic, cognitive utility) and connect it to known cognitive biases (illusion of control, automation bias, unrealistic optimism, impact bias, overconfidence, confirmation bias). They analyze how these factors affect whether users choose to engage with AI explanations, especially in agentic AI systems.

Result: They identify two main failure modes for explanation use: (1) excessive information-seeking that scatters attention without improving decisions, and (2) insufficient information-seeking that leaves risks and misunderstandings unexamined. They further argue that these problems are especially severe for agentic AI, where users must judge not just a single output but chains of actions and risks.

Conclusion: Explainable AI design should shift focus from merely making explanations available to making them actively sought and used. This requires embedding insights from information-seeking psychology into HCXAI, considering users’ instrumental, hedonic, and cognitive utilities and their cognitive biases when deciding what explanations to present, when, and how.

Abstract: This position paper argues that human-centered explainable AI (HCXAI) should incorporate insights from the psychology of information seeking. Drawing on Sharot and Sunstein's framework of information-seeking motives, we propose that people evaluate whether to engage with explanations based on three types of expected utility: instrumental (will it help me act better?), hedonic (will it make me feel better?), and cognitive (will it improve my understanding?). Each utility is estimated through a lens shaped by well-documented cognitive biases, including illusion of control, automation bias, unrealistic optimism, impact bias, overconfidence, and confirmation bias. These biases can lead to two failure modes: excessive information-seeking that fragments attention without improving decisions, and insufficient information-seeking that leaves critical risks and misunderstandings unexamined. This challenge is particularly acute for agentic AI systems, where explanations must support not just understanding a single output but anticipating cascading actions, assessing risks, and deciding when to intervene. By integrating information-seeking psychology into HCXAI, we advocate for a shift from making explanations available to making them sought: designing systems that account for when and why users actually want to know.

</details>


### [62] [Retrieving Relations, Detecting Fallacies: A RAG Approach to Political Debate Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27471)
*Deborah Dore,Greta Damo,Elena Cabrio,Serena Villata*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a retrieval-augmented method for automatically detecting and classifying logical fallacies in political debates, using argumentative relations to guide which external documents are retrieved, leading to improved performance over non-retrieval baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatically identifying logical fallacies is important in sensitive, high-stakes contexts like political debates, where flawed reasoning can influence public opinion. Detecting fallacies is difficult because it requires more than analyzing the surface text of an argument; it also demands world knowledge about the debate’s subject matter and understanding of how arguments relate to each other (supporting or attacking). Prior work has used argumentative discourse structure as static features, but this limits flexibility and adaptability. The authors are motivated to develop a method that dynamically uses argument relations to bring in relevant external knowledge for better fallacy detection and classification.

Method: The authors introduce a guided retrieval-augmented approach to fallacy detection and classification. Instead of only using the text of an argument and static discourse features, they leverage argumentative relations of support and attack between arguments to steer the retrieval of external documents from a large political-related knowledge base (15GB). The system dynamically selects documents that are most relevant given the argumentative context and feeds them to different models as additional evidence for predicting whether an argument is fallacious and, if so, which type of fallacy it represents. They systematically evaluate 42 retrieval configurations and 14 models on the ElecDeb60to20 benchmark.

Result: On the ElecDeb60to20 benchmark, the guided retrieval-augmented approach significantly outperforms non-retrieval baselines. The method achieves macro-F1 scores of up to 0.864 for binary fallacy detection (fallacious vs. non-fallacious) and up to 0.725 for multi-class fallacy classification. These gains demonstrate that including external knowledge, when its retrieval is guided by argumentative relations, leads to more accurate identification and categorization of fallacies.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that external knowledge is highly beneficial for automatic fallacy detection and classification, but its effectiveness depends on how it is integrated. By using argumentative relations of support and attack to guide retrieval, the proposed method dynamically brings in relevant contextual information and substantially improves performance over models that do not use retrieval. This suggests that argumentatively guided retrieval is a promising direction for enhancing critical reasoning and discourse analysis systems, especially in complex domains like political debates.

Abstract: Fallacies are arguments that employ invalid reasoning, making their automatic detection critical in sensitive contexts such as high-stakes political debates, where public opinion is shaped. Spotting a fallacious argument requires contextual knowledge beyond its pure surface text. This entails world knowledge pertaining to the subject matter under discussion, as well as knowledge of the relationships that exist between arguments within the argumentative discourse. Prior work on fallacy analysis has shown that argumentative discourse structure can beneficially improve classification performance. However, such structure is typically encoded only as static classifier features, limiting its flexibility. Building on this intuition while addressing this limitation, we introduce a guided retrieval-augmented methodology for fallacy detection and classification that leverages argumentative relations of support and attack to dynamically steer the extraction of relevant documents. We evaluate our approach on the ElecDeb60to20 benchmark across 42 retrieval configurations and 14 models, performing retrieval over a 15GB knowledge base of collected political-related documents. Our approach improves macro-F1 up to 0.864 for fallacy detection and up to 0.725 for classification over non-retrieval baselines. These results show that incorporating external knowledge significantly enhances fallacy detection and classification when retrieval is argumentatively guided.

</details>


### [63] [LLM-Augmented Causal Discovery: Probabilistic Fusion of Edge Existence and Orientation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27472)
*Neville K. Kitson,Anthony Constantinou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose Probabilistic Dependency Graphs to fuse Bayesian network structure learning with LLM-derived causal edges, achieving better causal graph recovery than either source alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bayesian network structure learning from observational data has difficulty identifying edge directions, while LLMs contain broad causal knowledge but are noisy and unreliable. There is a need for a principled way to combine data-driven causal discovery with knowledge from LLMs to improve both edge existence and orientation in learned causal graphs.

Method: Introduce Probabilistic Dependency Graphs (PDGs), where each potential edge between variables has a probability distribution over three states: directed in one direction, undirected, or absent. They obtain PDGs separately from BNSL algorithms (FGES, Tabu, PC) and from multiple LLMs (Gemini, Claude, GPT) under various prompts and random seeds, and fuse them by weighted averaging of these edge-state distributions. They then convert the fused PDG into a final graph and evaluate on 26 benchmark Bayesian networks, comparing performance to BNSL-only and LLM-only PDGs.

Result: A simple 50/50 averaging of BNSL and LLM PDGs increases F1 score relative to the better individual source in 22 of 26 benchmark networks, with a statistically significant mean F1 improvement of 0.056 (p<0.001). Empirically, BNSL yields a high-recall undirected skeleton (about 80% recall vs 60% for LLMs), whereas LLMs produce more accurate edge orientations when they predict an edge (about 96% orientation accuracy vs 77% for BNSL). The fusion leverages these complementary strengths to produce more accurate causal graphs overall.

Conclusion: Modeling both BNSL outputs and LLM causal judgments as probabilistic uncertainty over edge existence and direction, and fusing them within the Probabilistic Dependency Graph framework, is an effective and practical strategy to improve causal structure learning from observational data. The complementary strengths of BNSL (good skeleton) and LLMs (good orientations) can be systematically combined to yield more accurate causal graphs than either approach alone.

Abstract: Bayesian network structure learning (BNSL) from observational data struggles with orientation identifiability, while large language models (LLMs) offer broad but often unreliable causal knowledge. We propose combining these complementary sources through a novel representation, termed Probabilistic Dependency Graphs (PDGs). In a PDG, each edge is associated with a distribution over directed, undirected, and absent states, enabling fusion via weighted averaging. We evaluate this approach on 26 benchmark networks, combining ensembles of three BNSL algorithms (FGES, Tabu, PC) with three LLMs (Gemini, Claude, GPT) across multiple prompts and random seeds. A simple 50/50 fusion improves F1 over the better of either source alone in 22 of 26 networks, with a statistically significant mean improvement of $0.056$ $(p<0.001)$. Analysis reveals that the two sources play complementary roles: BNSL contributes a high-recall edge skeleton (80\% vs 60\% for LLM), while LLM contributes accurate edge orientation (96\% vs 77\% for BNSL). Our results show that representing both sources as probabilistic uncertainty over edge existence and orientation is a practical and effective way to improve causal graph accuracy.

</details>


### [64] [Hypothesize, Evaluate, Refine: A Scientific Agent for PDE Discovery with Unknown Spatial Coefficient Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27475)
*YuJie Huang,WenWu He,ZhuoEr Lin,Congcong Liu,Dong Liang,Zhuo-Xu Cui*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces HER-PDE, an agent-based framework that jointly discovers PDE structures and spatially varying coefficient fields from noisy data in heterogeneous media.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In heterogeneous media, the governing PDEs depend on spatially varying, unknown coefficient fields. Discovering these PDEs from data is hard because the identification of the operator structure and the coefficient fields are tightly coupled: changing where and how fields appear alters the differential law, and flexible fields can mask missing or incorrect PDE terms on any single trajectory. Existing methods often assume parametric forms for coefficients or struggle to separate structural errors from field variability. The authors aim to develop a method that can reliably recover both the compositional PDE structure and nonparametric coefficient fields from limited, noisy trajectories without strong parametric assumptions.

Method: The authors propose HER-PDE, a scientific-agent framework based on a Hypothesize–Evaluate–Refine loop. The agent: (1) analyzes two noisy trajectories generated by different excitations; (2) generates complete expression-tree hypotheses for the PDE, including explicit representation of time-invariant, spatially varying coefficient fields; (3) refines candidate structures locally. A key component is the Hypothesis Evaluation Interface (HEI), which, for a given structural hypothesis, estimates only the coefficient fields explicitly declared in that hypothesis, never supplements missing terms, and scores each PDE structure using bidirectional cross-excitation transfer between the two trajectories. The best-performing law is then audited on a separate, previously unused temporal interval to assess generalization.

Result: On five controlled 2D systems with 5% relative Gaussian noise on the state observations, the agent recovers the correct generating operator in all five cases, even when the true law admits multiple equivalent parameterizations (e.g., sign changes in fields or product-rule rearrangements). Across nine different unknown coefficient fields, the learned fields achieve a median Pearson correlation of about 0.85 with the ground truth and a median relative L2 error of about 0.28, indicating good quantitative recovery of spatial structure despite noise and nonparametric modeling.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that an agent-based Hypothesize–Evaluate–Refine framework, equipped with a strict hypothesis evaluation interface and cross-excitation scoring, can successfully discover heterogeneous PDEs and their nonparametric coefficient fields from noisy trajectory data. By not assuming a fixed parametric form for spatial coefficients and by explicitly coupling structural search with field estimation, HER-PDE can recover both the governing operator and heterogeneous fields with high accuracy, suggesting a promising route for data-driven discovery of complex physical laws in heterogeneous media.

Abstract: Discovering PDEs in heterogeneous media requires jointly identifying the governing operator and the unknown spatial fields that parameterize it. These tasks are coupled: changing field placement changes the differential law, while a sufficiently flexible field can conceal structural error on a single trajectory. We present Hypothesize, Evaluate, Refine for PDE Discovery (HER-PDE), a scientific-agent framework that discovers compositional PDE structure together with nonparametric, time-invariant coefficient fields. The Agent analyzes two noisy trajectories generated by different excitations, proposes complete expression-tree hypotheses, and combines creative structural exploration with local candidate refinement. Its Hypothesis Evaluation Interface (HEI) estimates only the fields explicitly declared in each hypothesis, never adds missing terms, and scores structures by bidirectional cross-excitation transfer. The selected law is subsequently audited on a sealed temporal interval. Across five controlled two-dimensional systems observed with 5 percent relative Gaussian state noise, the Agent recovers the generating operator in all five cases, including equivalent signed-field and product-rule parameterizations. Across nine unknown coefficient fields, the recovered fields attain a median Pearson correlation of approximately 0.85 and a median relative L2 error of approximately 0.28. These results show that agent-guided hypothesis refinement can recover heterogeneous governing laws without prescribing a parametric form for their spatial coefficients.

</details>


### [65] [Class-Based Heuristic Selection for Solving the Flying Block Puzzle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27476)
*Sanyar Ahmadi,Pedram Asadzadeh,Amanj Khorramian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a new heuristic A* variant (CBHA*) tailored to constrained spatial puzzles, giving big search-efficiency gains on a benchmark puzzle domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard heuristic search in planning (e.g., A*) struggles in tightly constrained spatial domains where space (vacancies) is limited, leading to catastrophic performance on harder instances. Existing generic heuristics ignore detailed geometric and kinematic structure such as clearance-to-size constraints common in multi-agent path finding and robotic navigation. The authors want a principled way to exploit these structural properties to improve planning efficiency.

Method: They use the two-column Flying Block Puzzle as a spatial planning testbed and analytical microworld, emphasizing its NP-complete nature and its resemblance to real-world clearance constraints. They design Class-Based Heuristic A* (CBHA*), which: (1) introduces a General Move Constraint that models minimum movement cost when free cells are scarce; (2) defines a kinematic taxonomy that partitions states into seven mutually exclusive classes based on vacancy ratio and geometry of the goal piece, with each class having a provably admissible heuristic; and (3) adds a class-conditional tie-breaking rule that switches between depth-first bias and vertical-distance ordering to escape f-value plateaus. They then compare CBHA* to several baselines (Standard A*, Depth-Prioritized A*, BFS) over 146 benchmark puzzle instances.

Result: On 146 benchmark instances of the Flying Block Puzzle, CBHA* solves 93.4% of cases, outperforming Depth-Prioritized A* (64%), Standard A* (39%), and BFS (17%). It also reduces node expansions by about 88% compared to Standard A* and maintains a low effective branching factor (~3), indicating substantially more efficient search.

Conclusion: By embedding structural knowledge about spatial constraints into the heuristic—via state classes, vacancy-aware move constraints, and adaptive tie-breaking—A* search becomes far more efficient on constrained spatial planning problems. The class-triggered adaptive heuristics form a principled approach likely to transfer to other physical systems with similar geometric and clearance constraints, improving planning performance in practical robotic and autonomous systems.

Abstract: Heuristic search underlies planning in autonomous systems ranging from warehouse logistics to robotic navigation, yet generic heuristics fail to exploit the structural constraints that govern constrained spatial domains, causing search performance to degrade catastrophically on harder instances. We study this problem through the two-column Flying Block Puzzle, a rigorously NP-complete spatial planning microworld whose bottleneck geometry mirrors clearance-to-size constraints encountered in multi-agent path finding, autonomous vehicle navigation, and block relocation systems. We introduce the Class-Based Heuristic A* (CBHA*) algorithm, which integrates a General Move Constraint to capture minimum displacement costs when vacant units are scarce, a formal kinematic taxonomy partitioning the state space into seven mutually exclusive classes with provably admissible heuristics based on vacancy ratio and goal-piece geometry, and a class-conditional tie-breaking mechanism that dynamically switches between depth-priority and vertical-distance ordering to overcome f-value plateaus. Over 146 benchmark instances, CBHA* achieves a 93.4% success rate against 64% for Depth-Prioritized A*, 39% for Standard A*, and 17% for BFS, while reducing node expansions by 87.98% relative to Standard A* and sustaining an average effective branching factor of approximately 3, demonstrating that class-triggered adaptive heuristics constitute a principled mechanism for efficient spatial planning that generalizes structurally to physical constraint systems.

</details>


### [66] [Benchmarking General Mobile Assistants in Challenging Real-World Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27477)
*Yiqi Zhu,Feiyu Gao,Jiaxing Fan,Jiahui Zeng,Minggang Wu,Chenliang Li,Haiyang Xu,Peng Li,Ming Yan,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces GMA, a new benchmark to evaluate general mobile assistant agents on realistic, complex GUI-based tasks, with diverse apps and task difficulties, and studies how agent harness design affects performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing mobile agent benchmarks like AndroidWorld and MobileWorld do not sufficiently cover the diversity, application range, and task complexity of real-world mobile usage scenarios, limiting our ability to rigorously evaluate and improve general mobile assistants.

Method: The authors build GMA, a benchmark consisting of seven open-source, GUI-based mobile applications from domains such as lifestyle sharing and travel planning. They design 300 tasks partitioned into four difficulty tiers ranging from simple atomic actions to complex multi-step workflows. They evaluate eight state-of-the-art models on these tasks, measuring performance as task complexity increases. Additionally, they run controlled ablation studies on different agent harness design choices—such as context retention strategies and explicit state tracking—while controlling for environment, model, and task taxonomy to isolate the effects of harness design.

Result: Model performance drops sharply as tasks become more complex, with current mobile agents struggling to reliably solve realistic multi-step workflows. Harness design—especially mechanisms for context retention and explicit state tracking—can significantly improve performance on harder tasks, but the best design choices differ across foundation models.

Conclusion: GMA extends prior mobile agent benchmarks by covering more applications and substantially richer task complexity, offering a challenging and realistic testbed. It shows that present-day agents are far from robust on real-world mobile workflows and that careful harness design is crucial, though model-dependent, for improving reliability in complex interactions.

Abstract: Graphical user interfaces have emerged as an important environment for evaluating autonomous AI agents on multimodal interactive tasks. Existing benchmarks such as AndroidWorld and MobileWorld provide strong foundations for mobile agent evaluation, but their application coverage and task design do not yet fully capture the diversity and complexity of realistic mobile use. We present GMA, a benchmark for evaluating general mobile assistants in challenging real-world scenarios. GMA introduces seven applications based on open-source projects, spanning domains such as lifestyle sharing and travel planning, and 300 tasks across four difficulty tiers, from atomic actions to complex multi-step workflows. We evaluate eight frontier models and find that performance declines substantially as task complexity increases, with current agents remaining far from reliably handling realistic user requirements. We further conduct controlled ablation studies of agent harness choices, including context retention and explicit state tracking, under a shared environment, model setting, and task taxonomy. Results show that appropriate harness design can meaningfully improve performance, particularly on demanding workflows, while the effectiveness of specific designs can vary across foundation models. Overall, GMA complements existing benchmarks by expanding application coverage and task complexity, providing a challenging testbed for evaluating mobile agents and studying how harness design supports reliable execution in complex mobile workflows.

</details>


### [67] [Effectiveness of IoT and Deep Learning for Detection and Severity Assessment of Postelectrotermes militaris in Tea Plantations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27480)
*D. K. C. Senevirathna,A. A. E. Nanayakkara,H. M. C. K. Kulathunga,J. K. D. P. Nadula,R. M. Mapatuna,Malithi Nawarathne,Jaliya L. Wijayaraja,P. D. Senanayake,Samitha Vidhanaarachchi,Kalpani Manathunga*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents an IoT- and deep-learning–based acoustic monitoring system that detects and quantifies termite infestations in tea trunks non-invasively, using CNNs on audio spectrograms plus geospatial severity mapping to guide plantation management.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Upcountry Live Wood Termite (Postelectrotermes militaris) infestations can severely damage tea plantations, especially when they remain undetected inside trunks. Traditional detection is labor-intensive, invasive, and often too late. There is a need for an automated, early-warning, field-deployable method that works under real plantation noise conditions and not only indicates presence/absence but also how severe each infestation is, to help managers allocate resources and interventions efficiently.

Method: The authors built an IoT-based field device using a high-sensitivity microphone and Raspberry Pi to record trunk acoustics and GPS coordinates non-invasively. They collected 2,000 ten-second audio segments (balanced between healthy and infested trunks), preprocessed them via trimming, resampling, and segmentation, and split them into training/validation/test sets. Fourier-based spectrograms were computed from audio and used to train a convolutional neural network (CNN) that outputs infestation probabilities. A severity scoring model then combined CNN probability, mean acoustic amplitude, and the density of nearby infested plants within a 5 m radius. Finally, they produced geospatial maps visualizing infestation and severity across the plantation.

Result: In field trials at a ULWT-affected tea plantation in Pundaluoya, the system operated under realistic environmental noise and achieved 81.5% accuracy, 80.6% precision, 83.0% recall, 81.8% F1-score, and 0.819 ROC-AUC on a held-out test set. The model not only classified infestation vs. healthy trunks but also generated continuous severity scores integrating infestation probability, acoustic signal strength, and local neighborhood infestation, which could be visualized geospatially. The dataset was released publicly on Kaggle (Senevirathna et al. 2026).

Conclusion: The study concludes that IoT-enabled acoustic sensing combined with CNN-based signal analysis is a feasible and effective approach for early, non-invasive detection of ULWT termite infestations in tea plantations, even in noisy field conditions. The added severity modeling and geospatial visualization provide actionable decision-support tools, enabling managers to identify high-risk zones, prioritize inspections, and apply targeted control measures, improving both efficiency and timeliness of pest management.

Abstract: Tea plantations are vulnerable to Postelectrotermes militaris, commonly known as the Upcountry Live Wood Termite (ULWT), which can cause substantial damage when infestations remain undetected. This study proposes an IoT-enabled acoustic monitoring framework integrated with deep learning for early detection and severity assessment of ULWT infestations in tea plantations.
  Research Method: Audio signals were captured non-invasively from tea trunks using a high-sensitivity microphone connected to a Raspberry Pi-based IoT device, with geographic coordinates recorded for spatial tracking. After trimming, resampling, and segmentation, 2,000 ten-second samples were obtained, comprising 1,000 healthy and 1,000 infested samples, and divided into 1,600 training, 200 validation, and 200 test samples. The dataset used in this study is publicly available on Kaggle (Senevirathna et al. 2026). Fourier-derived spectrograms trained a CNN for infestation classification and probability estimation. A weighted severity model combined CNN probability, mean acoustic amplitude, and nearby infested plants within 5 m, with geospatial mapping used to visualize infestation distribution.
  Findings and Values: Field trials in a ULWT-affected tea plantation in Pundaluoya demonstrated feasibility under realistic environmental noise. On the held-out test set, the CNN achieved 81.5% accuracy, 80.6% precision, 83.0% recall, 81.8% F1-score, and 0.819 ROC-AUC. Beyond binary infestation detection, the framework introduced quantitative severity assessment using infestation probability, acoustic amplitude, and nearby infested plants. The resulting severity and geospatial outputs can support plantation managers in identifying high-risk areas, prioritizing field inspections, and implementing more timely and targeted control measures.

</details>


### [68] [Context Localization for Generalized Level-Based Evaluation in Knowledge-Based Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27482)
*Ondrej Hutník,Natália Puškárová*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes when localizing evaluation scores to a context is equivalent to restricting admissible knowledge contexts, and characterizes this via structural conditions on monotone set functions in knowledge-based systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In knowledge-based systems, evidence, rules, or facts often receive scores that must be evaluated under varying contexts (subsets of available knowledge). Designers need to know when they can simplify evaluation by just restricting attention to a given context B without changing the outcome of more complex context-dependent aggregation procedures. This work aims to formally characterize when such simplification is valid, ensuring consistent and stable context-dependent evaluation and selection of evidence.

Method: The authors use a generalized level-based evaluation framework: given a structured nonnegative score on knowledge units and a family of admissible contexts, they consider conditional aggregation tests and define a generalized level measure as a maximization of a monotone set function over contexts whose aggregated support exceeds a threshold. They then prove a characterization theorem: filtering scores by a context B is equivalent to intersecting all admissible contexts with B for all monotone set functions if and only if two structural conditions hold—(1) monotonicity with respect to contexts and (2) a reduction property that forbids positive localized support outside B. They further analyze how this reduction property can arise via pointwise and block-generated mechanisms, and generalize the result to parameterized systems.

Result: The main result is a necessary and sufficient condition for the equivalence between score filtering by a context B and localization of admissible contexts by intersection with B, valid for all monotone set functions in the framework. This equivalence holds exactly when (i) the evaluation is monotone in the underlying contexts, and (ii) a reduction property ensures that no positive contribution to the level measure can come from outside B once B is fixed. Additional results identify mechanisms (pointwise and block-generated) that guarantee the reduction property and show how the characterization extends to parameterized families of knowledge-based systems.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that context localization for generalized level-based evaluation is structurally well-behaved under two interpretable conditions, providing a stability criterion for context-dependent evidence selection and non-additive support evaluation. When these conditions are met, practitioners can safely evaluate knowledge by simply restricting to a chosen context B without losing consistency relative to more complex aggregation procedures, and the characterization extends robustly to parameterized or dynamically varying systems.

Abstract: We study context localization for generalized level-based evaluation in knowledge-based systems. The framework models situations where a structured nonnegative score, defined on facts, rules, cases, criteria or evidence units, is evaluated through conditional aggregation tests on admissible knowledge contexts. The generalized level measure maximizes a monotone set function over all contexts whose aggregated support reaches a prescribed level. We characterize when filtering the score by a context $B$ is equivalent to localizing the admissible contexts by intersection with $B$. The main theorem shows that this consistency holds for all monotone set functions if and only if two structural conditions are satisfied: monotonicity with respect to contexts and a reduction property excluding positive localized support outside $B$. We analyze pointwise and block-generated mechanisms producing the reduction property, extend the result to parameterized systems, and interpret it as a stability criterion for context-dependent evidence selection, non-additive support evaluation and level-based knowledge aggregation.

</details>


### [69] [CareGraph: An Auditable Hybrid AI Framework for Evidence-Grounded Personalized Longitudinal Health Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27484)
*Pratik Ghawate,Tanvi Patil*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CareGraph is an auditable hybrid AI framework that organizes heterogeneous personal health data into safe, traceable summaries and prompts for clinical discussion, without making autonomous medical decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personalized healthcare increasingly relies on diverse data sources such as clinical records, self-reported information, and wearable data. However, this information is fragmented, difficult to interpret in aggregate, and often lacks clear provenance, making it hard for clinicians and patients to understand trends, identify missing context, and trust AI-supported insights. Existing large language model–based approaches risk hallucinations, opaque reasoning, and unsafe advice, especially if they implicitly diagnose or recommend treatments. The authors aim to create a framework that can structure and prioritize evidence, highlight gaps, and support conversation while remaining auditable, safety-bounded, and clearly distinct from clinical decision-making.

Method: The authors design CareGraph, a hybrid AI pipeline that combines deterministic rules, graph-based data organization, and constrained language model generation. The system converts heterogeneous patient data into a graph, applies deterministic trend analysis (e.g., an ordinary least squares trend rule with a sufficiency gate), detects missing context, and then uses constrained LLM prompts to produce prioritized health trends, indicators of missing context, bounded next steps, and discussion questions. Safety is enforced via explicit rulesets, evidence validation, provenance-aware graph retrieval, and release gating to block unsafe or unsupported outputs. The framework is evaluated on synthetic cohorts of 400 patients each for development, validation, and holdout, as well as an authored safety benchmark and an audit exercise requiring cross-patient graph retrieval. The authors also compare CareGraph to a monolithic GPT 5.6 baseline on matched patients in terms of speed, verbosity, lexical alignment, and evidence citation patterns.

Result: On holdout synthetic data, the deterministic ordinary least squares–based trend classifier with a sufficiency gate achieved 0.827 accuracy and 0.837 macro F1 (95% CI: 0.819–0.854), along with 0.974 F1 for detecting insufficient data. The improved missing context detector reached 0.815 strict micro F1, substantially outperforming a legacy detector at 0.318. The safety ruleset (v1.2) obtained 1.000 precision, 0.950 recall, and 0.974 F1 on an authored safety benchmark. In an 80-patient graph audit, CareGraph successfully produced 79 syntheses and 78 presentations without needing fallback; one output was blocked by safety gating and one failed closed because of an invalid evidence key. When compared against monolithic GPT 5.6 on 56 matched patients, CareGraph responded faster (40.15 vs. 49.62 seconds), generated more concise outputs (661 vs. 1,163 words), and displayed better exploratory lexical alignment with longitudinal targets, though the baseline used fewer tokens and cited more raw evidence. Graph auditing confirmed provenance and deterministic retrieval, while the marginal impact of the graph on generation remains to be fully quantified via paired evaluation.

Conclusion: CareGraph provides an auditable, safety-bounded hybrid AI foundation for organizing longitudinal, heterogeneous health data into interpretable, provenance-linked summaries and prompts, explicitly avoiding autonomous clinical decision-making. The framework demonstrates strong performance on trend classification, missing context detection, and safety benchmarks, and offers practical advantages over a monolithic LLM baseline in speed, conciseness, and alignment with longitudinal evidence. By combining deterministic analysis, graph-based structuring, constrained LLM synthesis, and rigorous safety and provenance controls, CareGraph positions itself as a trustworthy infrastructure component for intelligent personalized health systems, though further paired studies are needed to quantify the incremental benefits of the graph-driven generation.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence is transforming personalized healthcare, yet fragmented clinical, self reported, and wearable evidence remains difficult to interpret and trace. We present CareGraph, an auditable hybrid AI framework that converts heterogeneous records into prioritized trends, missing context indicators, bounded next steps, discussion questions, and provenance linked explanations. CareGraph organizes evidence without diagnosing, predicting outcomes, selecting treatment, or making autonomous clinical decisions. Its pipeline covers deterministic analysis, context detection, graph construction, constrained language model synthesis, evidence validation, safety controls, and release gating. Tests used synthetic cohorts of 400 patients each for development, validation, and holdout. On holdout data, a frozen ordinary least squares trend rule with a sufficiency gate achieved 0.827 accuracy, 0.837 macro F1 with a 95 percent confidence interval of 0.819 to 0.854, and 0.974 insufficient data F1. Missing context detection achieved 0.815 strict micro F1 versus 0.318 for the legacy detector. On an authored holdout benchmark, safety ruleset version 1.2 achieved 1.000 precision, 0.950 recall, and 0.974 F1. An audit requiring graph retrieval across 80 patients yielded 79 syntheses and 78 presentations without fallback; one output was blocked and one failed closed because of an invalid evidence key. Against monolithic GPT 5.6 on 56 matched patients, CareGraph was faster at 40.15 versus 49.62 seconds, shorter at 661 versus 1,163 words, and showed better exploratory lexical alignment with longitudinal targets; the baseline used fewer tokens and cited more raw evidence. Graph auditing verified provenance and deterministic retrieval; incremental graph effects on generation require paired evaluation. CareGraph offers a safety bounded foundation for intelligent personalized health systems.

</details>


### [70] [Thinking Costs Tokens: When More Structure is Worth the Price](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27506)
*Thomas Nolasque,John Grey,Calista Pham,Ankit Vani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies how much token budget is needed before structured inference with search, verification, and repair starts to outperform a single-shot (monolithic) LLM call on financial reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inference-time techniques like planning, search, and verification can improve reasoning but also consume tokens. When token budgets are tight, their overhead might reduce performance instead of helping. The authors want to know if there is a clear budget threshold where these structured methods begin to pay off compared to a simple single-call LLM approach.

Method: The authors compare two systems on financial QA benchmarks FinQA and TAT-QA using GPT-5.4 mini. One system is a monolithic single LLM call; the other is a verified search architecture that includes planning, label-blind checking, and repair. They vary the available token budget across 14 tiers from 250 to 42,000 output-equivalent tokens and measure accuracy over 1,000 instances, yielding 28,000 completed table cells. They then identify where the verified search system’s performance overtakes that of the monolith and statistically confirm the crossover point using an intersection-union test with p ≤ 0.001 at the endpoints.

Result: At very low budgets (250 and 500 tokens), both systems achieve 0% accuracy because the full prompt cannot fit. At 1,000 tokens, the monolithic system reaches 18% accuracy, while the verified search system remains near 0%, as planning and verification overhead leave insufficient tokens for answering. Starting at 1,500 tokens, the verified search system overtakes the monolith and consistently maintains a performance advantage at higher budgets, achieving around 44% accuracy at the largest budgets versus about 40% for the monolith. The crossover region is located between 1,000 and 1,500 tokens with strong statistical significance.

Conclusion: There is a token-budget threshold at which structured inference with search and verification changes from being harmful to beneficial relative to a single-shot LLM. Below roughly 1,500 output-equivalent tokens, the overhead of planning and verification degrades performance; above that range, the added structure improves accuracy and maintains a consistent edge. This suggests that when designing inference-time reasoning architectures, practitioners should account for token budget and may prefer monolithic calls under tight constraints but use verified search-like methods when more tokens are available.

Abstract: Adding inference structure to a language model lets it search, verify, and revise, but these actions consume the very budget they are supposed to use well. In this paper, we investigate whether there exists a token-budget threshold, below which the overhead of planning and verification hurts performance and above which it helps. We evaluate two systems on FinQA and TAT-QA financial reasoning tasks, using GPT-5.4 mini across 14 budget tiers ranging from 250 to 42,000 output-equivalent tokens. The first system is a monolith, which is a single LLM call. The second is a verified search architecture that adds planning, label-blind checking, and repair capabilities. We run 1,000 cases for a total of 28,000 completed cells. Both systems score 0% at the two lowest tiers, where neither can fit a complete prompt. At 1,000 tokens, the monolith reaches 18% accuracy while verified search scores near 0%, since the planning overhead leaves no room for an answer. From 1,500 tokens onward, verified search surpasses the monolith and maintains a consistent advantage, reaching approximately 44% at the highest tiers while the monolith reaches approximately 40%. The crossover occurs between 1,000 and 1,500 output-equivalent tokens, confirmed by a strict intersection-union test ($p \le 0.001$ at both endpoints).

</details>


### [71] [WM-R1: Training GUI Agents to Reason and leverage World Models with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27508)
*Yu Han,Tianwen Qian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WM-R1 is a reinforcement learning framework that trains mobile GUI agents entirely inside learned world models instead of the real Android environment, improving data efficiency, stability, and performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training GUI agents with reinforcement learning on real mobile environments is expensive, slow, and unstable because it requires many interactions with real devices or emulators. Existing methods that rely on real-environment rollouts or lightweight inference-time simulation struggle with scalability, fine-grained trajectory control, and robust credit assignment for complex GUI tasks. The authors want a way to fully decouple RL training from the real environment, making it cheaper, more scalable, and better at learning efficient, successful behaviors on challenging Android tasks.

Method: The authors introduce WM-R1, a framework that replaces the real Android environment with learned world models for all RL rollouts. First, they train world models that predict state transitions given a GUI state and action. During RL training, these models generate trajectories at scale, serving as the sole environment for the agent. WM-R1 also integrates the world model into the agent’s internal reasoning, allowing the agent to mentally simulate the outcomes of candidate actions (planning) before choosing a final action. They design a multi-dimensional, rule-based reward that balances task success, trajectory efficiency (e.g., shorter paths, fewer redundant clicks), and effective utilization of the world model’s predictions. For data, they construct a curated dataset of 2000 challenging mobile GUI tasks to train both the world models and the RL policy. They compare against GRPO-only (standard RL from real environments) and inference-time simulation baselines on Android benchmarks.

Result: On standard Android mobile benchmarks, agents trained with WM-R1 outperform baselines that use GRPO-only training or simulation only at inference time. The results indicate better task success rates and improved trajectory efficiency when training fully in world model space. The experiments show that the world-model-based rollouts are sufficient to learn strong GUI policies and that embedding simulation into the thinking process contributes to better action selection than methods without such integrated planning.

Conclusion: WM-R1 demonstrates that mobile GUI agents can be effectively trained entirely with learned world models, avoiding direct interaction with the real Android environment during RL training. This leads to scalable, parallelizable trajectory generation, improved data efficiency, and superior performance compared with traditional RL and inference-only simulation approaches. The framework suggests a promising path for training interactive mobile agents more safely and cheaply, and the released code provides a basis for future research on world-model-based RL in GUI domains.

Abstract: GUI agents trained with reinforcement learning (RL) have showcased strong environment learning capabilities on mobile platforms. However, RL typically demands extensive real-environment interactions, leading to high resource costs and instability, especially in GUI scenarios. To address these, we propose WM-R1, the first reinforcement learning framework that trains mobile GUI agents with world models instead of real environments. Specifically, world models serve as the source of state transitions during all rollouts, replacing the real Android environment within the training loop. WM-R1 also embeds world models directly into the thinking process, enabling agents to reason about the consequences of candidate actions before committing to the final action. Crucially, WM-R1 eliminates the need for real-environment interaction, supports massively parallelized and step-level granularized trajectory generation grounded in world models, and introduces a multi-dimensional rule-based reward that jointly optimizes task success, trajectory efficiency, and world model utilization. For efficient training, we curate a high-quality dataset of 2000 challenging tasks. Experiments on Android mobile benchmarks demonstrate that WM-R1-trained agents significantly outperform GRPO-only baselines and inference-time simulation methods. Code is available at https://github.com/genalyu/WM-R1 .

</details>


### [72] [SETU: An Agentic Ecosystem for Multilingual, Persona-Aware Communication Coaching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27524)
*Jonnalagadda Maruthi Tejas,Uponika Barman Roy,Tilottama Goswami,Samir Goswami,Mousita Dhar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SETU, an agent-based, multimodal coaching ecosystem to evaluate and improve corporate communication, especially sales pitches, across languages and audiences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Corporate training teams need scalable, auditable, and explainable tools to coach communication skills in multilingual, audience-specific contexts. Existing tools usually handle only one modality (text, audio, or video) and behave as black boxes, limiting their usefulness for fine-grained feedback and coaching oversight.

Method: The authors design SETU, an agentic ecosystem composed of specialized agents for video analysis, audio-speech processing, text relevance checking, scoring, notifications, and reporting. These agents are coordinated via a trust-aware orchestration mechanism to analyze multimodal communication sessions and produce structured, attributed coaching feedback. The system is scoped to two scenarios, with this paper focusing empirically on sales pitch sessions adapted to target audiences.

Result: For the evaluated sales-pitch scenario (ii), SETU is able to generate modality-attributed coaching reports that decompose performance across video, audio, and text dimensions, offering detailed feedback that can be used formatively by trainees and reviewed by humans. Quantitative or comparative metrics are not detailed in the abstract, but the system is presented as functioning effectively within available evaluation resources.

Conclusion: SETU provides a practical, explainable, and modular ecosystem for corporate communication coaching that integrates multimodal analysis through coordinated agents. It supports human-in-the-loop oversight via transparent, modality-specific feedback and aims to bridge communication gaps in multilingual corporate environments, particularly for recruiters and sales professionals.

Abstract: Corporate training teams need scalable and explainable tools to improve workforce communication in multilingual settings. Existing systems often score text, audio, or video in isolation, or produce black-box outputs that are difficult to audit for coaching use. This paper presents SETU, an agentic ecosystem for corporate communication coaching aimed at recruiters, frontline sales professionals and training units who prepare for audience specific conversations. SETU is designed for two scoped scenarios: (i) recruiter-candidate eligibility-and-interest calls with persona context and (ii) sales pitches with target-audience adaptation; owing to limited evaluation resources, this paper reports results on scenario (ii) only. The ecosystem decomposes analysis into specialized video, audio-speech, text-relevance, scoring, notification and reporting agents coordinated through trust-aware orchestration. It generates modality-attributed coaching reports for formative training, with human reviewers retaining final judgment. The name SETU (bridge in several Indic languages) reflects the goal of bridging communication gaps across regional languages and audience expectations.

</details>


### [73] [Nemotron 3.5 Content Safety Moderator: A Compact Multimodal, Multilingual, and Reasoning Enabled Content Safety Moderator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27548)
*Varun Singh,Anuj Doshi,Makesh Narsimhan Sreedhar,Shaona Ghosh,Katherine Luna*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Nemotron 3.5 CS is a compact 4B-parameter multilingual, multimodal safety moderator that classifies risks in prompts, images, and responses, supports custom policies with optional reasoning, and offers a practical tradeoff between coverage, robustness, and low latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deployed AI systems increasingly handle not just text but also images, documents, and screenshots in many languages, and must comply with diverse, often custom, safety policies. Existing safety guardrails tend to be narrow: they may only moderate text, lack image or document understanding, fail to support multiple languages or custom policies, or be too computationally heavy for low-latency deployment. There is a need for a single, efficient moderator that can provide broad multimodal and multilingual coverage, adhere to configurable policies, and be practical as a front-line safety layer in real-world applications.

Method: The authors build Nemotron 3.5 Content Safety Moderator, a ~4B-parameter vision-language model fine-tuned for safety classification across multiple languages and modalities. It jointly moderates user prompts, images, and assistant responses, outputting safety labels for fast decisions and, when requested, concise reasoning traces that reference specific custom policies and categories violated. They also construct and release a multimodal, multilingual safety dataset encompassing human-labeled real-image moderation examples, benign vision-language and document tasks, synthetic rare-risk and jailbreak scenarios, and custom-policy demonstrations. The model is trained and evaluated on this dataset and existing benchmarks to balance accuracy, coverage, and latency.

Result: Nemotron 3.5 CS achieves competitive performance with larger or more specialized guard models across several axes: multimodal safety detection, text-only moderation, multilingual robustness, adherence to custom policies, and reduction of false positives on benign content, all while maintaining low latency suitable for deployment. The model successfully extends safety moderation to be both image-conditioned and policy-conditioned, and demonstrates robust behavior on challenging cases like synthetic rare risks and jailbreak attempts.

Conclusion: A compact vision-language safety moderator like Nemotron 3.5 CS can feasibly act as a deployable, front-line safety layer for real-world AI systems. It offers a practical tradeoff between breadth of supported modalities and languages, custom policy conditioning, and computational efficiency. Reasoning capabilities can be invoked selectively for auditing and policy review rather than for every request, enabling scalable, policy-aware, multimodal content safety in deployed AI applications.

Abstract: Safety moderation for deployed AI applications is moving beyond text-only prompts: systems increasingly need to judge images, documents, screenshots, and generated responses under policies that vary across domains. Existing guardrails usually cover only part of this setting, making it difficult to combine broad coverage, custom policy control, and low compute cost. We present Nemotron 3.5 Content Safety Moderator, also referred to as Nemotron 3.5 CS in this paper for brevity, a compact 4B vision-language safety moderator that jointly classifies user prompts, images, and assistant responses across 12 languages. Nemotron 3.5 CS returns safety labels for latency-sensitive moderation and can additionally produce concise reasoning traces that apply supplied custom policies and identify violated categories when reasoning is requested. We also release a multimodal and multilingual safety dataset for guard training, spanning human-labeled real-image moderation, benign vision-language and document tasks, synthetic rare-risk and jailbreak cases, and custom-policy examples. Across evaluations spanning multimodal safety, text moderation, multilingual robustness, custom-policy following, benign false positives, and latency, Nemotron 3.5 CS demonstrates a practical coverage tradeoff: it adds image-conditioned and policy-conditioned moderation while remaining broadly competitive with specialized guard models. These results suggest that compact vision-language moderators can serve as deployable front-line safety components, with reasoning used selectively for audit and policy review.

</details>


### [74] [LongGuard: Mechanistic Analysis and Training-Free Mitigation of Long-Context Failure in Safety Guardrails](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27580)
*Ziyang Chen,Xing Wu,Songlin Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces LongGuard, a framework to evaluate and fix safety guardrails for LLMs on long-context inputs, showing that guardrails miss unsafe content as context grows and proposing training-free mitigations that significantly improve detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety guardrails for large language models are almost always trained and evaluated on short text, but real-world use increasingly involves very long contexts. Practitioners have observed that guardrails often fail to detect harmful content when it is embedded in long inputs, yet there is little systematic evaluation, mechanistic understanding, or mitigation of this problem. The authors aim to rigorously characterize how and why safety detection degrades with context length and to develop practical methods to restore robust performance without requiring retraining.

Method: 1) Formulate the evaluation as a Safety Needle-in-a-Haystack (SafetyNIAH) task where a short unsafe ‘needle’ sentence is embedded in otherwise benign ‘haystack’ text, with total context length varying from 0.25k to 32k tokens. 2) Benchmark 15 mainstream safety guardrails on this grid and use a Benign-Fill vs Needle-Repeat design to distinguish whether failures are due to absolute length or proportional dilution of the unsafe content. 3) Conduct a three-layer mechanistic analysis on six guardrails, tracking (a) attention mass on the unsafe needle, (b) unsafe-vs-safe logit margins, and (c) the final detection decision, including controls that partial out mere length effects. 4) Identify specific attention heads that act as guard-specialized retrieval heads and compare them to those in the base LLMs. 5) Propose two training-free mitigation strategies: Chunked Detection (CD), which runs safety checks on smaller chunks of the long context, and Attention-Head Sharpening (AHS), which boosts the influence or selectivity of key retrieval heads. 6) Introduce Context-Aware Hyperparameter Routing (CAHR), a deployment strategy that dynamically chooses guardrail configurations (e.g., thresholds, use of CD or AHS) based on context length and whether the check is pre- or post-generation. 7) Evaluate these mitigations on five benchmarks, including synthetic SafetyNIAH data, real long-context attack scenarios, and outputs from reasoning-focused models.

Result: The authors find that, across 15 commonly used guardrails, recall of unsafe content decreases monotonically with context length, with more than a 50% drop on average from short to long contexts. The Benign-Fill vs Needle-Repeat comparison shows that performance degradation is driven primarily by the dilution of the unsafe needle relative to the total text, not simply by absolute length. Mechanistic analysis on six guardrails reveals a consistent chain: as context grows, attention mass on the unsafe segment is diluted, which reduces the unsafe-over-safe logit margin, leading to a collapse in the binary safety decision. This chain persists even after controlling for length, indicating a genuine retrieval and decision bottleneck. The study also identifies a small set of guard-specific retrieval heads that are more specialized than those in the base models. When applying mitigations, the authors report that CAHR combined with Chunked Detection (CAHR-CD) improves average unsafe recall by about 22%, and CAHR with Attention-Head Sharpening (CAHR-AHS) improves it by about 13% across six guardrails and five diverse benchmarks.

Conclusion: Safety guardrails for LLMs, as currently deployed, are systematically unreliable on long-context inputs because they fail to retrieve and prioritize sparse unsafe content when it is diluted in large amounts of benign text. This failure can be traced mechanistically to diffusion of attention over the context, compression of safety-relevant logit margins, and consequent misclassification, and it is not just a simple function of longer sequences. However, the problem can be substantially mitigated without retraining by using context-aware deployment strategies such as Chunked Detection and Attention-Head Sharpening coordinated through Context-Aware Hyperparameter Routing. The work highlights that long-context robustness should be a core design and evaluation criterion for safety guardrails and provides both analytical tools and practical methods for improving them.

Abstract: Safety guardrails serve as the last line of defense against harmful inputs and outputs of large language models (LLMs), yet they are trained and evaluated almost exclusively on short text. We present LongGuard, a framework that evaluates, mechanistically analyzes, and mitigates long-context guardrail failure. We formulate the task as Safety Needle-in-a-Haystack (SafetyNIAH) over a 0.25k-32k length grid; across 15 mainstream guardrails, unsafe recall drops monotonically by more than 50% on average, and a paired Benign-Fill vs. Needle-Repeat design attributes the failure to proportional dilution of the unsafe needle rather than to absolute length. A three-layer attention-logit-behavior analysis on six guardrails locates the mechanism: attention mass on the unsafe needle is diluted, the unsafe-over-safe logit margin is compressed in lockstep, and the detection decision collapses accordingly, with this attention->logit->behavior chain remaining consistent after partialling out length. We further isolate a sparse set of guard-specialized retrieval heads that exhibit partial specificity relative to their base models. Building on the analysis, we propose two training-free mitigations - Chunked Detection (CD) and Attention-Head Sharpening (AHS) - and a deployment protocol, Context-Aware Hyperparameter Routing (CAHR), that selects configurations by context length and audit side. Across five benchmarks spanning synthetic data, long-context attacks, and reasoning-model outputs, CAHR-CD and CAHR-AHS improve the six-guardrail average by 22% and 13%, respectively. Code and data are available online.

</details>


### [75] [Generative AI Expands the Intellectual Reach of Course Based Undergraduate Research Experiences (CUREs)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27638)
*Aditi Babar,Kristin J. Davin,Alex Dornburg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper examines how integrating generative AI into a bioinformatics/genomics course-based undergraduate research experience changes students’ inquiry, collaboration, and scientific reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Course-based undergraduate research experiences aim to give many students access to authentic research, but instructor capacity and expertise limit how much individualized support and project diversity they can provide. Generative AI could offer scalable, adaptive support, yet its effects on student learning processes, collaboration dynamics, and scientific judgment in these research courses are not well understood.

Method: The authors embedded generative AI as a support tool throughout a bioinformatics and genomics CURE and collected longitudinal qualitative data across three semesters. They analyzed students’ interactions, projects, and reflections to characterize how AI influenced the scope of research questions, team cognition and coordination, and the development of students’ disciplinary judgment over time.

Result: GenAI support enabled all student teams to design distinct, self-directed projects that extended beyond the instructor’s domain expertise. AI acted as part of teams’ distributed cognitive system, helping members with different skills communicate and coordinate without collapsing their specialization. Over time, students did not simply accept AI outputs; they increasingly evaluated, modified, or discarded AI suggestions, using them as inputs to, rather than replacements for, their own reasoning.

Conclusion: Embedding GenAI in CUREs can expand what novice researchers are capable of investigating, enhance collaboration by augmenting team cognition, and support students in taking on greater intellectual responsibility, while still requiring and reinforcing human disciplinary judgment. Thus, GenAI can extend the reach and authenticity of undergraduate research experiences instead of undermining them, provided students remain accountable for validating and integrating AI contributions.

Abstract: Course-based undergraduate research experiences (CUREs) broaden access to authentic scientific inquiry through responsive instructor support as research problems become increasingly complex. Generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) may extend this support by providing individualized assistance that can adapt as student needs change. However, how embedding GenAI within a CURE to provide support across the research process impacts student inquiry, collaboration, and scientific reasoning remains unresolved. Here we use longitudinal qualitative data collected across three semesters of a bioinformatics and genomics CURE to show that GenAI expanded the intellectual reach of the research experience in three distinct ways. First, personalized, on-demand scaffolding allowed students to move beyond the boundaries of instructor expertise and transform their own interests into researchable inquiry, with all teams developing distinct self-directed projects rather than selecting instructor-provided topics. Second, GenAI became part of the distributed cognitive system of research teams, helping novice researchers communicate and coordinate across differentiated expertise without eliminating specialization. Third, expanded capability did not replace the need for disciplinary judgment. Students increasingly validated, revised, or rejected AI-generated contributions, such that research independence emerged through retained intellectual responsibility. Together, these findings suggest that GenAI can extend the reach of CUREs by expanding what novice researchers can investigate, how they can collaborate, and the level of responsibility they can assume while preserving human judgment central to authentic scientific inquiry.

</details>


### [76] [If Agents Were Angels, No Governance Would Be Necessary: Out-of-Band Policy Enforcement at a Trusted Tool Boundary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27646)
*Marc Millstone,Tyler Akidau,Johannes Brüderl,Marat Pekker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Out-of-Band Policy Enforcement (OBPE), a system that moves access control for LLM agents outside of prompts and model reasoning into a trusted, typed HTTP proxy that enforces data policies, significantly reducing data leaks while preserving useful automation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents operating with human credentials can overreach: they can access far more data and perform broader actions than intended because prompts and in-context instructions are fragile, hard to verify, and rely on a single fallible reasoning process. Existing guardrails based on prompting or model-side reasoning cannot reliably prevent an agent from reading or disclosing sensitive data once the data has entered the model context. There is a need for a principled, enforceable way to constrain what operations agents can perform and what data they can see, independent of how the agent reasons or is prompted, especially in enterprise systems like Jira or ServiceNow.

Method: The authors design Out-of-Band Policy Enforcement (OBPE), implemented as a trusted HTTP proxy that sits between the LLM agent and backend services. Policies, expressed in a typed Cedar-based language, govern which operations and resources can be accessed. OBPE (1) authorizes the operation and resource type, (2) narrows queries before they reach the backend, and (3) post-filters records and fields or masks values in responses. It also supports semantic gating, which can deny or delay calls based on argument values or external state. The policy model ensures that data policy owners define a maximal access envelope while agent-specific policies can only further restrict it. The paper provides proofs that under specified conditions, policy plans are order-independent and that agent policies cannot increase the access ceiling. They then build an open-source proxy prototype and a conformance test suite to tie model behavior to the typed policy core. Using mocked Jira and ServiceNow backends, they benchmark prompted agents with and without OBPE over four LLMs and 20 adaptive red-team tasks, tracking traces of policy violations and task fulfillment.

Result: In 3,621 experimental trials, moving from prompt-only control to OBPE reduced the overall rate of trace failures (any instance where protected data reached model context, a secret value appeared in an answer, or a forbidden effect completed) from 57.6% to 0.2%, a cluster-weighted reduction of 41.2 percentage points with a 95% confidence interval of [27.7, 54.9]. While task fulfillment (success regardless of safety) dropped from 79.1% to 60.9%, the rate of safe and useful completions—solutions that respected policy while still completing the task—rose by 21.8 percentage points with 95% CI [9.5, 35.2]. The experiments also surfaced residual leakage channels: models could sometimes reconstruct values that never entered context or use filtered row counts as an information oracle, showing that OBPE shapes single executions but does not fully guarantee noninterference.

Conclusion: Out-of-Band Policy Enforcement provides a robust, model-agnostic way to constrain LLM agents’ access to enterprise data and actions by moving security decisions into a typed, externally-auditable policy layer. This significantly reduces sensitive data exposure and harmful side effects compared with prompt-based guardrails alone, while still enabling many useful automations. However, OBPE does not eliminate all information-flow channels—agents can still infer or reconstruct values—and it does not cover more complex control dimensions such as write operations, durable approvals, or temporal and aggregate policies, which remain open areas for future work and extended enforcement designs.

Abstract: Give an agent a human's credential and it inherits the person's reach without the judgment that limits its use. It can sweep every reachable record into model context, where hidden instructions steer its next call, and every request stays credential-valid while the agent exceeds its job or absorbs a secret. Prompts are a brittle guardrail: one fallible reasoner interprets the task and enforces its limits.
  We present Out-of-Band Policy Enforcement (OBPE), a trusted boundary outside agent reasoning. It authorizes the typed operation and resource, narrows the query before the backend call, then filters records and fields or masks values in the response. Semantic gating can deny or hold an authorized call on argument values or external state. A data policy owner sets the maximum grant; agent policy can only narrow it. We prove, under stated conditions, that the policy plan is order-independent and agent policy cannot widen the ceiling. Field removal covers one execution; masking and history rules claim less.
  We release an HTTP proxy prototype simplified from our production system, with conformance tests tying its typed Cedar policy core to the model. Against Jira and ServiceNow mocks, our benchmark compares prompted agents with and without OBPE on four models, including 20 adaptive red-team tasks. A trace failure means protected data entered agent context, an exact value appeared in the answer, or a forbidden effect completed. In 3,621 trials it fell from 57.6% to 0.2%, a cluster-weighted reduction of 41.2 points [95% CI: 27.7, 54.9]; fulfillment fell from 79.1% to 60.9%, while paired safe-useful completion rose 21.8 points [9.5, 35.2]. Some answers reconstructed a value that never entered context or used filtered row counts as an oracle: shaping one execution is not noninterference. Write controls, durable approval, and temporal and aggregate policies lie outside this evaluation.

</details>


### [77] [A Framework for Object-Centric Predictive Monitoring of Collaborative Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27671)
*Daniel Calegari,Andrea Delgado,Leonel Peña,Martín Rubio*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper links collaborative predictive process monitoring with object-centric process mining by mapping extended collaborative event logs into an object-centric format, reformulating prediction tasks accordingly, and providing an implementation and evaluation on multiple real-life logs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional predictive process monitoring for collaborative, inter-organizational processes is limited by a single-case perspective that hides the rich structure of participants, messages, and local/global executions. There is a need to represent these interrelated entities explicitly to better support prediction tasks in collaborative settings.

Method: The authors propose a formal semantic mapping from extended collaborative event logs into an OCED-conformant object-centric representation serialized in OCEL 2.0. They reformulate collaborative prediction tasks as object-centric prediction tasks and implement a reproducible converter plus prediction pipeline. They then apply five predictive strategies across tabular, sequential, and graph-native encodings to fourteen reformulated tasks on five collaborative logs.

Result: The framework successfully converts collaborative logs to an object-centric representation and allows executing a variety of prediction tasks using different encoding and modeling strategies. The evaluation across four public collaborative logs and one derived log demonstrates that collaboration structure can be made explicit and exploited for predictive tasks, although it introduces additional relational complexity and relies on object-centric tooling.

Conclusion: Object-centric representations are a viable way to support predictive monitoring in collaborative, inter-organizational processes, as they make collaboration structure explicit and enable more natural formulation of prediction targets beyond a single-case view.
