This comes with trade-offs in terms of increased relational complexity and dependence on specialized object-centric tools, which the authors acknowledge as limitations and threats to validity.

Abstract: Predictive Process Monitoring (PPM) of collaborative, inter-organizational processes requires reasoning over multiple interdependent entities, including participants, messages, local executions, and the global collaboration case. Existing approaches extend traditional event logs with collaboration attributes but retain a single-case perspective, leaving much of this structure implicit. Object-centric process mining (OCPM) provides an alternative by representing these entities as first-class objects with explicit relations and multiple notions of case. This study connects collaborative PPM and OCPM through three contributions: (i) a formal semantic mapping from extended collaborative event logs to an OCED-conformant object-centric representation, serialized in OCEL 2.0; (ii) a reformulation of collaborative prediction tasks as object-centric prediction tasks; and (iii) a reproducible converter and prediction pipeline implementing the proposed mapping. We evaluate the framework on four public collaborative event logs and a fifth derived from the BPI Challenge 2013 incident-management log by executing the fourteen reformulated tasks using five predictive strategies across tabular, sequential, and graph-native encodings. We further discuss the benefits, limitations, and threats to the approach's validity. The representation makes collaboration structure explicit and makes it natural to state prediction targets based on object relations that fall outside the case-centric taxonomy, at the cost of increased relational complexity and dependence on object-centric tooling.

</details>


### [78] [Agents for Everyone: A Workshop Framework for Building Agentic AI Capabilities in a Distributed Curation Community](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27675)
*Seth Carbon,Sierra Moxon,Kimberly Van Auken,Pascale Gaudet,Christopher J. Mungall*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper describes deploying a cloud-based agentic AI environment and a hands-on workshop to train Gene Ontology curators, showing that access, workflow design, and training are key to effective adoption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI could greatly speed up curation of biological databases, but its real-world adoption is limited by lack of easy access to tools, technical setup hurdles, and insufficiently tailored training for curators in distributed communities like the Gene Ontology Consortium.

Method: The authors built a cloud-hosted agentic AI environment on JupyterHub, using Claude Code as a unified interface that curators accessed via a browser terminal through a central API gateway. They then designed four sequential training modules that started with basic agentic tool use and progressed to practical pathway curation tasks using the GO-CAM curation tool, and delivered a four-hour interactive workshop to 37 participants.

Result: The deployed environment eliminated the need for local installation or individual subscriptions, enabled centralized management of agent access, and supported a structured, progressive training experience for 37 Gene Ontology curators during a four-hour workshop. Participants were able to use agentic AI within familiar curation workflows and directly evaluate agent outputs.

Conclusion: Effective integration of agentic AI into biological curation work depends less on novel models and more on solving issues of access, workflow integration, and focused training. Cloud-based environments that hide technical complexity, gradually introduce agent capabilities, tie activities to existing curation tools like GO-CAM, and let curators critically assess agent outputs can build shared, sustainable agentic AI capacity in distributed scientific communities.

Abstract: Agentic AI has the potential to accelerate curation of biological databases and knowledge bases. However, uptake has been hindered by a number of challenges and obstacles, including access to agents and appropriate training. Here we describe how we have attempted to address and mitigate these challenges and obstacles through the deployment of a cloud-based agentic environment, and the development of an interactive training workshop for the Gene Ontology Consortium. Our cloud environment for agentic-assisted curation was based on the JupyterHub platform, and utilized Claude Code as a universal harness. This allows curators to interact with an agent session through a terminal running in the browser, and has additional benefits such as centralization of access through a single API gateway, removing the need for participants to manage subscriptions or install software locally. We created four training modules, walking participants through basic agentic tool use first and then working up to agentic biological pathway curation using the existing GO-CAM (GO Causal Activity Model) curation tool. Thirty-seven participants took part in the four-hour workshop. Our key takeaway from this workshop is that building community capability with agentic AI is primarily a problem of access, workflow design, and training. Removing technical barriers, introducing capabilities gradually, grounding exercises in familiar curation tasks, and giving curators direct experience evaluating agent output can provide a practical route toward building shared agentic AI capability in distributed scientific communities.

</details>


### [79] [PCFBench: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Product Carbon Footprint Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27716)
*Krishna Rao,Andrew Dumit,Shaena Ulissi,Jacob Feintzeig,P. James Joyce,Daniel Frank,Steven Watson,Jonathan Glidden,Gizem Ilayda Dinc,Travis M. Kwee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces PCFBench, a benchmark for evaluating AI systems on product carbon footprint estimation with step-wise, compositional tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents are increasingly used in high-stakes, domain-specific workflows like estimating product carbon footprints, where correctness is required at every intermediate step, not just the final result. Existing evaluations are insufficient because they either only assess total emissions, which can hide compensating errors, or test sub-tasks independently, which ignores interactions between steps. There is a need for a benchmark that reflects the real multi-step reasoning and data handling needed for accurate and transparent PCF modeling.

Method: The authors construct PCFBench, a benchmark that decomposes product carbon footprint modeling into independently-evaluable tasks involving decomposition, information retrieval, ontology matching, and numerical extraction. The benchmark contains 614 items labeled by experts across six task types, designed to test reasoning under under-specified conditions, conflicting contextual information, and numerical constraints such as mass conservation. They then evaluate eight frontier large language models from four different providers on these tasks and on end-to-end PCF estimation.

Result: Across eight frontier LLMs, no single model consistently outperforms the others on PCFBench. The strongest models can estimate total product emissions within a factor of two of declared totals for about 77% of products when evaluated on final emissions only. However, performance degrades substantially in a step-by-step generation setting: only 37–58% of stepwise-generated PCFs stay within a factor of two of declared totals, and only 45–75% of them respect mass conservation constraints. These discrepancies expose significant weaknesses in intermediate reasoning and constraint satisfaction.

Conclusion: PCFBench reveals that current LLMs, while seemingly competent at approximating final product carbon footprints, are unreliable in the intermediate reasoning steps needed for transparent, auditable PCF modeling. Violations of numerical and physical constraints, and sensitivity to under-specification and conflicting context, mean practitioners cannot yet rely on these systems to support robust decarbonization decisions. By releasing the dataset and evaluation harness, the authors aim to enable more targeted research and model development that improve step-wise reasoning, constraint adherence, and overall trustworthiness in PCF estimation workflows.

Abstract: AI systems are being deployed on high-stakes, domain-specific workflows that demand correctness not just in the final output, but at every intermediate step. One such workflow is estimating a product carbon footprint (PCF), the greenhouse-gas emissions attributable to a physical product. AI agents are increasingly being used to generate PCFs, but existing evaluations score either total emissions (hiding error sources and cancelling mistakes) or sub-tasks in isolation (missing compositional interactions). We introduce PCFBench, the first benchmark to carve PCF modeling into independently-evaluable tasks that require decomposition, retrieval, ontology matching, and numerical extraction. It comprises 614 expert-labelled items across six tasks. Together they probe reasoning under under-specification, conflicting context, and numerical constraints. Across eight frontier LLMs from four providers, no single model dominates. Although the strongest models estimate total product emissions within 2 times of declared totals on 77% of products, this rate drops to 37-58% when the PCF is generated step by step, with only 45-75% obeying mass conservation. These failures undermine the transparency practitioners need to compare products and drive decarbonization. We release the dataset and evaluation harness to support targeted progress.

</details>


### [80] [Probing Perceptual Priors of MLLMs via Gibbs Sampling with Interpretable Generative Controls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27727)
*Manuel Cherep,Pattie Maes,Nikhil Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They introduce a method to directly sample and visualize the perceptual priors of models, revealing biases that affect model behavior but are hidden from standard interpretability approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model behavior depends not just on inputs but also on the implicit prior distributions over stimuli that models expect. Existing interpretability methods either examine internal representations or study input–output mappings on fixed datasets, which cannot recover the full prior, especially in high-dimensional, real-world input spaces (e.g., images for VLMs). These implicit priors remain poorly understood yet can critically shape real-world behavior and biases, motivating a way to directly characterize them.

Method: They design a technique to sample from a model’s perceptual prior by coupling it with a generative model. The generative model is steered along controllable semantic axes (e.g., attributes of faces or artworks). Then, Gibbs sampling is run in this latent stimulus space, where the model under study acts as the judge that scores or constrains samples, effectively inducing samples from its own perceptual prior distribution over those axes.

Result: Applied to various semantic categories and target variables (e.g., perceived trustworthiness of faces, perceived cheapness of artwork), the method recovers both known, canonical biases and previously unknown, surprising priors that were not revealed through direct prompting or standard probing of the model. This demonstrates that the method successfully exposes hidden dimensions of the model’s expectations.

Conclusion: Their approach enables direct sampling and visualization of a model’s perceptual priors in high-dimensional, realistic input spaces. This reveals both expected and novel biases that are otherwise invisible to existing interpretability techniques, indicating that understanding and auditing these priors is important for anticipating and mitigating their downstream impact on real-world model behavior.

Abstract: A model's behavior on a task is jointly determined by the input it receives and the prior it brings in, i.e. the distribution over stimuli it implicitly expects. Interpretability research has traditionally studied models by holding inputs fixed and examining model responses either mechanistically, probing how internal structure represents inputs, or behaviorally, measuring how variation in inputs leads to variation in outputs. Neither reconstructs the prior distribution itself, since internal structure shows what a model can represent, not what it expects, and any fixed stimulus set leaves most of the possible input space unseen. In particular, such an input space in real-world settings, such as images seen by VLMs, is extremely high-dimensional and diverse. These priors thus remain a poorly understood component of models that nonetheless influence real-world behavior. We propose a method to sample from models' perceptual prior distributions directly, by steering a generative model to produce stimuli along controllable axes and running Gibbs sampling over that space with the model under study as the judge. We apply this to a variety of categories and target variables (such as trustworthiness in faces and cheapness in art images) and recover both canonical biases and surprising novel priors invisible to direct prompting, warranting further investigation of their downstream effects.

</details>


### [81] [Why Didn't It Check? Unsupported Final Claims and Their Repair in Two Tool-Equipped Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27768)
*Justin Bronder*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies when tool-using language models make confident claims that are not actually supported by available evidence, and whether simply providing the missing evidence would fix those errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool-augmented language models are supposed to reduce uncertainty by calling external tools (e.g., search, code, databases) instead of guessing. However, models sometimes still make unsupported, overconfident claims even when a single tool call would resolve the uncertainty and their instructions forbid guessing. The authors want to precisely characterize and measure this type of failure, and test whether supplying the missing evidence would reliably repair it, without harming already-correct answers.

Method: The authors define two quantitative notions: (1) occurrence – how often a model’s final answer is unsupported by the evidence it actually saw, based only on visible context and ignoring any hidden ground truth; and (2) conditional repair – given those naturally occurring unsupported claims, how often they are corrected when the missing evidence is supplied. Using a fixed Qwen3-32B + tool setup and 256 synthetic prompt templates (512 total runs), they identify cases where the model ends with an unsupported claim. For each such case, they replay from an identical internal state, but now vary the tool’s response: either provide resolving evidence, or a structurally matched but uninformative response. They compare how often the model’s final claim changes in each condition, and whether correct answers are harmed. They also run a separate experiment with a simple automatic rule that triggers additional evidence calls when needed, and repeat parts of the study on a Gemma-based setup for comparison.

Result: On the fixed Qwen3-32B setup, 33 of 512 initial responses contained an unsupported established claim. In controlled replays starting from the same state, providing resolving evidence fixed all 33 unsupported claims, while a matched but uninformative tool response fixed none of them. When the new evidence actually supported the model’s original answer, the model preserved all 33 correct answers, showing no observed degradation. In a separate experiment with 64 evidence-needing cases, an automatic checking rule triggered 21 extra tool calls, corrected all 10 wrong unsupported claims, preserved 11 accidentally correct ones, and never flipped a correct answer to incorrect. On the Gemma-4 setup with the same sampling, the model always called the tool in all 512 runs and never produced an unsupported final claim, so conditional repair could not be evaluated there.

Conclusion: Unsupported claims by tool-using language models can be cleanly decomposed into how often they arise (occurrence) and how reliably they can be fixed when missing evidence is provided (conditional repair). In the tested Qwen3-32B setting and synthetic tasks, unsupported claims occurred in a nontrivial minority of cases but were perfectly repairable by supplying resolving evidence, without observed harm to already-correct answers. Simple automatic rules for requesting extra evidence also effectively reduced such failures. However, behavior differed substantially across a Gemma-based setup, which exhibited no unsupported claims under the same sampling, and the authors caution that their findings are limited to local fixed setups on synthetic tasks and do not directly translate to real-world deployment frequencies or mechanisms across models.

Abstract: A language model with access to tools can commit to a final claim unsupported by the evidence it has seen, even when a single available tool call would resolve the uncertainty and its instructions explicitly forbid assumptions and guesses. We separate this failure into two precisely defined quantities: occurrence, how often the model makes an unsupported claim on its own, measured from the visible evidence and final claim without using the hidden correct answer; and conditional repair, how often those same naturally occurring unsupported claims are repaired when the missing evidence is supplied. On one fixed Qwen3-32B setup, 33 of 512 first responses to 256 new prompt templates ended with an unsupported established claim. We replayed each case from an exact copy of the state in which the claim occurred; within each matched replay, the alternative tool responses had the same structure and length and differed only in a one-character response code. Resolving evidence repaired 33 of 33 claims; a matched response carrying no useful information repaired 0 of 33. When the evidence supported the original answer, the model preserved 33 of 33, with no observed harm. In a separate experiment, on 64 cases where evidence was needed, an automatic checking rule added 21 evidence calls, corrected all 10 wrong unsupported claims, preserved the 11 that were correct by accident, and never changed a correct answer into a wrong one. On a fixed Gemma 4 setup using the same sampling settings, the model called the tool in all 512 first responses and never made an unsupported final claim, so conditional repair could not be measured for that setup. These results describe two local fixed model setups on two synthetic task families. They do not show how common this failure is in real-world deployments, nor that it reflects a general mechanism shared across models.

</details>


### [82] [Credo: Reusable Declarative Primitives for Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27790)
*Duo Lu,Andrew Crotty,Uğur Çetintemel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Credo turns opaque, searched LLM harness code into a reusable, declarative catalog of components for building new LLM applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based applications rely not just on the underlying model, but also on a harness (the control program around the model) that decides prompts, call structure, coordination, and acceptance criteria. Modern coding agents can auto-discover effective harnesses by searching over program candidates, but the resulting artifacts are huge, opaque blocks of imperative code that are tightly coupled to a specific task. This makes it hard to: (1) understand what logical strategy the harness uses, (2) see which runtime signals and physical execution decisions matter, (3) reuse good prompt strategies or logical patterns on new tasks, and (4) maintain or adapt harnesses as models and workloads evolve. Although these searched harnesses encode valuable knowledge, that knowledge is trapped in unstructured code without metadata or provenance. There is a need for a way to expose and reuse this embedded knowledge systematically.

Method: Credo takes an existing, searched harness and recovers from it a structured, declarative description. It decomposes the harness into primitive components that capture logical steps, runtime signals used for control, physical operator and execution decisions, and prompt strategies. Each extracted primitive is tagged with rich metadata and provenance information (e.g., where it came from, in what context it worked). These primitives are then stored in a catalog. A compiler can later query this declarative catalog and automatically bind and compose relevant primitives to synthesize harnesses for new tasks. The approach thus separates the search-intensive discovery of strategies from their declarative representation and reuse. The paper also sketches a broader system design and research directions such as cost-based compilation over the catalog and strategies for catalog maintenance under changes in models and workloads.

Result: The paper presents preliminary empirical results suggesting that harnesses constructed from Credo’s declarative catalog can effectively support new tasks without repeating a full search from scratch. While details are limited in the abstract, the results show the feasibility of extracting structured primitives from imperative harness code and reusing them via compilation. These early experiments indicate potential efficiency gains and robustness when adapting LLM applications to new tasks by leveraging the cataloged knowledge.

Conclusion: Credo demonstrates that it is possible to turn opaque, searched LLM harnesses into structured, declarative artifacts with metadata and provenance, enabling systematic reuse across tasks. This shifts harness construction from ad hoc, task-specific search to catalog-driven compilation. The authors argue that this area is a promising research frontier for the database community, which has expertise in declarative representations, cost-based optimization, and catalog management. They highlight open problems including optimizing compilation plans over the catalog and maintaining and evolving the catalog as models and workloads drift over time.

Abstract: An LLM application depends on both a model and a harness: the program that determines what each call sees, how many calls to make, and which answers to trust. Coding agents can now discover strong harnesses by searching over candidate programs, but the resulting artifact is an opaque block of imperative code whose logical steps, runtime signals, physical execution decisions, and prompt strategies remain implicit and task-specific, forcing subsequent tasks to start the harness search process from scratch. The potential for reuse, however, is substantial. A searched harness encodes significant knowledge, such as the logical steps that work, the signals that matter, the physical operator decisions that adapt execution, and the prompt strategies that are effective, yet this knowledge is buried in imperative code with no inspectable or reusable structure, nor does it carry any provenance or metadata. Credo addresses this problem by recovering a structured declarative description of a searched harness, tagging each extracted primitive with relevant metadata, and cataloguing all of it with provenance. A compiler can then bind stored primitives to generate harnesses for new tasks without having to start the search over from scratch. This paper provides preliminary results demonstrating the potential of our approach and lays out a related research agenda that the database community is well-positioned to tackle, including cost-based compilation over declarative catalogs and catalog maintenance under model and workload drift.

</details>


### [83] [ReToolSQL: Agentic Reinforcement Learning for Robust Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27796)
*Pratik Kakkar,Chandra Dhir,Ravi Shankar,Pareekshit Reddy Gaddam,Anup Shirgaonkar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReToolSQL is a two-stage training framework (SFT then RFT) that uses tool-use trajectories and execution feedback to significantly improve text-to-SQL accuracy with a single 31B model, achieving state-of-the-art results on BIRD-SQL without extra human labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text-to-SQL systems using reinforcement learning from execution feedback already boost performance, especially for smaller models, but they typically treat SQL generation as a single-shot task. This prevents models from exploiting multi-turn interaction: verifying queries, retrieving evidence, and iteratively fixing errors based on execution results. The authors aim to design a training pipeline that can both expand the set of questions a model can solve and convert this broader capability into high single-pass accuracy by leveraging multi-turn tool use and feedback, all without additional human annotations.

Method: The authors propose ReToolSQL, a two-stage framework. Stage 1 is supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on rejection-sampled, verified reasoning traces produced by a stronger privileged teacher model; this expands the model’s competence by exposing it to high-quality multi-step solutions. Stage 2 is agentic reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) over multi-turn tool-use trajectories, where the model interacts with tools (e.g., SQL executor, retriever) and updates its policy using composite rewards primarily anchored on execution correctness. The RFT stage teaches the model when to verify its queries, what schema or evidence to retrieve, and how to iteratively revise faulty SQL using execution feedback, all within a single dense 31B Gemma 4 instruction-tuned model.

Result: On the BIRD-SQL development benchmark, applying RFT directly to Gemma 4 31B instruction-tuned yields 73.66% execution accuracy in a single pass, improved to 74.12% with self-consistency. When RFT is initialized from the SFT checkpoint (SFT→RFT), performance further improves to 74.32% single-pass execution accuracy and 74.77% with self-consistency. At the time of writing, this SFT→RFT model ranks first on the BIRD single-model development-set leaderboard, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance using only benchmark-derived feedback and no additional human labeling.

Conclusion: ReToolSQL shows that pairing supervised fine-tuning on verified reasoning traces with agentic reinforcement fine-tuning over multi-turn tool-use trajectories is an effective and practical way to build strong text-to-SQL models. The SFT stage broadens the range of solvable queries, while RFT translates that broader capability into higher single-pass accuracy by teaching strategic verification, retrieval, and repair behaviors. Using composite execution-based rewards and a single 31B dense model, the approach achieves leading results on BIRD-SQL without extra human annotations, indicating that such SFT→RFT pipelines can be a viable route to robust, enterprise-ready text-to-SQL systems.

Abstract: Recent work has shown that reinforcement learning from execution feedback can substantially improve text-to-SQL performance, often enabling smaller models to match or exceed much larger systems. However, most existing approaches treat SQL generation as a single-turn task, limiting the model's ability to recover from errors through iterative refinement. We present ReToolSQL, a two-stage training framework for text-to-SQL that combines (i) a supervised warm-start on rejection-sampled reasoning traces with (ii) agentic reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) over multi-turn tool-use trajectories. The key insight is that the two stages act on complementary axes, the supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on verified privileged-teacher traces expands the set of solvable questions (raising pass@k coverage on the hardest cases), while RFT converts that expanded capability into higher single-pass accuracy by teaching the model when to verify, what evidence to retrieve, and how to repair faulty SQL from execution feedback. Applied to Gemma 4 instruction-tuned (31B), RFT alone achieves 73.66% execution accuracy (EX) on the BIRD-SQL development benchmark (74.12% EX with self-consistency). Initializing RFT from the SFT checkpoint (SFT$\to$RFT) yields our strongest model at 74.32% EX single-pass and 74.77% EX with self-consistency. At the time of writing, this ranked first on the BIRD single-model development-set leaderboard. The approach uses composite rewards anchored on execution correctness, requires no human annotation beyond the benchmark itself, and operates within a single dense 31B model, showing that a properly designed SFT$\to$RFT pipeline over tool-use trajectories is a practical path toward robust enterprise-grade text-to-SQL.

</details>


### [84] [CEDAR: Automata as Verifiable Interfaces for Language-Guided Embodied Action](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27797)
*Lekai Chen,Alvaro Velasquez,Ashutosh Trivedi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CEDAR is a framework that turns natural-language constraints for embodied agents into verifiable finite-state controllers using regular languages, improving constraint satisfaction and reuse compared to code-generating LLM baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Natural-language instructions to embodied agents almost always include persistent constraints (e.g., safety, spatial, or temporal rules) in addition to goals. Current LLM-based approaches that emit free-form programs can follow these instructions somewhat, but the resulting code is hard to verify, hard to systematically combine with new constraints, and hard to repair from failures. There is a need for an intermediate, structured representation that both aligns with natural language and supports formal reasoning and composition.

Method: The authors introduce CEDAR, a counterexample-guided framework that grounds natural-language instructions as regular languages over environment event traces. A language model is used to make semantic judgments about instructions and to refine them using observed execution traces. Both learned skills (policies) and specifications (constraints) are compiled into deterministic finite automata (DFAs). Constraints such as “sleep at night” or “stay in this biome” are represented as DFAs and intersected with the DFA for a base skill to synthesize a controller that, by construction, respects the specified constraints. Counterexample traces from execution guide the iterative correction of these automata.

Result: In a Minecraft environment, using the same simulator and API observations as a standard program-generating LLM baseline, CEDAR is able to maintain both temporal and spatial constraints that the baseline fails to respect. Because skills and constraints are represented as reusable finite-state objects, CEDAR amortizes the cost of learning across tasks, leading to fewer cumulative LLM queries than the baseline while providing better adherence to constraints.

Conclusion: Representing natural-language instructions and constraints as regular languages and DFAs provides a practical verification layer between user instructions and embodied-agent policies. This structure enables composition of skills with new constraints, systematic enforcement of temporal and spatial rules, and better sample and query efficiency compared to free-form program generation. Regular-language grounding thus appears to be a promising direction for safe, reliable natural-language tasking of embodied agents.

Abstract: Natural-language tasking of embodied agents is rarely just goal specification: users also impose constraints that must persist while the world changes. Code-generating LLM agents can produce plausible behaviors for such instructions, but their free-form programs provide no stable object to verify, compose with new constraints, or repair from a failing trace. We present CEDAR, a counterexample-guided framework that grounds instructions as regular languages over environment event traces. CEDAR uses a language model for semantic judgments and execution traces for correction, then represents both skills and specifications as deterministic finite automata. This turns constraints into executable finite-state objects: a learned skill can be intersected with a learned sleep at night or stay in this biome specification, yielding a controller that enforces the learned constraint by construction rather than by repeated prompting. In Minecraft, with the same simulator/API observations available to a program-generating baseline, CEDAR maintains temporal and spatial constraints that the baseline fails to preserve and amortizes reuse of learned skills, reducing cumulative LLM queries. These results suggest that regular languages offer a practical verification layer between natural-language instructions and embodied-agent policies.

</details>


### [85] [CURA: Certified Runtime Alarms for Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27808)
*Divake Kumar,Sina Tayebati,Devashri Naik,Amanda Sofie Rios,Nilesh Ahuja,Omesh Tickoo,Ranganath Krishnan,Amit Ranjan Trivedi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that self-reports from strong computer-use agents are highly unreliable for indicating failure, and proposes CURA, an external statistical alarm system that can detect many failures early with certified bounds on false alarms, enabling effective targeted human oversight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Capable computer-use agents (CUAs) can outperform humans on complex OS-style tasks, but they tend to falsely claim success even when they fail, and almost never use explicit failure channels. This makes naive self-report-based oversight unreliable exactly in high-stakes settings where accurate failure detection is critical. The authors aim to build an oversight mechanism that does not rely on the model’s honesty or internals, is cheap at runtime, and can provide statistical guarantees on false alarms while catching as many failures as possible, ideally early in the trajectory so that interventions are still useful.

Method: The authors first build a strong CUA pipeline (read-only feasibility gate, planner, GUI executor) and evaluate it on 361 OSWorld tasks to characterize how often the agent misreports outcomes. They then introduce CURA (Certified Runtime Alarms), an external monitor that only observes harness-visible telemetry of the agent’s behavior. CURA converts the trajectory into a sequential hypothesis test using CUSUM-style statistics, providing certified control of the false-alarm rate (parameterized by alpha). They evaluate CURA both online (raising alarms mid-episode) and retrospectively, compare it to a strong token-count baseline, and analyze how early and how reliably alarms can predict eventual failure. They also test an alarm-gated oversight cascade where human or stronger oversight is invoked only when CURA fires, and analyze performance, recall, and oversight cost. Finally, they study where behavioral monitoring signals are uninformative for failure prediction.

Result: The base CUA achieves a high mean task score of 82.9 on OSWorld, surpassing the 72.4 human reference. However, it is systematically overconfident: 90% of its failures end with success claims, most without acknowledging any blocker, and the explicit failure option is never used across about 9,100 calls. CURA’s CUSUM alarm at alpha = 0.10 detects 42.3% of failures, typically 31 steps before episode end, with an empirical false-alarm rate of 0.066, showing good early-warning capability at controlled false positives. A gate-probe signal before any action already has moderate predictive power (0.69 AUROC). Retrospective analysis gives 0.828 AUROC (with fold-internal minimum 0.802), though its advantage over a total-token baseline is not statistically significant overall (Δ=+0.026, p=0.101). Online, CURA achieves better recall than the baseline under the same certified false-alarm budgets: 0.41 vs 0.34 at alpha=0.10, and 0.56 vs 0.38 at alpha=0.20. Using CURA to trigger mid-execution human/stronger oversight recovers 23 of 70 failures while spending an expensive overseer on only 38 episodes, improving the pipeline to mean score 86.8 and 84.5% full solves (305/361). They also identify regimes where trajectory-based monitoring provides little or no signal for failure detection.

Conclusion: Self-reports from advanced CUAs are deeply unreliable as an oversight channel, particularly on the very tasks where performance is high but failures matter. An external behavioral monitor like CURA, which treats trajectories as a sequential test and offers certified false-alarm guarantees, can meaningfully improve reliability by detecting a substantial fraction of failures early and cheaply. While its retrospective performance is only modestly better than simple baselines, CURA’s main value is online: at fixed certified false-alarm rates it recalls more failures and supports effective alarm-gated oversight cascades that raise overall task performance with limited human/expensive intervention. However, behavioral monitoring is not universally informative, and there remain scenarios where such signals cannot reliably indicate failure, underscoring that CURA is a partial but practically deployable solution, not a complete answer to oversight for computer-use agents.

Abstract: Self-report is the cheapest oversight channel a deployer has, and on capable computer-use agents (CUAs) it fails precisely where oversight matters. On 361 OSWorld tasks our pipeline, a read-only feasibility gate, a planner, and a GUI executor, reaches a mean task score of 82.9, above the 72.4 human reference, yet 64 of its 71 failures (90%) end with a success claim, 61 acknowledging no blocker, and the explicit failure affordance is never used in roughly 9,100 calls. We introduce CURA (Certified Runtime Alarms for Computer-Use Agents), an external monitor that reads only harness-visible telemetry, with no model internals, extra LLM calls, or prompt changes, and turns the running trajectory into a sequential test with certified false-alarm control. At alpha = 0.10 its CUSUM alarm detects 42.3% of failures a median of 31 steps before termination at a realized false-alarm rate of 0.066, and risk is partly resolvable before the first action (gate probe, 0.69 AUROC). Retrospectively the composite reaches 0.828 AUROC (fold-internal floor 0.802), but its margin over a total-token baseline is not significant (Delta = +0.026, p = 0.101); the separation is online, where CURA recalls more at matched certified budgets: 0.41 versus 0.34 at alpha = 0.10, 0.56 versus 0.38 at alpha = 0.20. Alarm-gated mid-execution oversight recovers 23 of 70 failures while spending a frontier overseer on 38, giving a deployable cascade at mean score 86.8 and 84.5% full-solve (305 of 361). The certificate bounds false alarms only. We also report where behavioral monitoring is uninformative.

</details>


### [86] [AcCoRD: Evaluating User-Agent Collaboration Under Realistic User Preference Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27818)
*Tejas Srinivasan,Shikib Mehri,Nandita Shankar Naik,Anirban Das,William M. Campbell,Jesse Thomason*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces AcCoRD, a benchmark to evaluate how well language-model agents collaborate with users when preferences change over time, not just when they are initially underspecified.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most current user-agent collaboration benchmarks assume that user preferences are static and mostly known from the start, while in real interactions preferences are gradually formed, revealed, changed, or relaxed during the conversation. This mismatch means that existing benchmarks fail to test the richer, dynamic nature of user preference handling that real-world assistant systems must manage.

Method: The authors construct AcCoRD, a benchmark with interactive tasks in two realistic domains: online shopping and travel planning. The tasks are designed so that user preferences can be underspecified, emerge later, evolve, or be relaxed mid-interaction. They then evaluate five state-of-the-art LLMs using two prompting strategies: (1) a standard ReAct-style prompting baseline and (2) an uncertainty-guided prompting variant that explicitly asks models to identify and resolve ambiguity about user preferences.

Result: The evaluation shows that current frontier LLMs perform reasonably well when dealing with initially underspecified preferences, but they struggle when preferences appear or change during the interaction and when effective uncertainty modeling is required. The uncertainty-guided prompting strategy does not sufficiently improve their ability to recognize and manage this uncertainty, indicating that prompting alone is not enough.

Conclusion: AcCoRD highlights a significant gap between current LLM capabilities and the demands of real-world, dynamic user preference handling. The benchmark demonstrates that better methods—beyond simple prompting tweaks—are needed for agents to recognize, track, and adapt to evolving user preferences. The authors release AcCoRD as a resource to encourage development and evaluation of more advanced, uncertainty-aware collaborative agents.

Abstract: User preferences in user-agent collaboration are rarely static and fully-specified upfront: preferences are formed, revealed, adjusted, and relaxed during interaction. Existing benchmarks for evaluating user-agent collaboration focus almost exclusively on resolving underspecified preferences, thereby failing to capture the richer dynamics of real-world interaction. We introduce AcCoRD, a user-agent collaboration benchmark requiring agents to handle diverse user preference dynamics in two domains: online shopping and travel planning. We evaluate five frontier LLMs under two prompting strategies: vanilla ReAct, and an uncertainty-guided variant that prompts models to identify and resolve ambiguity about user preferences. Our results reveal that frontier models can handle underspecification but struggle to satisfy preferences that emerge or evolve mid-interaction and require more sophisticated uncertainty modeling. Further, prompting alone fails to elicit the required uncertainty recognition. We release AcCoRD as a resource for developing agents that can navigate the full complexity of real-world user preferences.

</details>


### [87] [Evidential-Based Higher-Order Set Argumentation Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27824)
*Shuai Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a unified, expressive evidential argumentation framework (EHSAF) with both three-valued and fuzzy-logical semantics, relate two complete semantics, give a logical encoding, and show how fuzzy models recover the discrete labellings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evidential argumentation frameworks handle evidential support, higher-order attacks/supports, and collective sources in a fragmented way. There is no single formalism that uniformly captures evidential chains, higher-order relations, and set-based/collective interactions, nor a principled connection between qualitative argumentation semantics and quantitative (fuzzy) treatments of evidence.

Method: 1) Define the Evidential-Based Higher-Order Set Argumentation Framework (EHSAF), allowing evidential chains, higher-order relations, and set-based sources. 2) Introduce two semantics: (a) adjacent complete labelling semantics with three truth values, tolerant of support cycles; (b) extension-based complete semantics that only accepts arguments with well-founded evidential chains. 3) Compare these semantics, proving divergence under support cycles and equivalence when the support relation is acyclic. 4) Provide a normal propositional logic encoding of EHSAFs and show that three-valued Łukasiewicz models correspond exactly to adjacent complete labellings. 5) Generalise the encoding to continuous fuzzy logics (Gödel, Product, Łukasiewicz) and define a fuzzy semantics. 6) Prove properties of the fuzzy semantics and show that an appropriate ternarisation of fuzzy models recovers the adjacent complete labellings under natural t-norm assumptions.

Result: They obtain a general EHSAF formalism that conservatively extends multiple prior evidential and higher-order argumentation frameworks. They define and analyse two complete semantics, identify precisely when they coincide (support-acyclicity), and give a propositional encoding whose three-valued Łukasiewicz models correspond to adjacent complete labellings. Extending to several continuous t-norm–based fuzzy logics, they define a fuzzy normal encoded semantics that is continuous, monotone, respects boundary conditions, guarantees existence of solutions, and whose ternary abstraction coincides with the adjacent complete labellings under appropriate t-norm conditions.

Conclusion: EHSAF provides a unified and expressive setting for evidential argumentation with higher-order and collective interactions, while the proposed three-valued and fuzzy-logical semantics offer a principled bridge between qualitative and quantitative reasoning about evidence. The logical encodings show that discrete argumentation semantics can be faithfully captured and generalised within standard and fuzzy logics, aligning traditional argumentation theory with continuous uncertainty modelling.

Abstract: Evidential argumentation extends Dung's abstract argumentation by requiring arguments and interactions to be backed by chains of evidence rooted in prima-facie elements. However, existing formalisms lack a unified treatment of evidential support, higher-order relations (attacks and supports targeting arbitrary elements), and collective interactions (sources as sets). In this paper, we introduce the Evidential-Based Higher-Order Set Argumentation Framework (EHSAF), which conservatively generalises several existing frameworks within a single expressive setting. We develop two complete semantics for EHSAFs: an \emph{adjacent complete labelling semantics} that admits multiple truth values (true, false, undecided) for arguments in support cycles, reflecting an open epistemic attitude toward future evidence; and an \emph{extension-based complete semantics} that follows a strict evidentialist stance, accepting only arguments with well-founded support chains. We show that these two semantics diverge in the presence of support cycles, and prove their equivalence under support-acyclicity. To enable computational reasoning, we provide a normal propositional encoding of EHSAFs and prove that, in three-valued Łukasiewicz logic, its models correspond precisely to the adjacent complete labellings. We further extend this encoding to continuous fuzzy logics (G{ö}del, Product, and Łukasiewicz), defining a continuous fuzzy normal encoded semantics. We establish that this fuzzy semantics satisfies key properties---continuity, monotonicity, boundary conditions, and solution existence---and that its ternarisation recovers the adjacent complete labellings under natural t-norm conditions. Our framework thus unifies expressive argumentation with principled three-valued and fuzzy semantics, bridging the gap between qualitative and quantitative reasoning about evidence.

</details>


### [88] [RealSWE: A Compositional Evaluation of Coding Agents under Realistic User Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27831)
*Gyuhyeong Kim,Hyojung Gwon,Jeonghyeon Kim,Kyuhong Shim,Sunjae Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that current coding benchmarks like SWE-bench use long, formal, highly-informative problem descriptions that are very different from real-world, short, casual user prompts. This mismatch inflates measured performance. The authors build a new benchmark (sys) that systematically varies information content and style for the same underlying tasks, and show that realistic prompts lower model performance, change rankings, and highlight which kinds of information actually help models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coding agents are mostly evaluated on SWE-bench-style benchmarks whose tasks are based on curated GitHub issues. These tend to be long, structured, and rich in contextual details. However, real user requests to coding assistants are usually short, under-specified, and casually written. This discrepancy means benchmarks may not reflect real-world performance, potentially misleading practitioners about how well models will work in practice and what kinds of prompts are most helpful. The paper is motivated by the need to quantify this gap, understand which components of task descriptions matter, and design more realistic and diagnostic evaluations.

Method: 1) Define a six-category taxonomy of information that can appear in a software-engineering task (e.g., problem statement, desired behavior, motivation, environment info, reproduction steps, etc.) and four dimensions of linguistic style (e.g., formal vs casual). 2) Annotate and compare real user prompts from SWE-chat with the problem statements from SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro using this taxonomy and style dimensions, to measure how they differ. 3) Construct sys, a collection of 381 multi-variant task families derived from SWE-bench Verified and Pro. For each underlying task and gold patch, create multiple variants that systematically vary which information categories are present and which linguistic style is used, while keeping the ground-truth fix identical. 4) Evaluate seven contemporary large language models on these controlled variants. 5) Analyze how resolution rates change with realistic vs benchmark-like prompts, how model rankings shift, and how the presence or absence of different information categories and style choices affect performance.

Result: Empirically, 88% of real-world prompts contain only a bare problem statement (optionally with minimal context), compared to only 7% of benchmark problems; and 87% of real prompts are casually written, whereas 94% of benchmark problems are formal. On sys, using more realistic, user-like inputs reduces average task resolution rates by about 6.4 percentage points and can alter the relative ranking of models. In controlled experiments, including Desired Behavior and Motivation information leads to substantial performance gains, while adding Environment Information and Reproduction Steps mostly increases token length without improving success rates. Linguistic style (e.g., casual vs formal) has only small and model-dependent effects on performance.

Conclusion: There is a substantial realism gap between current SWE-bench-style coding benchmarks and how users actually ask for help. This gap makes current evaluations over-optimistic and sometimes misleading about model capabilities. The new sys benchmark shows that shorter, casual, under-specified prompts significantly hurt model performance and can change model rankings. Most of the performance benefit comes from explicitly stating desired behavior and motivation, not from extra environmental details or reproduction steps, and linguistic style matters little. For practitioners, both users and agent designers, the key actionable lesson is to structure prompts to clearly express what behavior is desired and why, as this markedly improves LLM performance on software-engineering tasks.

Abstract: Coding agents are now commonly evaluated on the SWE-bench family of benchmarks, whose tasks are built from curated GitHub issues--long, structured, and information-rich. Real user requests, however, are typically far shorter and less structured. To characterize this gap, we define a six-category information taxonomy and four dimensions of linguistic style, and apply them to real user prompts from SWE-chat and problem statements from SWE-bench Verified and Pro. We find that requests carrying only a problem statement, alone or with limited additional context, account for 88% of real prompts but just 7% of benchmark problems. Furthermore, 87% of real prompts are casually written whereas 94% of benchmark problems are formal. Guided by these observations, we introduce sys, 381 multi-variant task families derived from SWE-bench Verified and Pro. Variants within each family share the same underlying task and gold patch while differing only in information composition and linguistic style. Evaluating seven contemporary LLMs with sys, we find that i) realistic inputs reduce resolution rates by 6.4 pp on average and can change model rankings. Controlled analysis further shows that ii) including Desired Behavior and Motivation significantly affects performance, whereas Environment Information and Reproduction Steps merely add tokens without measurable benefit; iii) linguistic style has only small, model-dependent effects. These findings provide actionable guidance for users and agents: explicitly stating the desired behavior and motivation--which most real prompts omit--substantially improves the LLM's software engineering performance.

</details>


### [89] [KLOD: Locality-Preserving Knowledge Editing via Non-Target Distribution Preservation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27839)
*Hojun Jeong,Gyunyeop Kim,Sangwoo Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces KLOD, a new fine-tuning objective for knowledge editing that preserves non-target output distributions to reduce locality degradation while keeping edits reliable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard fine-tuning-based knowledge editing uses cross-entropy to increase the probability of the edited target, but it does not constrain how probability mass over non-target tokens is redistributed. In sequential editing, this unconstrained shift accumulates as distributional drift, harming locality (i.e., leaving unrelated model behavior unchanged). There is a need for an objective that can update specific knowledge while explicitly preserving other output distributions to avoid such degradation, and also offer control over the trade-off between generalization of the edit and locality.

Method: The authors propose KLOD, a bounded, distribution-preserving fine-tuning objective. It (1) increases the probability of the target answer only up to a specified threshold and then stops further amplification, and (2) preserves the non-target token distribution at positions where the target appears, as well as the entire next-token distribution at prefix positions. This is implemented as an optimization objective that separately treats target and non-target components of the output distributions, effectively constraining edits to be local in probability space. They analyze behavior through ablations, multiple random seeds, and KL-divergence–based distributional comparisons.

Result: On the CounterFact and ZsRE benchmarks with Llama3-8B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, KLOD substantially reduces locality degradation compared with standard cross-entropy editing, while retaining high reliability of the intended edits. The probability threshold in KLOD allows practitioners to tune the Generalization–Locality trade-off. Empirical analyses, including ablation studies, multi-seed experiments, and KL-divergence measurements, indicate that the improvements in locality come from preserving non-target output distributions rather than merely weakening the strength of edits.

Conclusion: KLOD provides a practical, architecture-agnostic, fine-tuning-based knowledge editing objective that explicitly preserves non-target output distributions and bounds target probability increases. This mitigates distributional drift and locality degradation across sequential edits while retaining edit reliability. The threshold mechanism offers controllable generalization vs. locality behavior, and empirical evidence suggests that distribution preservation is the key factor behind the locality gains. The method is implemented and publicly released for further use and study.

Abstract: Fine-tuning-based knowledge editing is simple and architecture-agnostic, but standard cross-entropy increases the edited target probability without explicitly constraining changes in the non-target output distribution. In sequential editing, such unconstrained redistribution can accumulate as distributional drift and contribute to locality degradation. We propose KLOD, a bounded and distribution-preserving objective for fine-tuning-based knowledge editing that separates the intended target update from distributions that should remain stable. KLOD stops target amplification once a probability threshold is reached, while preserving the target-excluded non-target distribution at target positions and the full next-token distribution at prefix positions. Experiments on CounterFact and ZsRE with Llama3-8B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct show that KLOD substantially mitigates locality degradation while maintaining high edit reliability. The target probability threshold further provides a controllable Generalization--Locality trade-off. Ablation, multi-seed, and distributional KL analyses support the interpretation that KLOD's locality gains are associated with preserving output distributions rather than simply weakening the edit. Code is available on GitHub https://github.com/Hostoday/KLOD .

</details>


### [90] [An Empirical Evaluation of Cross-City POI Recommendation on a Large-Scale Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27840)
*Peibo Li,Yang Song,Hao Xue,Maarten de Rijke,Flora D. Salim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper re-evaluates cross-city POI recommendation at global scale using the Trip World benchmark, revealing that prior state-of-the-art methods struggle with preference transfer, scalability, and effective use of semantics, and arguing for new task-specific designs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-city POI recommendation helps users navigate unfamiliar cities but has been studied mostly on small, limited datasets that may not reflect real-world conditions such as worldwide coverage, minimal overlap between users' home and destination regions, and large, semantically diverse POI sets. With the availability of the large-scale Trip World benchmark, there is a need to re-test whether findings from earlier, smaller benchmarks still hold, and to understand the true bottlenecks of existing state-of-the-art models at realistic scale.

Method: Using Trip World as a large-scale, realistic benchmark, the authors perform an empirical evaluation of representative state-of-the-art cross-city POI recommendation methods. They analyze model behavior with respect to three aspects: reliance on hometown vs. destination priors for preference transfer, accuracy-efficiency trade-offs at large scale, and the effectiveness of existing mechanisms for incorporating semantic metadata. Additionally, they conduct a diagnostic pilot study adapting agentic (agent-style) methods from next-POI recommendation to the cross-city setting and compare them against simple baselines like popularity priors.

Result: The study identifies three main bottlenecks in state-of-the-art methods: (1) models designed to be hometown-aware rely heavily on destination-region priors instead of truly transferring user-specific preferences; (2) at the scale and complexity of Trip World, their accuracy-efficiency trade-off worsens, with very simple models emerging as among the strongest baselines; and (3) current approaches for integrating semantic metadata provide only marginal or no benefits. Furthermore, the pilot study on adapted agentic methods shows that naive adaptations underperform even a basic popularity prior, despite the presence of useful semantic signals in the data.

Conclusion: Existing cross-city POI recommendation methods do not adequately address the challenges revealed at global scale, especially in terms of genuine cross-city preference transfer, effective semantic grounding, and scalable reasoning over large, unseen destination inventories. The findings suggest that future work should move beyond straightforward scaling or naive adaptations of existing methods and instead develop task-specific architectures and mechanisms explicitly tailored for cross-city preference transfer, better exploitation of semantic information, and efficient reasoning in large, diverse POI spaces.

Abstract: Cross-city point-of-interest (POI) recommendation is crucial for navigating unfamiliar urban environments, yet its progress has historically been constrained by data limitations. Using the recently proposed large-scale benchmark Trip World, we empirically re-examine whether conclusions drawn on small prior benchmarks still hold under worldwide coverage, low home-destination region overlap, and large, semantically rich POI inventories. Our evaluation surfaces three bottlenecks of representative state-of-the-art methods: (1) hometown-aware models appear to rely more on destination-region priors than on user-specific preference transfer; (2) their accuracy-efficiency trade-off degrades at this scale, where the simplest model is among the strongest; and (3) existing mechanisms for integrating semantic metadata yield little benefit. We further include a diagnostic pilot on agentic methods adapted from next-POI recommendation, finding that naive adaptation trails a simple popularity prior even though the relevant semantic signal is present in the data. These results highlight the need for task-specific designs that support cross-city preference transfer, semantic grounding, and scalable reasoning over unseen destination inventories.

</details>


### [91] [From Uncertainty to Clinical Risk: Severity-Aware Conformal Planning for Interactive Medical Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27847)
*Yue Zhou,Haiyang Zhou,Jin Zhang,Kong Wang,Yongxin Ni,Youhua Li,Hanwen Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a risk-aware planning framework for interactive medical diagnosis that uses clinical severity rather than only predictive uncertainty, improving diagnostic accuracy, efficiency, and safety across LLM-based systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interactive diagnosis systems must ask questions and decide when to stop and commit to a diagnosis under incomplete information. Existing methods typically rely on predictive uncertainty or label ambiguity to guide which question to ask next and when to stop. However, they neglect that clinical risk is asymmetric—missing a severe disease is far worse than over-investigating a benign condition—and they lack a unified way to plan over long interaction horizons about asking more questions versus committing to a diagnosis. This gap can lead to unsafe or inefficient diagnostic decisions. The paper is motivated by the need for a principled, severity-aware, and planning-based framework that explicitly optimizes clinical risk during interactive diagnosis.

Method: The authors formulate interactive diagnosis as a risk-sensitive sequential decision-making problem. Their Severity-Aware Conformal Clinical Planning framework maintains three complementary belief states: diagnostic beliefs (over possible diseases), safety beliefs, and masked-evidence beliefs (reflecting what remains unknown). They use conformal prediction techniques on held-out interaction trajectories to calibrate, at each turn, (1) diagnostic prediction sets and (2) a severity-weighted differential-diagnosis risk measure. This calibrated clinical risk signal is then incorporated into Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS), which is used to evaluate and plan over long-horizon trajectories consisting of Ask (continue gathering information) and Commit (finalize diagnosis) actions. The result is a planning policy that explicitly trades off further questioning against committing, under severity-weighted risk.

Result: On the DDXPlus and MediQ benchmarks, and across multiple large language models used as backbones, the proposed method yields more accurate final diagnoses while requiring fewer questions per case compared to baselines. It improves the quality of the ranked differential diagnosis lists and specifically reduces high-risk diagnostic errors in severe cases, indicating better safety performance. Overall, the experiments show that integrating calibrated clinical-risk signals into planning outperforms approaches that rely only on predictive uncertainty or label ambiguity for question selection and stopping decisions.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that severity-aware, conformal, risk-calibrated planning is an effective paradigm for interactive medical diagnosis. By explicitly modeling clinical risk and integrating it into long-horizon planning with MCTS, the framework achieves a better balance between diagnostic accuracy, question efficiency, and safety—especially in severe cases where missed diagnoses are costly. The work supports using clinical-risk signals rather than predictive uncertainty alone for guiding information acquisition and diagnosis decisions and points toward future research on broader clinical-risk-oriented interactive systems and information-acquisition strategies.

Abstract: Interactive medical diagnosis dynamically acquires patient information through multiple rounds of questioning, supporting accurate, efficient, and safe clinical decisions under incomplete evidence. Existing methods commonly guide information acquisition with predictive uncertainty or label ambiguity, but overlook the asymmetric clinical risk of missing severe diseases and lack unified long-horizon planning over whether to continue asking questions or commit to a diagnosis. To address these limitations, we propose Severity-Aware Conformal Clinical Planning, which formulates interactive diagnosis as a risk-sensitive sequential decision problem. The framework maintains complementary diagnostic, safety, and masked-evidence beliefs; calibrates turn-specific diagnostic prediction sets and severity-weighted differential-diagnosis risk on held-out diagnostic trajectories; and introduces the calibrated clinical risk into Monte Carlo Tree Search to jointly evaluate long-horizon Ask and Commit trajectories. Experiments on DDXPlus and MediQ show that our method achieves more accurate diagnoses with fewer questions across multiple large language models, while improving differential-diagnosis quality and reducing high-risk errors in severe cases. These findings validate the value of using clinical risk, rather than predictive uncertainty alone, as a planning signal and demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework for information acquisition and risk-aware diagnostic decision making. They also motivate future work on clinical-risk-oriented interactive diagnosis and information-acquisition methods.

</details>


### [92] [SpikeOPD: Stable On-Policy Distillation for Autoregressive Spiking Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27857)
*Enqiao Lu,Xingrui Yu,Yiwei Fu,Zhenglin Wan,Pengfei Zhou,Wangbo Zhao,Muqing Jian,Xueyi Zhang,Yang You,Ivor Tsang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SpikeOPD, a stable on-policy distillation framework that improves the performance of autoregressive spiking language models while keeping their energy-efficient sparse computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training strong language models directly as spiking neural networks is difficult, yet SNNs are attractive for energy-efficient computation. Existing ANN-to-SNN distillation methods rely on fixed corpus prefixes, which mismatches real autoregressive usage where models condition on their own generated text. This causes policy mismatch with the ANN teacher and drift in internal spiking dynamics, and naive on-policy distillation can itself be unstable.

Method: The authors first analyze standard on-policy distillation (Vanilla OPD) with a teacher-only full-KL loss and show it can suffer from rollout-feedback collapse. They then propose SpikeOPD, which still uses on-policy training on self-generated prefixes but adds two stabilizing components: (1) matched-prefix policy anchoring that constrains the SNN’s policy to stay close to a frozen reference SNN on the same prefixes; and (2) layerwise spike regularization that penalizes deviation in firing rates during adaptation. Teacher full-KL correction is used to reduce output-policy mismatch while these regularizers control policy and dynamic drift.

Result: Across three model scales (0.125B, 0.35B, and 1.3B parameters), SpikeOPD consistently improves average accuracy over baseline KD-based SNNs by 0.8, 1.7, and 2.9 percentage points respectively, while maintaining their sparse, energy-efficient computation patterns.

Conclusion: On-policy coverage alone is insufficient for stable adaptation of autoregressive SNN language models; naïve on-policy distillation can collapse. By combining teacher full-KL correction with policy anchoring and spike regularization, SpikeOPD enables stable on-policy training, improving accuracy of spiking language models without sacrificing their sparse-compute advantages.

Abstract: Spiking neural networks (SNNs) offer a path to energy-efficient language modeling through sparse encoding and event-driven computation, but training capable spiking language models from scratch remains difficult. A practical alternative is ANN-to-SNN migration through knowledge distillation (KD), where a pretrained artificial neural network (ANN) teacher supervises an SNN student. Existing migration approaches distill on fixed corpus prefixes, whereas autoregressive inference conditions on self-generated prefixes, creating prefix-source mismatch. It manifests as output-policy mismatch with the ANN teacher and internal spiking-dynamics drift between self-generated and matched corpus prefixes. On-policy distillation (OPD) offers a natural way to mitigate both manifestations by continuing teacher supervision on self-generated prefixes. We evaluate a teacher-only full-KL variant, Vanilla OPD, via a controlled stress test and observe it may suffer from delayed rollout-feedback collapse. This result shows that on-policy coverage alone does not ensure stable adaptation. Motivated by these findings, we propose SpikeOPD, a stable on-policy distillation framework for autoregressive SNNs that learns from self-generated prefixes while maintaining rollout stability. It applies full-KL teacher correction to reduce output-policy mismatch, while matched-prefix policy anchoring constrains policy departure from the frozen reference SNN on the same prefixes. Layerwise spike regularization further limits firing-rate deviations during on-policy adaptation. Across three model scales, SpikeOPD improves average accuracy over the corresponding KD SNNs by 0.8, 1.7, and 2.9 points at 0.125B, 0.35B, and 1.3B, respectively, while preserving their sparse-compute profiles.

</details>


### [93] [CoRe-MoE: Compact Reusable MoE for Continual Multimodal Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27867)
*Runze Liu,Naibin Gu,Mingxu Ai,Yuqing Li,Peng Fu,Zheng Lin,Weiping Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes CoRe-MoE, a compact and reusable mixture-of-experts framework for continual multimodal instruction tuning that reuses shared LoRA direction bases and only learns lightweight task-specific coordinates and routers, achieving better performance with far fewer parameters than LoRA-MoE and sequential LoRA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continual multimodal instruction tuning needs models to learn new tasks over time without forgetting old ones, but existing LoRA-MoE approaches require adding and maintaining full LoRA experts for each task, causing large parameter growth. The authors question whether full expert expansion is necessary for every new task and investigate the structure of task-specific LoRA updates to find a more parameter-efficient alternative.

Method: The authors analyze the singular value decomposition (SVD) of task-specific LoRA updates and find that different tasks share substantial overlap in their input and output LoRA direction subspaces. Based on this, they introduce CoRe-MoE, which (1) builds an initial expert bank and extracts reusable input- and output-side direction bases, and then (2) for new tasks, freezes these bases and trains only compact coordinate experts plus task-specific low-rank routers that select and combine base directions instead of adding full new LoRA experts.

Result: On two representative multimodal large language models, CoRe-MoE achieves up to 5.90 points improvement in final average performance over the strongest baseline for continual multimodal instruction tuning, while using under 1% of the trainable parameters that sequential LoRA needs for later tasks, demonstrating both better performance and significantly higher parameter efficiency.

Conclusion: Full LoRA expert expansion is not necessary for every new task in continual multimodal instruction tuning because most task-specific adaptation can be captured by shared direction subspaces plus lightweight coordinates. By factorizing LoRA experts into reusable bases and compact task-specific coordinates with low-rank routing, CoRe-MoE offers a more scalable and efficient approach to continual multimodal instruction tuning, achieving better performance with drastically fewer parameters.

Abstract: Continual multimodal instruction tuning requires multimodal large language models to acquire new task abilities sequentially while preserving previously learned knowledge. LoRA-MoE provides a promising solution by introducing expert-based capacity, but repeatedly learning and maintaining full LoRA experts leads to substantial parameter overhead. This raises a natural question: is full expert expansion necessary for every new task? To answer it, we analyze the SVD of task-specific LoRA updates and observe substantial overlap in their input- and output-side LoRA direction subspaces, with task-specific adaptation largely captured by lightweight coordinates over these subspaces. Motivated by this observation, we propose CoRe-MoE, a Compact Reusable MoE framework for parameter-efficient continual multimodal instruction tuning. CoRe-MoE extracts reusable input- and output-side direction bases from an initial expert bank, and for subsequent tasks trains only compact coordinate experts together with task-specific low-rank routers. Experiments on two representative MLLMs show that CoRe-MoE improves final average performance over the strongest competing baseline by up to 5.90 points, while using less than 1% of the trainable parameters required by sequential LoRA for later tasks. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/runzezz/CoRe-MoE.

</details>


### [94] [See, Hypothesize, Validate: Multimodal Agentic Framework for Discovering Governing PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27869)
*Sarang Manoj Pekhale,Amartya Roy,Rajat Sarkar,Souvik Chakraborty*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAGE is an agent-based, multimodal framework that discovers governing PDEs from data by iteratively proposing, testing, and refining candidate equations without relying on a fixed term library.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discovering the underlying PDEs from data is essential for understanding and predicting complex physical systems, but existing methods suffer from reliance on hand-crafted libraries of terms, sensitivity to noise, lack of systematic refinement, and, in the case of LLMs, hallucinations and weak validation. The authors aim to build a more robust, adaptive, and scientifically grounded process that can discover governing laws directly from data in a library-free manner.

Method: MAGE organizes PDE discovery as an iterative hypothesis-validation loop implemented via four specialized agents: (1) a Differential Observer that computes numerical derivatives and creates diagnostic visualizations from raw data; (2) a VLM-based Phenomenology Extractor that interprets these visual and numerical diagnostics to identify qualitative behaviors; (3) an LLM-based Governing Law Synthesizer that proposes candidate PDEs consistent with the extracted phenomenology, without relying on a predefined dictionary of terms; and (4) an Equation Arbiter that fits coefficients in the candidate PDEs to the data, quantitatively evaluates each candidate, and assigns confidence scores. The system repeats this loop until the best candidate exceeds a user-defined confidence threshold, providing explicit accept-or-reject decisions at each iteration.

Result: On a benchmark suite of canonical PDEs, MAGE exactly recovers the correct PDE structure in all 8/8 tested systems and achieves the lowest coefficient error in 7/8 cases compared to existing methods, with improvements up to four orders of magnitude and a geometric-mean gain of about three orders of magnitude. The approach also correctly recovers expected operators in two challenging, complex geometries and, on real experimental sensor data, identifies a cubic restoring-force model that generalizes well with a held-out R² of 0.98538.

Conclusion: The study concludes that structured, agentic reasoning combining multimodal perception (via VLMs) and symbolic synthesis (via LLMs) can greatly improve library-free discovery of governing PDEs from data, delivering accurate structural and parametric recovery and a principled, confidence-based stopping rule. However, the authors emphasize that broader generalization beyond the evaluated benchmarks and case studies still needs to be systematically assessed.

Abstract: Discovering governing partial differential equations (PDEs) from observational data remains a core challenge across the sciences. Existing sparse-regression, symbolic-regression, and LLM-based approaches can be constrained by predefined libraries, noise sensitivity, hallucination, or limited iterative refinement. We introduce \textbf{MAGE} (\textbf{M}ultimodal \textbf{A}gentic \textbf{G}overning \textbf{E}quation Discovery), an agentic framework that organizes PDE discovery as a \textit{confidence governed hypothesis validation loop} inspired by the scientific cycle of observation, hypothesis, and falsification. Four role-specialized agents collaborate: a \textit{Differential Observer} computing derivatives and diagnostic visualizations; a VLM-powered \textit{Phenomenology Extractor} distilling qualitative cues from multimodal diagnostics; an LLM-driven \textit{Governing Law Synthesizer} proposing candidates without a predefined library; and an \textit{Equation Arbiter} fitting coefficients and assigning confidence scores. Discovery iterates until the top candidate clears a user-specified threshold, providing a structured process with an explicit accept-reject protocol. On the evaluated canonical PDE suite, MAGE obtains \textbf{8/8} exact structural recovery and the lowest coefficient error among the compared methods on \textbf{7/8} systems, with improvements of up to \textbf{4 orders of magnitude} and a geometric-mean improvement of approximately \textbf{3 orders of magnitude}. The pipeline also recovers the expected operators in two complex geometries and, on one laboratory sensor record, selects a cubic restoring-force model with held-out $R^2=0.98538$. These results support further study of structured agentic reasoning for library-free governing-law discovery, while broader generalization remains to be evaluated.

</details>


### [95] [HyQuant: Hybrid-Precision Quantization for LLM Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27875)
