*Jiatong Ding,Bingxin Xing,Yu Zhang,Dian Ding,Xiaodong Yi,Xianbin Ouyang,Feihu Zhou,Kun Zhang,Zhenyu Guo,Hao Pan,Guangtao Xue,Yiming Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HyQuant is a hybrid-precision quantization framework for LLM attention that keeps a small subset of important tokens and local windows in high precision while quantizing most attention states to low-bit, achieving near-lossless accuracy with higher efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-bit quantization greatly improves LLM efficiency, but the attention module suffers large errors at very low bit-widths, especially due to outlier or critical tokens, leading to accuracy drops. Existing approaches mostly smooth or mitigate outliers but do not flexibly mix precisions across attention states. There is a need for a method that can selectively preserve high precision only where it matters most, to balance efficiency and accuracy.

Method: The paper proposes HyQuant, a hybrid quantization design for attention. It identifies accuracy-critical regions ("vertical-line" tokens and local-window states) using lightweight, attention-pattern-based signals. Most attention states are quantized to low-bit formats, while a small subset of vertical-line tokens and a local sliding context window are kept in high precision. In the Prefill stage, a hybrid-precision attention operator preserves these selected tokens and local windows in full precision while quantizing the rest. In the Decode stage, the same selection principle is applied to KV-cache compression, and KV dequantization is fused with attention computation to improve memory and hardware efficiency.

Result: Across various models, tasks, and datasets, HyQuant achieves nearly lossless accuracy compared to full-precision baselines while using low-bit quantization for the majority of attention computations. It demonstrates that the overhead of keeping a small high-precision subset is limited, yet significantly reduces quantization error and preserves model quality.

Conclusion: Hybrid quantization of attention—keeping only a small, adaptively chosen subset of tokens and local windows in high precision while quantizing the rest—offers a practical way to attain near-lossless LLM performance at low bit-widths. HyQuant provides a simple, efficient realization of this idea, validates its effectiveness empirically, and suggests that attention-specific, pattern-aware hybrid quantization is a promising direction for efficient LLM deployment.

Abstract: Quantization has been widely adopted in LLM training and inference to reduce cost and improve efficiency. However, low-bit quantization of the \emph{attention} module often introduces large errors at very low bit-widths, causing performance degradation. Existing methods mainly rely on smoothing techniques to handle outliers, while we propose a hybrid quantization design to better balance accuracy and efficiency. Specifically, we propose \textbf{HyQuant}, an efficient hybrid quantization framework for LLM attention. HyQuant quantizes most attention states into low-bit formats while retaining a small set of vertical-line tokens and local-window states in high precision. These accuracy-critical regions are selected using lightweight vertical-line-aware attention-pattern signals, reducing quantization error with limited overhead. In the Prefill stage, HyQuant uses a hybrid-precision quantized attention operator that preserves vertical-line tokens and a local sliding window in full precision while quantizing the remaining context. In the Decode stage, HyQuant applies the same principle to KV-cache compression and fuses KV dequantization with attention computation to improve memory and hardware efficiency. Across diverse tasks, models, and datasets, HyQuant maintains nearly lossless accuracy with an extremely simple design, demonstrating the efficiency and practical feasibility of hybrid quantization for LLM attention. Code is available at: https://github.com/jerrysfls/HyQuant .

</details>


### [96] [Resource Constraints and Performance in Agentic AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27886)
*Amaz Salman,Malka Halgamuge,Teo Susnjak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper compares two complete agentic AI systems, OpenClaw and NanoBot, showing small or statistically unclear differences in full task completion but large differences in time and memory use, and argues that evaluations must jointly consider verified outcomes, resource costs, and detailed execution traces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI moves toward more autonomous, tool-using agents, performance depends not only on raw model capability but also on how systems manage tools, memory, state, and multi-step workflows. Existing evaluations often focus on task success alone and ignore resource use and execution details, making it hard to compare agent architectures or understand trade-offs between capability and operational burden. The authors want a principled way to compare full agentic stacks and to show why evaluations must link outcomes with resource consumption and execution provenance.

Method: They construct a paired benchmark to evaluate two end-to-end agentic systems—OpenClaw and NanoBot—on the same tasks. They run a primary benchmark to measure overall full task completion rates and a more detailed, instrumented subset of prompts to capture partial completion, wall-clock time, and peak memory. They then compute completion rates, bootstrap confidence intervals for differences, and geometric mean ratios for time and memory usage, and analyze dominance relationships (where one system is at least as good on all measured dimensions and strictly better on some) across prompts.

Result: In the primary benchmark, OpenClaw achieved 31% full task completion and NanoBot 25%, with a bootstrap 95% interval for the difference ranging from -3 to 15 percentage points, implying no statistically established advantage in full completion. In the instrumented subset, both reached 26% full completion, but NanoBot achieved at least partial completion on 43% of prompts versus 26% for OpenClaw. OpenClaw used substantially more resources, taking longer on 83% of prompts and showing higher peak memory on every prompt, with geometric mean ratios of 2.98× in wall time and 19.44× in peak memory. On the ten prompts where at least one system partially or fully succeeded, NanoBot weakly dominated on eight, though many dominance cases corresponded to cheaper joint failures rather than better successes. Outcome labels and dominance patterns differed notably between the coarse primary benchmark and the detailed instrumented layer.

Conclusion: Neither OpenClaw nor NanoBot shows a clear, statistically robust edge in full task completion, but they differ substantially in resource consumption and partial progress behavior. This demonstrates that evaluating agentic AI systems requires more than aggregate success rates: it must incorporate verified task completion, fine-grained labels for partial progress, explicit measurements of time and memory, and traceable links between scores and the executions that generated them. Such multi-dimensional, provenance-aware evaluation is essential for understanding trade-offs in the design of increasingly autonomous AI agents and for making meaningful claims about progress.

Abstract: Progress toward more autonomous AI increasingly depends on agentic systems that combine a language model with tools, memory, state management, and multi-step execution. These mechanisms shape both task capability and operational burden. We compare OpenClaw and NanoBot as complete agentic systems using a paired primary benchmark and a more detailed instrumented subset of paired prompts. In the primary benchmark, the rate of full task completion was 31% for OpenClaw and 25% for NanoBot, a six-percentage-point difference with a 95% task-bootstrap interval from -3 to 15 percentage points, providing no statistically established full-completion advantage for either system. In the instrumented layer, both systems achieved 26% full completion, while NanoBot reached at least partial completion on 43% of prompts compared with 26% for OpenClaw. OpenClaw took longer on 83% of prompts and had a higher recorded peak-memory value on every prompt, with geometric mean ratios of 2.98 for wall time and 19.44 for peak memory. Among the ten detailed-layer prompts on which at least one system achieved partial or full completion, NanoBot weakly dominated on eight; across all 23 prompts, however, ten of its eighteen dominance cases were cheaper joint failures. Outcome labels differ across the two evidence layers, showing why agent-system evaluation should connect capability and resource measurements to attempt-level execution and scoring provenance. These findings show that progress toward more autonomous AI should be evaluated through verified task completion, observed resource use and records linking each result to the execution that produced it.

</details>


### [97] [Rubric-to-Code Credit Assignment for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27906)
*Rui Jin,Jikai Chen,Yihan Chen,Hao Zhou,Demin Zhu,Kaichen Yang,Dong Wang,Chenyi Zhuang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces RCCA, a reinforcement learning framework that improves interactive web app generation from natural language by assigning fine-grained, rubric-based rewards to specific code regions instead of a single sequence reward, yielding state-of-the-art performance on MiniAppBench and ArtifactsBench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL methods for code generation, like standard GRPO, use a single sequence-level reward that is applied uniformly to all tokens. For interactive web app generation, this is inadequate because app quality depends on multiple distinct functional requirements (e.g., event handling, state updates, DOM structure, CSS), each often localized to specific code spans. This weakens credit assignment and limits performance on realistic, user-facing web app tasks. The authors aim to design an RL framework that can turn structured, rubric-level feedback into localized learning signals that better reflect which parts of the code are responsible for which functional successes or failures.

Method: The authors propose Rubric-to-Code Credit Assignment (RCCA). First, they define training tasks around explicit functional rubrics describing requirements of interactive web apps. They then design a hierarchical reward that decomposes evaluation into format, source-code, runtime, and functional aspects, enabling more precise error categorization. Next, they use evaluator-generated textual attributions that explain which parts of the app fail which rubric items, and align these attributions with specific code regions (e.g., handlers, DOM fragments, selectors) and the underlying generated tokens. These localized alignments are used to compute token-level or span-level advantages instead of a uniform sequence reward. The framework is used to train a model, Ling-RCCA-Flash, starting from an existing base (Ling-3.0-Flash).

Result: Ling-RCCA-Flash achieves a score of 41.25 on MiniAppBench, improving over the base Ling-3.0-Flash model by 32.20 points and slightly surpassing Claude Opus 4.5 on this benchmark. On ArtifactsBench, Ling-RCCA-Flash reaches 76.19, which is 4.48 points better than the supervised fine-tuned (SFT) model. This score also sets a new state of the art on the official ArtifactsBench leaderboard, outperforming GPT-5 by 3.64 points. These results demonstrate substantial gains in interactive web app generation quality and show that improvements transfer across benchmarks.

Conclusion: RCCA effectively turns rubric-level functional feedback into localized rewards that improve credit assignment in RL for code generation. By decomposing rewards hierarchically and aligning textual attributions with code spans and tokens, the method yields a stronger model for interactive web app generation. The resulting Ling-RCCA-Flash model achieves large performance gains over its base and surpasses leading closed models on MiniAppBench and ArtifactsBench, suggesting that fine-grained, attribution-based RL is a powerful approach for complex, user-facing code generation tasks and that the learned improvements generalize across evaluation settings.

Abstract: Interactive web application generation requires models to produce usable HTML, CSS, and JavaScript applications from natural language requests. Unlike conventional code generation, application quality depends on multiple user-facing functional requirements, each often tied to localized code regions such as event handlers, state updates, DOM fragments, or CSS selectors. Standard GRPO collapses these structured outcomes into a single sequence-level reward and applies the resulting advantage uniformly to all tokens, weakening credit assignment. We propose \textbf{Rubric-to-Code Credit Assignment} (RCCA), a reinforcement learning framework that converts rubric-level functional feedback into localized optimization signals over generated code. RCCA builds training tasks around explicit functional rubrics, uses a hierarchical reward to separate format, source-code, runtime, and functional failures, and aligns evaluator-generated textual attributions with responsible code spans and generated tokens. The resulting model, \textbf{Ling-RCCA-Flash}, scores 41.25 on MiniAppBench, improving Ling-3.0-Flash by 32.20 points and slightly surpassing Claude Opus 4.5. It also reaches 76.19 on ArtifactsBench, improving the SFT model by 4.48 points and establishing a new top score under the official ArtifactsBench leaderboard setting by surpassing the GPT-5 score by 3.64 points, suggesting transferable implementation-level gains.

</details>


### [98] [AI Alignment through a Game-theoretic Lens: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27910)
*Yanan Cai,Zhongrui Zhao,Zhigang Lu,Ickjai Lee,Wei Emma Zhang,Minhui Xue,Yihong Zhang,Shuchao Pang,Wei Xiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Survey of AI alignment methods using a game-theoretic perspective, focusing on how game theory helps address complex, real-world value alignment challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI systems and large language models are deployed in high‑risk, multi‑stakeholder environments, traditional alignment methods that assume stable, simple, or single‑agent preferences are insufficient. Real-world human values are diverse, context-dependent, and shaped by strategic interactions among many actors, so a more structured framework is needed to reason about these complexities.

Method: Conduct a survey of the AI alignment literature and reinterpret or organize existing work around fundamental game-theoretic concepts. Structure the discussion around three core challenges—preference diversity, alignment priority, and temporal dynamics—and analyze when and how game-theoretic tools have been concretely applied versus when they are used more loosely or metaphorically.

Result: The survey categorizes recent alignment methods according to key game-theoretic elements (e.g., players, payoffs, equilibria, dynamics) and maps them onto the three identified challenge areas. It identifies specific domains where game-theoretic analysis has yielded clear benefits for alignment design, highlights gaps where current usage lacks rigor or is incomplete, and surfaces open problems where additional game-theoretic modeling could be impactful.

Conclusion: Viewing AI alignment through a game-theoretic lens clarifies both the strengths and limitations of current methods and suggests that robust, adaptive, and verifiable alignment will require explicitly modeling heterogeneous preferences, priority-setting among stakeholders, and the evolving strategic dynamics over time. The survey argues for deeper and more principled integration of game theory into future alignment research to better capture real-world complexity.

Abstract: As large language models and increasingly capable AI agents are deployed in high-risk settings, aligning them with complex human values has become a central challenge. Existing alignment methods, while effective in improving helpfulness, harmlessness, and controllability, often struggle to capture real-world preferences that are context-dependent, non-transitive, and shaped by dynamic multi-party interactions. This survey reviews AI alignment through a game-theoretic lens. Specifically, it organizes recent progress around key game-theoretic elements and synthesizes the literature along three challenges: preference diversity, alignment priority, and temporal dynamics. This perspective clarifies where current alignment methods genuinely benefit from game-theoretic analysis, where the framework is looser, and what challenges remain in building robust, adaptive, and verifiable AI systems.

</details>


### [99] [From Documents to Reasoning: A Validated Synthetic Data Pipeline and Semantic-Aware Fine-Tuning for Financial Numerical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27919)
*Lokendra Birla,Milind Savagaonkar,Visnu Srinivasan,Sowmya Rasipuram,Shubhashis Sengupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build a pipeline that uses high-quality synthetic data and a new expression-based evaluation and loss to significantly improve smaller models on financial QA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial QA on tables, charts, and text is a key testbed for domain-specific reasoning, but current LLMs show inconsistent performance, and standard metrics like Exact Match are unreliable because they penalize harmless variations like units or formatting. There is also a need to make smaller models competitive via efficient fine-tuning rather than relying solely on huge LLMs.

Method: 1) Generate synthetic financial QA data and aggressively validate it for correctness and relevance; 2) fine-tune smaller language models with QLoRA for parameter‑efficient adaptation; 3) introduce a new evaluation metric that compares arithmetic expressions by computing their values instead of doing plain string or EM comparison; 4) design a modified loss that jointly considers semantic similarity between predicted and reference expressions, the new expression-based metric, and standard cross‑entropy to better align model outputs with correct reasoning chains.

Result: On the ConvFinQA benchmark, models fine‑tuned with the synthetic dataset and the proposed composite loss show substantial increases in QA accuracy compared to baselines that use standard training procedures and EM-based objectives.

Conclusion: Carefully validated synthetic data plus expression-aware training and evaluation can substantially boost the reasoning quality of smaller models on financial QA, and expression‑level metrics are more reliable indicators of reasoning ability than traditional EM, especially in domains with numerical and unit variations.

Abstract: Financial question answering (QA) has emerged as a key benchmark for evaluating the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) on domain-specific tasks involving complex data formats such as tables, charts, and rich textual narratives. While recent advancements have enabled models to reason across modalities and perform multi-step arithmetic operations, limitations remain in performance consistency, and evaluation reliability. In particular, standard evaluation metrics like Exact Match (EM) often fail to account for minor variations such as differences in units or formats, misleading performance assessments.
  In this work, we propose a comprehensive pipeline for improving financial QA systems through high-quality synthetic data generation and fine-tuning of smaller language models (SLMs) using Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA). Our pipeline includes aggressive data validation for synthetic question answer generation to ensure the relevance and correctness of synthetic question-answer pairs. We introduce a novel evaluation metric that matches answers computed from arithmetic expressions rather than ground-truth answers; providing a more accurate reflection of model reasoning capability. Furthermore, we propose a modified loss function that aligns predicted and reference expressions using semantic similarity, our novel evaluation metric and standard cross-entropy, resulting in improved performance. Experimental results on benchmark datasets, ConvFinQA demonstrate significant gains in QA accuracy after fine-tuning using synthetic dataset and proposed loss function.

</details>


### [100] [A Deep Learning-Based Stacking Ensemble Framework for Turbofan Engine Remaining Useful Life Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27940)
*Limon Bin Hossain,Md. Salehin Seyam,Md Rashedul Islam,Abdur Rahman,Md Sharifuzzaman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Two-level stacking ensemble (LSTM, CNN, CNN‑LSTM, CNN‑GRU + XGBoost) significantly improves RUL prediction accuracy on C-MAPSS turbofan datasets FD001 and FD003 versus prior state of the art.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurately predicting Remaining Useful Life of turbofan engines is critical for safety and cost in aerospace, but individual deep models (LSTM, CNN, hybrids) capture degradation patterns imperfectly and can be biased or unstable. Existing methods, including strong baselines like TCAT, still leave room for error reduction and robustness improvements on standard benchmarks such as NASA C-MAPSS.

Method: The paper designs a two-level stacking ensemble for RUL prediction. At level one, four heterogeneous deep learners—LSTM, CNN, CNN-LSTM, and CNN-GRU—are trained on the C-MAPSS FD001 and FD003 subsets. Out-of-fold predictions from these base learners are generated to avoid information leakage. At level two, an XGBoost regressor acts as a meta-learner, taking the base learners’ predictions as inputs and learning how to optimally combine them to model complex degradation trajectories. The study includes feature correlation analysis, residual diagnostics, and training convergence analysis to assess behavior and robustness.

Result: On C-MAPSS FD001, the ensemble attains RMSE 9.989, MAE 7.081, and R² 0.899; on FD003, it achieves RMSE 8.613, MAE 5.195, and R² 0.906. Relative to the best published baseline TCAT (RMSE 11.12 on FD001 and 11.02 on FD003), the proposed method reduces RMSE by 10.2% and 21.8%, respectively. Auxiliary analyses (feature correlations, residuals, convergence curves) indicate stable training and error distributions, supporting the claim of robustness.

Conclusion: A heterogeneous deep learning stacking ensemble with an XGBoost meta-learner can substantially outperform strong single-model and prior state-of-the-art methods for RUL prediction on standard turbofan benchmarks. The combination of diverse temporal and convolutional architectures with a powerful tabular meta-learner yields more accurate and robust prognostics, suggesting stacking ensembles as a promising direction for safety-critical Prognostics and Health Management in aerospace.

Abstract: This study proposes a two-level stacking ensemble framework for Remaining Useful Life (RUL) prediction of turbofan engines, evaluated on the NASA C-MAPSS benchmark using the FD001 and FD003 subsets. The framework integrates four heterogeneous deep learning base learners: Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM), Convolutional Neural Network (CNN), CNN-LSTM, and CNN-GRU, whose out-of-fold predictions are combined by an XGBoost meta-learner to capture complex degradation patterns while mitigating individual model biases. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that the stacking ensemble achieves superior predictive performance, with Root Mean Square Error (RMSE) of 9.989 and 8.613, Mean Absolute Error (MAE) of 7.081 and 5.195, and R-squared values of 0.899 and 0.906 for FD001 and FD003, respectively. Compared to the best-reported baseline (TCAT: RMSE 11.12 and 11.02), the proposed method achieves RMSE reductions of 10.2 percent and 21.8 percent for FD001 and FD003, respectively. Feature correlation analysis, residual diagnostics, and training convergence curves validate the model's robustness. These findings underscore the efficacy of stacking ensemble methods for prognostics and health management in safety-critical aerospace applications.

</details>


### [101] [CASTANET: Causality-Aware Spatio-Temporal Adversarial Network Using Traffic Incident Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27942)
*Toshiya Kitahara,Ryu Shirakami,Koh Takeuchi,Hisashi Kashima*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes CASTANET, a model that improves prediction of sudden, non-periodic traffic congestion by combining spatio-temporal graph neural networks with causal treatment effect estimation on incident records.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing traffic forecasting models work well for regular, periodic patterns (e.g., rush hours) but struggle with sudden congestion caused by rare incidents such as accidents or road damage. These incidents are sparse, highly localized in space and time, and their effects vary with the surrounding traffic context. Moreover, many deep learning models either ignore explicit incident records or use them naively, failing to handle the strong spatial-temporal bias in where and when incidents occur. There is a need for a method that can exploit incident records effectively while correcting for selection bias to better predict non-periodic congestion, which is critical for intelligent transportation systems.

Method: The paper introduces CASTANET, a framework that integrates spatio-temporal graph neural networks with causal treatment effect estimation. Traffic networks are modeled as graphs over time, and the model learns traffic dynamics while explicitly incorporating incident records as treatments. By using tools from causal inference, the method aims to estimate the effect of incidents on traffic while mitigating selection bias due to the non-random occurrence of incidents in specific locations and times. This enables the model to better generalize the impact of incidents under varying traffic contexts.

Result: On real-world traffic and accident data from Tokyo, where accidents are treated as incident events, CASTANET achieves a 4.0% reduction in RMSE compared to the best existing baseline when evaluated overall. When evaluation is conditioned on the presence of incidents, CASTANET yields a 10.1% RMSE improvement, and its relative gains increase further to 14.55% under severe congestion scenarios.

Conclusion: CASTANET effectively leverages incident records through a combination of spatio-temporal graph neural networks and causal effect estimation, leading to more accurate prediction of non-periodic, incident-driven traffic congestion. By explicitly addressing selection bias and heterogeneity in incident impacts, the approach significantly outperforms existing baselines, especially in conditions with actual incidents and severe congestion, demonstrating its value for advanced intelligent transportation systems.

Abstract: Predicting non-periodic traffic congestion caused by sudden incidents (e.g., accidents and road damage) is crucial for advanced intelligent transportation systems. However, incident-driven congestion is difficult to forecast because incidents are extremely sparse, occur at specific times and locations, and have heterogeneous impacts depending on the traffic context. While recent deep learning approaches have significantly improved periodic traffic forecasting, their performance on non-periodic congestion remains limited, partly because incident records are not explicitly incorporated and their occurrence is strongly biased in space and time. To address these challenges, we propose CASTANET, which integrates spatio-temporal graph neural networks and causal treatment effect estimation to utilize incident records while mitigating selection bias. Experiments on real-world traffic data and accident records from Tokyo, which we treat as incidents, show that CASTANET reduces RMSE by 4.0% overall compared to the best baseline and by 10.1% on incident-conditioned evaluation, with gains reaching 14.55% under severe congestion.

</details>


### [102] [Cross-Session Decomposition Attacks: Scaling Risk and Intent-Aligned Retrieval Defense](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27945)
*Disen Liao,Yihan Wang,Freda Shi,Yaoliang Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how scaling language models affects safety in a specific attack setting called cross-session decomposition attacks, and proposes a theoretical framework, empirical evaluation, and a defensive retriever model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As language models scale, their lower language-modeling loss usually implies better capabilities, but this may also mean they are better at reconstructing harmful instructions from benign-looking pieces spread across sessions. There was a need to formalize and quantify this compositional safety risk and understand how scaling affects it, as well as to develop defenses.

Method: The authors formalize cross-session decomposition attacks as a problem of compositional safety risk and derive a conditional risk-transfer bound that relates the difference in risk between deployment and a reference environment to the model’s excess loss on allowed subqueries. They run synthetic withholding experiments to see how models infer held-out instructions from supporting facts. They also conduct a large-scale evaluation over 600 intents on different sizes of Qwen3 and Gemma3 models under a fixed decomposition–composition pipeline, and design an intent-aligned 22M-parameter retriever (IntentAlign-MiniLM) and compare it with larger embedding models and guardrails.

Result: They show theoretically that when risky reconstructions are already latent in the environment, the additional compositional risk from deployment is controlled by the model’s excess language-modeling loss on permitted subqueries. Empirically, wider transformers assign lower loss to held-out, never-seen instructions that can be reconstructed from injected facts, indicating stronger harmful reconstruction capability. Larger Qwen3 and Gemma3 models produce greater harmful-capability uplift in the given decomposition–composition pipeline. The proposed IntentAlign-MiniLM retriever outperforms larger embedding models on held-out intent retrieval and achieves the best harmful recall performance among tested guardrails.

Conclusion: Scaling language models can increase compositional safety risks by making them better at reconstructing harmful objectives from benign fragments, and this effect can be understood via a conditional risk-transfer bound linked to excess loss. Larger models can therefore be more dangerous in cross-session decomposition attacks, even under fixed pipelines. However, specialized, small, intent-aligned retrievers like IntentAlign-MiniLM can act as effective defenses, sometimes outperforming much larger models at retrieving and filtering risky intents.

Abstract: Scaling laws are usually read as a capability story: lower language-modeling loss yields more useful models. We study a safety consequence of this mechanism in \emph{cross-session decomposition attacks}, where benign-looking subqueries are asked across independent interactions and later recomposed toward a forbidden objective. We formalize this setting as \emph{compositional safety risk} and prove a conditional risk-transfer bound: when the reference environment already contains dispersed evidence for a risky reconstruction, the gap between deployed composed risk and reference composed risk is controlled by the model's excess loss on allowed subqueries. Synthetic withholding experiments show that wider transformers assign lower loss to held-out instructions that never appear verbatim in training but are recoverable from injected supporting facts. A 600-intent pretrained-LLM evaluation shows that larger Qwen3 and Gemma3 family members can yield greater harmful-capability uplift under a fixed decomposition-composition pipeline. As a defense, IntentAlign-MiniLM, our 22M-parameter intent-aligned retriever, outperforms much larger embedding models on held-out intent retrieval and yields the best learned-retriever harmful recall across tested guardrails. Code is available in \href{https://github.com/liaodisen/Cross-Session-Decomposition-Attacks}{our GitHub repository}.

</details>


### [103] [The Illusion of $\textit{What If}$: Evaluating the Breakdown of Counterfactual Reasoning in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27953)
*Yucheng Wang,Yuetian Du,Zhengyi Liu,Rongyu Zhang,Bing Zhao,Boyu Yang,Ming Kong,Lin Qu,Hu Wei,Jie Liu,Qiang Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces WhatIfBench and PRISM to evaluate long-horizon, open-domain counterfactual causal reasoning in LLMs, showing current models perform far from human-level.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing counterfactual reasoning benchmarks mostly use closed, bounded settings with fixed variables and single gold-standard outcomes. They fail to capture open-domain, long-horizon scenarios where models must explain how hypothetical changes ripple through complex causal processes. The authors aim to create a benchmark and evaluation method that diagnose these richer capabilities and expose where current LLMs fall short.

Method: They build WhatIfBench, a dataset of 220 open-ended what-if questions spanning STEM, humanities/social sciences (HSS), and hybrid scenarios that demand multi-step causal reasoning and explanations. To score free-form answers, they introduce PRISM, which converts each natural-language explanation into a Response-Derived Semantic Causal Graph representing events, states, and mechanisms. On this graph they define: (1) a Process Metric that evaluates graph-level causal validity and structure, and (2) a Rubric Metric that evaluates how adequate and explanatory the answer is overall. They then apply this framework to six state-of-the-art LLMs.

Result: Using WhatIfBench and PRISM, they evaluate six strong LLMs and find that performance is far from ceiling: the best model attains only 64.62% on the combined metrics. Detailed analyses of the resulting causal graphs and explanations uncover systematic issues like causal gaps in reasoning chains, drifting away from the initial premises, and fragmented causal topologies, even when the surface narrative is fluent and coherent-sounding.

Conclusion: WhatIfBench plus PRISM provides a challenging, diagnostic framework for assessing open-domain, long-horizon counterfactual causal reasoning in LLMs. Current frontier models still exhibit substantial weaknesses in maintaining coherent, complete causal processes, indicating that apparent fluency does not equate to robust counterfactual understanding. The benchmark and tools are released to facilitate future research and progress on causal reasoning in language models.

Abstract: Counterfactual reasoning requires models to reason beyond the observed world and explain how altered conditions propagate through downstream consequences. Existing benchmarks largely target bounded settings with fixed variables or single gold outcomes, overlooking open-domain scenarios requiring causal-process evaluation. To this end, we present $\textbf{WhatIfBench}$, a diagnostic benchmark for open-domain, open-form, long-horizon counterfactual causal reasoning, containing 220 what-if questions across STEM, HSS, and Hybrid scenarios. To evaluate free-form responses, we further propose $\textbf{PRISM}$, which first converts each natural-language explanation into a Response-Derived Semantic Causal Graph of events, states, and mechanisms. On top of this graph, PRISM then jointly applies a Process Metric assessing graph-level causal validity and a Rubric Metric assessing answer-level explanatory adequacy. Evaluating six frontier LLMs with this framework, we find that WhatIfBench remains far from saturated: even the strongest model reaches only a 64.62% final score. Further analysis reveals persistent causal gaps, premise drift, and topology fragmentation, suggesting that fluent counterfactual narratives often mask fragile causal processes. The benchmark, code, and evaluation scripts are available at $\href{https://github.com/zju-gt/WhatIfBench}{WhatIfBench}$.

</details>


### [104] [When Teacher Guidance Misleads: Reward-Aligned On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27960)
*Siyuan Gan,Yuhan Li,Xiran Wang,Linjian Meng,Boyan Wang,Zhen Zhao,Jing Huo,Yang Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Reward-Aligned On-Policy Distillation (RA-OPD), a method that filters out teacher-student training trajectories whose distillation signals disagree with outcome rewards, leading to more reliable knowledge transfer and better performance on math and code benchmarks than standard OPD variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-policy distillation uses a strong teacher to guide a student model on trajectories generated by the student itself. However, the teacher’s token-level preferences along these prefixes are not always aligned with actual task success (outcome reward). This misalignment can push the student away from correct solutions, corrupting training signals and hurting performance, especially in settings like math and code where correctness is sharply defined. The paper aims to make OPD more robust by ensuring that only teacher signals that are consistent with outcome rewards are used for learning, without incurring extra computational cost.

Method: The authors introduce Reward-Aligned On-Policy Distillation (RA-OPD). For each sampled student trajectory, they compute a trajectory-level ‘distillation return’ that summarizes the teacher’s guidance along the trajectory, and compare it with the trajectory’s outcome reward (e.g., correctness of the final answer). A consistency check determines whether the teacher’s guidance pushes the student toward correct behavior or away from incorrect behavior. Trajectories whose distillation return is misaligned with the outcome reward are filtered out and excluded from gradient updates. This selective filtering is applied within a standard OPD pipeline, so no extra forward passes or environment interactions are required.

Result: On models from the Qwen3 and DeepSeek-R1 families, RA-OPD is evaluated across seven math benchmarks and three code benchmarks. In all reported settings, RA-OPD achieves higher accuracy than standard OPD and several alternative OPD variants. The improvements are described as significant and consistent across benchmarks, demonstrating that filtering reward-misaligned trajectories yields better student performance.

Conclusion: Reward-misaligned teacher guidance is a key failure mode of on-policy distillation for LLMs. By enforcing agreement between a trajectory’s distillation return and its outcome reward, RA-OPD removes misleading training signals while maintaining computational efficiency. This leads to more reliable knowledge transfer from teacher to student and better performance on math and code tasks. The approach suggests that incorporating simple reward-consistency checks into distillation can substantially improve post-training of LLMs.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) has recently emerged as a popular post-training paradigm for large language models (LLMs), providing an efficient way to transfer the knowledge and capabilities of teacher models into student models. However, teacher guidance on student-generated prefixes is not always reliable. Training should optimize the model to generate responses that are more likely to be correct, or equivalently, to get higher outcome rewards. But during OPD, the teacher model may provide guidance that discourages the student from moving toward correct trajectories or moves the student toward incorrect ones, which is misaligned with outcome reward. Such misaligned guidance is unreliable, as it would mislead the optimization process and ultimately degrade model performance. To mitigate misaligned teacher guidance, we propose Reward-Aligned On-Policy Distillation (RA-OPD). The key insight is to keep only trajectories whose induced updates move the student toward correct trajectories or discourage the student from moving toward incorrect ones. Specifically, for each sampled trajectory, RA-OPD checks whether its trajectory-level distillation return is consistent with its outcome reward and then filters out the misaligned trajectories. RA-OPD selects more reliable trajectories to improve student model performance without requiring additional computational cost. We evaluate RA-OPD on math and code benchmarks using models from the Qwen3 family and the DeepSeek-R1 family. Across seven math benchmarks and three code benchmarks, RA-OPD significantly outperforms standard OPD and other tested OPD variants.

</details>


### [105] [SABER: Stability-Aware Early Exit for LLM Reasoning via Adversarial Branch Probing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27963)
*Wanli Cheng,Haiya Xiang,Juntao Li,Hongling Wang,Wenliang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SABER, a training-free early-exit framework for Large Reasoning Models that detects when further reasoning is unlikely to change the final answer, cutting computation by about one-third while preserving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-chain reasoning with Large Reasoning Models is expensive and often wasteful once the intermediate answer has stabilized. Existing early-exit methods based on confidence or entropy do not capture whether the reasoning has actually stabilized, and consistency-based methods require multiple sequential rollouts, which themselves are costly. A more efficient and reliable way to detect reasoning stability is needed.

Method: The authors propose SABER, a training-free, stability-aware early-exit framework. At intermediate reasoning states, SABER creates simple semantic perturbations to generate adversarial branches of the reasoning process. Instead of fully rolling out these branches, it uses lightweight probing to estimate their eventual final answers. If the estimated outcomes across branches are consistent, SABER decides that the reasoning is stable and exits early; if not, it continues the reasoning process.

Result: Across multiple reasoning benchmarks and model architectures, SABER reduces the number of reasoning tokens generated by about 30.2%–39.8% on average, while maintaining accuracy that is competitive with full, non-early-exited reasoning runs.

Conclusion: Adversarial branch probing provides an effective signal of reasoning stability, enabling SABER to perform training-free early exit that significantly cuts computation with minimal accuracy loss. This approach offers a practical way to improve the efficiency of Large Reasoning Models during long-chain reasoning without requiring model retraining.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) achieve strong reasoning capabilities, yet long-chain reasoning becomes inefficient once the intermediate answer stabilizes across reasoning steps: additional reasoning yields little marginal benefit while incurring substantial inference cost. Existing early-exit methods based on confidence or entropy poorly capture reasoning stability, while consistency-based approaches rely on multi-step trajectory agreement, requiring sequential evaluations that delay exit. To better balance efficiency and reliability, we propose SABER, a training-free framework for stability-aware early exit via adversarial branch probing. SABER constructs simple yet effective semantic perturbations around intermediate reasoning states to form adversarial branches, and applies lightweight probing to estimate their likely final outcomes without full trajectory rollouts. When the probed outcomes remain consistent across branches, SABER exits early; otherwise, it continues reasoning. Experiments across multiple reasoning benchmarks and model architectures show that SABER reduces reasoning token consumption by 30.2\%--39.8\% on average while maintaining competitive accuracy with full-length reasoning.

</details>


### [106] [AERA: Adaptive Evidence Residual Allocation for Efficient Test-Time Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27964)
*Ziming Wang,Ivor Tsang,Hangwei Qian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes AERA, an adaptive controller that decides when to stop or continue test-time scaling for language-model reasoning, dramatically cutting computation while maintaining accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test-time scaling improves reasoning by generating many candidate solutions, but using a fixed, large inference budget for every question is wasteful. Existing adaptive stopping criteria assume that higher current confidence implies less need for further computation. The authors observe that this assumption can fail because the correctness of intermediate checkpoints is non-monotonic: answers can look increasingly confident before collapsing, or look worse before later recovery. This mismatch motivates a more principled way to decide whether additional computation is likely to yield a better answer.

Method: The authors introduce Adaptive Evidence Residual Allocation (AERA), a learned sequential controller that observes partial generation checkpoints and predicts whether allocating more computation is likely to improve the final answer. AERA repeatedly makes stop/continue decisions based on rich features of the cumulative response prefix, including (1) answer-distribution features capturing diversity and consensus among candidates, (2) temporal features tracking dynamics over checkpoints, (3) re-solving features indicating how re-attempts evolve, (4) semantic features about the content of the partial solutions, and (5) compute features summarizing used vs remaining budget. Future checkpoint correctness is used only offline to supervise the controller; at inference time, AERA has access only to checkpoint-observable evidence and operates in a frozen-threshold, incremental-generation regime.

Result: On GSM8K and GPQA Diamond benchmarks, AERA effectively identifies per-question residual opportunities for improvement and substantially trims inference cost. In a held-out evaluation on 300 new GSM8K questions with incremental generation and a fixed decision threshold, AERA reaches 92.61% accuracy compared to 93.01% using a full 128-response budget, while cutting completion tokens by 95.99%. This shows that the method preserves almost all of the accuracy benefits of heavy test-time scaling at a fraction of the computational expense.

Conclusion: Adaptive reasoning for language models should explicitly estimate the expected future value of additional computation rather than treating current confidence, agreement, or stability as proxies for correctness. By learning from offline supervision based on future checkpoint correctness and relying only on observable features at inference time, AERA can adaptively stop or continue generation on a per-question basis. The approach yields large compute savings with negligible loss in accuracy, advancing the practicality of test-time scaling for reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Test-time scaling improves language-model reasoning by generating additional candidate solutions, but allocating the same inference budget to every problem is computationally wasteful. Existing adaptive stopping methods commonly rely on confidence, agreement, or answer stability, implicitly assuming that stronger current evidence indicates that further computation is unnecessary. We show that this assumption can fail: checkpoint-level correctness evolves non-monotonically, and observable evidence may strengthen before an answer collapses or weaken before it recovers. Motivated by this mismatch, we introduce Adaptive Evidence Residual Allocation (AERA), a sequential controller that learns whether additional computation is likely to recover a better answer from checkpoint-observable evidence. AERA characterizes cumulative response prefixes using answer-distribution, temporal, re-solving, semantic, and compute features, and repeatedly decides whether to stop or allocate the next response block. Future checkpoint correctness is used only to construct offline supervision and is never available to the controller at inference time. Across GSM8K and GPQA Diamond, AERA identifies question-specific residual opportunities while substantially reducing inference computation. In a frozen-threshold incremental-generation evaluation on 300 untouched GSM8K questions, AERA achieves 92.61% accuracy versus 93.01% with 128 responses while reducing completion tokens by 95.99%. These results suggest that adaptive reasoning should estimate the future value of computation rather than equating present confidence with correctness.

</details>


### [107] [openJiuwen: Beyond Static Harnesses for Long-Horizon Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27969)
*openJiuwen Team,Tao Yu,Xinyu Zhang,Qianqian Chen,Xiaoneng Xiang,Chia Kwangyang,Xingchen Huang,Ran Chen,Yangkai Ding,Zheng Wang,Yeo Boon Hong,Bingzheng Gan,Enrui Hu,Shuo Cheng,Deyang Li,Ruifeng Shi,Hongbo Wang,Qi Ye,Xuefeng Jin,Zhangchun Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces openJiuwen, an open-source agent harness for long-horizon coding agents that emphasizes structural composability and runtime adaptivity, and demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on complex coding benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As coding agents tackle long-horizon software engineering tasks, they must operate over evolving repositories, coordinate multiple heterogeneous tools and agents, and adapt to dynamic signals such as diagnostics and execution feedback. Existing systems make it hard for developers to flexibly compose and reconfigure these complex multi-agent setups without repeatedly rebuilding orchestration logic, and they typically lack mechanisms to let new runtime evidence meaningfully and systematically affect subsequent decisions. This creates a need for a unified harness that simplifies capability composition while enabling adaptive control over the task as it progresses.

Method: The authors design and implement openJiuwen, an open-source harness that provides: (1) a shared execution substrate with unified semantics across single agents, delegated sub-agents, and Swarm Flow (multi-agent) configurations; and (2) Rail-based capability composition, allowing developers to plug together and reconfigure tools and agents without changing core orchestration. On top of a fixed model policy, openJiuwen introduces a framework-level mechanism for runtime adaptivity, where evolving evidence (semantic diagnostics, execution outcomes, task progress, context relevance) can dynamically adjust context selection, feedback routing, and control flow of the task. The system is evaluated on standard complex coding benchmarks SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.1.

Result: On SWE-bench Verified, openJiuwen attains an accuracy of 82.6%, and on Terminal-Bench 2.1 it reaches 87.19%. These scores exceed the strongest reported official-leaderboard point estimates selected by the authors by 3.4 and 3.39 percentage points, respectively, indicating that the harness delivers competitive or superior performance compared with prior systems on challenging long-horizon coding tasks.

Conclusion: openJiuwen demonstrates that a carefully designed harness emphasizing structural composability and runtime adaptivity can achieve strong empirical performance on complex software engineering benchmarks. By offering a shared execution substrate and Rail-based composition for single and multi-agent flows, along with adaptive runtime control over a fixed model policy, the framework enables developers to more easily build, scale, and experiment with sophisticated coding agents that effectively leverage evolving runtime evidence.

Abstract: Long-horizon coding agents operate over evolving repository states while increasingly relying on heterogeneous capabilities, delegated agents, and multi-agent coordination. These trends pose two complementary challenges for the agent harness. First, developers need to compose capabilities, reconfigure execution logic, and scale increasingly complex agent systems without repeatedly rebuilding orchestration. Second, complex coding tasks continuously produce new evidence---such as semantic diagnostics, execution outcomes, task progress, and changing context relevance---that should dynamically influence subsequent runtime decisions. We characterize these challenges as Structural Composability and Runtime Adaptivity. We present openJiuwen, an open-source harness designed for both developer composability and adaptive task execution. openJiuwen provides a shared execution substrate and Rail-based capability composition across single agents, delegated sub-agents, and Swarm Flow, enabling developers to construct sophisticated agent harnesses under common execution semantics. It further adapts framework-controlled runtime decisions around a fixed model policy, allowing evolving evidence to dynamically affect context, feedback, and task control toward successful completion. We systematically evaluate openJiuwen on SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.1, where it achieves 82.6% and 87.19%, respectively, exceeding the strongest selected official-leaderboard point estimates by 3.4 and 3.39 percentage points. These results show that openJiuwen achieves strong performance on complex coding tasks while providing a composable and adaptive harness design.

</details>


### [108] [Learning from Hard Prompts: Difficulty-aware Advantage Amplification in Dynamic Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27982)
*Siyuan Gan,Yuhan Li,Xiran Wang,Linjian Meng,Boyan Wang,Zhen Zhao,Jing Huo,Lei Bai,Yang Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies a training-efficiency flaw in Dynamic Sampling within DAPO/GRPO, then proposes Direct Advantage Amplification (DAA), yielding DA3PO, which better leverages hard but correct responses and improves performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While DAPO (a GRPO variant) improves accuracy via Dynamic Sampling, its way of filtering prompts introduces asymmetric advantage amplification: on difficult prompts, incorrect responses get more amplified than correct ones, wasting valuable hard-to-sample correct signals and lowering training efficiency. There is a need to retain DAPO’s stability/accuracy benefits while using hard correct responses more effectively.

Method: The authors theoretically analyze Dynamic Sampling in DAPO, showing it asymmetrically amplifies advantages for different responses to the same prompt, particularly penalizing hard correct responses on hard prompts. Based on this, they propose Direct Advantage Amplification (DAA), a simple modification that explicitly boosts the advantages of hard-to-sample correct responses identified by Dynamic Sampling. They integrate DAA into DAPO to form DA3PO, which requires fewer than 30 extra lines of code. They then empirically compare DA3PO against GRPO and several GRPO variants.

Result: Dynamic Sampling is shown to reduce training efficiency by over-emphasizing avoidance of observed incorrect responses rather than learning from rare correct ones on hard prompts. DA3PO, with DAA added to DAPO, significantly outperforms vanilla GRPO and other classical GRPO variants in experimental benchmarks, while preserving or improving training stability and accuracy.

Conclusion: Dynamic Sampling’s naive filtering strategy harms training efficiency by asymmetrically amplifying advantages, particularly underutilizing hard-to-sample correct responses. Direct Advantage Amplification (and the resulting DA3PO algorithm) corrects this by deliberately amplifying the advantages of those hard correct responses, leading to more efficient learning and superior empirical performance, with minimal implementation overhead.

Abstract: Decoupled Clip and Dynamic Sampling Policy Optimization (DAPO) is a prominent variant of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). DAPO introduces several improvements over GRPO. Among these, Dynamic Sampling contributes the most to DAPO's accuracy gains relative to GRPO. To improve accuracy, Dynamic Sampling enhances training stability by eliminating zero policy gradients from zero advantages. Specifically, it avoids such zero gradients by filtering out prompts where sampled responses are either entirely correct or incorrect. However, our theoretical analysis shows that Dynamic Sampling decrease training efficiency as it cannot effectively utilize hard-to-sample correct responses on hard prompts. Formally, it asymmetrically amplifies the advantages of distinct responses to the same prompts. On hard prompts, incorrect responses undergo greater amplification than correct ones. This leads the model to avoid generating the observed incorrect responses rather than capitalizing on the hard-to-sample correct ones on hard prompts, resulting in low training efficiency. To improve training efficiency, we propose Direct Advantage Amplification (DAA), which amplifies the advantages of hard-to-sample correct responses on hard prompts, as obtained by Dynamic Sampling. This ensures that, when Dynamic Sampling is used, these hard-to-sample responses can be effectively capitalized on, implying higher training efficiency. By integrating DAA into DAPO, we obtain Difficulty-aware Advantage Amplification Policy Optimization (DA3PO), which is implemented with fewer than 30 lines of code from DAPO. Experiments show that DA3PO significantly outperforms GRPO and other classical GRPO variants.

</details>


### [109] [When Evidence Shapes Collaboration: Knowledge-Conditioned Topology Generation for Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27984)
*Yangxiao Jiang,Jiarun Fan,Mingcong Xu,Yanxi Guo,Jiwen Feng,Shanqing Xu,Mengchen Qian,Wei Chen,Xiaojin Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework (K-GAT) that generates multi-agent collaboration topologies directly guided by external knowledge, improving accuracy and efficiency on knowledge-intensive tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent systems increasingly use dynamic collaboration graphs, but current topology generation mostly depends on LLM internal parameters, treating external retrieval or search only reactively. This causes a mismatch between the collaboration structure and the actual knowledge needed, leading to redundant communication or weak verification, especially on knowledge-intensive problems. A method is needed to align agent collaboration structures explicitly with external evidence.

Method: They introduce K-GAT, a neuro-symbolic framework that casts multi-agent topology design as a knowledge-conditioned structure learning problem. Concretely, they perform autoregressive graph generation where the structure of agent interactions is directly conditioned on external knowledge or evidence, instead of relying solely on latent LLM knowledge. Symbolic aspects (knowledge/evidence) guide the neural graph generator to decide which agents are involved, how they connect, and how information flows.

Result: On multiple knowledge-intensive benchmarks, K-GAT is both more accurate and more efficient than baselines. On the expert-level GPQA dataset, it surpasses the LLM-Debate baseline by +15.7 percentage points in accuracy while using less than half the computational tokens, indicating better use of resources and more targeted collaboration.

Conclusion: Explicitly integrating external knowledge into the process of generating multi-agent collaboration topologies reduces structure-knowledge misalignment. K-GAT shows that neuro-symbolic, knowledge-guided graph generation can significantly improve the performance and token efficiency of multi-agent LLM systems on challenging, knowledge-heavy tasks compared to debate-style or purely parametric approaches.

Abstract: Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) have recently moved from static workflows toward dynamically generated collaboration topologies. However, existing topology generation methods rely primarily on the parametric knowledge of large language models, with external search or retrieval used only as a reactive tool rather than an explicit determinant of collaboration structure. This leads to structure-knowledge misalignment, where systems exhibit redundant interactions or insufficient verification in knowledge-intensive tasks. We propose K-GAT (Knowledge-Guided Agent Topology Generator), a neuro-symbolic framework that formulates collaboration topology design as a knowledge-conditioned structure learning problem, integrating external evidence directly into autoregressive graph generation. Extensive experiments on knowledge-intensive benchmarks demonstrate K-GAT's efficiency and effectiveness: notably on the expert-level GPQA dataset, K-GAT outperforms the LLM-Debate baseline by a substantial margin of +15.7% in accuracy, while consuming less than half the computational tokens.

</details>


### [110] [GOD: Govern, Observe, and Direct - A Real-Time Control Room for Agent Societies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27992)
*Yige Luo,Ran Guan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents GOD, a local-first control room that makes it easier to inspect, intervene in, and share generative multi-agent runs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generative-agent systems are easy to launch but hard to inspect or debug. Operators typically only see an end-to-end replay or raw logs, which makes it difficult to understand why agents behaved a certain way, perform precise interventions, or package experiments for other researchers.

Method: The authors design and implement GOD, a browser-based system that unifies inspection and control for agent societies. It includes a setup wizard, an Agent Studio and Map Studio for configuring agents and environments, a spatial replay interface, and Ask/Intervene commands for targeted queries and manipulations. They introduce a command-and-artifact loop where live controls and replay evidence share a common command model, and where package contracts cleanly separate scenario, map, and profile data from local runtime state. They release hosted replays, code, and downloadable packs, and evaluate the system on 15 completed run slots with intervention runs that test how well the system records and reports state and commands.

Result: In 14 intervention runs, the system correctly recorded the commanded destination in 78 of 84 target-agent checks, and 169 of 182 queried state answers matched a previously saved location or action string. They also demonstrate hosted replays, an open-source implementation, and portable experiment/map/agent packs as proof of deployability and sharing.

Conclusion: GOD provides a practical, local-first control room for generative-agent societies that makes it easier to inspect behaviors, perform targeted interventions, and package runs for reuse. The command-and-artifact loop and packaging contracts enable consistent control and replay, and the evaluation suggests the system reliably tracks and surfaces agent states and commanded actions.

Abstract: Generative-agent systems are easier to start than to inspect. A run can contain many agents, locations, messages, commands, and model calls, yet the operator often gets either a finished replay or raw logs. That makes it hard to ask why an agent moved, test a small intervention, or package a run for another researcher. GOD is a local-first control room for agent societies. From the same browser workflow, an operator can issue targeted questions or interventions and inspect the resulting replay state. The system combines a setup wizard, Agent Studio, Map Studio, a spatial replay interface, Ask and Intervene commands, and portable experiment, map, and agent packs. Its technical contribution is the command and artifact loop: live controls and replay evidence share the same operator command model, while package contracts separate scenario, map, and profile data from local runtime state. The public release includes hosted Smallville-style and PKU replays, the open-source repository, and downloadable packs. We evaluate this path on 15 completed run slots. Across the 14 intervention runs, 78 of 84 target-agent checks recorded the commanded destination, and 169 of 182 state answers matched a saved location or action string.

</details>


### [111] [Should I Use This Synthetic Dataset for Training? How to Test with Minimal Real Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27996)
*Zhenyu Tao,Wei Xu,Xiaohu You,Petar Popovski,Osvaldo Simeone*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paper proposes a statistical test to decide whether adding synthetic data (from digital twins or learned world models) actually improves real-world performance of AI models, while using very few real test samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Synthetic data from digital twins and learned world models is widely used to augment scarce real data for training AI in engineering systems, but due to the sim-to-real gap it is unclear, for any given synthetic dataset and learning pipeline, whether augmentation truly helps on the real distribution. Practitioners need a principled, data-efficient way to decide whether to trust and use a given synthetic dataset.

Method: They formulate the problem as hypothesis testing on performance differences between a model trained only on real data and a model trained on real+synthetic data, given a fixed learning algorithm. They study two tests: (1) a direct test on the mean loss difference and (2) a symmetry-based paired loss-difference test that assumes exchangeability/symmetry to gain efficiency. For the second, they develop the adaptive e-process sign-flip test (aeSFT): a sequential, anytime-valid e-value-based procedure that uses Monte Carlo sign flips of paired loss differences. aeSFT adaptively chooses both how many sign-flip samples to run (controlling computation) and how many real test points to draw (controlling data usage), while guaranteeing Type-I error control without pre-fixing the test-set size.

Result: aeSFT is proven to provide anytime-valid control of Type-I error for the symmetry-based null. In experiments on three tasks—a synthetic classification problem, a digital-twin-supported wireless packet-scheduling problem, and a radio-map prediction problem—aeSFT detects when synthetic data is beneficial using substantially fewer real test samples than mean-based sequential tests. Its statistical power closely matches that of fixed-sample sign-flip tests and the classical paired t-test, while empirically keeping the false-positive rate under the nominal level.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the proposed aeSFT is an efficient and reliable tool for deciding whether a candidate synthetic dataset from a digital twin or world model genuinely improves real-world performance of a learning system. By providing anytime-valid Type-I error control and adaptivity in both computation and test-data consumption, aeSFT allows practitioners to vet synthetic data with significantly fewer real test samples, without sacrificing statistical power compared to standard fixed-sample tests.

Abstract: Digital twins (DTs) and learned world models are increasingly used to generate synthetic data that augment the scarce real datasets available for training artificial intelligence (AI) models in engineering systems. Owing to the inevitable simulation-to-reality (sim-to-real) gap, however, augmentation may fail to improve the performance of the trained model on the real data distribution. This paper addresses the resulting decision problem: Given a real dataset, a candidate synthetic dataset, and a fixed learning algorithm, decide whether training on the augmented dataset improves the true, population-level performance, while consuming as few real test data points as possible. Two formulations are considered: a direct test on the mean loss difference between the two trained models, and a symmetry-based test on the paired loss difference, which trades a stronger null assumption for faster evidence accumulation. For the latter, we introduce the {adaptive e-process sign-flip test} (aeSFT), a doubly adaptive procedure that adapts both the number of Monte Carlo sign-flip rounds, and hence the computational cost, and the amount of real test data consumed. aeSFT yields anytime-valid Type-I error control, with no need to pre-specify the test-set size. Experiments on a synthetic-data classification task, a DT-aided wireless packet-scheduling task, and a radio-map prediction task show that aeSFT identifies useful synthetic data using substantially fewer real test samples than mean-based sequential testing, matches the power of fixed-sample sign-flip testing and the paired $t$-test, while keeping the false-positive rate below the target level.

</details>


### [112] [Automated Analysis Framework for Multilingual Climate-Health Literature Based on Multi-Agent Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27998)
*Yuze Sun,Shihui Zhang,Jiancheng Pan,Yunjia Ye,Wentao Luo,Jiahao Li,Quan Zhang,Wenjia Cai,Xiaomeng Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build and test a multi‑agent LLM framework that automatically screens, extracts, and standardizes information from large-scale multilingual (Chinese–English) climate–health literature, achieving high accuracy and generating structured city–paper links.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ways of analyzing scientific literature—manual reading or single algorithms—do not scale to today’s huge, interdisciplinary, multilingual corpora and often fail to adapt to specific domains like climate–health. Researchers in climate–health need reliable, large-scale, structured evidence (e.g., which cities, which climate factors, which health outcomes) from many papers, but current tools are inefficient, brittle across languages, and hard to trust. The paper is motivated by this gap: to create an efficient, scalable, domain-adaptable and trustworthy automated analysis pipeline for multilingual scientific literature, focusing on climate–health as an exemplar field.

Method: They design a multi-agent framework built around a central coordination module and three specialized LLM-based agents: (1) a document evaluation agent that screens and judges the relevance/quality of papers; (2) an information extraction agent that pulls out structured core fields from full texts (e.g., locations, climate variables, health outcomes); and (3) an analytical review agent that synthesizes and standardizes extracted information. The system mimics domain experts’ stepwise reasoning. To improve reliability, they implement a four-layer hallucination control strategy (multiple safeguards within prompts, cross-checking across agents, consistency checks, etc.) plus a final manual verification stage. They validate the framework on a large bilingual Chinese–English corpus of 32,642 climate–health papers (China, 1993–2023), measuring extraction performance (F1) and generating structured outputs such as city–literature association pairs.

Result: On the Chinese–English climate–health corpus, the framework achieves an F1 score of 0.92 for extracting core information, indicating high precision and recall. It also successfully extracts and standardizes 2,012 city–literature association pairs, turning unstructured text into structured data usable for downstream analysis. The system processes the full corpus end-to-end, demonstrating scalability and feasibility for large-scale multilingual literature analysis.

Conclusion: A multi-agent LLM-based framework can automate the full pipeline of multilingual literature analysis—screening, extraction, and integration—with high accuracy and scalability in a complex interdisciplinary domain like climate–health. The combination of specialized agents, central coordination, and layered hallucination control plus manual checks yields reliable structured data from tens of thousands of papers. This offers a practical technical solution for large-scale evidence mining in climate–health and is likely generalizable to other interdisciplinary, multilingual research areas that require systematic literature analysis.

Abstract: The rapid proliferation of interdisciplinary and multilingual scientific literature has left traditional manual analysis and single-algorithm methods plagued by low efficiency, poor scalability, and insufficient domain adaptability. Targeting the literature analysis needs of the typical interdisciplinary climate-health field, this study proposes a multi-agent large language model automated analysis framework for multilingual scientific literature, which realizes full-process automation covering literature screening, structured information extraction, and standardized integration. With a central coordination module as the core, the framework deploys three dedicated agents for document evaluation, information extraction, and analytical review to mimic the literature analysis thinking of domain experts, and adopts a four-layer hallucination control strategy together with a manual verification procedure to ensure the accuracy and reliability of analytical outcomes. Validated on a bilingual Chinese-English corpus of 32,642 climate-health papers covering China from 1993 to 2023, the framework achieves an F1 score of 0.92 in core information extraction, and completes the extraction and standardization of 2,012 city-literature association pairs, offering effective technical support for large-scale evidence mining in the climate-health research domain.

</details>


### [113] [PhenoIntel: A Lifecycle-Aligned Multi-Agent Web Application for Verified, Accessible Plant Phenotype Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27999)
*Narendren S,Soumyashree Kar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PhenoIntel is a browser-based, multi-agent, end-to-end plant phenotyping platform that enforces reliability, uncertainty-aware predictions, and full provenance across the ML lifecycle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current conversational plant-phenotyping systems are hard for plant scientists to use and scientifically unreliable: they treat failed analyses as valid data, ignore statistical assumptions, omit uncertainty quantification, and often depend on specialised hardware, making rigorous and reproducible phenotyping difficult.

Method: The authors design PhenoIntel as a lifecycle-aligned, web-based, multi-agent system where nine specialised agents handle distinct stages of the ML phenotyping workflow—image collection, model selection, inference, statistics, and reporting—communicating through a shared, fixed-structure record. Each stage has independent validation to catch inconsistencies early. Uncertainty estimation is tailored to model families (e.g., conformal prediction, confidence-spread for detection, Monte Carlo Dropout). Quality thresholds are task- and crop-specific. If no suitable model exists, the system can recommend, validate, and incorporate a new model. A model repository supports 10 models across 5 crops and 4 imaging modalities, and a 1,200-test automated suite validates end-to-end operation.

Result: Classification models on the platform achieve Macro F1 scores between 0.78 and 0.996; object-detection models reach 0.96 mAP@50 and reduce counting error by 54% relative to an unoptimized baseline; a temporal model achieves held-out Macro F1 of 0.7050. The complete pipeline, including uncertainty estimates and statistical checks, runs in a browser on standard hardware without a GPU, validated by a 1,200-test automated suite.

Conclusion: PhenoIntel delivers a reliable, user-friendly, and hardware-accessible conversational phenotyping platform that integrates rigorous uncertainty quantification, assumption-checked statistics, and FAIR-compliant provenance, surpassing existing tools that lack these combined guarantees across the full ML workflow.

Abstract: Existing conversational plant-phenotyping platforms are difficult for plant scientists to use and lack the reliability scientific research demands: failed analyses are reported as valid measurements rather than flagged as missing, statistical tests run without checking assumptions, predictions carry no uncertainty estimate, and specialised hardware limits accessibility. We present PhenoIntel, a lifecycle-aligned multi-agent web platform that turns the full machine-learning workflow into a reliable, user-friendly phenotyping system. Nine specialised agents divide the analysis into stages, from image collection through model selection, inference, and reporting, rather than handing the whole task to one AI manager. Independent checks separate these stages, and every agent reads from and writes to one shared, fixed-structure record, so an inconsistent output from one stage is caught before it reaches the next. Uncertainty is matched to each model family, conformal prediction, detection-confidence spread, or Monte Carlo Dropout, rather than applied uniformly, and quality thresholds adapt to crop and task instead of one global cutoff. When no suitable model exists, PhenoIntel can propose, validate, and integrate a new one on its own. The model repository spans ten trained models across five crops and four imaging modalities. Classification models reach Macro F1 of 0.78-0.996; object-detection models reach 0.96 mAP@50 with a 54% reduction in counting error over an unoptimised baseline; and a temporal model reaches held-out Macro F1 of 0.7050. PhenoIntel runs in a browser on standard hardware, requiring no GPU, and a 1,200-test automated suite confirms complete pipeline execution. Every result carries calibrated uncertainty, validated statistics, and FAIR-compliant provenance, a combination existing conversational phenotyping tools do not offer.

</details>


### [114] [Coverage, Not Credit: Failure-Credit Routing of Zeroth-Order Perturbation Budgets Does Not Improve On-Pool Sample Efficiency for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28011)
*Yuxu Ge*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Study tests whether using trajectory-level credit assignment to route zeroth-order (ZO/ES) perturbation budget across modules of tool-using LLM agents improves optimization vs uniform allocation; finds essentially no reliable benefit and sometimes harm, with one small generalization exception.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool-using LLM agents are modular and can fail due to specific components (e.g., caller vs tool/synthesizer). Trajectory-level credit assignment can identify which module is responsible for failures using only verifiable signals. A natural hypothesis is that this credit signal can be used to allocate more ZO/ES optimization effort (perturbation budget) to the failing modules to improve sample efficiency and performance. However, it is unclear whether such routing actually helps compared with simply distributing optimization uniformly, especially in frozen LLM settings where subtle experimental pathologies can arise.

Method: The authors freeze Qwen2.5-1.5B, Qwen2.5-3B, and SmolLM2-1.7B agents in a tool-using setting and optimize them with zeroth-order/evolution-strategies methods. They construct a synthetic environment and three BFCL-derived task families and compare six different perturbation-budget allocation schemes, including uniform, joint soft-plus-sigma routing using trajectory-level credit, and concentrating all budget on the credit argmax module.
