They run credit-noise sweeps, use paired random seeds, and perform exact sign-flip tests to assess statistically detectable gains (≥2 percentage points AUC) over uniform. They also test inverse-propensity debiasing, examine the effect of misrouting (wrong module favored), scan six fixed-step schedules to study how performance degrades as the identified bottleneck module is starved of steps, and compare burst vs step-compensating catch-up schedules to separate cumulative parameter movement from update frequency effects. The primary estimand is optimization efficiency on a fixed task pool, with additional evaluation on unseen BFCL functions/endpoints.

Result: Across all agents, task families, routing schemes, and noise settings, the authors find no statistically detectable improvement of credit-based routing over uniform perturbation allocation on the main on-pool benchmarks (no gain ≥ 2 percentage points AUC). The joint soft-plus-sigma routing scheme is effectively identical to uniform within ±0.02 AUC on both 1.5B and 3B models. Concentrating the entire budget on the credit argmax module is roughly equivalent to uniform for the 1.5B model—where that module is indeed the verified bottleneck—but significantly worse for the 3B model. Inverse-propensity debiasing fails to recover any routing benefit. Misrouting can be costly, reducing AUC by up to −0.074 in-house and −0.118 end-to-end on the BFCL-derived family. For six fixed-step schedules, performance loss scales linearly with the rate at which the bottleneck module is starved of optimization steps (R^2 = 0.94, descriptive). Introducing a preregistered credit-free “coverage floor” that guarantees minimum updates to all modules eliminates the observed harms. Burst and step-compensating catch-up schedules, matched on total budget, suggest that harms stem from insufficient cumulative parameter movement rather than update frequency per se. On unseen BFCL functions, the single exception is that a soft routing scheme outperforms uniform on held-out endpoints by +0.047 AUC (p = 0.031, n = 6).

Conclusion: Trajectory-level credit assignment, as instantiated in this study, does not reliably improve zeroth-order optimization efficiency of modular, tool-using LLM agents when used to route a fixed perturbation budget across modules. Uniform allocation is robust and at least as effective in almost all examined settings, while naive or miscalibrated routing can be harmful, particularly when it starves true bottleneck modules of cumulative updates. Ensuring a minimum coverage floor over modules mitigates these harms. The single observed generalization benefit of soft routing on held-out BFCL endpoints suggests a plausible—but untested—interpretation: routing may preferentially improve the caller component in ways that generalize, whereas uniform’s apparent on-pool gains might overfit to synthesizer behavior specific to the evaluation harness. The authors emphasize the need for care in ZO/ES experiments on frozen LLMs and document three subtle failure modes that can silently invalidate such studies, underscoring that experimental artifacts can easily masquerade as routing benefits.

Abstract: Trajectory-level credit assignment can localize which module of a tool-using LLM agent causes failures using only verifiable signals. We ask whether such failure credit should route a fixed zeroth-order/evolution-strategies (ZO/ES) perturbation budget. Across a synthetic environment and frozen Qwen2.5-1.5B/3B and SmolLM2-1.7B agents, three task families, six allocation schemes, a credit-noise sweep, paired seeds, and exact sign-flip tests, we find no statistically detectable improvement over uniform allocation in any on-pool comparison (no gain of at least 2 percentage points). The joint soft-plus-sigma scheme is equivalent to uniform within a +/- 0.02 AUC margin on 1.5B and 3B; concentrating the full budget on the credit argmax is marginally equivalent on 1.5B, where that module is the verified bottleneck, and significantly worse on 3B. Inverse-propensity debiasing does not rescue routing, and misrouting costs up to -0.074 AUC in-house and -0.118 end-to-end on the BFCL-derived family. Across six fixed-step schedules, loss is linear in bottleneck starvation rate (R^2 = 0.94, descriptive), and a preregistered credit-free coverage floor removes detected harm. Matched-budget burst and step-compensating catch-up schedules are consistent with harm arising from insufficient cumulative parameter movement rather than update frequency. Our primary estimand is optimization efficiency on a fixed task pool. On unseen BFCL functions, the study's one exception is that soft routing exceeds uniform on held-out endpoints (+0.047, p = 0.031, n = 6). A plausible but untested reading is that routing-favored caller improvements transfer while uniform's on-pool gains reflect a synthesizer behavior specific to our harness. We report this exception explicitly and document three failure modes that can silently invalidate ZO/ES experiments on frozen LLMs.

</details>


### [115] [String: An Agentic OS Where Every App Is a Markdown File](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28027)
*Jookyung Song,Nojun Kwak,Simyung Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces String, an open-source runtime and interface layer that exposes tools and applications to LLM agents via a concise markdown-based OS-like abstraction, improving accuracy and token efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing web pages, APIs, and tool schemas are designed for humans or traditional programs, not for LLM agents that must repeatedly parse large contexts and pay token costs each turn. Agents waste tokens re-reading tool descriptions and web content, suffer from poorly staged information, and face security risks when mixing tools, secrets, and untrusted inputs. There is a need for a dedicated interface/runtime that treats LLM agents as first-class users, optimizing both usability and safety.

Method: The authors design and implement String, a runtime that defines applications using a single String-Flavored Markdown (SFMD) document. This document specifies views, typed actions (tools), navigation structure, and credentials. The runtime exposes only partial, staged views per turn via two core commands (/open for reading views and /act for executing actions), centralizes tool knowledge outside the LLM context, enforces provenance-based privilege separation (e.g., limiting which calls can reach shell vs HTTP), and prevents caller text from expanding stored secrets. They operationalize curated task procedures as on-demand String apps and run a benchmark of 87 tasks across six LLMs, measuring accuracy, wrong-action selection, token usage, and interface size.

Result: Experiments show that revealing detailed information too early can reduce task accuracy by up to 23 percentage points, while String’s staged, causal disclosure reduces wrong-action selection from 28% to 2%. On an 87-task benchmark with curated skills implemented as String apps, models using String achieve similar or slightly better aggregate task success (+1.3 percentage points) compared to baselines, while using 33.5% fewer tokens for completed episodes. The resident interface footprint remains small and constant (53 tokens) regardless of catalog size.

Conclusion: Treating LLM-agent interaction as an operating-systems problem and giving agents a dedicated, markdown-based interface/runtime can significantly reduce token overhead, improve action selection accuracy, and enhance security. String demonstrates that web and app interactions can be unified through a single SFMD grammar that supports both human and agent users without per-site integration, making LLM agents more efficient and reliable in real-world, multi-tool environments.

Abstract: LLM agents have become a new class of software user, but every surface they work through was designed for someone else. Pages are built for human eyes, which can skim and ignore; tool schemas for programs, which pay nothing to carry definitions they never call. An agent has neither luxury: it re-reads, and pays again for, everything it is shown on every turn. We present String, an open-source runtime that gives this user an interface of its own and treats the job as an operating-systems problem. Tool knowledge moves out of the agent's context and into a common layer that renders it back one view at a time as Markdown. A single SFMD (String-Flavored Markdown) document declares an application's views, typed actions, navigation, and credentials, and the runtime handles discovery, validation, execution, state, and secrets behind two core verbs: /open to see and /act to do. Web and app turn out to be two renderings of one architecture: an SFMD site serves styled HTML to browsers and the raw document to agents, so one grammar reaches apps, files, shells, and the web, even legacy HTML, with no per-site integration. Views stay partial by design, and the staging is causal: disclosing one tier of detail a single turn too early costs up to 23 accuracy points, while proper staging drops wrong-action selection from 28% to 2%. Privilege follows provenance: a remote page may call HTTP but never the shell, and caller-supplied text never expands a stored secret. On an 87-task benchmark that pairs each task with curated skills, operationalizing those procedures as on-demand String apps yields comparable aggregate success across six models from frontier to small (+1.3pp) while using 33.5% fewer tokens among completed episodes, and the resident interface stays a constant 53 tokens at any catalog size. We report the design, the evaluation, and what three months of production use taught us.

</details>


### [116] [WeAgent-MMSearch: Native Text-Vision Interaction for Multimodal Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28062)
*Zongkai Liu,Hui Zhang,Liqiang Niu,Zhen Cao,Han Li,Juntao Liu,Wenchao Chen,Chengduo Zhao,Chao Yu,Fandong Meng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WeAgent-Harness is a multimodal agent framework that keeps images in-context and can recover from failed trajectories, enabling better multimodal search agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal search agents typically degrade retrieved images into text-only descriptions and lack robust handling for long-horizon failures (timeouts, budget overruns, partial tool failures), which wastes good partial trajectories and weakens learning. There is a need for an agentic environment that natively supports vision-text interaction across long rollouts and can systematically salvage and use imperfect trajectories.

Method: 1) Propose WeAgent-Harness, an agentic framework where images returned by tools are stored as persistent disk references so the model can repeatedly inspect and cite them across steps, with built-in runtime recovery from abnormal tool/rollout failures.
2) Build WeAgent-MMSearch, an end-to-end system (data construction, post-training, rollout) that uses this harness: a strong MLLM is used to automatically construct MMSearch-style tasks and expert trajectories.
3) Propose Failure-Aware GSPO (FA-GSPO), a training strategy that detects abnormal rollouts, recovers the salvageable parts, and discards invalid ones, improving learning for bounded multimodal planning and search.
4) Introduce VisTarget-Bench, a human-verified benchmark where every question is paired with a hidden target image, allowing separation of retrieval errors from visual understanding errors.
5) Evaluate on VisTarget-Bench and seven other public benchmarks, comparing against similarly sized and much larger models.

Result: The WeAgent-MMSearch system trained with FA-GSPO achieves a 19.22-point average improvement after agentic post-training across the evaluated benchmarks. It surpasses other open-source models with similar parameter counts and approaches the performance of models roughly ten times larger, demonstrating strong gains from better multimodal harnessing and failure-aware training.

Conclusion: Native multimodal agentic infrastructure plus failure-aware post-training significantly improves the effectiveness of multimodal search agents. Persistently referencing retrieved images throughout trajectories and recovering salvageable abnormal rollouts leads to stronger long-horizon multimodal planning and search, yielding performance competitive with much larger models and enabling clearer diagnosis of retrieval vs. perception failures through the new VisTarget-Bench benchmark.

Abstract: Multimodal search agents extend parametric knowledge with newly emerging and long-tail evidence from the open web. Yet many existing agentic search environments often expose retrieved evidence only as text and omit tool-returned images from subsequent context, reducing visually grounded trajectories to text-only reasoning. Long-horizon interaction also compounds tool-call, response-length, timeout, and budget failures, which can discard salvageable trajectories, waste rollout computation, and disturb policy updates. To address these issues, we introduce WeAgent-Harness, a multimodal agentic harness that supports native text-vision interaction and runtime recovery. Retrieved images receive persistent disk references, allowing the model to inspect, process, and cite them throughout the trajectory. Based on this harness, we develop WeAgent-MMSearch, an integrated system spanning data construction, agentic post-training, and multimodal rollout. For data construction, a strong MLLM uses WeAgent-Harness to discover, synthesize, and verify MMSearch-style tasks and collect expert trajectories. During post-training, our Failure-Aware GSPO (FA-GSPO) recovers salvageable abnormal rollouts and filters invalid ones to improve bounded multimodal planning and search.We also introduce VisTarget-Bench, a 150-task human-verified benchmark that pairs each question with a held-out target image, distinguishing image-retrieval failures from visual-perception failures. Evaluation on VisTarget-Bench and seven public benchmarks shows that agentic post-training improves the average score by 19.22 points, enabling our model to outperform similarly sized open-source models and rival models with roughly ten times its parameter count.

</details>


### [117] [Learning to Allocate Incentives for Incentivized Advertising via Offline Model-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28065)
*Zilin Zhao,Han Yang,Tianpei Yang,Fangsheng Huang,Yanfei Cui,Kan Peng,Yi Li,Yiming Zong,Hao Zhang,Yinsong Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper tackles how to optimally allocate monetary incentives in incentivized ads to maximize net profit, by modeling it as a sequential decision problem with delayed and carryover effects and solving it via offline model-based reinforcement learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Incentivized ads require platforms to promise users a bonus before actual ad revenue is realized. Too low a bonus loses engagement and monetization opportunities; too high a bonus erodes profit. Moreover, current incentives shape users’ expectations and future engagement, making this a sequential, path-dependent problem with delayed rewards. Existing auto-bidding focuses on available ad opportunities assuming they already exist, and targeted promotions typically act outside the core ad monetization loop, so there is a gap: no dedicated decision algorithm for sequential, cost-aware incentive allocation within the ad pipeline.

Method: The authors formulate sequential incentive allocation as a Markov Decision Process that captures user feedback, ad revenue, costs, and carryover effects. They design an offline, model-based RL (MBRL) framework: (1) learn a world model from logged data to predict user responses and revenue; (2) perform conservative policy optimization on this learned model to respect cost constraints; and (3) use an independent counterfactual scorer to evaluate candidate policies offline on held-out logs, enabling policy selection before online deployment. They compare variants like MB-IQL versus TD3+BC and plain IQL.

Result: On large-scale industrial datasets and live A/B tests, the counterfactual scorer correlates well with online performance, enabling reliable offline policy selection. The proposed MB-IQL method improves per-user net profit by 7.96% over a strong offline RL baseline (TD3+BC), while removing model-based components and reverting to plain IQL leads to a 6.56% drop in net profit, with both differences statistically significant (p < 0.0001).

Conclusion: Sequential, cost-controllable incentive allocation for incentivized ads can be effectively addressed by an offline model-based RL framework. Learning a world model and using conservative policy optimization, combined with a counterfactual scorer for offline evaluation, yields policies that significantly increase per-user net profit in real-world production systems. The deployment path from causal inference methods to offline RL and then to Offline-MBRL demonstrates the practical viability and added value of the model-based approach.

Abstract: Complete your ad view and grab a 5-cent bonus! In incentivized advertising, a platform promises users a bonus before observing downstream ad revenue, encouraging them to click and complete ads. It must balance the incentive promised in advance against the revenue realized afterward: insufficient incentives forfeit monetization opportunities, whereas excessive incentives reduce net profit. Because current incentives may also shape user expectations and future engagement, incentive allocation is a sequential decision problem with delayed revenue, cost sensitivity, and carryover effects.
  Existing work has not studied decision-making algorithms for this setting. Auto-bidding assumes available ad opportunities, while targeted promotion optimizes incentives outside the ad monetization pipeline. We formulate the problem as an MDP and develop an offline model-based RL framework for cost-controllable sequential incentive allocation. It learns a world model of user feedback and ad revenue, then performs conservative policy optimization. An independent counterfactual scorer evaluates each learned policy on held-out logs, enabling pre-launch selection without costly online exposure. Experiments on large-scale industrial data and online A/B tests show that the scorer provides a stable offline signal. The deployment path from causal inference to offline RL and then Offline-MBRL further validates the framework: MB-IQL improves per-user net profit by 7.96\% over TD3+BC, whereas reverting to plain IQL reduces it by 6.56\% (both \(p<0.0001\)).

</details>


### [118] [Speculative Probing: LLM Monitoring at Speculative-Decoding Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28099)
*Collin Zhang,Tingwei Zhang,Vitaly Shmatikov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They repurpose the speculative-decoding module in modern LLMs as an almost-free, accurate classifier by attaching a soft prompt, avoiding the usual accuracy–efficiency trade-off of hidden-state probes and full guard models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing real-time safety and monitoring classifiers for LLMs either use simple hidden-state probes that are fast but not context-aware, or full-blown classifier LLMs / heavy pooling over all token states that are accurate but slow and expensive. This creates an intrinsic trade-off between efficiency and accuracy for online classification during inference. The authors want a method that is both highly accurate and has negligible extra cost during normal speculative decoding.

Method: They leverage the speculative-decoding module already present in many LLMs and convert it into a classifier. Concretely, they train a small probe implemented as a soft prompt appended to the end of the target sequence. During inference with speculative decoding, the key–value cache of the main model is already in GPU memory, so the classifier can operate by adding this soft prompt and using the speculative module without substantial extra computation. This yields a sequence classifier that exploits full context while being very efficient. They test this on multiple models (Qwen3.5 4B/9B/27B and MiniCPM4.1-8B) across four classification tasks.

Result: Across the evaluated tasks and models, their speculative-decoding-based probes consistently outperform a strong zero-shot baseline (GPT-5.4-mini). On multilingual prompt safety classification, they match or exceed the performance of specialized 8B guard models (Qwen3Guard-Gen-8B, Llama-Guard-3-8B), while avoiding the cost of running a separate full LLM classifier. Overhead at inference is negligible because they reuse the existing KV cache in GPU memory.

Conclusion: The speculative-decoding module in LLMs can be effectively repurposed as a high-quality, low-overhead sequence classifier by appending a trained soft prompt. This approach largely breaks the usual efficiency–accuracy trade-off seen in hidden-state probes and specialized guard models, enabling real-time, fine-grained classification for safety and monitoring without significant additional compute.

Abstract: Real-time classification during language model inference is valuable for safety filtering, behavioral analysis, and model monitoring, but current approaches force a trade-off between accuracy and efficiency. Hidden-state probes are fast but limited: they are either not context-aware: operating on a single vector and cannot model interactions across positions; or they are very costly: having dedicated classifier models (Llama Guard, Qwen Guard, LLM-as-judge) or performing computation on hidden states for all tokens and then pooling the results (MultiMax). This shows an intrinsic trade-off between efficiency and accuracy.
  However, we find that the speculative-decoding module in recent LLMs can be repurposed for efficient high-quality classification. By appending a trained soft prompt at the end of the target sequence, we can repurpose the speculative-decoding module into a sequence classifier. At inference time in a speculative-decoding pipeline, the KV cache is already in GPU memory, so classification adds negligible overhead. We evaluate on four classification tasks across four models (Qwen3.5-4B, 9B, 27B, MiniCPM4.1-8B). Our small probes consistently outperform zero-shot GPT-5.4-mini and, on multilingual prompt safety, match or beat specialized 8B safety classifiers (Qwen3Guard-Gen-8B, Llama-Guard-3-8B) without running a full LLM.

</details>


### [119] [SEPO: Evidence-Grounded Prompt Optimization via Structural Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28067)
*Xiaoyu Ma,Haoyue Liu,Yiwen Li,Jionghao Zhu,Zhichao Wang,Ye Chen,Xiaoying Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SEPO is a prompt optimization method that performs structured, local edits with explicit edit-effect tracking, improving accuracy and efficiency over prior API-only optimizers like GEPA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing API-based prompt optimizers claim interpretability but only allow post-hoc inspection of whole-prompt rewrites. They cannot easily attribute performance changes to specific, local prompt edits because prompts are treated as opaque strings and optimizers only record full-prompt diffs. This limits debuggability, reusability of partial improvements, and principled search over the space of prompts.

Method: SEPO (Structural, Evidence-grounded Prompt Optimization) represents prompts with a two-layer schema composed of stable, typed units (e.g., sections or components). Instead of rewriting the entire prompt at each iteration, SEPO performs local edits to these units. For every edit, it records (1) the intended structural operation, (2) the realized operation in the new prompt, and (3) which examples are now correctly solved or broken compared to before. This creates an edit-effect lineage: a trace that links specific structural changes to their empirical impact. The optimizer then uses this lineage to guide subsequent edit decisions along each search branch, enabling multi-trajectory search with explicit credit assignment to edits.

Result: On a held-out suite of 14 tasks, SEPO outperforms the strongest prior baseline, GEPA, by 3.1 percentage points on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and 2.2 percentage points on Qwen3-8B, achieving 61.9% and 73.3% macro accuracy, respectively. It is also more efficient, using 2.9 million optimization tokens compared with GEPA’s 4.1 million, and it produces prompts that are over five times shorter. SEPO lies on the Pareto front both with respect to optimization-time cost and test-time performance.

Conclusion: Structuring prompts into typed units and tracking the lineage between local edits and their empirical effects yields a more interpretable and effective prompt optimization process. SEPO’s evidence-grounded edit lineage makes optimization addressable, attributable, and actionable, while also providing better accuracy, shorter prompts, and lower optimization cost than prior API-based optimizers such as GEPA.

Abstract: Existing API-only prompt optimisers are often described as interpretable, but in practice, this usually means only post-hoc inspectability: each iteration still rewrites the prompt as one opaque string, leaving a trace of full-prompt diffs rather than localisable, machine-readable edits. This paper introduces SEPO (Structural, Evidence-grounded Prompt Optimization), a multi-trajectory prompt optimiser centred on edit-effect lineage feedback. Rather than treating each iteration as an isolated whole-prompt rewrite, SEPO locally edits stable, typed units in a two-layer prompt schema, links the target and realised structural operations of each edit to the examples it newly fixes or breaks, and carries this edit-effect record forward to guide later architect calls on the same search branch. This makes prompt optimisation addressable, attributable, and actionable. Across a 14-task held-out suite, SEPO improves over the strongest baseline, GEPA, by 3.1 pp on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and 2.2 pp on Qwen3-8B, reaching 61.9% and 73.3% macro accuracy. SEPO also lies on both the optimisation-time and test-time Pareto frontiers, spending 2.9M optimisation tokens versus 4.1M for GEPA and producing prompts over 5x shorter.

</details>


### [120] [Stay Within Your Bounds: Distance-Guided Decoding for Guaranteed Context-Free Grammar Compliance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28229)
*Vincenzo Collura,Karim Tit,Eleonora Giunchiglia,Mike Papadakis,Maxime Cordy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They improve grammar-constrained decoding for LLMs so outputs are always syntactically valid and of higher quality, using lookahead on pushdown automata.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing grammar-constrained decoding typically enforces only local prefix feasibility: every next token must keep the partial string extendable according to a context-free grammar. However, due to mismatches between tokenization and grammar symbols and limited token budgets, a prefix that is locally feasible can still be unable to reach an accepting parse, causing syntactic errors or truncation. There is a need for a decoding method that anticipates these issues and ensures global syntactic validity while maintaining or improving output quality.

Method: They design a lookahead-guided decoding framework grounded in pushdown automata for context-free grammars. Offline, they precompute bounded pushdown summaries that annotate states with reachability labels and upper bounds on the distance (in steps) to an accepting configuration. During decoding, these precomputed estimates are used online to perform horizon-aware pruning and beam search, effectively steering the generation away from dead ends that cannot reach acceptance within the token budget.

Result: The proposed decoder is syntactically sound by construction: all generated sequences are guaranteed to be accepted by the specified grammar. Empirical evaluation on tasks involving JSON, SQL, and Linear Temporal Logic (LTL) generation shows that the method not only maintains 100% syntactic validity but also yields better completion quality compared with prior grammar-constrained decoding baselines.

Conclusion: Lookahead-guided decoding with pushdown-automata-based summaries overcomes the limitations of purely local grammar constraints in LLM decoding. By integrating offline reachability and distance-to-acceptance information into online token selection and pruning, the approach ensures grammatically valid outputs and improves practical performance across several structured generation domains.

Abstract: Grammar-constrained decoding helps large language models produce syntactically valid structured outputs, such as code, JSON, and SQL. For context-free grammars, many practical decoders enforce local prefix feasibility: each token must keep the current prefix extendable to some valid completion. Yet, under tokenizer-grammar mismatch and finite token budgets, feasible prefixes may still fail to reach acceptance. We propose a lookahead-guided decoding framework for context-free grammars based on pushdown automata. Offline, we compute bounded pushdown summaries with reachability labels and upper-bound distances to acceptance. Online, these estimates guide horizon-aware pruning and beam search. The resulting decoder is syntactically sound: every output is accepted by the target grammar. Experiments on JSON, SQL, and Linear Temporal Logic (LTL) show both consistent syntactic validity and improved completion quality over existing baselines.

</details>


### [121] [When Robots Mishear Us: Mapping the Safety Risks of Voice-Controlled Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28518)
*Sihan Jia,Oliver Lemon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how errors from automatic speech recognition (ASR) in spoken commands can cause embodied AI agents to execute unsafe actions, showing that such errors significantly undermine current safety mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embodied AI agents are increasingly voice-controlled, relying on ASR to interpret user commands. Safety research typically assumes clean, correctly-transcribed inputs, but real-world speech recognition introduces substitutions, deletions, and insertions. These errors may distort user intent in ways that bypass safety filters or weaken refusal behaviors, making it important to understand whether and how ASR noise can translate into real-world unsafe behavior.

Method: The authors simulate different classes of ASR errors and inject them into user instructions drawn from existing embodied AI safety benchmarks (SafeAgentBench and POEX). They then evaluate an EAI system’s responses under these corrupted inputs, analyzing how different error types affect the agent’s safety behavior—whether it refuses, generates unsafe plans, or executes harmful actions. They further test automatic correction mechanisms on ASR-corrupted inputs to see if post-processing can mitigate the risks.

Result: The experiments show that ASR-induced perturbations can substantially degrade safety: some error types maintain the high-level structure of the command but introduce ambiguity that leads the model to misinterpret intent in harmful ways, while others directly weaken refusal patterns and cause the system to accept and operationalize unsafe instructions. Automatic correction can partially reduce these risks in some cases, but fails to fully prevent unsafe plans or actions and is unreliable across error types and tasks.

Conclusion: ASR errors are a significant and underexplored safety vulnerability for voice-controlled embodied AI. Safety mechanisms that work well on clean text inputs may fail when faced with realistic speech recognition noise. While automatic error correction can help, it is insufficient as a standalone defense, implying that robust safety for embodied agents must explicitly account for noisy, imperfect input channels such as ASR rather than assuming idealized text instructions.

Abstract: We investigate whether automatic speech recognition (ASR) errors in user input can lead to unsafe outputs from Embodied AI (EAI) models. We find that ASR errors can lead to harmful instructions being accepted and executed by EAI models, thereby reducing safety. We simulate ASR errors and combine them with existing safety benchmarks (SafeAgentBench and POEX) to evaluate how different errors affect embodied AI safety. We find that some of them preserve semantic structure but increase harmful ambiguity, while others weaken the model refusal behaviour and allow unsafe plans to be generated and executed. We show that in some cases automatic correction of ASR errors can reduce the risk, but this is not always effective. Overall, we show that ASR errors lead to significant safety risks for embodied AI.

</details>


### [122] [The Shape of Power: A Multilingual Framework for Social Power Reasoning in Dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28144)
*Farah Atif,Sougata Saha,Monojit Choudhury*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces a multilingual, cross-cultural framework and corpus for studying social power in movie dialogues, and shows current LLMs struggle with nuanced, relational power reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computational work on social power is limited to a few languages and cultures and lacks rich demographic and relational detail, preventing robust cross-cultural analysis and limiting our understanding of how power is expressed and perceived across contexts.

Method: Design a theory-driven annotation schema for social power in dialogue; build a native-speaker annotation pipeline with a custom interface; apply it to French and Egyptian Arabic movie scenes to create an annotated corpus capturing demographics, context, power asymmetries, and intentions; then evaluate several LLMs and multimodal LLMs on tasks involving cross-cultural social power and theory-of-mind reasoning using this dataset.

Result: Produced a corpus of 15,836 annotated instances from 100 multilingual movie scenes, found high inter-annotator agreement on concrete demographic and contextual features but lower agreement on interpretive aspects like power asymmetry and intention alignment, and showed that six tested LLMs exhibit substantial performance gaps relative to humans on relational and theory-of-mind dimensions of social power reasoning.

Conclusion: The work establishes the first extensible multilingual framework and dataset for studying social power in dialogue, reveals the inherent difficulty and cultural variability of socially interpretive power judgments, and offers an initial but challenging benchmark indicating that current LLMs remain far from human-level cross-cultural social reasoning about power.

Abstract: Social power plays a fundamental role in shaping human interaction, yet computational studies of power remain limited to narrow linguistic and cultural settings. Existing datasets further lack the demographic and relational depth needed for robust cross-cultural analysis. To address this gap, we introduce a theoretically grounded framework for studying social power in naturalistic multilingual dialogue through movie screenplays. The framework integrates a schema informed by social science theory, a native speaker annotation pipeline refined through pilot studies, and a custom interface for scalable cross-lingual analysis. Using this framework, we constructed an initial corpus containing 15,836 annotated instances from 100 scenes in French and Egyptian Arabic movies. Our analysis reveals strong agreement on observable demographic and contextual attributes, while socially interpretive aspects, such as power asymmetry and intention alignment, remain more contested, highlighting the complexity of social power across cultures. We evaluated 6 Large Language Models (LLMs) and Multimodal LLMs on cross-cultural social power reasoning, finding persistent gaps between human and model agreement in relational and theory-of-mind reasoning. Our work introduces the first extensible multilingual framework for studying social power in dialogues and provides an initial evaluation setting for studying cross-cultural social reasoning.

</details>


### [123] [Under-Mattress Temporal Sensing for Next-Day Agitation Risk Scoring in Dementia Wards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28152)
*Zhen Liu,Marta Bono,Robbe Decloedt,Ajda Flisar,Maarten Van Den Bossche,Maarten De Vos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether contactless under-mattress physiological signals collected overnight can predict next-day agitation risk in hospitalized people with dementia, comparing summary-based and minute-level temporal modeling approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agitation in people with dementia fluctuates rapidly, and clinicians lack continuous, objective tools to anticipate short-term changes in agitation risk. Existing approaches usually rely on sparse observations or coarse nightly summaries, providing limited predictive power. There is a need to understand whether rich, contactless, minute-level physiological data collected during sleep can improve next-day agitation risk prediction and inform proactive care.

Method: The authors collected 423 nights of data from 65 dementia patients in a specialized hospital unit using two under-mattress sensing systems that captured activity, heart rate, and respiratory rate. They created a unified benchmark with four modeling paradigms: (1) nightly handcrafted summary features; (2) handcrafted features for three time periods during the night; (3) full-night sequence models using minute-level data; and (4) sliding-window multiple-instance learning on minute-level data. They applied source-specific preprocessing, used five-fold cross-validation grouped by patient to avoid leakage, and evaluated models with AUROC, AUPRC, balanced accuracy, calibration, and period-signal attribution analyses.

Result: The full-night sequence model using minute-level data achieved the best predictive performance, with AUROC of 0.692, AUPRC of 0.849, and balanced accuracy of 0.658. Both minute-level temporal pipelines outperformed simple nightly summaries in discrimination, while their advantage over three-period handcrafted features was less clear. Attribution analysis across temporal models highlighted activity, heart rate, and respiratory rate during the central overnight period as the most informative signals. However, all models showed limited calibration, indicating that predicted probabilities did not map well to true risk levels.

Conclusion: Overnight contactless physiological signals can provide modest discrimination of next-day agitation risk in hospitalized dementia patients, and preserving minute-level temporal structure generally improves performance over nightly summaries. Nevertheless, the models remain poorly calibrated and require prospective calibration and external validation before they can be used in individual clinical decision-making. The benchmark framework and findings suggest that under-mattress sensing and temporal modeling are promising directions for future biomedical engineering research on agitation risk in dementia cohorts.

Abstract: Agitation fluctuates over short time horizons in people living with dementia, yet continuous physiological information for anticipating next-day risk is limited. We assessed whether contactless under-mattress signals from the preceding night inform next-day agitation risk and whether preserving minute-level temporal structure improves performance over conventional nightly summaries. We analyzed 423 patient-nights from 65 subjects in a specialized hospital dementia unit using two under-mattress sensing systems. A unified four-paradigm benchmark compared nightly handcrafted summaries, three-period handcrafted features, full-night sequence modeling, and sliding-window multiple-instance learning. Source-specific preprocessing and five-fold patient-grouped cross-validation were used, with performance estimated from pooled out-of-fold predictions. Evaluation included discrimination, calibration, fixed-threshold metrics, and a comparison of period-signal attribution patterns across two temporal models. Full-night sequence modeling achieved the highest discrimination (AUROC, 0.692; AUPRC, 0.849) and balanced accuracy (0.658). Both minute-level pipelines had higher AUROC than nightly summaries, but differences from three-period handcrafted features were uncertain. Cross-model attribution prioritized activity, heart rate, and respiratory rate during the core overnight period. Calibration remained limited. The preceding night's signals supported modest next-day risk discrimination, with minute-level temporal modeling outperforming nightly summaries. Prospective calibration and external validation are needed before use in individual care decisions. This patient-grouped benchmark identifies contactless overnight sensing as a promising biomedical engineering direction for agitation-risk research in hospitalized dementia cohorts.

</details>


### [124] [CrabOS: An Operating System for Human-AI Co-inhabitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28165)
*Qi Yang,Yun Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Human-AI Co-inhabitation and CrabOS, an OS-level environment where humans and AI share the same text-based work state, enabling seamless turn-taking on complex tasks without custom bridges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI agent systems keep human and AI workspaces separate, so agents cannot directly access or modify the task’s work state. This forces developers to build bespoke integration bridges or users to manually copy information via screenshots or text, which is expensive, error-prone, and doesn’t scale. The authors want a more native, scalable way for humans and AI to co-work on long-running, complex tasks with alternating leadership.

Method: The authors define the concept of Human-AI Co-inhabitation—an environment where humans and AI operate on the same shared work state—and implement CrabOS as a realization of this concept. CrabOS encodes the work state as natural-language-readable text objects that are equally accessible to humans and AI through a single auditable interface. They then conduct case studies to demonstrate how CrabOS supports complex tasks with alternating control between humans and AI, contrasting it with prior bridge-based solutions.

Result: CrabOS successfully allows humans and AI agents to directly share and manipulate the same text-based work state without custom integration bridges. In the case studies, this shared environment elevates support for complex, alternating human-AI workflows from ad-hoc, application-level integrations to a general OS-level capability, simplifying development and operation of AI agents across tasks and applications.

Conclusion: Human-AI Co-inhabitation, instantiated in CrabOS, provides a new operating-system-level foundation for building and running AI agents that can seamlessly take turns with humans on complex tasks. By representing work state as shared natural-language text objects, it removes the need for fragile bridges, reduces handoff costs, and offers a scalable, auditable mechanism for human-AI collaboration across applications.

Abstract: AI agents are evolving into long-running computational entities that can invoke tools, maintain memory, and complete complex tasks across applications. In real-world settings, completing a task often requires humans and AI to take turns leading its execution. Such alternation depends on the seamless handoff of the work state of the task between humans and AI. Existing agent systems, however, provide humans and AI with separate work environments. AI agents must therefore rely on additional bridges to continue work: either developers build task-specific interfaces to access the work state, or users manually transfer relevant parts of it through screenshots or textual descriptions. Both approaches make handoffs costly and scale poorly.
  We propose Human-AI Co-inhabitation, a type of work environment that enables humans and AI to seamlessly take turns continuing work on the same task, and design and implement CrabOS to realize this concept. CrabOS represents the work state as natural-language-readable text objects shared by humans and AI, allowing both to access and manipulate it directly through the same auditable interface without bridges. Case studies show that CrabOS elevates support for complex tasks with alternating human and AI leadership from bridge-dependent application-level solutions to native operating-system capabilities, which provide a new foundation for developing and running AI agents.

</details>


### [125] [Expert Knowledge & Machine Understanding: Bridging Reactome's Ontology with LLM Semantic Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28178)
*Susanna Bravi,Riccardo De Luca,Rosa Sicilia,Christine Nardini,Mario Santoro*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They test if text descriptions in the Reactome database are sufficient to automatically reconstruct its expert-defined biological pathway hierarchy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Curated pathway databases like Reactome are accurate but hard to scale because they rely on manual expert curation. Many NLP tools try to use text to expand these knowledgebases, but it’s unclear whether the text really captures the same higher-level biological relationships that experts encode in the pathway hierarchy. The authors want to know if the human-written descriptions (metadata) alone can reproduce the global hierarchical structure defined by experts.

Method: They extract the Homo sapiens pathway and reaction hierarchy from Reactome as the reference “Reactome Hierarchy.” Using only the associated textual metadata, they embed descriptions with a sentence-transformer model (SPECTER2), then apply a modified agglomerative nesting (hierarchical clustering) algorithm plus a graph reconstruction procedure to build a “Semantic Hierarchy.” They compare this semantic hierarchy to the original one using quantitative measures such as Laplacian Spectral Distance and bootstrapping, and qualitative assessments via global topological network metrics.

Result: The reconstructed Semantic Hierarchy, derived solely from textual metadata, is quantitatively similar to the Reactome Hierarchy, as indicated by low Laplacian Spectral Distance and stable results under bootstrapping. Qualitative network measures further show that key global topological properties of the expert-defined hierarchy are recovered from the text-based reconstruction.

Conclusion: Human-written textual descriptions in Reactome contain enough information to recover the experts’ global hierarchical organization of biological pathways. This supports the use of NLP methods on metadata to automatically infer higher-order biological relationships and suggests that text-based automation can scale and complement manual curation in biological knowledgebases.

Abstract: Biological knowledgebases like Reactome provide high-quality pathways that include biological elements' relationships and textual descriptions (metadata). The quality of such pathways is granted by manual curation, that presents, however, significant scalability challenges. Lately, numerous NLP tools have been proposed to cope with this issue, leveraging textual information to automatically expand biological knowledgebases. However, little exploration has been done so far to assess whether relationships among textual descriptions mirror higher order biological relationships. This study explores whether human-written descriptions in Reactome can be used to infer the experts' defined global hierarchical structure. To test this, we extracted from Reactome the Homo Sapiens hierarchy of pathways and their reactions (Reactome Hierarchy), and used textual metadata to reconstruct a Semantic Hierarchy, combining a sentence transformer model (SPECTER2) with a modified agglomerative nesting algorithm and a graph reconstruction algorithm. Quantitative (Laplacian Spectral Distance and Bootstrapping) and qualitative (global topological metrics) analyses confirm our hypothesis and indicate that the global hierarchical structure of pathways can be inferred by experts textual metadata.

</details>


### [126] [Generative AI Alignment with Hinduism's Theological Plurality and Sacred Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28228)
*Dipto Das,Arpita Kundu,Nusrat Jahan Mim,Shion Guha,Syed Ishtiaque Ahmed*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how Bangladeshi Hindu users interact with generative AI for religious purposes, finding that AI is both helpful and ethically problematic, and argues for “interpretive alignment” that respects plurality and avoids simulating sacred authority.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most AI alignment and ethics discussions assume secular, Western, or Abrahamic perspectives and overlook other faith traditions. As generative AI increasingly mediates personal and religious life, there is a need to understand how underrepresented communities—here, Bangladeshi Hindus—experience AI’s religious outputs and what ethical issues arise.

Method: Qualitative study using 15 semi-structured interviews with Bangladeshi Hindu participants. The authors analyze how participants understand and use AI for religious representations, explanations of scripture, devotional interactions, and synthetic religious media, focusing on users’ interpretations and concerns.

Result: AI was experienced as accessible and useful for exploring scripture, visualizing devotional themes, and supporting religious storytelling. However, participants also reported problems such as theological flattening of complex traditions, cultural misrepresentation, manipulation of devotional sentiments, and troubling simulations of sacred presence and authority by AI systems.

Conclusion: Religious alignment in generative AI should go beyond content filters and doctrinal correctness towards “interpretive alignment.” Systems should transparently disclose their limits, maintain and represent religious plurality rather than flattening it, and avoid simulating sacred authority or overly personalized, sycophantic devotional responses that can mislead or manipulate users.

Abstract: Generative AI systems are increasingly used to answer personal questions and mediate everyday practices, including religion. However, existing discussions around AI alignment and ethics have largely centered secular, Western, and Abrahamic assumptions about religion, offering limited attention to other faith-based traditions. In this paper, we examine how Hindu users engage with generative AI systems in relation to their religious knowledge, belief, and practice. Drawing on 15 semi-structured interviews with Bangladeshi Hindu participants, we analyze how users interpret AI-generated religious representations, scriptural explanations, devotional interactions, and synthetic religious media. We found that AI can be both accessible and ethically troubling. While AI supported scriptural inquiry, devotional visualization, and religious storytelling, our study also identified concerns about theological flattening, cultural misrepresentation, devotional manipulation, and the simulation of sacred presence and authority. We conclude by arguing that religious alignment in generative AI requires interpretive alignment: systems that disclose their limits, preserve plurality, and avoid simulating sacred authority and sycophantic personalization.

</details>


### [127] [REINS: Refusal-Enhanced Inhibitory Steering with Sparse Autoencoder Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28233)
*Kai-Xuan Ding,Hao-Xiang Xu,Ji-Hua Peng,Zi-Qi Chen,Jiaqi Wang,Zhen-Hua Ling*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes REINS, a new steering method using sparse autoencoders that both suppresses harmful features and boosts refusal features, improving safety on complex harmful prompts while preserving model capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SAE-based steering methods that only enhance refusal directions fail on prompts with complex wrappers, where the harmful continuation pathway remains active. There is a need for a systematic evaluation of this failure mode and for stronger, more targeted interventions that maintain model capabilities without relying on collapse or over-censorship.

Method: 1) Construct GUISE, a benchmark of harmful prompts wrapped in complex, obfuscating instructions to systematically test safety under adversarial-like conditions. 2) Analyze prior SAE steering approaches that apply a single steering direction aimed at increasing refusals, and show that these are insufficient on GUISE. 3) Propose REINS (Refusal-Enhanced INhibitory Steering), which operates in the SAE feature space to both inhibit features associated with harmful continuations and enhance features associated with safe refusals simultaneously. 4) Empirically compare REINS to prior steering baselines on GUISE and other datasets, measuring harmful response rate, refusal quality, and preservation of general capabilities.

Result: On GUISE and other benchmarks, prior SAE steering methods either (a) intervene too weakly to reliably prevent harmful continuations or (b) create an illusion of safety via model collapse that degrades general performance. REINS, in contrast, significantly lowers the rate of harmful responses, substantially increases the rate and quality of safe refusals, and maintains the model’s general capabilities much better than competing methods.

Conclusion: Combining inhibitory steering of harmful features with enhancement of refusal features in the SAE feature space yields a more robust and practical safety control mechanism for LLMs than single-direction refusal enhancement. The GUISE dataset reveals important weaknesses in existing methods and provides a systematic way to test safety under complex prompt wrappers, while results with REINS suggest that targeted multi-direction steering can achieve stronger safety without sacrificing usability.

Abstract: Steering with Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) offers a lightweight inference-time path for adapting the behavior of large language models without retraining. By exposing sparse and interpretable features, SAE steering provides a promising interface for safety control that guides harmful continuations toward refusal. However, we observe that complex wrappers can still undermine existing SAE steering methods on harmful prompts. To evaluate this failure mode systematically, we construct Generalized Undercover Instruction Safety Evaluation (GUISE), a dataset of harmful prompts with complex wrappers. Existing single direction SAE steering methods do not reliably produce refusals on harmful prompts, suggesting that refusal enhancement alone can be too weak when the harmful continuation path remains active. This motivates us to propose Refusal-Enhanced INhibitory Steering (REINS), which suppresses harmful continuation features and enhances safe refusal features in the same SAE feature space. Experiments on GUISE and other datasets show that prior methods either intervene too weakly or achieve only apparent safety through collapse, while REINS substantially reduces harmful responses, markedly improves safe refusals and largely preserves general capabilities.

</details>


### [128] [Beyond Task-Only Matching: Personalized Skill Routing with Counterfactual Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28241)
*Tianle Wang,Yanghe Zou,Xiang Liu,Ziyao Huang,Chenchen Fu,Weiwei Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose personalized skill routing for LLM agents that accounts for both the task and the user profile, and introduce a new benchmark and method (SkillFeed) that significantly improve retrieval accuracy over task-only routing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With the growth of reusable skill repositories for LLM agents, correctly routing user queries to the right skills is crucial. Existing routers only consider task semantics, ignoring user-specific constraints. This causes failures when different users pose the same request but require different skills due to their profiles. The authors aim to formalize and solve this overlooked problem of user-aware, personalized skill routing.

Method: They formulate personalized skill routing as profile-conditioned retrieval, where relevance depends jointly on the task and the user profile. They build a profile-counterfactual benchmark where the task is fixed but changing user profiles change the appropriate skill, providing a clear testbed. They construct paired counterfactual supervision signals and propose SkillFeed, a progressive retrieve-and-rerank framework: first retrieve skills based on task–skill alignment, then rerank semantically similar skills using profile-aware discrimination. SkillFeed leverages body-level evidence from candidate skills to decide which ones conflict with user constraints and which satisfy both task requirements and profile constraints.

Result: On their new benchmark, SkillFeed-Bench, SkillFeed achieves 75.1% top-1 retrieval accuracy, outperforming a strong pretrained routing baseline by 23.1 percentage points. On the subset of queries where changing the user profile changes the correct skill, adding profile conditioning yields a 35.1-point accuracy gain, indicating that profile information is particularly valuable in those critical, profile-sensitive cases.

Conclusion: Personalized skill routing should depend on both task semantics and user profiles, not just the task alone. The authors show that task-only routing conflates task relevance with skill suitability and fails on profile-sensitive cases. Their SkillFeed framework, combined with a profile-counterfactual benchmark, demonstrates that profile-conditioned retrieve-and-rerank substantially improves routing performance, especially when user constraints alter which skills are appropriate.

Abstract: The rapid expansion of reusable skill repositories makes skill routing a critical capability for large language model (LLM) agents. Existing methods treat routing as task-only semantic matching. However, when users with incompatible constraints issue an identical request, this assumption conflates task relevance with skill suitability: a task-only router can select a semantically plausible skill that is unsuitable for the requesting user. To expose this failure mode, we formulate \textit{personalized skill routing} as profile-conditioned retrieval, in which relevance depends jointly on the task and the user profile. We first introduce a profile-counterfactual benchmark, in which the task is held fixed while changes in the user profile induce changes in the reference skill. We further construct paired counterfactual supervision and propose SkillFeed, a progressive retrieve-and-rerank framework that first establishes task--skill alignment and then learns profile-conditioned discrimination. By retrieving body-level evidence and reranking semantically similar but profile-conflicting candidates, SkillFeed identifies skills that satisfy both task requirements and user constraints. On SkillFeed-Bench, SkillFeed attains 75.1\% top-1 retrieval accuracy, a 23.1-point improvement over the corresponding pretrained routing baseline. Adding profile conditioning yields a 35.1-point gain on queries where user profile changes the reference skill. This contrast shows that user profiles are most consequential precisely when they change skill suitability. Our website is publicly available at http://www.aiskillfeed.com .

</details>


### [129] [Regime-Aware Portfolio Management via Retrieval-Augmented LLM-Guided Expert Switching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28252)
*Ahmad Asadi,Reza Safabakhsh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a retrieval-augmented expert-switching framework that uses historical market situations and expert performance, encoded via a dual-stream VAE and evaluated by an instruction-tuned LLM, to adaptively select portfolio-management experts and improve risk-adjusted returns in non-stationary financial markets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional portfolio-management strategies often fail in non-stationary financial markets because their performance is highly dependent on current market conditions, and a single fixed strategy rarely remains optimal over time. Existing expert-switching or ensemble methods may not fully exploit historical context about when specific experts perform well, and they sometimes lack theoretical guarantees that adding new experts will not hurt performance. The authors aim to develop a robust, adaptive selection mechanism that leverages historical market states and expert outcomes to choose the most suitable expert under current conditions, while providing theoretical assurance that expanding the expert set with locally better strategies is beneficial or at least harmless.

Method: The authors propose a retrieval-augmented expert-switching framework with three main components. First, a dual-stream variational autoencoder encodes both asset-level features and market-wide information into a latent representation of the current market situation. Second, a retrieval-based knowledge base stores historical market situations, their latent representations, and the corresponding performance of different portfolio experts under those conditions. Third, at inference time, the current situation is encoded, similar historical states and their expert-performance records are retrieved, and an instruction-tuned large language model reasons over these retrieved examples to select the most appropriate expert instead of directly proposing trades. The framework includes a theoretical analysis establishing a monotonicity property: adding an expert that is locally superior in some situations cannot worsen the overall performance of the switching mechanism.

Result: Across three types of financial markets—cryptocurrency, stock, and foreign exchange—the proposed selector consistently outperforms baseline expert-selection strategies. It achieves the highest cumulative return and Sharpe ratio in all three markets among the evaluated methods. In the stock market example, the best single fixed expert yields a 26% cumulative return and a Sharpe ratio of 0.74, whereas the proposed switching framework increases cumulative return to 34% and improves the Sharpe ratio to 0.96. Ablation studies show that removing retrieval or LLM reasoning degrades performance, indicating that both components are critical. Additional experiments varying the size of the expert pool demonstrate that including complementary experts improves results, in line with the theoretical monotonicity property.

Conclusion: The study concludes that retrieval-grounded expert switching is an effective solution for adaptive portfolio management in non-stationary financial environments. By encoding market states with a dual-stream VAE, retrieving historically similar situations, and using an instruction-tuned LLM to reason over expert performance, the framework can dynamically select the most suitable expert and achieve superior risk-adjusted returns compared with fixed or simpler selection schemes. The monotonicity guarantee and empirical gains across multiple asset classes suggest that expanding and diversifying the expert pool is beneficial, and that the combination of retrieval and LLM-based reasoning is key to robust performance.

Abstract: Financial markets are inherently non-stationary, making the effectiveness of individual portfolio-management strategies highly dependent on changing market conditions. This work proposes a retrieval-augmented expert-switching framework that dynamically selects portfolio management experts based on their historical performance under similar market situations. A dual-stream variational autoencoder represents asset-level and market-wide information, while a retrieval-based knowledge base stores historical situations and expert performance. During inference, an instruction-tuned LLM reasons over the retrieved evidence to identify the most appropriate expert rather than directly generating portfolio actions. We further establish a monotonicity property showing that adding a locally superior expert cannot degrade the switching mechanism's performance. Experiments across cryptocurrency, stock, and foreign-exchange markets show that the proposed selector achieves the highest cumulative return and Sharpe ratio among the evaluated selection strategies in all three markets. In the stock market, for example, cumulative return increases from 26% for the best fixed expert to 34%, while the Sharpe ratio improves from 0.74 to 0.96. Ablation results confirm the importance of both retrieval and LLM reasoning, while experiments with different expert-pool sizes demonstrate the value of complementary expertise. Overall, the findings support retrieval-grounded expert switching as an effective approach to adaptive portfolio management in non-stationary financial environments.

</details>


### [130] [Physics-Guided Flow Matching for CT Image Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28256)
*Davide Evangelista*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes using Flow Matching generative models as priors for CT reconstruction and shows they outperform diffusion-based priors in quality and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models are strong priors for CT inverse problems but suffer from stochastic sampling, long trajectories, and sensitivity to noise schedules, which hurt efficiency and stability for high-resolution reconstructions. The authors seek a generative prior that maintains or improves reconstruction quality while being more stable and computationally efficient.

Method: Train a high-resolution Rectified Flow Matching generative model on 256x256 chest CT images from the Mayo Low-Dose CT dataset using a two-stage procedure: (1) heavy, anatomically informed data augmentation to increase variability and prevent overfitting, then (2) fine-tuning with reduced/no augmentation to improve structural fidelity. Use this model as a prior within several Flow Matching–specific reconstruction schemes (Plug-and-Play Flow, FlowDPS, Flower, Flow-Priors/ICTM) and compare them to leading diffusion-based reconstruction algorithms (DDRM, DPS, DiffPIR) across different CT inverse problem settings.

Result: The trained Flow Matching model can generate high-quality, anatomically coherent CT-like images. Across multiple CT inverse problems, Flow Matching–based reconstruction methods consistently achieve higher PSNR, SSIM, and better perceptual quality than diffusion-based counterparts, while also requiring fewer sampling steps.

Conclusion: Flow Matching is an effective generative prior for high-resolution CT reconstruction, offering better image quality and perceptual realism than diffusion models, with improved stability and computational efficiency. The released model and code support reproducibility and encourage further research on Flow Matching for medical imaging inverse problems.

Abstract: Deep generative models have recently emerged as powerful priors for solving ill-posed inverse problems in CT, with diffusion-based approaches achieving state-of-the-art reconstruction performance. However, diffusion models typically rely on stochastic sampling procedures, long inference trajectories, and carefully tuned noise schedules, which can limit computational efficiency and numerical stability, especially at high spatial resolutions. In this work, we investigate Flow Matching as an alternative generative prior for CT reconstruction. We train a high-resolution Rectified Flow Matching model on 256x256 chest images from the Mayo Clinic Low-Dose CT dataset. To mitigate overfitting and limited anatomical variability, we employ a two-stage training strategy consisting of an initial phase with strong, anatomically informed data augmentation, followed by a fine-tuning phase with reduced or no augmentation to refine structural fidelity. The resulting model is capable of generating high-quality and anatomically coherent CT-like images, serving as a strong learned prior. We then evaluate multiple reconstruction methods specifically designed for Flow Matching models, including Plug-and-Play Flow, FlowDPS, Flower, and Flow-Priors (ICTM), and compare them against state-of-the-art diffusion-based reconstruction algorithms such as DDRM, DPS, and DiffPIR. Experimental results across several CT inverse problem settings show that Flow Matching-based approaches consistently outperform diffusion-based methods in terms of PSNR, SSIM, and perceptual quality, while requiring fewer sampling steps. Finally, we publicly release the trained Flow Matching model and accompanying code to facilitate reproducibility and future research. Overall, this work demonstrates that Flow Matching provides a stable, efficient, and effective alternative to diffusion models for high-resolution CT image reconstruction.

</details>


### [131] [Finding Where the Buck Stops: An Automated Failure Attribution-Based Reflection Framework for Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28264)
*Xiaoqing Wang,Keman Huang,Bin Liang,Hongyu Li,Xiaoyong Du,Wuqiong Pan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces DoCtOR, a reflection framework for multi-agent LLM systems that selectively diagnoses and corrects the specific agent and step that caused failure, improving success rates on several benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent systems with LLMs can tackle complex tasks but often fail. Current self-reflection approaches make all agents reflect on failures, assuming every agent contributed equally to the error. In reality, failures usually result from a specific agent’s decisive mistake, while other agents behave normally. Forcing all agents to reflect can pollute their memory with incorrect insights and reduce overall effectiveness. There is a need for a targeted reflection strategy that isolates and corrects the true source of failure without harming well-performing agents.

Method: The authors propose DoCtOR (Diagnose-then-Correct PPO-enhanced Reflection), a structured reflection framework for MAS. DoCtOR executes three main stages: (1) Automated failure attribution to locate the decisive error step and identify the decisive error agent responsible for the failure; (2) Counterfactual reasoning to generate a corrected version of the decisive error step, effectively modeling what should have happened; (3) Targeted reflection, where only the decisive error agent reflects on this corrected step, updating its behavior based on this localized correction. The framework is also analyzed for generalizability and examined under low-resource constraints, including reflection focused only on post-error steps rather than the entire trajectory.

Result: On three benchmarks—HotPotQA, ChartQAPro, and Mind2Web—DoCtOR yields 22%, 26%, and 27% relative improvements over the initial success rates, respectively. It outperforms existing reflection baselines such as Reflexion, Retroformer, and COPPER. The experiments further show that the diagnose-then-correct design is generally applicable and that, in low-resource regimes, performing reflection only on reasoning steps after the decisive error step can match the performance of reflecting over the entire failure trajectory.

Conclusion: Targeted, diagnosis-driven reflection in multi-agent LLM systems is more effective than blanket reflection across all agents. By isolating the decisive error step and agent, and then applying counterfactual correction and localized reflection, DoCtOR significantly boosts task success while avoiding contamination of correct agents’ memories. The approach generalizes across tasks and remains efficient and effective even when only partial trajectories are used for reflection.

Abstract: Multi-agent systems (MAS) powered by large language models have shown promise for complex tasks but suffer from high failure rates. Current self-reflection methods for MAS require all agents to reflect upon failure, overlooking a critical reality: failures typically stem from a specific agent leading the task astray, namely the decisive error agent, while others merely fulfill their regular duties. Forcing regular-behaving agents to reflect contaminates their memory with wrong insights. Hence, we propose DoCtOR (Diagnose-then-Correct PPO-enhanced Reflection), a novel reflection framework that enhances multi-agent collaboration. DoCtOR first identifies the decisive error step and decisive error agent through automated failure attribution, then employs counterfactual reasoning to generate a corrected decisive error step, and finally engages only the decisive error agent to produce targeted reflections. Experimental results show DoCtOR achieves 22%, 26%, and 27% improvements over initial success rates on HotPotQA, ChartQAPro, and Mind2Web datasets, outperforming Reflexion, Retroformer, and COPPER. We further establish the generalizability of our diagnose-then-correct paradigm and demonstrate that in low-resource settings, focusing reflection on reasoning steps after the decisive error step achieves comparable quality to reflecting on the complete failure trajectory.

</details>


### [132] [RECAST: Recent & Context-Aware Sampling for Test-Time Adaptation in Streaming Biosignals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28271)
*Yong-Yeon Jo,Junho Song,Joon-myoung Kwon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces RECAST, a sampling module for test-time adaptation in streaming biosignal models, which selects the most useful buffered samples based on recency, contextual similarity, and predictive reliability to improve blood-pressure estimation accuracy with low latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Streaming biosignals such as blood pressure signals change from person to person and drift over time, causing models trained on population data to lose accuracy during long-term use. Test-time adaptation can personalize models online by updating them on new incoming data, but a core unresolved issue is determining which samples from the buffered stream should be used for each adaptation step: using all samples mixes irrelevant information, while using only the latest makes updates noisy and unstable. There is thus a need for a principled, efficient way to pick useful samples for adaptation in continuous monitoring settings.

Method: The authors propose RECAST (REcent & Context-Aware Sampling for TTA), a lightweight sampling module that plugs into existing buffered test-time adaptation frameworks without changing the underlying model or training objective. For each adaptation batch, RECAST scores buffered samples using three signals—temporal recency, contextual similarity to the current segment (reflecting current physiological state), and predictive reliability (e.g., model confidence or consistency)—and then builds the update batch from samples that best balance these criteria. This changes only which data points are used for adaptation and not how adaptation itself is performed.

Result: On two blood-pressure datasets, integrating RECAST into TTA frameworks yields improved blood-pressure estimation accuracy and better tracking of temporal trends compared with baseline methods and ablated versions that omit some of RECAST’s signals. The improvements are statistically significant at the per-patient level across both datasets, with broad gains on a regular benchmark and particularly strong benefits for the most challenging patients in an emergency-department scenario. Computationally, RECAST remains practical, adding less than a second of additional latency per segment on a single GPU and CPU core.

Conclusion: RECAST offers an effective and efficient solution to the sample selection problem in test-time adaptation for streaming biosignals by leveraging recency, contextual similarity, and predictive reliability to pick informative adaptation batches. It enhances accuracy and robustness of blood-pressure estimation in both standard and challenging clinical settings without modifying model architectures or loss functions and incurs only modest computational overhead, making it suitable for real-time or near–real-time deployment.

Abstract: Streaming biosignals vary across subjects and drift over time, so population-trained models lose accuracy during long-term monitoring. Test-time adaptation (TTA) enables online personalization by updating the model on incoming samples. But in a stream, a basic question is left open: \emph{which samples should drive each update?} Using all buffered samples blurs the update with irrelevant segments. Using only the latest segment makes the update noisy and unstable. The most useful samples are recent, aligned with the current physiological state, and reliable enough to learn from. We propose \textbf{RECAST} (REcent \& Context-Aware Sampling for TTA), a lightweight sampling module for buffered TTA frameworks. RECAST builds each adaptation batch from three signals: temporal recency, contextual similarity, and predictive reliability. It changes only which samples are used, leaving the model and the training objective unchanged. On two blood-pressure datasets, RECAST improves estimation accuracy and trend tracking over baselines and ablations. The per-patient gains are statistically significant on both datasets, with broad improvement on the regular benchmark and gains concentrated on the hardest patients in the emergency-department setting. RECAST stays practical, adding only sub-second latency per segment on a single GPU and CPU core.

</details>


### [133] [LoopArena: Benchmarking Models as Runtime Controllers for Loop Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28281)
*Yi Wang,Haopeng Zhang,Chengxiang Huang,Rui Dai,Kaikui Liu,Piotr Koniusz,Xiangxiang Chu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LoopArena is a benchmark to evaluate how well a separate controller model can orchestrate a coding agent via iterative loops, rather than evaluating coding ability alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations conflate the capabilities of a coding model with the quality of the control loop around it; success or failure of an end-to-end run does not reveal whether issues come from the loop design or the agent’s coding ability. As loop-based “coding agents” become common, there is a need to separately measure and improve the controller that plans, monitors, and decides next actions over long horizons.

Method: The authors introduce LoopArena, a benchmark that separates the roles of a Controller and a Worker (coding agent). After each coding round, the Controller receives a structured summary of the run and must decide what the Worker should do next (e.g., what to code or verify) or whether to stop. LoopArena defines three evaluation types: Type I evaluates next-step Loop Contract selection without running the Worker, using execution-validated questions; Type II evaluates repeated control over a partial slice of a full task with real execution; Type III evaluates complete end-to-end tasks from the initial state with the Controller–Worker pair. The Worker model is fixed so differences in results reflect Controller quality.

Result: On full tasks (Type III), the best Strict Success Rate achieved is 24.69%, indicating that current controllers are far from mastering long-horizon loop control.
