Despite this, controllers substantially reduce estimated inference cost by an average of 64.4% compared to baselines, showing efficiency gains. Rankings of controllers under Type II correlate strongly with full-task performance under the main Core criterion (Spearman’s ρ = 0.9747), suggesting that the cheaper partial-task evaluation is predictive of full-task behavior.

Conclusion: LoopArena effectively isolates and measures the ability of controller models to guide coding agents through long-running tasks, highlighting that long-horizon loop control remains a major challenge. The strong correlation between partial and full-task evaluations supports using the benchmark’s cheaper settings for iterative development. The work provides open benchmark data and code to foster research on better control strategies for coding agents.

Abstract: Loop Engineering is emerging as a practice for organizing development work around coding agents. Instead of writing each prompt by hand, practitioners design loops that monitor progress, assign work, run checks, and decide what the agent should do next. Even with a capable coding agent, a loop may trust a stale progress note, skip needed verification, spend its budget in the wrong direction, or stop before the task is safe to submit. Yet the final outcome of one end-to-end run cannot tell whether success or failure reflects the loop's guidance or the coding agent's ability to carry out the task. We introduce LoopArena, a benchmark for evaluating how well one model can guide a separate coding agent through a long-running task. The model under evaluation is the \textbf{Controller}: after each coding round, it receives a structured summary of the run and instructs a separate, fixed coding agent, the \textbf{Worker}, on what to do or verify next, or decides whether to stop. LoopArena evaluates this ability in three complementary settings that differ in execution scope and cost. Type I scores next-step Loop Contract selection through execution-validated questions without running the Worker at evaluation time. Type II executes repeated control over a selected slice of a full task, while Type III evaluates the paired full task from its original state. On full tasks, the best observed Strict Success Rate is \textbf{24.69\%}, leaving substantial room for improvement in long-horizon loop control. Across Controllers, the paired reduction in estimated inference cost averages \textbf{64.4\%}, and Type II produces a similar ordering under the main Core criterion (Spearman's \(ρ=\textbf{0.9747}\)). We release the benchmark data and evaluation code at https://github.com/AMAP-ML/LoopArena .

</details>


### [134] [Memristive-Friendly Hadamard Reservoir Computing: Structured, Multiplier-Free Recurrences at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28295)
*Andrea Ceni,Gianluca Milano,Carlo Ricciardi,Claudio Gallicchio*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a highly efficient, structured-orthogonal recurrent operator for reservoir computing that replaces dense recurrent matrices, enabling multiplier-free, low-memory, and fast reservoirs while preserving or improving performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional reservoir computing architectures, including memristive-friendly ones, typically use dense recurrent matrices that are costly in hardware: they need many parameters, multipliers, and memory, which limits scalability and neuromorphic implementation. There is a need for reservoir structures that are both mathematically sound (e.g., with good echo state properties) and highly efficient in terms of computation and hardware resources.

Method: The authors replace the dense recurrent matrix with a structured orthogonal operator composed of sign-diagonal matrices, a permutation, and a fast Walsh-Hadamard transform (FWHT). This operator is never instantiated as a full matrix; instead, it is applied via fast transforms, resulting in multiplier-free computation with O(N) parameters and O(N log N) operations per recurrent step. They instantiate this operator in both standard Echo State Networks and in memristive-friendly RC with one binary input connection per unit. They provide mathematical analysis of echo state conditions and noise response, and perform extensive empirical evaluations on multiple classification and regression benchmarks, including large reservoirs, as well as timing experiments on several hardware platforms and ablation studies regarding robustness to noise, quantization, device mismatch, and faults.

Result: Theoretical results show that exact orthogonality of the structured operator yields a tight echo state condition with respect to recurrent scaling, enables predictable noise behavior at design time, and ensures full state mixing in a single application. Empirically, across 20 classification and 7 regression tasks with reservoirs up to N = 8192, the structured reservoirs match the performance of dense orthogonal reservoirs and outperform cycle reservoirs, with the performance gap growing with reservoir size. Hardware timing experiments reveal that the proposed recurrent step can be up to 50× faster than dense matrix multiplication and uses 10^4× less memory. Ablation studies further characterize how performance degrades under noise, quantization, device mismatch, and discrete faults, indicating robustness and clear trade-offs.

Conclusion: Structured orthogonal operators based on sign diagonals, permutations, and Walsh-Hadamard transforms provide a practical and theoretically grounded replacement for dense recurrent matrices in reservoir computing. They preserve or improve predictive performance, significantly reduce computational and memory costs, and are well-suited to neuromorphic and memristive hardware due to their multiplier-free, parameter-efficient nature and predictable noise and echo-state behavior.

Abstract: Reservoir Computing (RC) designs Recurrent Neural Networks around a fixed, i.e., untrained, recurrent layer, and is a natural candidate for neuromorphic hardware. Memristive-friendly reservoirs derive the neuron dynamics from memristive-device kinetics, but still rely on dense recurrent matrices, which are expensive to realize physically. In this paper, we replace the dense matrix with a structured orthogonal operator, built from sign diagonals, a permutation, and a fast Walsh-Hadamard transform. The operator is multiplier-free, requires $O(N)$ parameters and $O(N\log N)$ operations per step, and is never materialized as a matrix. We instantiate it in a standard and in a memristive-friendly Echo State Network, with one binary input connection per unit.
  Our mathematical analysis shows that exact orthogonality yields an echo state condition that is tight in the recurrent scaling, and a noise response that is predictable at design time. Moreover, the operator mixes the whole state in a single application. Experiments on twenty classification and seven regression benchmarks, at reservoir sizes up to $N = 8192$, show that the structured models match dense orthogonal reservoirs, and achieve better mean performance than the cycle reservoir by a margin that widens with size. Furthermore, we time the recurrent step on three hardware platforms, where it is up to $50\times$ faster than a dense product and $10^4\times$ smaller in memory. Finally, we ablate the operator and measure the response to noise, quantization, device mismatch and discrete faults.

</details>


### [135] [MAIL: Memory-driven, Adaptive, Incremental, and Literature-grounded Framework for Hypothesis Generation in Chemistry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28315)
*Mahdi Babaei,Xueshen Li,Yutao Kuang,Jolene P. Reid,Yu Gan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAIL is a memory-augmented LLM framework that incrementally reads chemistry literature to autonomously generate novel, mechanistically plausible chemical hypotheses, outperforming baselines and expert benchmarks on multiple datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The chemical literature is growing too fast for humans to manually synthesize all available knowledge and derive new, testable hypotheses efficiently. Current LLM-based approaches are constrained by static corpora, rigid heuristics, and human-intensive pipelines, which restrict scalability, adaptability over time, and the novelty/quality of generated hypotheses. There is a need for an autonomous, dynamic system that can reason over evolving literature, retain and reuse prior insights, and produce high-quality, experimentally meaningful chemical hypotheses.

Method: The authors introduce MAIL (Memory-augmented, Adaptive, Incremental, and Literature-grounded), which casts hypothesis generation as a temporally grounded reasoning process. An LLM is augmented with an evolving memory that accumulates concepts and findings from literature over time, builds and updates a conceptual path, and adaptively reinterprets prior knowledge as new information arrives. The system automatically explores literature, stores and recalls relevant knowledge, and uses this evolving context to propose new chemical hypotheses without heavy predefined heuristics or continuous human steering. Performance is evaluated on the TOMATO-Chem dataset and a new high-novelty Nature/Science-style dataset (HN-NS).

Result: On both TOMATO-Chem and the new HN-NS benchmark, MAIL produces hypotheses that are structurally coherent and mechanistically plausible, as judged by domain experts. Quantitatively, it achieves the best MIOS (methodological idea overlap score) and MPOS (methodological process overlap score), indicating superior recovery of the key ideas and methodological elements underlying historical ground-truth hypotheses. Expert evaluations also rate MAIL highest overall for scientific quality compared to baselines.

Conclusion: The MAIL framework shows that LLMs, when equipped with adaptive memory and incremental literature grounding, can autonomously explore chemical research spaces and generate hypotheses that balance novelty with chemical plausibility. This approach overcomes limitations of static or heavily human-curated methods and suggests a path toward scalable, automated hypothesis generation pipelines in chemistry and potentially other scientific domains.

Abstract: The ever-expanding volume of the chemical literature offers unprecedented opportunities to generate novel and impactful hypotheses. However, the bottleneck lies in efficiently navigating this vast knowledge base to formulate high-quality, experimentally meaningful insights.
  While Large Language Models (LLMs) show promise for this task, existing methods often rely on static inspiration corpora, predefined heuristics, or laborious human-in-the-loop pipelines and decision-support frameworks that limit scalability and novelty. In this work, we propose an automated approach, a Memory-augmented, Adaptive, Incremental, and Literature-grounded (MAIL) framework for hypothesis generation in chemistry. Our MAIL method formulates hypothesis generation as a temporally grounded, memory-driven reasoning process, where hypotheses emerge from an evolving conceptual path that continuously accumulates and reinterprets prior knowledge. We evaluated the MAIL framework on a public TOMATO-Chem dataset and a newly curated and disseminated high-novelty nature/science challenge (HN-NS) dataset. Across both datasets, MAIL generates structurally coherent and mechanistically plausible hypotheses, achieves the highest MIOS and MPOS by more effectively recovering the central ideas and methodological elements of the historical target hypotheses, and obtains the highest overall expert-evaluation scores for scientific quality. These results demonstrate the potential of LLMs to autonomously explore chemical domains and generate hypotheses that are both innovative and chemically plausible.

</details>


### [136] [Real-Valued Hyperdimensional Sequence Representations with Hadamard Product Binding and Shift Equivariance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28334)
*Kenny Schlegel,Dmitri A. Rachkovskij,Denis Kleyko,Amy Loutfi,Stefan Streif,Evgeny Osipov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces real-valued temporal position encodings for Hyperdimensional Computing that preserve the benefits of Fractional Power Encoding while enabling efficient Hadamard-product binding and exact shift-equivariant operations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In Hyperdimensional Computing, representing sequences requires encodings that capture temporal order and support algebraic operations like binding and shifting. Fractional Power Encoding (FPE) provides similarity-preserving position vectors and shift-equivariant transformations, but its standard formulations are designed around circular convolution or complex-valued multiplication. This makes them poorly compatible with purely real-valued Hadamard product binding, which is attractive for efficiency and hardware simplicity. The paper aims to bridge this gap by creating real-valued position encodings that retain the kernel-like and shift-equivariant properties of FPE while working naturally with Hadamard product operations.

Method: The authors reinterpret Fractional Power Encoding through the lens of Random Fourier Features. They construct three real-valued position-encoding schemes: (1) a baseline derived via the inverse Fourier transform of the complex FPE representation, and (2) Sinusoid and (3) Cosine-only variants based on Random Fourier Features. These encodings map temporal positions to real-valued vectors whose inner products approximate shift-invariant kernels, similar to FPE, but are directly compatible with Hadamard product binding. The Sinusoid variant is designed to admit a closed-form algebraic shift operator, so that temporal shifts can be applied to an already-encoded sequence vector without recomputing encodings from scratch. The methods are evaluated empirically on time-series classification benchmarks, comparing accuracy and computational efficiency against standard FPE.

Result: Across time-series classification datasets, the proposed real-valued position encodings perform on par with standard Fractional Power Encoding in terms of classification accuracy. They additionally enable the use of Hadamard product binding, which is computationally simpler and more hardware-friendly than circular convolution or complex multiplication. The Sinusoid variant not only matches FPE performance but also supports an explicit, exact algebraic shift operation on the encoded sequence vector, demonstrating practical advantages in efficiency for shift-equivariant transformations.

Conclusion: Real-valued position encodings grounded in Random Fourier Features can successfully replicate the main benefits of Fractional Power Encoding—similarity preservation and shift-equivariant sequence transformations—while enabling efficient Hadamard product binding. Among the variants studied, the Sinusoid representation achieves the best balance between implementation simplicity, computational efficiency, and exact shift-equivariant behavior, making it a strong candidate for sequence encoding in Hyperdimensional Computing systems that favor real-valued operations.

Abstract: Encoding temporal order is a fundamental requirement for sequence representations in Hyperdimensional Computing. Fractional Power Encoding provides similarity-preserving position vectors whose inner products approximate shift-invariant kernels, and it supports shift-equivariant transformations of encoded sequence representations. However, standard formulations of Fractional Power Encoding are primarily designed for binding operations such as circular convolution or complex-valued multiplication, which limits their compatibility with Hadamard product binding of real-valued vectors. This paper develops real-valued position encodings motivated by Random Fourier Features, aiming to retain the desirable properties of Fractional Power Encoding while supporting Hadamard-based operations. We propose three real-valued position-encoding variants: a real-valued baseline based on the inverse Fourier transform, and Sinusoid and Cosine-only representations derived from Random Fourier Features. Among them, the Sinusoid variant provides an explicit algebraic shift operator, allowing temporal shifts to be applied directly to the vector-encoded sequence representation without re-encoding the shifted sequence. Experiments on time-series classification datasets show that the proposed real-valued representations achieve performance comparable to standard Fractional Power Encoding while enabling computationally efficient Hadamard product binding. The Sinusoid variant offers the most favorable trade-off, combining efficient real-valued implementation with exact shift-equivariant transformations.

</details>


### [137] [AGENT-O: A Semantic Agent Card Framework for Interoperable and Governed Healthcare AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28345)
*Pengze Li,Cui Tao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AGENT-O is an ontology-based framework that defines a semantic Agent Card for health AI agents and enables automated assessment of reporting completeness in research papers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Health-oriented AI agent systems are increasingly complex and multifaceted, yet their description in scientific publications is often incomplete and inconsistent, particularly around runtime architecture, governance, and reproducibility. Existing reporting guidelines and checklists lack a machine-readable, semantic representation that can systematically assess and compare how completely AI agent systems are reported. The authors aim to create a standardized, ontology-based representation to support structured documentation, automated checks, and identification of reporting gaps for AI agents in healthcare.

Method: The authors developed AGENT-O as an ontology in OWL 2/RDF to represent key aspects of AI agent systems: runtime, models, workflow, tools, clinical use, evaluation, provenance, governance, and reporting assessment. They built a semantic Agent Card profile on top of this ontology. Evaluation involved: (1) ontology inventory and consistency checks; (2) reasoning using OWL-RL; (3) three SHACL validation suites to test shapes on example graphs; (4) 12 SPARQL competency queries to verify that the ontology can answer core questions; (5) three use cases; and (6) a model-assisted workflow to assess reporting completeness of 279 published papers across five dimensions using the ontology structures.

Result: AGENT-O resulted in an ontology containing 1,962 RDF triples and 1,922 Protégé axioms, including 252 active classes, 198 active object properties, and 51 datatype properties. All SHACL validation suites successfully conformed on their example graphs, and all 12 competency SPARQL queries returned the expected evidence, demonstrating that the ontology supports the intended queries. The reporting-completeness workflow successfully scored all 279 papers. The analysis showed substantial incompleteness in reporting for runtime/architecture (84.6% incomplete), governance/safety (82.8%), and provenance/reproducibility (78.1%), whereas evaluation (25.8% incomplete) and benchmark-process alignment (29.8% incomplete) were relatively better reported.

Conclusion: AGENT-O enables a semantic Agent Card representation and automated assessment of reporting completeness for health-oriented AI agent systems. It uncovers a notable evaluation-specification gap: researchers more reliably describe evaluation and benchmark procedures than they do runtime architecture, governance, and reproducibility aspects. The framework offers a reusable ontology, a structured Agent Card profile, and a workflow for reporting-completeness assessment and gap identification. However, AGENT-O is limited to describing and checking completeness of reporting; it does not evaluate the intrinsic quality of agents or their readiness for clinical deployment.

Abstract: AGENT-O is a modular ontology framework that defines a semantic Agent Card for representing health-oriented AI agent systems and supports assessment of reporting completeness in scientific publications. AGENT-O was developed as an OWL 2/RDF ontology covering runtime, models, workflow, tools, clinical use, evaluation, provenance, governance, and reporting assessment. Evaluation included ontology inventory, OWL-RL reasoning, three SHACL suites, 12 SPARQL competency queries, three cases, and model-assisted reporting-completeness assessment of 279 papers across five dimensions. The ontology contained 1,962 RDF triples and 1,922 Protege axioms, with 252 active classes, 198 active object properties, and 51 datatype properties. All SHACL suites conformed on example graphs, all competency queries returned prespecified evidence, and all 279 papers were scored. Incomplete reporting was highest for runtime/architecture (84.6%), governance/safety (82.8%), and provenance/reproducibility (78.1%), compared with evaluation (25.8%) and benchmark-process alignment (29.8%). AGENT-O supported semantic Agent Card representation and reporting assessment while revealing an evaluation-specification gap: evaluation and benchmark procedures were reported more consistently than runtime architecture, governance, and reproducibility. AGENT-O provides a reusable ontology, semantic Agent Card profile, and reporting-completeness workflow for structured reporting and gap identification, but does not assess agent quality or deployment readiness.

</details>


### [138] [Propagating construction-time knowledge quality into medical question answering: A framework grounded in clinical guidelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28360)
*Jie Hu,Junjie Wang,Shan Lu,Yifang Hu,Gong Cheng,Yun Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They introduce a quality-aware framework that carries triple-quality signals from KG construction into KG-augmented clinical QA, improving evidence selection and answer reliability on Chinese diabetes guidelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KG construction from clinical guidelines using LLMs produces triples of uneven structural validity and evidential grounding, but downstream graph-based QA systems mostly ignore these construction-time quality assessments, focusing only on query relevance at retrieval. This wastes rich information about which triples are trustworthy and creates a gap between KG construction quality control and inference-time evidence use, which is especially problematic in safety-critical clinical applications.

Method: They define a per-triple quality signal Q(t) that fuses two complementary dimensions: SchemaConf (how well triples conform to a schema) and EvidScore (how well triples are supported by underlying guideline text). Instead of discarding low-quality triples, they store Q(t) and derived quality tiers as graph attributes. These attributes are then propagated into two components of the QA pipeline: (1) quality-weighted subgraph retrieval, which biases retrieval toward higher-quality triples while preserving the original text provenance; and (2) tier-conditioned evidence prompting, which informs the LLM about the quality tiers of retrieved evidence when generating answers. They evaluate on KGs built from Chinese diabetes clinical guidelines under distribution shifts across guideline versions and LLM generators.

Result: The fused Q(t) signal outperforms its components in discriminating triple quality under cross-version and cross-model shifts, achieving an AUC of 0.748 versus 0.703 for EvidScore alone and 0.645 for SchemaConf alone. In guideline-grounded QA, using construction-time quality during retrieval and prompting reduces omission of required knowledge from 16.3% to 5.3% and conflicting outputs from 16.3% to 2.7%. The framework attains an evidence-grounded precision of 81.6% with nearly no invalid citations. Blinded clinician raters prefer the full framework over no retrieval (mean scores 4.68 vs. 4.21 on a 5-point scale), and performance approaches an oracle evidence condition (4.80). Similar benefits are observed when changing the answer-generation model, indicating robustness.

Conclusion: Persisting and propagating triple-level quality assessments from KG construction into downstream QA yields more reliable and clinically acceptable guideline-grounded answers. Modeling structural schema conformance and evidential support as complementary signals, and using their fused measure Q(t) during retrieval and prompting, improves triple-quality discrimination under distribution shifts and reduces omissions, conflicts, and invalid citations in clinical QA. This demonstrates that construction-time quality metadata can serve as a durable signal for evidence selection and presentation in graph-augmented LLM systems.

Abstract: Large language models have facilitated knowledge graph (KG) construction from clinical guidelines, but extracted triples vary in structural validity and evidential support. Meanwhile, graph-augmented question answering (QA) systems typically optimize query relevance during retrieval, with limited reuse of quality information produced during KG construction. This creates a disconnect between construction-time quality control and inference-time evidence use. We investigate whether construction-time triple quality can serve as a persistent signal for downstream evidence selection and presentation. We propose a quality-aware framework that models structural conformance (SchemaConf) and evidential support (EvidScore) as complementary dimensions and fuses them into a per-triple quality signal, Q(t). Rather than using quality solely for filtering, the framework retains Q(t) and derived quality tiers as graph attributes and propagates them into quality-weighted subgraph retrieval and tier-conditioned evidence prompting, while preserving passage-level provenance. Experiments on Chinese diabetes clinical guidelines show that the utility of the quality signal is distribution dependent. Under cross-version and cross-model shift, the fused Q(t) provides stronger triple-quality discrimination than either component alone (AUC 0.748 vs. 0.703 for EvidScore and 0.645 for SchemaConf). In guideline-grounded QA, propagating construction-time quality reduces required-knowledge omission from 16.3% to 5.3% and conflicting outputs from 16.3% to 2.7%, with an evidence-grounded precision of 81.6% and near-zero invalid citations. Blinded clinician ratings favor the full framework over no retrieval (4.68 vs. 4.21 on a five-point scale) and approach the oracle condition (4.80), while cross-generator experiments show consistent trends.

</details>


### [139] [GRACE:Gradient-guided Coreset Selection for LLM Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28361)
*Praveen Bushipaka,Andrea D'Angelo,Lucia Passaro,Tommaso Cucinotta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose GRACE, a gradient-guided coreset selection method to automatically build both forget and retain datasets for LLM unlearning, improving utility while keeping similar forgetting quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing machine unlearning for LLMs assumes that we are given clean, pre-defined forget and retain sets. In realistic situations, users only provide a handful of examples of the behavior they want removed, and those must be expanded into larger forget and retain sets drawn from heterogeneous data. There is currently no principled, scalable way to infer these sets so that unlearning is both effective (forgets what is requested) and preserves model utility elsewhere.

Method: GRACE is a gradient-guided coreset construction procedure. It (1) takes a small seed set of examples that exhibit the undesired behavior and computes a ‘forget direction’ in gradient space; (2) selects a compact forget coreset whose gradients best approximate this forget direction using non-negative orthogonal matching pursuit; and (3) to preserve utility, it selects retain examples only from gradient components orthogonal to the forget direction, applying a clustered orthogonal matching pursuit in this projected gradient space. The output is a pair of compact yet representative forget and retain sets that can be fed to any downstream unlearning algorithm.

Result: On two target domains, two different LLM families, and four different unlearning algorithms, GRACE yields better overall model utility than competing selection baselines while maintaining similar quality of forgetting the targeted behavior. The gains over previous gradient-based selection methods are especially consistent across settings.

Conclusion: Coreset selection for unlearning data can and should be informed by gradient geometry relative to the undesired behavior. By explicitly modelling a forget direction and enforcing orthogonality when choosing retain data, GRACE provides a general, algorithm-agnostic way to build effective forget and retain sets, enabling more practical and higher-utility LLM unlearning from limited user-provided examples.

Abstract: Machine Unlearning methods for Large Language Models typically assume pre-specified forget and retain sets. In realistic settings, however, requests may provide only a few examples of undesired behavior, requiring forget and retain sets to be inferred from heterogeneous corpora. We study this data-selection problem and propose GRACE , a gradient-guided coreset selection method that constructs both forget and retain sets for LLM unlearning. GRACE first computes a forget direction from seed examples that elicit the undesired behavior, then selects a compact forget coreset whose gradients approximate this direction using non-negative orthogonal matching pursuit. To preserve model utility, it selects retain examples after projecting out the forget direction and applying clustered orthogonal matching pursuit in the remaining gradient space. Across two target domains, two model families, and four unlearning algorithms, GRACE improves model utility while maintaining comparable forget quality, with particularly consistent gains over prior gradient-based selection methods.

</details>


### [140] [EvoUndo: Recoverability-Constrained Self-Evolution for LLM Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28363)
*Tanmay Sah,Dolly Sah,Harshul Jain,Tanya Sah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces EvoUndo, a framework to verify and repair whether LLM agents’ self-modifications can be safely undone across different future states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents increasingly change their own prompts, tools, and runtime setups to improve performance. Some of these self-modifications succeed in the moment but leave behind changes that cannot be safely reversed later, especially when the agent’s state has changed. This creates reliability and safety risks for long-running or autonomous systems. There is a need for a principled way to represent such self-evolution, check whether it is recoverable across counterfactual states, and repair unrecoverable cases.

Method: The authors propose EvoUndo, a framework that: (1) defines recovery representations (a recovery language/calculus) for how to undo self-modifications; (2) synthesizes potential recovery procedures; (3) diagnoses whether a given self-modification is recoverable across different counterfactual states; and (4) independently verifies recoverability. They run 600 one-shot self-evolution tasks on LLM agents, identify mutations that improve capability but fail recoverability, and then evaluate various repair and oracle strategies under different recovery languages (L0 vs an extended calculus) and state-grounding conditions (including exact state-address grounding). A protocol-locked 2×2 experiment manipulates grounding (coarse vs exact state addressing) and expressivity (original vs extended recovery language) to identify which factors drive successful recovery.

Result: From 600 tasks, they find 197 beneficial self-modifications that are not verifiably recoverable. Naive or conventional repair strategies cannot fix any of these under the original recovery representation (0/197). Using a deterministic oracle with the original language L0 recovers 48/197. Extending the recovery calculus raises oracle recovery to 191/197, showing a big expressivity effect. In a controlled 2×2 intervention, adding exact state-address grounding raises recovery from 0/48 to 38/48 (79.2%) when L0 is actually sufficient, while extending the language yields 142/143 (99.3%) recovery in the oracle S1 stratum. On the main gpt-oss-120b model, combining richer language with exact-address diagnostics slightly hurts recovery to 133/143 (93.0%), a negative interaction not seen with Qwen3.8-27B, suggesting model-specific behavior.

Conclusion: Reliable self-evolving LLM agents cannot depend solely on iterative prompting or ad hoc repair. Robust self-modification requires jointly designing: (1) the verification procedures that check recoverability; (2) how the agent grounds and references its internal state (state addressing); (3) the semantics and witnesses used to show that a modification is recoverable; and (4) the expressivity of the recovery language itself. With appropriate co-design of these elements, most beneficial but initially unrecoverable self-modifications become safely recoverable; without it, even beneficial mutations can leave the system in states that are hard or impossible to undo in future contexts.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly modify their own prompts, tools, middleware, resources, and execution harnesses at runtime. Such self-evolution can improve capability, but a successful mutation may leave persistent effects that cannot be safely reversed in states different from the one in which it was created. We introduce EvoUndo, a framework for representing, synthesizing, diagnosing, and independently verifying recoverability of model-generated self-modifications across counterfactual states. Across 600 unseen one-shot self-evolution tasks, we identify 197 capability-improving mutations that fail recoverability verification. Under the original recovery representation, conventional repair strategies recover 0/197 of these natural failures. Deterministic oracle analysis recovers 48/197 under the original recovery language L0, while the extended recovery calculus increases empirical oracle recovery to 191/197. A protocol-locked 2x2 grounding-by-expressivity intervention then separates two bottlenecks: exact state-address grounding increases successful recovery from 0/48 to 38/48 (79.2%) when the original language is sufficient, while extending the recovery language enables recovery on 142/143 (99.3%) failures in the oracle-defined S1 stratum. On the primary gpt-oss-120b backbone, adding exact-address diagnostics to the richer language reduces recovery to 133/143 (93.0%); a Qwen3.8-27B replication preserves the grounding and expressivity effects but not this negative interaction, indicating that the latter is model-dependent. These results indicate that reliable agent self-evolution requires co-designing verification, state grounding, witness semantics, and recovery-language expressivity rather than relying on iterative prompting alone.

</details>


### [141] [MAP: A Benchmark on Multimodal Accessibility Planning for Real World Places](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28384)
*Jason Armitage,Ioannis Tsochantaridis,Linda Mazzone,Chuqiao Yan,Srini Narayanan,Sarah Ebling*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents MAP, a benchmark for evaluating multimodal AI systems that help users with accessibility needs plan visits to real-world locations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal AI systems are not systematically evaluated for how well they support users with accessibility requirements in real-world, place-based planning. Accessibility information about points of interest is often incomplete, unreliable, or changes over time, and there is a need to rigorously measure whether AI assistants can correctly verify and recommend locations that meet specific accessibility needs using both textual and visual data.

Method: The authors design MAP, a benchmark composed of tasks where AI systems receive user requests to verify or recommend points of interest satisfying stated accessibility requirements. MAP includes two key assessments: (1) Claim verification for accessibility planning, where systems must determine whether given claims about accessibility features at specific places are supported and identify locations that meet the requested features; and (2) Visual evidence retrieval for accessibility planning, where systems must select appropriate visual evidence (e.g., images) that demonstrate the relevant accessibility features of a place. The benchmark combines automatic scoring with human evaluation on a subset of responses and incorporates a methodology for periodically refreshing ground-truth place and accessibility information to handle real-world changes over time.

Result: MAP provides a structured way to compare multimodal AI systems in the context of accessibility-focused place planning. The benchmark successfully operationalizes the two new assessments—claim verification and visual evidence retrieval—and demonstrates that performance can be automatically rated with supplemental human judgments for a portion of system outputs. It also shows that the periodic refresh mechanism allows evaluation to stay aligned with evolving real-world accessibility data.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that MAP is an effective and novel benchmark for evaluating multimodal AI assistants in accessibility-oriented trip and place planning tasks. By focusing on claim verification and visual evidence retrieval, and by incorporating time-aware updating of ground truth, MAP enables more realistic, robust, and comparable assessments of how well AI systems can support users with accessibility needs when choosing and validating places to visit.

Abstract: We introduce MAP, the first benchmark to evaluate multimodal AI systems as assistants for users with accessibility requirements when planning visits to places in the real world. In our evaluation, systems are presented with requests to verify or recommend a point of interest meeting an accessibility requirement. MAP contains two novel assessments: Claim verification for accessibility planning assesses if information on places and stated accessibility features is supported and identifies places that satisfy requested accessibility features. Visual evidence retrieval for accessibility planning checks if a multimodal AI system can select visual evidence for the requested place and accessibility feature. Our methodology supports comparison of AI systems in a setting where place information and accessibility information can change over time by evaluating systems and refreshing ground truth data at scheduled times. The benchmark is based on automatic rating and human rating for a proportion of responses.

</details>


### [142] [Timing-Aware Repurchase Prediction for Web-Scale E-Commerce: Survival Models for Multi-Surface Grocery Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28393)
*Akshay Kekuda,Shreeranjani Srirangamsridharan,Ishan Bhatt,Yanan Cao,Sinduja Subramaniam,Evren Korpeoglu,Kaushiki Nag,Kannan Achan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They replace multiple binary repurchase models with a single survival model that predicts time-to-repurchase, showing better efficiency, strong ranking, and carefully studied calibration for e-commerce grocery data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing repurchase recommenders treat each prediction horizon (e.g., buy again in 7, 14, 30 days) as a separate binary task, requiring a different model per horizon. This is inefficient and ignores the continuous nature of time-to-repurchase. The authors want a unified, time-aware model that can provide both accurate rankings and well-calibrated probabilities across multiple horizons, and they wish to understand the empirical hazard structure of grocery repurchase behavior.

Method: They model time-to-repurchase using survival analysis, specifically parametric Accelerated Failure Time (AFT) models (e.g., Exponential, Weibull, Log-Normal) trained on large-scale grocery e-commerce data. They conduct an empirical hazard analysis, compare different parametric forms for marginal and conditional fit, and evaluate many ablation configurations. They then introduce a 4-parameter calibration layer that maps raw survival CDF outputs to accurate per-horizon repurchase probabilities without cross-horizon monotonicity violations, and compare calibration and ranking performance across AFT variants.

Result: (1) Hazard analysis shows a slightly decreasing marginal hazard (shape k≈0.9), contradicting the common assumption of increasing hazard for groceries; Log-Normal provides the best marginal fit (R²≈0.998) and ranking, while Weibull fits conditional residuals best, leading to an analyzed discrepancy. (2) A single AFT model matches or surpasses three separate per-horizon binary classifiers while using about 3× fewer trees, and the survival objective changes feature importance, emphasizing channel-cadence and recency over aggregate frequency. (3) The 4-parameter calibration ensures monotone probabilities across horizons; Exponential AFT achieves very low expected calibration error (~1e-4), about 10× better than Log-Normal, while all AFT variants have extremely similar ranking performance (within 0.3% relative).

Conclusion: Survival-based time-to-repurchase models can replace stacks of per-horizon binary classifiers in e-commerce, offering more efficient training, strong or superior ranking, and flexible, well-calibrated probability estimates across horizons. There is an inherent trade-off between ranking quality and calibration quality within the AFT family: Log-Normal is best for ranking, whereas Exponential AFT is best for probability calibration, making it suitable for applications that consume probabilities directly.

Abstract: Repurchase recommenders in e-commerce are commonly framed as a binary question asking "will this customer buy this item within W days", a formulation that requires a separately trained model for every horizon of interest. We replace this stack with survival models that predict time-to-repurchase directly, and evaluate them on millions of customers from a major grocery e-commerce platform across more than thirty ablation configurations. Our study makes three contributions. First, an empirical hazard analysis reveals a slightly decreasing marginal hazard (k ~ 0.9), differing from the common intuition that grocery items become more likely to be repurchased the longer since the last purchase (increasing hazard, k > 1). Log-Normal achieves the best marginal fit (R^2 = 0.998) and the best ranking, despite Weibull providing the best conditional residual fit, revealing an apparent discrepancy we analyze in detail. Second, a single Accelerated Failure Time (AFT) model replaces three per-horizon binary classifiers, matching or exceeding each at its own horizon while using roughly 3x fewer total trees. Feature importance reshuffles under the survival objective: channel-cadence and recency signals rise while aggregate frequency counts fall. Third, a 4-parameter parametric calibration maps raw survival CDFs to per-horizon probabilities with zero cross-horizon monotonicity violations. Calibration quality varies by an order of magnitude across the AFT family: Exponential AFT (Weibull k=1) achieves expected calibration error (ECE) ~1e-4, roughly 10x lower than Log-Normal, while ranking metrics agree within 0.3% relative. We adopt Exponential AFT for probability-consuming surfaces and Log-Normal for pure ranking, exposing a principled calibration-ranking trade-off within a single AFT family.

</details>


### [143] [RetailAgent: Structured Adverse Timing in Self-Conditioned Multimodal LLM Trading Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28399)
*Yupeng Zhang,Liuyuan Jiang,Hongyi Huang,Bingheng Li,Lisha Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper tests whether large language model (LLM) trading agents show predictable directional patterns when trading intraday stocks and finds that they systematically time the market poorly in a stable, measurable way.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In markets, if a trader follows a systematic rule that maps past prices to future actions, other participants may learn and exploit that rule if it is predictable. LLM-based agents are increasingly used for sequential decision-making, including trading, but it is unclear whether their behavior contains stable directional patterns that could be detected and potentially arbitraged. The authors want to understand whether LLM agents’ trading policies show such exploitable structure.

Method: The authors build RetailAgent, an experimental setup where an LLM repeatedly decides for each short intraday interval whether to be long (hold the stock) or flat (stay out) based only on anonymized intraday price paths and allowed state. After each decision, the next interval’s return is revealed, and the process repeats. They measure timing by comparing the realized returns during intervals when the agent is long versus flat along the same stock’s path, adjusting for its overall propensity to be long (exposure-matched). They test this across different modalities, horizons, state inputs, and LLM model families. They also randomly shuffle saved action sequences to break the temporal alignment, and separately allow the agent to condition on its own past decisions via self-authored memories to study persistence.

Result: Across models, settings, and horizons, the exposure-matched timing measure is persistently negative: the LLM agents tend to be long in intervals that subsequently perform worse than those when they are flat. When the researchers shuffle action sequences (breaking the alignment between actions and subsequent returns), this negative timing sharply diminishes, implying that the effect is specifically tied to how the policy maps states to actions over time. Adding self-authored memory increases the persistence of the trading policy and worsens timing, and days where the agent makes use of both actions (long and flat) show even more negative timing outcomes.

Conclusion: LLM trading agents display stable, predictable directional structure in their sequential financial decisions: they systematically choose positions that are negatively timed relative to subsequent returns. This structure is robust across model families and settings and can be recovered from observed behavior, suggesting that such agents may be exploitable by other market participants. The findings provide a clear behavioral signal and a testbed for studying how strategic opponents might respond to predictable or miscalibrated LLM-based trading policies.

Abstract: In financial markets, a sequential policy that reacts systematically to price movements may become predictable to other market participants. This paper studies whether large language model (LLM) agents exhibit such directional structure through RetailAgent, an experimental framework in which an LLM observes anonymized intraday equity price histories and permitted state, then repeatedly chooses long (hold the stock) or flat (stay out) before the subsequent interval return is revealed. We compare returns during long and flat intervals along the same stock's intraday path after removing the overall fraction of long decisions. This exposure-matched measure reveals persistent negative timing across modality, horizon, state, and model family. Shuffling saved action sequences substantially attenuates the effect, showing that alignment between actions and subsequent returns drives the negative score. Feeding self-authored memories into decisions further increases policy persistence, while timing becomes more negative among stock-days on which the agent uses both actions. These results reveal stable, recoverable directional structure in sequential LLM financial decisions and a behavioral signal for studying how another participant could respond to a predictable policy.

</details>


### [144] [VERA-8B: Evidence-Grounded Audit Risk Reasoning from SEC Filings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28402)
*Menghan Liu,Elynn Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces VERA-8B, an audit-focused language model and system designed to generate evidence-grounded, auditable judgments, including abstention when evidence is insufficient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional financial language models optimize for fluent, general reasoning, often producing plausible but weakly grounded answers. This is problematic in auditing, where conclusions must be tightly supported by explicit evidence and unjustified claims are unacceptable. Additionally, there has been no prior ML work specifically targeting pre-enforcement audit risk prediction, leaving a gap for tools that can identify audit risks early enough to be practically useful for auditors and regulators.

Method: The authors develop VERA-8B, an end-to-end audit reasoning system. Methodologically, they (1) construct a model trained with a unified combination of supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and GRPO-style preference optimization under a single, explicit evidence standard; (2) incorporate mechanisms for abstention and uncertainty qualification so the model can defer on cases where evidence is incomplete or confidence is low; and (3) build an "AuditBridge" pipeline that converts raw financial filings into verified structured records and then into reviewer-ready reports, thereby grounding the model’s reasoning in checked data suitable for audit workflows.

Result: VERA-8B outperforms all evaluated baseline models on tasks related to evidence-grounded audit reasoning and pre-enforcement audit risk identification. It is able to produce more reliable, explicitly supported outputs, and can recognize and flag cases where the available evidence is insufficient, rather than hallucinating confident answers. The AuditBridge component successfully turns unstructured filings into verified, structured inputs and professional-style audit reports.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that audit-focused language models need to be explicitly optimized for evidence-grounded reasoning rather than mere fluency. By unifying SFT and GRPO under a single evidence standard, adding principled abstention mechanisms, and grounding outputs through the AuditBridge data pipeline, VERA-8B demonstrates that auditable, review-ready automated reasoning is feasible for practical audit work and can surpass generic financial LMs on relevant tasks.

Abstract: Across audit applications, judgments must be supported by reasonable evidence. However, standard financial language models prioritize fluency over evidence. They are built for general financial reasoning and may produce plausible but ambiguous answers, creating a grounding gap that makes them unsuitable for audit work. We address this gap with VERA-8B, a new end-to-end audit reasoning system that identifies audit risks before enforcement actions occur. Constructing such a model raises several challenges, as no prior machine learning work targets pre-enforcement audit prediction. To our knowledge, we are the first to unify SFT and GRPO for evidence-grounded audit reasoning under one evidence standard, achieving performance that surpasses all evaluated baselines. Because auditing cannot tolerate unsupported claims, we introduce abstention and uncertainty qualification to defer uncertain or evidence-incomplete cases. Finally, we design an AuditBridge to ground model reasoning for practical audit work. It transforms raw filings into verified records and then into reviewer-ready reports, bridging finance and computation with broad generality. Together, these components produce auditable, review-ready outputs suitable for practical audit work.

</details>


### [145] [Program Learning with Verifiable Rewards: Symbolic Backpropagation for Post-Training LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28421)
*Vishvesh Bhat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes PLVR, a method that learns explicit, verifiable programs outside a language model’s weights, achieving better reasoning and code performance than RL-based post-training at similar compute cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing post-training methods like supervised finetuning and RL embed reasoning ability directly into model weights, making it opaque, hard to inspect, hard to verify step-by-step, and not easily transferable to other models. For tasks where intermediate reasoning steps can be checked (e.g., code execution, formal reasoning), it would be advantageous to represent reasoning as explicit programs that can be verified, debugged, and reused across models and tasks. The authors want a method that can learn such programs from examples, with dense, per-step feedback instead of sparse terminal rewards, and that reduces the data and compute cost of adapting models to new reasoning-heavy tasks.

Method: They introduce PLVR (Program Learning with Verifiable Rewards), a post-training procedure that learns explicit programs composed of deterministic and neural primitives. Each program layer is associated with a typed ontology; primitive operations have typed signatures. Given input-output training examples, PLVR performs symbolic backpropagation: it computes a loss at the program output relative to ground truth, then propagates back constraints on required input ontologies by type inference over primitive signatures. This acts as an analytic, symbolic analogue to gradient backpropagation, enabling exact credit assignment over the program structure rather than estimated rewards. The search space is constrained by a shared primitive library and type system; the backward pass guides program search over this type-admissible space. Rewards are verifiable at each step via contract checking, yielding dense feedback instead of only terminal success/failure.

Result: On LiveCodeBench v6 and Tau2Bench, PLVR applied to 30B-parameter base models outperforms reinforcement learning approaches trained with the same budget by an average of 27.8 points and even surpasses frontier models more than 10x larger by 13.6 points. A single primitive library supports both benchmarks, and adapting to a new task only requires around 100 examples for program search without additional finetuning data. An ablation replacing the loss-guided symbolic backward pass with uniform sampling over the same type-constrained space drastically reduces median program performance from 65.6 to 17.5, indicating that the main advantage comes from symbolic backpropagation rather than the type system alone. They also release an open-source symbolic backpropagation library and conformance checker.

Conclusion: Placing reasoning outside of a language model’s weights as explicit, typed programs—learned via PLVR—enables verifiable, stepwise, and transferable reasoning that can outperform standard RL-based post-training, even relative to much larger models. Symbolic backpropagation provides effective, dense credit assignment over program structure and is the key driver of PLVR’s performance gains, while a shared primitive library and conformance tools make the approach reusable across tasks without additional finetuning data.

Abstract: Post training a language model to reason means updating its weights. Supervised finetuning and reinforcement learning both place the acquired capability inside the model where it cannot be inspected cannot be checked step by step and cannot be moved to another model. We argue that for tasks whose intermediate steps admit verification, reasoning is better placed outside the base models weights as an explicit program composed from deterministic and neural primitives. We introduce PLVR (Program Learning with Verifiable Rewards): a post training method that learns such programs directly from input-output examples. Its mechanism is symbolic backpropagation: each program layer carries a typed ontology a loss is computed at the output against ground truth and required input ontologies are propagated backward by type inference over primitive signatures: an analogue of the chain rule in which credit assignment is a derivation rather than an estimate. Where RLVR verifies a terminal outcome, PLVRs reward is a per step contract verdict dense over program structure. On LiveCodeBench v6 and Tau2Bench, 30B base models with PLVR outperform RL at matched budget by 27.8 points on average and frontier models an order of magnitude larger by 13.6 points. A single primitive library serves two benchmarks, so the marginal cost of a new task is 100 examples of program search and no new finetuning data. Replacing the loss guided search with uniform sampling over the same type admissible space at equal budget collapses the median program from 65.6 to 17.5, identifying the backward pass rather than the type system as the source of the advantage. We release the symbolic backpropagation library and a conformance checker so the method can be applied to primitive libraries other than our own.

</details>


### [146] [Prove2Me: An Open Collaborative Platform for Scaling Math Formalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28433)
*Shuze Chen,Kunal Marwaha,Xiaoyang Lu,Henry Yuen,Tianyi Peng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents Prove2Me, an open platform where humans and AI agents collaboratively formalize mathematics in Lean 4, turning proof development into a scalable, crowd-sourced activity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although proof assistants can guarantee correctness of mathematics, large-scale formalization has been limited by the need for deep expertise and the time-consuming nature of writing formal proofs. Recent AI coding agents can generate Lean proofs from natural language, suggesting a new opportunity for broad, internet-scale collaboration between humans and AI, but an infrastructure for organizing and scaling such collaboration is missing.

Method: The authors design and implement Prove2Me, a web-based collaborative environment where users post formalization "missions" and AI agents submit Lean 4 proofs toward their completion. The system includes mechanisms and a specialized technical harness that allow multiple agents to build on each other’s work, reuse previously proven results, and coordinate contributions at scale.

Result: Prove2Me operationalizes a workflow in which human users specify formalization goals and AI agents iteratively produce and refine proofs in Lean 4. The platform successfully supports shared proof development and reusable results, demonstrating that agent-based collaboration on formal proofs can be organized and scaled via a dedicated infrastructure.

Conclusion: By integrating AI coding agents into a collaborative platform, Prove2Me lowers barriers to formalization and transforms formal proof development into a crowd-sourced, internet-scale activity. This approach provides a path toward large, community-driven repositories of machine-checked mathematics, open to participants who may not be experts in formal verification.

Abstract: Proof assistants such as Lean 4 promise the paradigm of formally verified mathematics, but large-scale formalization projects have faced major barriers to entry, including the need for expertise in formal verification (as well as the underlying mathematics) and the significant time required for writing formal proofs. AI coding agents have dramatically reduced these barriers; human users can now use natural language to prompt agents to write complex proofs in Lean. This opens up the intriguing possibility of internet-scale mathematical collaboration involving both humans and AI agents, where correctness is machine-checked.
  To realize this possibility, we introduce Prove2Me (https://prove2.me), an open collaborative platform for formalizing mathematics. Users launch formalization "missions", to which AI agents contribute formal proofs toward completion. We designed mechanisms and a specialized harness in Prove2Me that enable large-scale collaboration so that agents can build on one another's work and freely reuse existing results. In doing so, Prove2Me aims to turn math formalization into a scalable, crowd-sourced effort open to anyone with an agent.

</details>


### [147] [Learning to Use Tools: Reinforcement Learning for Tool-Integrated Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28447)
*Minghui Xu,Zi Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates using a calculator tool plus reinforcement learning to improve large language models’ mathematical reasoning on the Countdown numbers game, finding large gains in accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models often fail on math tasks not because they lack general reasoning ability but due to basic arithmetic mistakes and lack of verification. The Countdown task is a controlled setting where reliable computation and checkable final answers matter. The authors are motivated to understand whether explicit calculator tool use, combined with reinforcement learning on verifiable rewards, can reduce arithmetic/verification errors and significantly boost performance on this task.

Method: 1) Analyze failure cases on the Countdown task to quantify how many errors stem from incorrect calculations. 2) Design supervised fine-tuning datasets that show the model when and how to call a calculator tool and how to interpret its outputs. 3) Train a tool-using policy via SFT. 4) Further optimize this policy with several on-policy RL algorithms (RLOO, RLOO++, GRPO, DAPO) using automatically computed rewards based on whether the final answer to a Countdown problem is correct. 5) Build a new held-out benchmark of 1,024 Countdown problems with no exact overlap with training data to fairly evaluate performance. 6) Compare pass@k metrics across models with and without tool use and across different RL methods.

Result: Calculator tool integration consistently improves both the supervised fine-tuned baseline and all RL variants by about 10 percentage points across pass@k metrics. Among the RL methods, Tool-DAPO performs best: pass@1 increases from 35.8% with the tool-trained SFT model to 66.0% with Tool-DAPO. Analyses reveal that RL training leads to more effective and disciplined tool use patterns, despite the RL signal depending only on whether the final answer is correct, not on intermediate reasoning steps.

Conclusion: Explicit calculator tool calling substantially improves LLM mathematical reasoning on the Countdown task by reducing arithmetic and verification errors. Reinforcement learning on verifiable final-answer rewards further enhances performance and encourages higher-quality tool-usage strategies, even without direct supervision on intermediate reasoning. Tool-DAPO in particular yields large gains, suggesting that combining structured tool integration with suitable RL objectives is a powerful approach for strengthening LLM reasoning on math-like tasks where answers can be automatically checked.

Abstract: Current large language models (LLMs) increasingly benefit from external tool integration, especially for tasks requiring reliable computation and verification. Motivated by this, we study calculator tool calling for improving mathematical reasoning on the Countdown task. We first analyze reasoning failures and find that calculation errors account for a substantial portion of incorrect responses. We then construct supervised fine-tuning datasets to teach the model useful tool-use patterns and how to interpret returned outputs. Building on this tool-formatted policy, we apply several on-policy reinforcement learning methods, including RLOO, RLOO++, GRPO, and DAPO, using automatically verifiable final-answer rewards. To enable a more reliable evaluation, we construct a fresh 1,024-problem held-out Countdown benchmark with no exact overlap with the training data. Our results show that calculator tool integration consistently improves both SFT and RL baselines, yielding roughly 10 percentage-point gains across pass@k. Among the RL methods, Tool-DAPO achieves the strongest performance, improving pass@1 from 35.8% for Tool-SFT to 66.0%. Further analysis shows that RL encourages more effective tool use even when only final-answer rewards are provided. These findings suggest that tool integration reduces arithmetic and verification errors, while RL increases the probability of correct reasoning traces.

</details>


### [148] [COVER: Identifiable Evaluation of Coalition Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28475)
*Raghul Sugumar,Amrit Gopinath*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces COVER, a methodology to cleanly measure the impact of multi-agent routing policies by fixing the evaluation stack and team family in advance, and shows how it quantifies “route regret” and selection headroom without overstating routing benefits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-agent systems, changing the routing policy (which agents or tools are selected) also changes the messages and sometimes the downstream evaluation pathway, so a simple end-to-end accuracy difference cannot be confidently attributed to routing itself. There is a need for an auditable way to measure the causal effect of routing decisions—separating them from confounds like different scoring pipelines, interface access, or agent sets—especially when benchmarking tool-use and team-based LLM systems.

Method: The authors propose COVER, an evaluation contract that must be specified before running experiments. It fixes: (1) a public information boundary; (2) a downstream evaluation stack G (e.g., how answers are post-processed and scored); and (3) a finite legal family of agent teams. Under this contract, they define and measure finite-benchmark oracle regret: the gap between a policy’s performance and the best team in the legal family, conditional on G. For any finite collection of frozen routing policies, they show that running the union of all distinct teams selected by any policy is the minimal assumption-free support needed to compare all policies pairwise. They then apply COVER on multiple benchmarks (MuSiQue-12, HotpotQA-4, Llama fixed-stack execution, and a ToolSandbox variant-shift setting) with controlled tables and source-ID-disjoint splits, using pre-specified and retrospective controls to isolate routing effects and quantify route regret.

Result: Across datasets, COVER reveals that routing policies often have substantial “route regret” (unrealized performance relative to the best team in the declared family) even when raw answer accuracy shows small or ambiguous gains. On MuSiQue-12, a pre-specified privileged positive control routing setup reduces oracle regret from 0.532 to 0.402; a later public-interface control reaches 0.424 versus 0.554, though it is retrospective. On HotpotQA-4, a pre-specified public direct scorer reduces regret from 0.313 to 0.110. In a fixed-stack Llama setting, verified route regret improves by 0.190 while the raw-answer accuracy gain is only 0.010, with confidence intervals overlapping zero, highlighting how end-to-end metrics can obscure routing benefits. In a ToolSandbox variant-shift validation over 16 teams and 14 new task variants, the best oracle in the declared family achieves 0.768 safe-evidence completion, while the prospectively frozen router achieves 0.637 (regret 0.131), failing the predeclared 0.10 regret target; a later retrospective router reaches 0.655, equaling an all-workers baseline with fewer workers on average. A crossed-stack diagnostic further shows that absolute performance depends strongly on the downstream stack G, but there is no detectable interaction between the router and the finalizer in this setup.

Conclusion: COVER provides an auditable, contract-based framework for evaluating multi-agent routing policies that cleanly attributes performance differences to routing, under a fixed downstream stack and team family. It characterizes route regret and selection headroom, demonstrating that many routing policies underutilize the available team portfolio, even when raw answer gains appear minimal. The method emphasizes that conclusions are conditional on the chosen stack G and team family and does not assert stack-invariant or universal superiority of any routing scheme. Instead, it offers a principled way to compare routing policies, detect missed opportunity in team selection, and avoid overstating routing advantages in multi-agent LLM systems.

Abstract: When a multi-agent system changes its team, it also changes the messages and final answer it produces, so an end-to-end accuracy gap does not by itself identify a routing effect. We introduce method, an evaluation contract that fixes a public information boundary, downstream stack G, and finite legal team family before outcomes are generated. Complete coverage identifies exact finite-benchmark oracle regret conditional on that stack. For any finite collection of frozen policies, executing the union of their distinct selected teams is the minimal assumption-free support for every pairwise policy contrast, though not for absolute oracle regret. Two controlled tables with source-ID-disjoint splits test the instrument. On MuSiQue-12, a pre-specified privileged positive control improves regret from 0.532 to 0.402; a later public-interface control reaches 0.424 versus 0.554 but is retrospective. On HotpotQA-4, a pre-specified public direct scorer improves regret from 0.313 to 0.110. In fixed-stack Llama execution, verified route regret improves by 0.190, while the raw-answer gain is 0.010 with an interval crossing zero. A five-family ToolSandbox variant-shift validation exhaustively evaluates 16 declared teams on 14 untouched task variants (224/224 valid rows): the declared-family oracle reaches 0.768 safe-evidence completion, while the prospectively frozen router gets 0.637 (regret 0.131), failing the predeclared 0.10 criterion. A later retrospective comparator reaches 0.655, matching all-workers with 4.57 versus 5.00 workers on average. Thus COVER exposes selection headroom without manufacturing a routing win. A crossed-stack diagnostic shows absolute scores depend on G but finds no detectable router-by-finalizer interaction. COVER is an auditable measurement methodology, not a claim of stack-invariant or universal agent-routing superiority.

</details>


### [149] [AcrossVAM1.0: Particle World Modeling for Text-Assisted Robot Video Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28491)
*Yafei Zhang,Nan Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AcrossVAM1.0 is a lightweight, text-conditioned robot video prediction model that factorizes motion and appearance using object-centric semantic particles and a dual-stream decoder, improving trajectory and pixel-wise metrics but still struggling with perceptual quality and language grounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robot video prediction is hard because models must reason accurately about future motion while also preserving high-frequency appearance details. Standard end-to-end pixel prediction models entangle these two goals, often looking good on simple baselines like copying the last frame while hiding whether they truly understand motion. The paper aims to build a more interpretable, object-centric, and efficient interface for predicting robot videos, and to rigorously evaluate where such a factorized design helps or fails.

Method: The authors propose AcrossVAM1.0, which factorizes future video prediction into object-centric motion and appearance. First, a frozen SAM3-DLP codec encodes four context frames into semantic particles corresponding to robot, arm, and gripper, plus a background latent. A small (0.28M parameters) spatio-temporal Transformer aligns particle identities over time and rolls their states forward, modulated via FiLM by a frozen OpenCLIP embedding of the language instruction. A causal dual-stream decoder then decodes future frames: one stream renders motion from the predicted particles, while the other reuses appearance encoded only from the last observed frame. A residual refiner and a learned delivery mask combine these streams to generate five future frames without seeing any future appearance. The system is evaluated on a new VRS benchmark of diverse real-robot trajectories, using multiple metrics and control experiments.

Result: On the VRS benchmark, modeling explicit particle dynamics reduces trajectory error by 21.0% relative to a persistence baseline. Using three different delivery-mask seeds, AcrossVAM1.0 improves future-frame PSNR/SSIM from 19.97/0.796 to 20.573/0.8004, and raw particle-based generation increases motion-region PSNR from 11.89 to 13.23. However, the method does not yet beat persistence in LPIPS (a perceptual similarity metric), and swapping correct versus shuffled language instructions changes trajectory error by only 2.8–3.1%, indicating weak language grounding. The paper further reports oracle, negative-control, multi-seed, and per-robot analyses to characterize performance and limitations.

Conclusion: Explicit particle-based dynamics provide a promising low-dimensional and object-centric interface for robot video prediction, leading to improvements in trajectory and pixel-wise metrics over simple baselines. The AcrossVAM1.0 architecture demonstrates that factoring motion and appearance, aided by a dual-stream decoder and a lightweight Transformer, can yield better motion-region fidelity. Nonetheless, the model’s failure to surpass persistence in LPIPS and its modest sensitivity to language instructions highlight major remaining challenges in robust appearance delivery and language grounding. Future work should strengthen the coupling between language, particle dynamics, and high-frequency appearance to fully realize the benefits of this factorized design.

Abstract: Predicting robot videos requires both precise motion reasoning and preservation of high-frequency appearance, yet monolithic pixel models entangle these objectives and often conceal their progress behind a strong last-frame baseline. We present AcrossVAM1.0, a lightweight, text-assisted video action model that factorizes future prediction into object-centric motion and dense appearance. A frozen SAM3-DLP codec decomposes four context frames into semantic particles for the robot, arm, and gripper, together with a background latent. A 0.28M-parameter spatio-temporal Transformer aligns particle identities, rolls their states forward, and is modulated by a frozen OpenCLIP instruction embedding through FiLM. A causal dual-stream decoder combines particle-rendered motion with appearance encoded exclusively from the last observed frame; a residual refiner and learned delivery mask produce five future frames without access to future appearance. On our VRS benchmark constructed from diverse real-robot trajectories, particle dynamics reduce trajectory error by 21.0\% over persistence. Across three delivery-mask seeds, AcrossVAM1.0 improves future-frame PSNR/SSIM from 19.97/0.796 to 20.573/0.8004, while raw particle generation improves motion-region PSNR from 11.89 to 13.23. The delivered model does not yet beat persistence in LPIPS, and correct-versus- shuffled language changes trajectory error by only 2.8--3.1%. We report these limitations alongside oracle, negative-control, multi-seed, and per-robot analyses. The results show that explicit particle dynamics are a promising low-dimensional interface for robot video prediction, while robust language grounding and appearance delivery remain the principal open challenges.

</details>


### [150] [Training Communication-Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Language Models with Layer Re-Configuration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28511)
*Simeng Sun,Roger Waleffe*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a more communication-efficient training strategy for Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language models by restructuring where MoE layers are placed, cutting training cost without hurting performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Expert-parallel MoE models suffer from high communication overhead due to all-to-all token dispatch and combine operations, which takes a large fraction of end-to-end training time. The authors aim to reduce this communication cost while preserving or improving model quality and efficiency.

Method: They introduce Communication-Efficient MoE (CE-MoE), which uses a heterogeneous layer pattern that decouples token-mixing depth (e.g., attention or Mamba-2 layers) from channel-mixing depth (MoE/dense FFNs). Instead of inserting MoE layers after each token-mixing layer (full-MoE), they concentrate expert capacity into a small set of routed MoE layers and fill remaining depth with token-mixing and dense FFN layers. They evaluate across a scaling ladder from 2B to 31.5B parameters, controlling for total and activated parameters when comparing to baselines.

Result: Across model scales, CE-MoE matches validation loss and downstream benchmark performance of full-MoE baselines while consistently reducing training cost. At 31.5B parameters, CE-MoE achieves the same or better quality with 33.3% fewer GPU-hours and improved inference throughput.

Conclusion: Reorganizing MoE layers into a heterogeneous pattern that concentrates expert capacity can significantly cut communication and training cost without degrading model quality. CE-MoE is therefore a more efficient design for scaling MoE language models under expert parallelism.

Abstract: When training Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language models with expert parallelism, all-to-all token dispatch and combine collectives can consume a substantial fraction of end-to-end training time. In this work, we study communication-efficient MoE models (CE-MoE), in which we adopt a heterogeneous layer pattern that decouples token-mixing and channel-mixing depth. Compared to conventional models which interleave MoE layers after each token-mixing layer (e.g., attention, Mamba-2), CE-MoE models concentrate expert capacity in a select few routed MoE layers, while maintaining depth by adding additional token-mixing and dense-FFN layers. Across a scaling ladder from 2B to 31.5B total parameters, under matched total and activated parameters, CE-MoE models consistently reduce training cost while matching validation loss and downstream benchmarks with full-MoE baselines. At the 31.5B scale, CE-MoE uses 33.3\% fewer GPU-hours while improving average downstream score and inference throughput.

</details>


### [151] [InstructMesh: Selective Refinement of Generative 3D Models for Fabrication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28534)
*Faraz Faruqi,Ahmed Katary,Demircan Tas,Theresa Hradilak,Ning Zhang,Jiaji Li,Fabian Manhardt,Martin Nisser,Vrushank Phadnis,Ruofei Du,Federico Tombari,Megan Hofmann,Stefanie Mueller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: InstructMesh is an interactive tool to fix 3D models generated by AI so they become fabrication-ready, using region selection and natural-language or slider-based edits on latent representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative AI can now create 3D models from text/images, but these models often have geometric defects (e.g., incorrect thickness, holes, non-manifold parts) that break downstream uses like 3D printing or manufacturing.
