Current generative systems optimize for visual plausibility, not physical/fabrication correctness, and fixing their outputs requires expert 3D modeling skills. There is a need for an accessible way for non-experts to repair these models so they can be reliably fabricated.

Method: The authors introduce InstructMesh, an interactive post-generation refinement tool. Users selectively mark problematic regions in a generative 3D model and apply targeted geometric operations such as opening or sealing voids and adjusting local thickness. Edits are triggered either by natural language instructions or GUI sliders. Instead of working at the low-level mesh, InstructMesh manipulates an intermediate latent representation of the model, which provides robustness and reduces the expertise required. The design is informed by a prior analysis of common fabrication-related failure modes in state-of-the-art generative 3D outputs, and the system is evaluated via two user studies focusing on novice users.

Result: From the analysis, the authors catalog common fabrication-related failure modes in generative 3D outputs. In user studies, novices successfully identify fabrication-relevant issues and use InstructMesh to perform appropriate repairs. The studies also indicate that users prefer a hybrid interaction style, combining natural-language prompts with explicit slider controls, over using either modality alone.

Conclusion: InstructMesh enables non-expert users to transform visually plausible but geometrically flawed generative 3D models into fabrication-ready shapes through intuitive, localized edits. Operating in latent space yields robust geometric corrections without professional modeling skills, and user studies support both the effectiveness of this approach and the desirability of hybrid NL-plus-slider interfaces for 3D model repair tasks.

Abstract: Recent advances in generative AI allow users to create 3D models from text or images. However, these models prioritize visual plausibility over geometric accuracy, often generating results with flaws that compromise their intended use post-fabrication. We present InstructMesh, an interactive post-generation refinement tool that enables selective repair of generative 3D models through region selection and targeted operations, such as opening or sealing voids, or adjusting local thickness. Users can invoke edit operations via natural language prompts or slider controls. By operating directly on the intermediate latent representation, InstructMesh allows users to apply robust geometric corrections without requiring expert modeling skills. To inform our design, we first analyze common fabrication-related failure modes in outputs from state-of-the-art generative tools. We then conduct two user studies, demonstrating that novices can identify and perform fabrication-relevant repairs on generative outputs using InstructMesh, and revealing user preference for hybrid interfaces that combine slider controls with natural language input.

</details>


### [152] [Logos: An Agent Harness on a Cross-Process Bus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28553)
*Hanzhang Jia,Liheng Zeng,Hao Cheng,Yi Gao,Bo Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They generalize a formal calculus for dynamically composed agents to allow cross-process, fault-isolated plugins and demonstrate a concrete ROS-like harness (Logos) that preserves correctness while improving fault isolation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern software agents are built from multiple runtime-composed capabilities, often hosted within a single process. This creates a single physical failure domain: a crash or fault in one component can suspend or kill all co-resident sessions. While the spatiotemporal-composability (STC) calculus formally captures dynamic composition of such capabilities, existing implementations effectively bind all plugins to one process, inheriting this fragility. The authors want to know whether the underlying model truly requires single-process deployment, and if not, how to exploit the calculus to build a more robust, cross-process agent architecture with better fault isolation but the same formal guarantees.

Method: They analyze the spatiotemporal-composability calculus and its language model to check whether agents are intrinsically tied to one process. By exploiting the statelessness of the language model—where all cross-step state lives outside the model and soundness is defined purely over the state space—they derive four lemmas. These lemmas formalize that the calculus’s hypotheses and stateless inference rules do not require co-residency in a single process. Using these lemmas, they design and implement Logos, a ROS-like harness in which each plugin runs as its own process and the only shared state is an append-only transcript that records interactions. They then experimentally evaluate Logos, running 80 sessions and injecting process kills at four points in the tool-call cycle, comparing behavior to a single-process reference configuration under equivalent fault scenarios.

Result: From the theoretical analysis, they show that neither the STC modeling assumptions nor the calculus itself impose a single-process constraint on agents. The four lemmas establish that, given stateless inference and an externalized state space, agents can be decomposed into separate processes that jointly manipulate a shared, append-only transcript while preserving the calculus’s soundness invariant. Empirically, the Logos implementation demonstrates that 80 concurrent sessions can resume after injected faults without any repeated side effects, even when faults occur at any of the four boundaries of the tool-call cycle. In contrast, a single-process baseline shows that a fault in that process interrupts every session it hosts, confirming that traditional monolithic-process agents form a single large failure domain.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the spatiotemporal-composability calculus does not inherently bind agents to a single process and that its stateless language model naturally supports cross-process realizations. On top of this, the Logos harness demonstrates a practical design in which each plugin is an isolated process and the only coordination mechanism is an append-only transcript. This architecture preserves the formal soundness guarantees of the calculus while significantly improving fault isolation: under comparable faults, failures are confined to a single node rather than propagating to all co-resident sessions. Thus, dynamic agent systems can achieve robust, fault-tolerant behavior without sacrificing their formal composability properties.

Abstract: Modern agent systems assemble capabilities at runtime, and this dynamic composition has recently received a complete formal treat ment in the spatiotemporal-composability calculus, in which a capability is a component carrying a tracked inverse, and agents are assembled as plugins. This plugin form is carried by a single process sharing one context, a carrier that places all components in one physical failure domain, a fault suspends every component at once, and process death interrupts every session the process hosts. This paper shows that neither the modeling nor the calculus binds an agent to one process, the statelessness of the language model keeps all cross-step state outside the model, and the soundness invariant is defined on the state space alone. These observations condense into four lemmas whose premises are the hypotheses of the calculus and the statelessness of language-model inference. On these lemmas this paper constructs Logos, a ROS-like cross process agent harness in which a plugin is a process and the only shared state is an append-only transcript. Eighty sessions resume with no repeated effect after kills placed at the four boundaries of the tool-call cycle, and a same-fault comparison with a single process reference configuration shows one fault interrupting every co-resident session while under the peer-process construction one fault ends at one node.

</details>
